In Java, each statement (like the boxed line in [Figure 3-3](#c03.xhtml_c03-fig-0003)) ends with a semicolon. The other lines in [Figure 3-3](#c03.xhtml_c03-fig-0003) don't end with semicolons, because the other lines in [Figure 3-3](#c03.xhtml_c03-fig-0003) aren’t statements. For instance, the method header (the line with the word *main* in it) doesn’t directly tell the computer to do anything. The method header announces, “Just in case you ever want to do `main`, the next few lines of code tell you how to do it.”

![Remember](images/remember.png) Every complete Java statement ends with a semicolon.

### Brace yourself

Long ago, or maybe not so long ago, your schoolteachers told you how useful outlines are. With an outline, you can organize thoughts and ideas, help people see forests instead of trees, and generally show that you're a member of the Tidy Persons Club. Well, a Java program is like an outline. The program in [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001) starts with a header line that says, “Here comes a method named `main`*.*” After that header, a subheader announces, “Here comes a call to the `println` method.”

Now, if a Java program is like an outline, why doesn't a program *look* like an outline? What takes the place of the Roman numerals, capital letters, and other items? The answer is twofold:

*   In a Java program, curly braces enclose meaningful units of code.
*   You, the programmer, can (and should) indent lines so that other programmers can see at a glance the outline form of your code.

In an outline, everything is subordinate to the item in Roman numeral *I.* In a Java program, everything is subordinate to the top line. In [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001), the top line is the line with the word *main* in it. To indicate that everything else in the code is subordinate to this `main` line, you use curly braces. Everything else in the code goes inside these curly braces.

In [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml), when you start using classes, you put a program's `main` method inside its own class. Look at [Listing 3-4](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0005). In that listing, one pair of curly braces indicates that the `main` method is subordinate to a class named `TwoPlusTwo`. Another pair of curly braces indicates that the call to `println` is, in turn, subordinate to the `main` method's header line.

[LISTING 3-4](#c03.xhtml_rc03-fea-0005) Curly Braces for a Java Class

`import static io.IO.println; // Ignore this line for now // (or skip to Chapter 11).`
`class TwoPlusTwo` **`{`**
`void main()` **`{`**
`println(2 + 2);`
**`}`**
**`}`**

In an outline, some items are at the bottom of the food chain. In [Listing 3-4](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0005), the corresponding line is the line that begins with `println`. Accordingly, this `println` line goes inside all the other curly braces and is indented more than any other line.

![Remember](images/remember.png) Never lose sight of the fact that a Java program is, first and foremost, an outline.

If you put curly braces in the wrong places or omit curly braces where the braces should be, your program probably won't work. If your program works, it'll probably work incorrectly.

If you don’t indent lines of code in an informative manner, your program will still work correctly, but neither you nor any other programmer will be able to figure out what you were thinking when you wrote the code.

If you’re a visual thinker, you can picture outlines of Java programs in your head. One friend of mine visualizes an actual numbered outline morphing into a Java program. (See [Figure 3-5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fig-0005).) Another person, who shall remain nameless, uses more bizarre imagery. (See [Figure 3-6](#c03.xhtml_c03-fig-0006).)

![The image shows a flowchart illustrating the steps to create a simple Java program that prints the result of 2 + 2\. The flowchart has three levels: the first level describes the main method of the TwoPlusTwo class, which prints 2 + 2\. The second level shows the structure of the class and method in Java syntax. The third level provides the complete Java code for the TwoPlusTwo class with the main method that prints the result of 2 + 2.](images/9781394289240-fg0305.png)

[FIGURE 3-5:](#c03.xhtml_rc03-fig-0005) An outline turns into a Java program.

![Three fish with angry expressions are depicted in a row. Each fish contains a segment of Java code. The first fish has 'class TwoPlusTwo { }', the second fish has 'void main() { }', and the third fish has 'println(2 + 2);'.](images/9781394289240-fg0306.png)

[FIGURE 3-6:](#c03.xhtml_rc03-fig-0006) A class is bigger than a method; a method is bigger than a statement.

![Tip](images/tip.png) I appreciate a good excuse as much as the next guy, but failing to indent your Java code is inexcusable. In fact, many IDEs have tools to indent your code automatically. In your favorite IDE, look for menus such as Source ⇒   Format or Code ⇒   Reformat Code.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Here are some things for you to try to help you understand the material in this section. If trying these things builds your confidence, that's good. If trying these things makes you question what you've read, that's good too. If trying these things makes you nervous, don't be discouraged. You can find answers and other help at this book's website ([`https://JavaForDummies.allmycode.com`](https://javafordummies.allmycode.com/)). You can also email me with your questions ([`JavaForDummies@allmycode.com`](mailto:JavaForDummies@allmycode.com)).

#### BEGIN THE BEGUINE

*   If you've downloaded the code from this book's website, import [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001) (from the downloaded `03-01` folder) into your IDE. If you don't plan to download the code, create a new project in your IDE. In the new project, create a Java file containing the code from [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001). With the downloaded code, or with your own, newly-created code, run the program and look for the number `4` in the output.
*   Try running the code in [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001) with the text `2 + 2` changed to “Four”. (Remember to surround `Four` with quotation marks (`"`).) What happens?
*   Try running the code in [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001) with the text `2 + 2` changed to Four. (In this example, try *not* surrounding `Four` with quotation marks (`"`).) What happens?

#### CAPITAL PAINS

*   Try to run the code in [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001) with the word `main` (all lowercase) changed to `Main` (starting with an uppercase letter). What happens?
*   Try to run the code in [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001) with the word `println` (all lowercase) changed to `PrintLn` (with two uppercase letters). What happens?
*   In [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001), change the line

    `println(2 + 2);`

    to

    `System.out.println(2 + 2);`

    Make sure that this modified code runs.

*   Following up on the previous experiment, try to run the code in [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001) with the word `System` (starting with an uppercase letter) changed to `system` (all lowercase). What happens?

#### STOLEN SEMICOLON

*   Try to run the code in [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001) with the semicolon missing. What happens?
*   Try to run the code in [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001) with additional semicolons added at the ends of some of the lines. What happens?

#### MISCELLANEOUS MISCHIEF

*   Try to run the code in [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001) with the indentation changed. For example, don't indent the middle line. What happens?
*   Try to run the code in [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001) with no line breaks. Put all the code on one long line. What happens?
*   Try to run the code in [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001) with the word `println` changed to `print1n` (with the digit 1 near the end). What happens?
*   Try to run the code in [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001) with the text `2 + 2` changed to `"Use a straight quote \", not a curly quote \u201D"`. (Remember to type all three quotation marks (`"`).) What happens?

## And Now, a Few Comments

People gather around campfires to hear the old legend about a programmer whose laziness got her into trouble. To maintain this programmer's anonymity, I call her Jane Pro. Jane worked many months to create the holy grail of computing: a program that thinks on its own. If completed, this program could work independently, learning new concepts without human intervention. Day after day, night after night, Jane Pro labored to give the program that spark of creative, independent thought.

One day, when Jane Pro was almost finished with the project, she received a disturbing piece of paper mail from her health insurance company. No, the mail wasn't about a serious illness. It was about a routine office visit. The insurance company’s claim form had a place for Jane’s date of birth, as though her date of birth had changed since the last time she sent in a claim. She had absentmindedly scribbled *2016* as her year of birth, so the insurance company refused to pay the bill.

Jane dialed the insurance company’s phone number. Within 20 minutes, she was talking to a live person. “I’m sorry,” said the live person. “To resolve this issue, you must dial a different number.” Well, you can guess what happened next. “I’m sorry. The other operator gave you the wrong number.” And then, “I’m sorry. You must call back the original phone number.”

Five months later, Jane’s ear ached, but after 800 hours on the phone, she had finally gotten a tentative promise that the insurance company would eventually reprocess the claim. Elated as she was, she was anxious to get back to her programming project. Could she remember what all those lines of code were supposed to be doing?

No, she couldn’t. Jane stared and stared at her own work and, like a dream that doesn’t make sense the next morning, the code was completely meaningless to her. She had written a million lines of code, and not one line was accompanied by an informative explanatory comment. She had left no clues to help her understand what she’d been thinking, so in frustration she abandoned the whole project.

### Adding comments to your code

[Listing 3-5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006) holds an enhanced version of this chapter’s sample program. In addition to all the keywords, identifiers, and punctuation, [Listing 3-5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006) has text that’s meant for human beings to read.

[LISTING 3-5](#c03.xhtml_rc03-fea-0006) Three Kinds of Comments

**`/*`**
`* Listing 3-5 in "Java For Dummies, 9th Edition"`
`*`
`* Copyright 2024 Wiley Publishing, Inc.`
`* All rights reserved.`
**`*/`**

**`/**`**
`* This code adds 2 and 2.`
`*`
`* @author Barry Burd`
`* @version 1.0 6/24/24`
`* @see java.io.IO`
**`*/`**

**`/**`**
`* The main method is where execution of the code begins.`
`* This main method uses Java 23 preview features. These`
`* features might not be available in future Java releases.`
**`*/`**

`void main() {`
`println(2 + 2);` **`//`**`Replace 2 with Math.sqrt(2)`
`}`

A *comment* is a special section of text, inside a program, whose purpose is to help people understand the program. A comment is part of a good program’s documentation.

The Java programming language has three kinds of comments:

*   **Traditional comments:** The first six lines of [Listing 3-5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006) form one *traditional* comment. The comment begins with `/*` and ends with `*/`. Everything between the opening `/*` and the closing `*/` is for human eyes only. No information about `"Java For Dummies, 9th Edition"` or `Wiley Publishing, Inc.` is translated by the compiler.

    ![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) To read about compilers, see [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml).

    The second, third, fourth, and fifth lines in [Listing 3-5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006) have extra asterisks (*). I call them “extra” because these asterisks aren't required when you create a comment. They just make the comment look pretty. I include them in [Listing 3-5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006) because, for some reason that I don't entirely understand, most Java programmers add these extra asterisks.

*   **End-of-line comments:** The text `//Replace 2 with Math.sqrt(2)` in [Listing 3-5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006) is an end-of-line comment. An *end-of-line* comment starts with two slashes and goes to the end of a line of type. Once again, the compiler doesn’t translate the text inside the end-of-line comment.
*   **Javadoc comments:** A *Javadoc* comment begins with a slash and two asterisks (`/**`). [Listing 3-5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006) has two Javadoc comments: One starts with the text `This code adds 2 and 2.` and another starts with `The main method is where`… .

    A *Javadoc* comment, which is a special kind of traditional comment, is meant to be read by people who never even look at the Java code. But that doesn't make sense. How can you see the Javadoc comments in [Listing 3-5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006) if you never look at [Listing 3-5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006)?

    Well, a certain program called *Javadoc* (what else?) can find all the Javadoc comments in [Listing 3-5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006) and turn these comments into a nice-looking web page. [Figure 3-7](#c03.xhtml_c03-fig-0007) shows the page.

![Screenshot of a Java class documentation page for a class named TwoPlusTwo. The class extends java.lang.Object and contains a single method, main(), which is package-private and void. The main method is where the execution of the code begins. The page also lists methods inherited from java.lang.Object, including clone(), equals(), finalize(), getClass(), hashCode(), notify(), notifyAll(), toString(), wait(), and wait(long, int). The Method Details section provides a brief description of the main method, noting that it uses Java 23 preview features.](images/9781394289240-fg0307.png)

[FIGURE 3-7:](#c03.xhtml_rc03-fig-0007) The Javadoc page generated from the code in [Listing 3-5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006).

Javadoc comments are great. Here are several great things about them:

*   The only person who has to look at a piece of Java code is the programmer who writes the code. Other people who use the code can find out what the code does by viewing the automatically generated web page.
*   Because other people don't look at the Java code, other people don’t make changes to the Java code. (In other words, other people don’t introduce errors into the existing Java code.)
*   Because other people don’t look at the Java code, other people don’t have to decipher the inner workings of the Java code. All these people need to know about the code is what they read on the code’s web page.
*   The programmer doesn’t create two separate files — some Java code over here and some documentation about the code over there. Instead, the programmer creates one piece of Java code and embeds the documentation (in the form of Javadoc comments) right inside the code.
*   The generation of web pages from Javadoc comments is automatic. So everyone’s documentation has the same format. No matter whose Java code you use, you find out about that code by reading a page like the one in [Figure 3-7](#c03.xhtml_c03-fig-0007). That’s good because the format in [Figure 3-7](#c03.xhtml_c03-fig-0007) is familiar to anyone who uses Java.
*   Best of all, Java's online API Specification, which describes all of Java's standard identifiers, comes entirely from runs of the *Javadoc* program on comments in Java code. There's nothing arbitrary or haphazard about Java's API Specification. The Specification is always in sync with the actual Java code.

![Tip](images/tip.png) You can generate your own web pages from the Javadoc comments that you put in your code. From your IDE's menu bar, choose Tools or Project and look for a command labeled Generate JavaDoc.

### What’s Barry’s excuse?

For years I’ve been telling my students to put comments in their code, and for years I’ve been creating sample code (like the code in [Listing 3-1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0001)) with no comments in it. Why?

Three little words: *Know your audience.* When you write complicated, real-life code, your audience is other programmers, information technology managers, and people who need help deciphering what you’ve done. When I write simple samples of code for this book, my audience is you — the novice Java programmer. Rather than read my comments, your best strategy is to stare at my Java statements — the statements that Java’s compiler deciphers. That’s why I put so few comments in this book’s listings.

Besides, I’m a little lazy.

### Using comments to experiment with your code

You may hear programmers talk about *commenting out* certain parts of their code. When you’re writing a program and something’s not working correctly, it often helps to try removing some of the code. If nothing else, you find out what happens when that suspicious code is removed. Of course, you may not like what happens when the code is removed, so you don’t want to delete the code completely. Instead, you turn your ordinary Java statements into comments. For instance, you turn the statement

`println(2 + 2);`

into the comment

`//println(2 + 2);`

This change keeps the Java compiler from seeing the code while you try to figure out what’s wrong with your program.

Traditional comments can be tricky when you're trying to comment out code. The big problem is that you can’t put one traditional comment inside another. Suppose that you want to comment out the following statements:

`println(2 + 2);`
`println(2 + 3);`
`/*`
`* Intentionally displays on four separate lines`
`*/`
`println(2 + 4);`
`println(2 + 5);`

If you try to turn this code into one traditional comment, you get the following mess:

**`/*`**
`println(2 + 2);`
`println(2 + 3);`
`/*`
`* Intentionally displays on four separate lines`
**`*/`**
`println(2 + 4);`
`println(2 + 5);`
`*/`

The first `*/` (after `Intentionally displays`) ends the traditional comment prematurely. Then the `2 + 4` and `2 + 5` statements aren't commented out, and the last `*/` chokes the compiler.

![Tip](images/tip.png) Most IDEs can comment out sections of your code for you automatically. From your IDE's menu bar, choose Code or Source and look for menu items pertaining to comments.
