Chapter 4

# Making the Most of Variables and Their Values

IN THIS CHAPTER

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Assigning values to things**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Making things store certain types of values**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Applying operators to get new values**

The following conversation between Mr. Van Doren and Mr. Barasch never took place:

*   *Charles:* A sea squirt eats its brain, turning itself from an animal into a plant.
*   *Jack:* Is that your final answer, Charles?
*   *Charles:* Yes, it is.
*   *Jack:* How much money do you have in your account today, Charles?
*   *Charles:* I have fifty dollars and twenty-two cents in my checking account.
*   *Jack:* Well, you had better call the IRS, because your sea squirt answer is correct. You just won a million dollars to add to your checking account. What do you think of that, Charles?
*   *Charles:* I owe it all to honesty, diligence, and hard work, Jack.

Some aspects of this dialogue can be represented in Java by a few lines of code.

## Varying a Variable

No matter how you acquire your million dollars, you can use a variable to tally your wealth. [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001) shows the code.

[LISTING 4-1](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fea-0001) Using a Variable

`amountInAccount = 50.22;`
`amountInAccount = amountInAccount + 1000000.00;`

![Warning](images/warning.png) The code in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001) isn't a complete Java program. If you try to run this code the same way you run the example in [Chapter 3](#c03.xhtml), you'll get an error message. For a complete, runnable program, see [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002).

The code in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001) makes use of the `amountInAccount` variable. A *variable* is a placeholder. You can stick a number like 50.22 into a variable. After you place a number in the variable, you can change your mind and put a different number into the variable. (That's what varies in a variable.) Of course, when you put a new number in a variable, the old number is no longer there. If you didn't save the old number somewhere else, the old number is gone.

[Figure 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0001) gives a before-and-after picture of the code in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001). After the first statement in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001) is executed, the variable `amountInAccount` has the number 50.22 in it. Then, after the second statement of [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001) is executed, the `amountInAccount` variable suddenly has 1000050.22 in it. When you think about a variable, picture a place in the computer's memory where wires and transistors store 50.22, 1000050.22, or whatever. On the left side of [Figure 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0001), imagine that the box with 50.22 in it is surrounded by millions of other such boxes.

![The image shows a black background with the word “hello” written in white lowercase letters in the center. This simple and clear design can be used for various purposes, such as a greeting or a minimalist graphic element.](images/9781394289240-fg0401.png)

[FIGURE 4-1:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0001) A variable (before and after).

Now you need some terminology. The thing stored in a variable is a *value.* A variable's value can change during the run of a program (when Jack gives you a million bucks, for instance). The value that’s stored in a variable isn’t necessarily a number. (For instance, you can create a variable that always stores a letter.) The kind of value that’s stored in a variable is a variable’s *type.*

You can read more about types in the section “[The types of values that variables may have](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0005),” later in this chapter.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) A subtle, almost unnoticeable difference exists between a variable and a variable’s *name.* Even in formal writing, I often use the word *variable* when I mean *variable name.* Strictly speaking, `amountInAccount` is a variable name, and all the memory storage associated with `amountInAccount` (including the type that `amountInAccount` has and whatever value `amountInAccount` currently represents) is the variable itself. If you think this distinction between *variable* and *variable name* is too subtle for you to worry about, join the club.

Every variable name is an identifier — a name that you can make up in your own code. In preparing [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001), I made up the name *amountInAccount.*

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) For more information on the kinds of names in a Java program, see [Chapter 3](#c03.xhtml).

Before the sun sets on [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001), you need to notice one more part of the listing. The listing has `50.22` and `1000000.00` in it. Anybody in their right mind would call these things *numbers,* but in a Java program it helps to call these things *literals.*

And what's so literal about `50.22` and `1000000.00`? Well, think about the variable `amountInAccount` in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001). The variable `amountInAccount` stands for 50.22 some of the time, but it stands for 1000050.22 the rest of the time. You could use the word *number* to talk about `amountInAccount`. But really, what `amountInAccount` stands for depends on the fashion of the moment. On the other hand, 50.22 literally stands for the value 50![math](images/eq04001.png).

![Tip](images/tip.png) You can add underscores to numeric literals. Rather than use the plain old `1000000.00` in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001), you can write `amountInAccount = amountInAccount + 1_000_000.00`. Unfortunately, you can't easily do what you're most tempted to do. You can't write `1,000,000.00` (as you would in the United States), nor can you write `1.000.000,00` (as you would in Germany). If you want to display a number such as `1,000,000.00` in the program's output, you have to use some fancy formatting tricks. For more information about formatting, check [Chapters 7](#c07.xhtml) and [12](#c12.xhtml).

### Assignment statements

Statements like the ones in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001) are called assignment statements. In an *assignment statement,* you assign a value to something. In many cases, this something is a variable.

I recommend getting into the habit of reading assignment statements from right to left. [Figure 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0002) illustrates the action of the first line in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001).

![A flowchart diagram illustrating the assignment of the value 50.22 to the variable amountInAccount. The diagram shows the statement 'amountInAccount = 50.22;' with arrows indicating the assignment process. The text 'Assign 50.22...' is at the top, and '...to the amountInAccount variable.' is at the bottom.](images/9781394289240-fg0402.png)

[FIGURE 4-2:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0002) The action of the first line in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001).

The second line in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001) is just a bit more complicated. [Figure 4-3](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0003) illustrates the action of the second line in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001).

![The image is a diagram that illustrates the process of updating a variable in a programming context. It breaks down the steps of adding a specific value to an existing variable and then updating the variable with the new value. This is relevant for understanding basic programming operations involving variables and arithmetic.](images/9781394289240-fg0403.png)

[FIGURE 4-3:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0003) The action of the second line in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001).

![Remember](images/remember.png) In an assignment statement, the thing being assigned a value is always on the left side of the equal sign.

### The types of values that variables may have

Have you seen the TV commercials that make you think you're flying among the circuits inside a computer? Pretty cool, eh? These commercials show 0s (zeros) and 1s (ones) sailing by because 0s and 1s are the only things that computers can deal with. When you think a computer is storing the letter *J,* the computer is really storing 01001010. Everything inside the computer is a sequence of 0s and 1s. As every computer geek knows, a 0 or 1 is called a *bit.*

As it turns out, the sequence 01001010, which stands for the letter *J,* can also stand for the number 74\. The same sequence can also stand for 1.0369608636003646 × 10^(–43). In fact, if the bits are interpreted as screen pixels, the same sequence can be used to represent the dots shown in [Figure 4-4](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0004). The meaning of 01001010 depends on the way the software interprets this sequence of 0s and 1s.

![A 3x3 grid of squares, each containing a circle. The circles are either filled in black or left empty. The pattern alternates between filled and empty circles in a specific sequence.](images/9781394289240-fg0404.png)

[FIGURE 4-4:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0004) An extreme close-up of eight black-and-white screen pixels.

How do you tell the computer what 01001010 stands for? The answer is in the concept of type. The *type* of a variable is the range of values that the variable is permitted to store. I copied the lines from [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001) and put them into a complete Java program. The program is in [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002). When I run the program in [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002), I get the output shown in [Figure 4-5](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0005).

![The image shows a text message indicating that an account has a balance of $1,000,050.22\. This is interesting or relevant because it shows a substantial amount of money in the account, which could be significant for financial or personal reasons.](images/9781394289240-fg0405.png)

[FIGURE 4-5:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0005) Running the program in [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002).

[LISTING 4-2](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fea-0002) Adding a Million to Your Account

`void main() {`
`double amountInAccount;`

`amountInAccount = 50.22;`
`amountInAccount = amountInAccount + 1000000.00;`

`print("You have $");`
`print(amountInAccount);`
`println(" in your account.");`
`}`

In [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002), look at the first line in the body of the main method:

`double amountInAccount;`

This line is called a *variable declaration.* Putting this line in your program is like saying, “I'm declaring my intention to have a variable named *amountInAccount* in my program.” This line reserves the name *amountInAccount* for your use in the program.

In this variable declaration, the word *double* is a Java keyword. This word *double* tells the computer what kinds of values you intend to store in `amountInAccount`. In particular, the word *double* stands for numbers between –1.8 × 10^(308) and 1.8 × 10^(308). (These are enormous numbers with 308 zeros before the decimal point. Only the world's richest people write checks with 308 zeros in them. The second of these numbers is one-point-eight gazazzo-zillion-kaskillion. The number 1.8 × 10^(308), a constant defined by the International Bureau of Weights and Measures, is the number of eccentric computer programmers between Sunnyvale, California, and the M31 Andromeda Galaxy.)

More important than the humongous range of the `double` keyword's numbers is the fact that a `double` value can have digits beyond the decimal point. After you declare `amountInAccount` to be of type `double`, you can store all sorts of numbers in `amountInAccount`. You can store `50.22`, `0.02398479`, or `–3.0`. In [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002), if I hadn't declared `amountInAccount` to be of type `double`, I may not have been able to store `50.22`. Instead, I would have had to store plain old `50`, with no digits beyond the decimal point.

Another type — type `float` — also allows you to have digits beyond the decimal point. But `float` values aren't as accurate as `double` values.

![Tip](images/tip.png) In many situations, you have a choice. You can declare certain values to be either `float` values or `double` values. But don't sweat the choice between `float` and `double`. For most programs, just use `double`. With today's fancy processors, the space you save using the `float` type is almost never worth the loss of accuracy. (For more details, see the nearby sidebar, “[To the decimal point and beyond!](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0007)”)

![On the web](images/ontheweb.png) The big million-dollar jackpot in [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002) is impressive. But [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002) doesn't illustrate the best way to deal with dollar amounts. In a Java program, the best way to represent currency is to shun the `double` and `float` types and opt instead for a type named `BigDecimal`. For more information, see this book's website ([`https://JavaForDummies.allmycode.com`](https://javafordummies.allmycode.com/)).

### How to hold the line

The last three statements in [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002) use a neat formatting trick. You want to display several different items on a single line on the screen. You put these items in separate statements. All but the last of the statements are calls to `print`. (The last statement is a call to `println`.) Calls to `print` display text on part of a line and then leave the cursor at the end of the current line. After executing `print`, the cursor is still at the end of the same line, so the next `print` or `println` can continue printing on that same line. With several calls to `print` capped off by a single call to `println`, the result is just one nice-looking line of output. (Refer to [Figure 4-5](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0005).)

![Remember](images/remember.png) A call to `print` writes some things and leaves the cursor sitting at the end of the line of output. A call to `println` writes things and then finishes the job by moving the cursor to the start of a brand-new line of output.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Run the code in [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002) to make sure that it runs correctly on your computer. Then see what happens when you make the following changes:

#### NUMBER FORMAT EXPERIMENTS

*   Add thousands-separators to the number `1000000.00` in the code. For example, if you live in the United States, where the thousands-separator is a comma, change the number to `1,000,000.00` and see what happens. (Hint: Nothing good happens.)
*   Try using underscores as thousands-separators in the code. That is, change `1000000.00` to `1_000_000.00` and see what happens.
*   Add a currency symbol to the number `50.22` in the code. For example, if you live in the United States, where the currency symbol is `$`, see what happens when you change the first assignment statement to `amountInAccount = $50.22`.

#### HOW TO DISPLAY VALUES

*   [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002) has two `print` statements and one `println` statement. Change all three to `println` statements and then run the program.
*   The code in [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002) displays one line of text in its output. Using the `amountInAccount` variable, add statements to the program so that it displays a second line of text. Have the second line of text be “Now you have even more! You have 2000000.00 in your account.”

### Numbers without decimal points

“In 1995, the average family had 2.3 children.”

At this point, a wise guy always remarks that no real family has exactly 2.3 children. Clearly, whole numbers have a role in this world. Therefore, in Java, you can declare a variable to store nothing but whole numbers. [Listing 4-3](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004) shows a program that uses whole number variables.

[LISTING 4-3](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fea-0004) Stuffing People in an Elevator

`void main() {`
`int weightOfAPerson;`
`int elevatorWeightLimit;`
`int numberOfPeople;`

`weightOfAPerson = 150;`
`elevatorWeightLimit = 1400;`
`numberOfPeople = elevatorWeightLimit / weightOfAPerson;`

`print("You can fit ");`
`print(numberOfPeople);`
`println(" people on the elevator.");`
`}`

The story behind the program in [Listing 4-3](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004) takes some heavy-duty explaining. Here goes:

You have a hotel elevator whose weight capacity is 1,400 pounds. One weekend the hotel hosts the Brickenchicker family reunion. A certain branch of the Brickenchicker family has been blessed with identical decuplets (ten siblings, all with the same physical characteristics). Normally, each of the Brickenchicker decuplets weighs exactly 145 pounds. But on Saturday the family has a big catered lunch, and, because lunch included strawberry shortcake, each of the Brickenchicker decuplets now weighs 150 pounds. Immediately after lunch, all ten of the Brickenchicker decuplets arrive at the elevator at exactly the same time. (Why not? All ten of them think alike.) So, the question is, how many of the decuplets can fit on the elevator?

Now remember, if you put one ounce more than 1,400 pounds of weight on the elevator, the elevator cable breaks, plunging all decuplets on the elevator to their sudden (and costly) deaths.

The answer to the Brickenchicker riddle (the output of the program of [Listing 4-3](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004)) is shown in [Figure 4-6](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0006).

![The image shows a text message indicating that an elevator can accommodate up to 9 people. This information is relevant for ensuring safety and avoiding overloading the elevator.](images/9781394289240-fg0406.png)

[FIGURE 4-6:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0006) Save the Brickenchickers.

At the core of the Brickenchicker elevator problem, you have whole numbers — numbers with no digits beyond the decimal point. When you divide 1,400 by 150, you get 9⅓, but you shouldn't take the ⅓ seriously. No matter how hard you try, you can't squeeze an extra 50 pounds’ worth of Brickenchicker decuplet onto the elevator. This fact is reflected nicely in Java. In [Listing 4-3](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004), all three variables (`weightOfAPerson`, `elevatorWeightLimit`, and `numberOfPeople`) are of type `int`. An `int` value is a whole number. When you divide one `int` value by another (as you do with the slash in [Listing 4-3](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004)), you get another `int`. When you divide 1,400 by 150, you get 9 — not 9⅓. You see this in [Figure 4-6](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0006). Taken together, the following statements display 9 onscreen:

`numberOfPeople = elevatorWeightLimit / weightOfAPerson;`

`print(numberOfPeople);`

#### TAKE A FLYING LEAP

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) My wife and I were married on February 29, so we have one anniversary every four years. Write a program with a variable named `years`. Based on the value of the `years` variable, the program displays the number of anniversaries we've had. For example, if the value of `years` is 4, the program displays the sentence `Number of anniversaries: 1`. If the value of `years` is 7, the program still displays `Number of anniversaries: 1`. But if the value of years is 8, the program displays `Number of anniversaries: 2`.

### Combining declarations and initializing variables

Look back at [Listing 4-3](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004). In that listing, you see three variable declarations — one for each of the program's three `int` variables. I could have done the same thing with just one declaration:

`int weightOfAPerson, elevatorWeightLimit, numberOfPeople;`

![Warning](images/warning.png) If two variables have completely different types, you can't create both variables in the same declaration. For instance, to create an `int` variable named *weightOfFred* and a `double` variable named *amountInFredsAccount,* you need two separate variable declarations.

You can give variables their starting values in a declaration. In [Listing 4-3](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004), for instance, one declaration can replace several lines in the `main` method (all but the calls to `print` and `println`):

`int weightOfAPerson = 150, elevatorWeightLimit = 1400,`
`numberOfPeople = elevatorWeightLimit / weightOfAPerson;`

When you do this, you don't say that you're assigning values to variables. The pieces of the declarations with equal signs in them aren’t really called assignment statements. Instead, you say that you’re *initializing* the variables. Believe it or not, keeping this distinction in mind is helpful.

For example, when you initialize a variable inside a program's `main` method, you don't have to specify the new variable's type. You can start [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002) like this:

`void main() {`
**`var`** `amountInAccount = 0.0;`

If you want, you can start [Listing 4-3](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004) this way:

`void main() {`
**`var`** `weightOfAPerson = 150;`
**`var`** `elevatorWeightLimit = 1400;`
**`var`** `numberOfPeople = elevatorWeightLimit / weightOfAPerson;`

In either case, the word `var` replaces the name of a type. This trick works because Java is smart. When you write

**`var`** `amountInAccount = 0.0;`

Java looks at the number `0.0` and realizes that `amountInAccount` is a `double` value. It's a number with digits to the right of the decimal point. In the same way, Java sees

**`var`** `weightOfAPerson = 150;`

and figures out on its own that `weightOfAPerson` has to be an `int` value.

This `var` business doesn't work if you don't initialize your new variable. For example, the lines

**`// BAD CODE:`**
`var numberOfCats;`
`numberOfCats = 3;`

are unacceptable as far as Java is concerned. Java can't wait until the assignment statement `numberOfCats = 3` to decide what type of variable `numberOfCats` is. Java wants to know immediately.

![Warning](images/warning.png) There are other situations in which the use of `var` is illegal, even with an initialization. For details, see [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml).

Like everything else in life, initializing a variable has advantages and disadvantages:

*   **When you combine six lines of** [**Listing** **4-3**](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004) **into just one declaration, the code becomes more concise.** Sometimes concise code is easier to read. Sometimes it's not. As a programmer, it's your judgment call.
*   **By initializing a variable, you might automatically avoid certain programming errors.** For an example, see [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml).
*   **In some situations, you have no choice. The nature of your code forces you either to initialize or not to initialize.** For an example that doesn't lend itself to variable initialization, see the deleting-evidence program in [Chapter 6](#c06.xhtml).

## Experimenting with JShell

[Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001) comes with a big warning. The listing is missing its `void main()` header, so the code in the listing is incomplete. And yet, the text in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001) isn't entirely meaningless. You should be able to experiment with these two lines of code.

And, in fact, you can! Java comes with a tool named *JShell*. With one tiny modification, you can use JShell to run the bare-bones code in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001). In fact, JShell allows you to experiment interactively with all kinds of code snippets. When you use JShell, you hardly ever type an entire program. Instead, you type a Java statement, and then JShell responds to your statement, and then you type a second statement, and then JShell responds to your second statement, and then you type a third statement, and so on. A single statement is enough to get a response from JShell.

Fun fact: A tool like JShell, which responds immediately to each statement the programmer types, is called a *Read Evaluate Print Loop* (REPL). Many programming languages have REPLs. JShell is the “official” REPL of Java.

![On the web](images/ontheweb.png) Instructions for launching JShell differ from one computer to the next. For some tips to help you run JShell on your computer, visit this book's website ([`http://JavaForDummies.allmycode.com`](http://javafordummies.allmycode.com/)).

![Warning](images/warning.png) Some folks have tweaked JShell to make its behavior a bit different from what I describe in this book. If you're running IntelliJ IDEA's JShell Console or some other specialized JShell variant, be sure to check the vendor's documentation.

In [Figure 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0007), I use JShell to find out how Java responds to the assignment statements in [Listings 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002) and [4-3](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004).

![Screenshot of a JShell session displaying various arithmetic operations and variable assignments, including defining and modifying variables, performing arithmetic operations like addition and division, and displaying results.](images/9781394289240-fg0407.png)

[FIGURE 4-7:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0007) An intimate conversation between JShell and me.

When you run JShell, the dialogue goes something like this:

`jshell>` *`You type a statement`*
*`JShell responds`*

`jshell>` *`You type another statement`*
*`JShell responds`*

For example, in [Figure 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0007), I type `double amountInAccount` and then press Enter. JShell responds by displaying

`amountInAccount ==> 0.0`

Here are a few things to notice about JShell:

*   **You don't have to type an entire Java program.**

    Typing a few statements such as

    `double amountInAccount`
    `amountInAccount = 50.22`
    `amountInAccount = amountInAccount + 1000000.00`

    does the trick. It's like running the code snippet in [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001) (except that [Listing 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0001) doesn't declare `amountInAccount` to be a `double`).

*   **In JShell, semicolons are (to a large extent) optional.**

    In [Figure 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0007), I type a semicolon at the end of only one of my nine lines.

    ![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) For some advice about using semicolons in JShell, see [Chapter 5](#c05.xhtml).

*   **JShell responds immediately after you type each line.**

    After I declare `amountInAccount` to be `double`, JShell responds by telling me that the `amountInAccount` variable has the value `0.0`. After I type `amountInAccount = amountInAccount + 1000000.00`, JShell tells me that the new value of `amountInAccount` is `1000050.22`.

*   **You can mix statements from many different Java programs.**

    In [Figure 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0007), I mix statements from the programs in [Listings 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0003) and [4-3](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004). JShell doesn't care.

*   **You can ask JShell for the value of an expression.**

    You don't have to assign the expression's value to a variable. For example, in [Figure 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0007), I type

    `elevatorWeightLimit / weightOfAPerson`

    JShell responds by telling me that the value of `elevatorWeightLimit / weightOfAPerson` is `9`. JShell makes up a temporary name for that value. In [Figure 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0007), the name happens to be `$8`. So, on the next line in [Figure 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0007), I ask for the value of `$8 +1`, and JShell gives me the answer `10`.

*   **You can even get answers from JShell without using variables.**

    On the last line in [Figure 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0007), I ask for the value of `42 + 7`, and JShell generously answers with the value `49`.

![Tip](images/tip.png) While you're running JShell, you don't have to retype commands that you've already typed. You don't even have to copy and paste commands. If you press the up-arrow key once, JShell shows you the command that you typed most recently. If you press the up-arrow key twice, JShell shows you the next-to-last command that you typed. And so on. When JShell shows you a command, you can press the left- and right-arrow keys to move to any character in the middle of the command. You can modify characters in the command. Finally, when you press Enter, JShell executes the newly modified command.

To end your run of JShell, you type **/exit** (starting with a slash). But `/exit` is only one of many commands you can give to JShell. To ask JShell what other kinds of commands you can use, type **/help**.

With JShell, you can test your statements before you put them into a full-blown Java program. That makes JShell a truly useful tool.

![Tip](images/tip.png) There are many ways to run JShell. If you can't get it running on your own computer, visit [`https://tryjshell.org/`](https://tryjshell.org/) or [`https://onecompiler.com/jshell/`](https://onecompiler.com/jshell/) to run JShell in your web browser. For a user-friendly interface to JShell, you can install Java and the JShell Easy extension in Visual Studio Code. If you're familiar with Python’s Jupyter notebook environment, you can visit [`https://github.com/dflib/jjava`](https://github.com/dflib/jjava) and download the JJava kernel for JShell. (See [Figure 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0008).)

![The image demonstrates how the same Java code snippet behaves in two different interactive environments: JShell and a Jupyter notebook. This comparison is interesting because it highlights the versatility and consistency of Java code execution across different platforms, which can be relevant for developers working in various development environments.](images/9781394289240-fg0408.png)

[FIGURE 4-8:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0008) JShell Easy and the JJava Jupyter kernel.

#### SHELL GAME

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Visit this book's website ([`http://JavaForDummies.allmycode.com`](http://javafordummies.allmycode.com/)) for instructions on launching JShell on your computer. After launching JShell, type a few lines of code from [Figure 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0007). See what happens when you type some slightly different lines.

## The Atoms: Java's Primitive Types

The words *int* and *double* that I describe in the previous sections are examples of *primitive types* (also known as *simple* types) in Java. The Java language has exactly eight primitive types. As a newcomer to Java, you can pretty much ignore all but four of these types. (As programming languages go, Java is nice and compact that way.) [Table 4-1](#c04.xhtml_c04-tbl-0001) shows the complete list of primitive types.

| **Type Name** | **What a Literal Looks Like** | **Range of Values** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| *Whole number types* |  |  |
| `byte` | `(byte)42` | –128 to 127 |
| `short` | `(short)42` | –32768 to 32767 |
| `int` | `42` | –2147483648 to 2147483647 |
| `long` | `42L` | –9223372036854775808 to 9223372036854775807 |
| *Decimal number types* |  |  |
| `float` | `42.0F` | –3.4 × 10^(38) to 3.4 × 10^(38) |
| `double` | `42.0` | –1.8 × 10^(308) to 1.8 × 10^(308) |
| *Character type* |  |  |
| `char` | `'A'` | Thousands of characters, glyphs, and symbols |
| *Logical type* |  |  |
| `boolean` | `true` | true, false |

[TABLE 4-1](#c04.xhtml_rc04-tbl-0001) Java's Primitive Types

The types that you shouldn't ignore are `int`, `double`, `char`, and `boolean`. Previous sections in this chapter cover the `int` and `double` types. So the next two sections cover `char` and `boolean` types.

### The char type

What in the world is a Jhlzhy Jpwoly?

In the Roman (English) alphabet, the letter *J* comes seven letters after the letter *C*, and *h* comes seven letters after *a*. If you do this 7-letter trick with all the letters in *Jhlzhy Jpwoly*, you spell *Caesar Cipher*. It's the way Roman general Julius encrypted his secret messages.

The Java type that's used to store characters is called *char.* The program in [Listing 4-4](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0006) uses Java's `char` type to find the letter in the alphabet that comes seven places before *J*. The code interprets only one letter, and it wouldn't work if that letter came before *H* in the alphabet. But let's not worry about that now.

[LISTING 4-4](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fea-0006) Seven Letters Before *J*

`void main() {`
`char oneLetter = 'J' - 7;`
`println(oneLetter);`
`}`

[Figure 4-9](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0009) shows the program's output.

![Screenshot of an IDE showing the output of a program. The output includes a file path, a single character 'C', and a message indicating the process finished with exit code 0.](images/9781394289240-fg0409.png)

[FIGURE 4-9:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0009) An exciting run of the program of [Listing 4-4](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0006) as it appears in IntelliJ's Run tool window.

In [Listing 4-4](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0006), notice how *J* is surrounded by single quote marks. In Java, every `char` literal starts and ends with a single quote mark.

![Remember](images/remember.png) In a Java program, single quote marks surround the letter in a `char` literal.

In the section entitled “[The types of values that variables may have](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0005),” it says that the bit sequence 01001010 stands for the letter *J*, the number 74, or 1.0369608636003646 × 10^(–43) depending on how you want to interpret it. [Table 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-tbl-0002) shows you what some other bit sequences may represent.

| **Sequence of Bits** | **What the Sequence Means When We Interpret it as a `char`** | **What the Sequence Means When We Interpret it as an `int`** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1000001 | `'A'` | 65 |
| 1000010 | `'B'` | 66 |
| 1000011 | `'C'` | 67 |
| 1000100 | `'D'` | 68 |
| 1000101 | `'E'` | 69 |
| 1000110 | `'F'` | 70 |
| 1000111 | `'G'` | 71 |
| 1001000 | `'H'` | 72 |
| 1001001 | `'I'` | 73 |
| 1001010 | `'J'` | 74 |
| … | … | … |
| 1100001 | `'a'` | 97 |

[TABLE 4-2](#c04.xhtml_rc04-tbl-0002) How Java Represents Certain Characters

In many ways, Java treats `char` as a numeric type. In fact, the literal `'J'` really stands for the number 74\. The fact that this literal can look like a letter on the computer screen is just Java's way of making you feel as if you're dealing with symbols in the alphabet. So, in [Listing 4-4](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0006), when you subtract 7 from `'J'`, you get the numeric value 67\. And, when you call `println`, that number appears on the screen as the letter *C*.

![Warning](images/warning.png) If you're tempted to write the following statement,

`char myLittleChars = 'barry'; //Don't do this`

please resist the temptation. You can’t store more than one letter at a time in a `char` variable, and you can't put more than one letter between a pair of single quotes. If you’re trying to store words or sentences (not just single letters), you need to use something called a `String`.

For a look at Java's `String` type, see the section “[The Molecules and Compounds: Reference Types](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0042),” later in this chapter.

![Warning](images/warning.png) Java's handling of characters has some subtle twists and turns. For example, in [Listing 4-4](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0006), you've declared `oneLetter` to be of type `char`. So, the statement `println(oneLetter)` displays the letter `C`. But if your program contains the statement `println('J' - 7)`, Java thinks you're asking it to subtract 7 from 74, so the output you see is the number `67`.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) If you're used to writing programs in other languages, you may be aware of something called ASCII character encoding. Many languages use ASCII; Java uses Unicode. In the old ASCII representation, each character takes up only 8 bits, but in Unicode, each character takes up 8, 16, or 32 bits. Whereas ASCII stores the letters of the Roman alphabet, Unicode has room for characters from many of the world’s languages. Java's `char` type is 16-bit Unicode. So, in [Table 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-tbl-0002), the sequence 1001010 actually stands for 000000001001010.

### The boolean type

A variable of type `boolean` stores one of two values: `true` or `false`. [Listing 4-5](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0007) demonstrates the use of a `boolean` variable. [Figure 4-10](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0010) shows the output of the program in [Listing 4-5](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0007).

![A text-based image with a true or false question asking if all ten of the Brickenchicker decuplets can fit on the elevator. The answer provided is 'false.'](images/9781394289240-fg0410.png)

[FIGURE 4-10:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0010) The Brickenchicker decuplets strike again.

[LISTING 4-5](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fea-0007) Using the boolean Type

`void main() {`
`println("True or False?");`
`println("You can fit all ten of the");`
`println("Brickenchicker decuplets");`
`println("on the elevator:");`
`println(""); // or println();`

`int weightOfAPerson = 150;`
`int elevatorWeightLimit = 1400;`
`int numberOfPeople = elevatorWeightLimit / weightOfAPerson;`

**`boolean allTenOkay = numberOfPeople >= 10;`**

`println(allTenOkay);`
`}`

In [Listing 4-5](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0007), the `allTenOkay` variable is of type `boolean`. To find a value for the `allTenOkay` variable, the program checks to see whether `numberOfPeople` is greater than or equal to `ten`. (The symbols `>=` stand for *greater than or equal to.*)

At this point, it pays to be fussy about terminology. Any part of a Java program that has a value is an *expression.* If you write

`weightOfAPerson = 150;`

then `150` is an expression (an expression whose value is the quantity `150`). If you write

`numberOfEggs = 2 + 2;`

then `2 + 2` is an expression (because 2 + 2 has the value `4`). If you write

`int numberOfPeople = elevatorWeightLimit / weightOfAPerson;`

then `elevatorWeightLimit / weightOfAPerson` is an expression. (The value of the expression `elevatorWeightLimit / weightOfAPerson` depends on whatever values the variables `elevatorWeightLimit` and `weightOfAPerson` have when the code containing the expression is executed.)

![Remember](images/remember.png) Any part of a Java program that has a value is an *expression.*

In [Listing 4-5](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0007), the code `numberOfPeople >= 10` is an expression. The expression's value depends on the value stored in the `numberOfPeople` variable. But, as you know from seeing the strawberry shortcake at the Brickenchicker family's catered lunch, the value of `numberOfPeople` isn't greater than or equal to ten. As a result, the value of `numberOfPeople >= 10` is `false`. So, in the statement in [Listing 4-5](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0007), in which `allTenOkay` is assigned a value, the `allTenOkay` variable is assigned a `false` value.

In [Listing 4-5](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0007), the call `println("")` adds a blank line to the program's output. That's because `""` represents the empty string — the string that doesn't contain any characters. For more information about strings, see this chapter's “Java’s String type” section.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) With the preview features in Java 23, you can display a blank line by calling `System.out.println()`, but not by calling `println()` alone. By the time you read this book, a simple `println()` call will probably be available. In fact, the masters of Java plan to add that simple `println()` in Java 24\. I don't have a time machine, so I can't try that feature before I send this chapter to Wiley.

## Creating New Values by Applying Operators

What could be more comforting than your old friend the plus sign? It was the first topic you learned about in elementary school math. Almost everybody knows how to add 2 and 2\. In fact, in English usage, adding 2 and 2 is a metaphor for something that's easy to do. Whenever you see a plus sign, a cell in your brain says, “Thank goodness — it could be something much more complicated.”

Java has a plus sign. You can use it for several purposes. You can use the plus sign to add two numbers, like this:

`int apples, oranges, fruit;`
`apples = 5;`
`oranges = 16;`
`fruit = apples + oranges;`

You can also use the plus sign to paste `String` values together:

`String startOfChapter =`
`"It's three in the morning. I'm dreaming about the" +`
`"history course that I failed in high school.";`
`println(startOfChapter);`

This can be handy because, in Java, you can't make an ordinary quoted `String` literal straddle from one line to another. In other words, the following code wouldn't work:

`String thisIsBadCode =`
**`"`**`It's three in the morning. I'm dreaming about the`
`history course that I failed in high school.`**`"`**`;`
`println(thisIsBadCode);`

If you want a single string to span several lines, you need another trick. For details, see [Chapter 5](#c05.xhtml).

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) The correct way to say that you're pasting `String` values together is to say that you’re *concatenating*`String` values.

You can even use the plus sign to paste numbers next to `String` values:

`int apples, oranges, fruit;`
`apples = 5;`
`oranges = 16;`
`fruit = apples + oranges;`
`println("You have" + fruit + "pieces of fruit.");`

Of course, the old minus sign is available, too (but not for `String` values):

`apples = fruit - oranges;`

Use an asterisk (`*`) for multiplication and a slash (`/`) for division:

`double rate, pay;`
`int hours;`

`rate = 6.25;`
`hours = 35;`
`pay = rate * hours;`
`println(pay);`

For an example using division, refer to [Listing 4-3](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004).

![Warning](images/warning.png) When you divide an `int` value by another `int` value, you get an `int` value. The computer doesn't round. Instead, the computer chops off any remainder. If you put `println(11 / 4)` in your program, the computer prints `2`, not `2.75`. To get past this, make either (or both) of the numbers you're dividing `double` values. If you put `println(11.0 / 4)` in your program, the computer prints `2.75`.

Another useful arithmetic operator is called the *remainder* operator. The symbol for the remainder operator is the percent sign (`%`). When you put `println(11 % 4)` in your program, the computer prints `3`. It does this because 4 goes into 11 who-cares-how-many times with a remainder of 3\. The remainder operator turns out to be fairly useful. [Listing 4-6](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0008) has an example.

[LISTING 4-6](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fea-0008) Making Change

`void main() {`
`int total = 248;`
`int quarters = total / 25;`
`int whatsLeft = total % 25;`

`int dimes = whatsLeft / 10;`
`whatsLeft = whatsLeft % 10;`

`int nickels = whatsLeft / 5;`
`whatsLeft = whatsLeft % 5;`

`int cents = whatsLeft;`

`println("From " + total + " cents you get");`
`println(quarters + " quarters");`
`println(dimes + " dimes");`
`println(nickels + " nickels");`
`println(cents + " cents");`
`}`

[Figure 4-11](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0011) shows a run of the code in [Listing 4-6](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0008). You start with a total of 248 cents. Then

`quarters = total / 25`

divides 248 by 25, giving 9\. That means you can make 9 quarters from 248 cents. Next,

`whatsLeft = total % 25`

divides 248 by 25 again and puts only the remainder, 23, into `whatsLeft`. Now you're ready for the next step, which is to take as many dimes as you can out of cents.

![“A text box showing the breakdown of 248 cents into coins. It states: 'From 248 cents you get 9 quarters, 2 dimes, 0 nickels, and 3 cents.'](images/9781394289240-fg0411.png)

[FIGURE 4-11:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0011) Change for $2.48.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) The code in [Listing 4-6](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0008) makes change in US currency with the following coin denominations: 1 cent, 5 cents (one nickel), 10 cents (one dime), and 25 cents (one quarter). In fact, [Listing 4-6](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0008) gives you the *smallest number of coins* that add up to 248 cents. With some minor tweaking, you can make the code work in any country's coinage. You can always get a set of coins adding up to a total. But, for some kinds of coinage, you won't always get the *smallest number of coins* that add up to a total. For example, in the mid-1970s, England had coins with values 25p, 20p, 10p, and 5p. To put 40p together, a program like the one in [Listing 4-6](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0008) would suggest 25p + 10p + 5p. But you could use fewer coins by shelling out 20p + 20p.

#### SMOOTH OPERATORS

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Find the values of the following expressions by typing each expression in JShell:

*   `5 / 4`
*   `5 / 4.0`
*   `5.0 / 4`
*   `5`.`0 / 4.0`
*   `"5" + "4"`
*   `5 + 4`
*   `" " + 5 + 4`

### Initialize once, assign often

[Listing 4-6](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0008) has three lines that put values into the variable `whatsLeft`:

**`int`** `whatsLeft = total % 25;`

`whatsLeft = whatsLeft % 10;`

`whatsLeft = whatsLeft % 5;`

Only one of these lines is a declaration. The other two lines are assignment statements. That's good because you can't declare the same variable more than once (not without creating something called a *block*). If you goof and write

**`int`** `whatsLeft = total % 25;`

**`int`** `whatsLeft = whatsLeft % 10;`

in [Listing 4-6](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0008), you see an error message (such as `Duplicate variable whatsLeft` or `Variable 'whatsLeft' is already defined`) when you try to compile your code.

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) To find out what a block is, see [Chapter 5](#c05.xhtml). Then, for some honest talk about redeclaring variables, see [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml).

### The increment and decrement operators

Java has some neat little operators that make life easier (for the computer's processor, for your brain, and for your fingers). Altogether, four such operators exist — two increment operators and two decrement operators. The increment operators add 1, and the decrement operators subtract 1\. The increment operators use double plus signs (`++`), and the decrement operators use double minus signs (`––`). To see how they work, you need some examples. The first example is shown in [Figure 4-12](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0012).

[Figure 4-13](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0013) shows a run of the program in [Figure 4-12](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0012). In this horribly uneventful run, the count of bunnies prints three times.

The double plus signs go by two names, depending on where you put them. When you put the `++` before a variable, the `++` is called the *preincrement* operator. (The *pre* stands for *before.*)

![“The image contains a Java code snippet with detailed comments. The code initializes an integer variable named numberOfBunnies to 27 and demonstrates how pre-increment and post-increment operations affect its value. The program prints the variable's value three times, highlighting that the variable becomes 28 and then 29 due to the increment operations.”](images/9781394289240-fg0412.png)

[FIGURE 4-12:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0012) Using preincrement.

![Three numbers in a column inside a box: 28, 29, 29.](images/9781394289240-fg0413.png)

[FIGURE 4-13:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0013) A run of the code in [Figure 4-12](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0012).

The word *before* has two meanings:

*   You put `++` before the variable.
*   The computer adds 1 to the variable's value before the variable is used in any other part of the statement.

To understand this, look at the bold line in [Figure 4-12](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0012). The computer adds 1 to `numberOfBunnies` (raising the value of `numberOfBunnies` to `29`) and then prints `29` onscreen.

![Remember](images/remember.png) With `println(++numberOfBunnies)`, the computer adds 1 to `numberOfBunnies` before printing the new value of `numberOfBunnies` onscreen.

An alternative to preincrement is *postincrement.* (The *post* stands for *after.*) The word *after* has two different meanings:

*   You put `++` after the variable.
*   The computer adds 1 to the variable's value after the variable is used in any other part of the statement.

To see more clearly how postincrement works, look at the bold line in [Figure 4-14](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0014). The computer prints the old value of `numberOfBunnies` (which is `28`) on the screen, and then the computer adds 1 to `numberOfBunnies`, which raises the value of `numberOfBunnies` to `29`.

![The image displays a Java code snippet with comments and output. The code declares an integer variable numberOfBunnies initialized to 27 and demonstrates the effect of the post-increment operator (++). The first print statement outputs 28, the second print statement outputs 28 again while incrementing numberOfBunnies to 29, and the final print statement outputs 29\. This sequence illustrates how the post-increment operator works in Java.](images/9781394289240-fg0414.png)

[FIGURE 4-14:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0014) Using postincrement.

![Remember](images/remember.png) With `println(numberOfBunnies++)`, the computer adds 1 to `numberOfBunnies` after printing the old value that `numberOfBunnies` already had.

[Figure 4-15](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0015) shows a run of the code in [Figure 4-14](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0014). Compare [Figure 4-15](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0015) with the run in [Figure 4-13](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0013):

*   With preincrement in [Figure 4-13](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0013), the second number is `29`.
*   With postincrement in [Figure 4-15](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0015), the second number is `28`.

    In [Figure 4-15](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0015), `29` doesn't show onscreen until the end of the run, when the computer executes one last `println(numberOfBunnies)`.

![Three numbers in a column inside a box: 28, 28, 29.](images/9781394289240-fg0415.png)

[FIGURE 4-15:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0015) A run of the code in [Figure 4-14](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0014).

![Tip](images/tip.png) Are you trying to decide between using preincrement or postincrement? Try no longer. Most programmers use postincrement. In a typical Java program, you often see things like `numberOfBunnies++`. You seldom see things like `++numberOfBunnies`.

In addition to preincrement and postincrement, Java has two operators that use `––`. These operators are called *predecrement* and *postdecrement:*

*   With **predecrement** (`--numberOfBunnies`), the computer subtracts 1 from the variable's value before the variable is used in the rest of the statement.
*   With **postdecrement** (`numberOfBunnies--`), the computer subtracts 1 from the variable's value after the variable is used in the rest of the statement.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) Rather than write `++numberOfBunnies`, you can achieve the same effect by writing `numberOfBunnies = numberOfBunnies + 1`. So, some people conclude that Java's `++` and `––` operators are for saving keystrokes — to keep those poor fingers from overworking themselves. This is entirely incorrect. The best reason for using `++` is to avoid the inefficient and error-prone practice of writing the same variable name, such as `numberOfBunnies`, twice in the same statement. If you write `numberOfBunnies` only once (as you do when you use `++` or `––`), the computer has to figure out what `numberOfBunnies` means only once. On top of that, when you write `numberOfBunnies` only once, you have only one chance (instead of two chances) to type the variable name incorrectly. With simple expressions like `numberOfBunnies++`, these advantages hardly make a difference. But with more complicated expressions, such as `inventoryItems[(quantityReceived--*itemsPerBox+17)]++`, the efficiency and accuracy that you gain by using `++` and `––` are significant.

#### PROGNOSTICATION GAME

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Before you run the following code, try to predict what the code's output will be. Then run the code to find out whether your prediction is correct:

`void main() {`
`int i = 10;`
`println(i++);`
`println(--i);`
`--i;`
`i--;`
`println(i);`
`println(++i);`
`println(i--);`
`println(i);`
`i++;`
`i = i++ + ++i;`
`println(i);`
`i = i++ + i++;`
`println(i);`
`}`

#### SEE PLUS PLUS

Type the boldface text, one line after another, into JShell and see how JShell responds:

**`int i = 8`**
**`i++`**
**`i`**
**`i`**
**`i++`**
**`i`**
**`++i`**
**`i + i++`**
**`i++ + i`**

### Assignment operators

If you read the preceding section, which is about operators that add 1, you may be wondering whether you can manipulate these operators to add 2 or add 5 or add 1000000\. Can you write `numberOfBunnies++++` and still call yourself a Java programmer? Well, you can't. If you try it, an error message appears when you try to compile your code.

What can you do? As luck would have it, Java has plenty of assignment operators you can use. With an *assignment operator,* you can add, subtract, multiply, or divide by anything you want. You can do other cool operations, too. [Listing 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0010) has a smorgasbord of assignment operators (the ones with equal signs). [Figure 4-16](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0016) shows the output from running [Listing 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0010).

![The image shows a breakdown of how 248 cents can be converted into coins. It states that from 248 cents, you get 9 quarters, 2 dimes, 0 nickels, and 3 cents.](images/9781394289240-fg0416.png)

[FIGURE 4-16:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0016) A run of the code in [Listing 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0010).

[LISTING 4-7](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fea-0010) Assignment Operators

`void main() {`
`int numberOfBunnies = 27;`
`int numberExtra = 53;`

`numberOfBunnies += 1;`
`println(numberOfBunnies);`

`numberOfBunnies += 5;`
`println(numberOfBunnies);`

`numberOfBunnies += numberExtra;`
`println(numberOfBunnies);`

`numberOfBunnies *= 2;`
`println(numberOfBunnies);`

`println(numberOfBunnies -= 7);`

`println(numberOfBunnies = 100);`
`}`

[Listing 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0010) shows how versatile Java's assignment operators are. With the assignment operators, you can add, subtract, multiply, or divide a variable by any number. Notice how `+= 5` adds 5 to `numberOfBunnies`, and how `*= 2` multiplies `numberOfBunnies` by `2`. You can even use another expression's value (in [Listing 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0010), `numberExtra`) as the number to be applied.

The last two lines in [Listing 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0010) demonstrate a special feature of Java's assignment operators. You can use an assignment operator as part of a larger Java statement. In the next-to-last line of [Listing 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0010), the operator subtracts 7 from `numberOfBunnies`, decreasing the value of `numberOfBunnies` from `172` to `165`. Then the whole assignment business is stuffed into a call to `println`, so `165` prints onscreen.

Lo and behold, the last line of [Listing 4-7](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0010) shows how you can do the same thing with Java's plain old equal sign. The thing that I call an assignment statement near the start of this chapter is really one of the assignment operators that I describe in this section. Therefore, whenever you assign a value to something, you can make that assignment be part of a larger statement.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) Each use of an assignment operator does double duty as a statement and an expression. In all cases, the expression's value equals whatever value you assign. For example, before executing the code `println(numberOfBunnies -= 7)`, the value of `numberOfBunnies` is `172`. As a statement, `numberOfBunnies -= 7` tells the computer to subtract 7 from `numberOfBunnies` (so the value of `numberOfBunnies` goes from `172` to `165`). As an expression, the value of `numberOfBunnies -= 7` is `165`. So the code `println(numberOfBunnies -= 7)` really means `println(165)`. The number `165` displays on the computer screen.

For a richer explanation of this kind of thing, see the sidebar “[Statements and expressions](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0034),” earlier in this chapter.

#### THE OPERATION IS A SUCCESS

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Before you run the following code, try to predict what the code's output will be. Then run the code to find out whether your prediction is correct:

`void main() {`
`int i = 10;`
`i += 2;`
`i -= 5;`
`i *= 6;`
`println(i);`
`println(i += 3);`
`println(i /= 2);`
`}`

## The Molecules and Compounds: Reference Types

By combining simple things, you get more complicated things. That's the way things always go. Take some of Java’s primitive types, whip them together to make a primitive type stew, and what do you get? You get a more complicated type called a *reference type.*

The program in [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011) uses reference types. [Figure 4-17](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0017) shows you a run of the program in [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011).

![A rectangular box contains the text 'NE ER GI E A SUCKER AN E EN BREAK' and below it, 'You can win $550'.](images/9781394289240-fg0417.png)

[FIGURE 4-17:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0017) A word puzzle and a wheel spin.

[LISTING 4-8](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fea-0011) Using Reference Types

`void main() {`
`String solvedPuzzle = "NEVER GIVE A SUCKER AN EVEN BREAK";`
`String unsolvedPuzzle = solvedPuzzle.replace('V', ' ');`
`println(unsolvedPuzzle);`

`Random myRandom;`
`myRandom = new Random();`
`int number = 500 + 50 * myRandom.nextInt(9);`
`println("You can win $" + number);`
`}`

What use would anyone have for the code in [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011)? Picture this: On a certain TV game show, contestants solve a puzzle by guessing which letters are missing from a well-known phrase or sentence. They spin a wheel that can stop on any of the numbers 500, 550, 600, …, up to 900\. A number on the wheel is the amount of money a contestant may win.

The program in [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011) uses two reference types – *String* and *Random*. Both types are defined in the Java API.

### Java's String type

Roughly speaking, an instance of the `String` class is a bunch of characters. It's like having several `char` values in a row. Here are some examples:

*   `"x% z "` is a `String` literal containing five characters, two of which are blank spaces.
*   `""` is the empty string – the `String` literal that doesn't contain any characters.
*   `"A"` is a `String` literal that contains only one character. Despite what some people think, this isn't the same as the `char` literal '`A'`. It's important to remember this because there are things you can do with `String` values that you can't do with `char` values, and vice-versa.

### Java's Random type

An instance of the `Random` class is a formula that can pump out numbers that appear to be randomly generated (although they're not *really* randomly generated). [Figure 4-18](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0018) shows you why the expression `500 + 50 * myRandom.nextInt(9)` generates one of the numbers 500, 550, 600, …, up to 900.

![The image shows a completely black square with no discernible features, text, or objects. It represents a blank or empty space, which could be used for various purposes such as a placeholder, background, or to convey a sense of emptiness or void.](images/9781394289240-fg0418.png)

[FIGURE 4-18:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0018) Spinning the wheel.

### Coding with classes, objects, and their methods

You can look up `String` and `Random` in Java's API documentation. But, even before you do, I can tell you what you'll find. You’ll find that `String` and `Random` are the names of Java classes. So that's the big news. Every class is the name of a reference type.

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) To review the notion of a Java class, see the section on object-oriented programming (OOP) in [Chapter 1](#c01.xhtml). And, to read more about Java classes, see [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml).

In [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011), I do four things with the classes `String` and `Random`:

*   I reserve variable names to refer to instances of `String` and `Random`.
*   I create instances of `String` and `Random`.
*   I make the variables refer to the instances.
*   I call a method on each of the instances.

#### Reserving a variable name

In [Listing 4-2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002), I reserve the variable name `amountInAccount` for storing `double` values by writing `double amountInAccount`. In the same way, in [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011), I reserve `myRandom` for referring to a `Random` instance by writing `Random myRandom`. (See Item 1 in [Figure 4-19](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0019).)

#### Reserving a variable name

![The image shows a Java code snippet that initializes an integer variable numberOfBunnies to 27\. It demonstrates pre-increment and post-increment operations, showing the variable's value at different points. The comments explain how the value changes from 28 to 29 due to the increment operations.](images/9781394289240-fg0419.png)

[FIGURE 4-19:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0019) Making `myRandom`.

#### Creating an instance and making a variable refer to the instance

In [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011), I create an instance of the `Random` class by writing `new Random()`. The statement

`myRandom = new Random();`

makes the variable `myRandom` refer to that newly-created instance. (See Items 2 and 3 in [Figure 4-19](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0019).) In [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011), I could have written one statement instead of two:

`Random myRandom = new Random();`

This single statement would do double duty by reserving the name `myRandom` and making `myRandom` refer to a newly-created object. I could even have written

`var Random myRandom = new Random();`

and let Java figure out the variable's type.

![Remember](images/remember.png) When you use Java's `new` keyword, you create a brand-new object.

[Figure 4-20](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0020) illustrates the same business for the `solvedPuzzle` variable in [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011).

![An annotated Java code snippet showing a string assignment. The code line is String solvedPuzzle = “NEVER GIVE A SUCKER AN EVEN BREAK”;. The annotation explains: 1) The variable solvedPuzzle is assigned to a String class instance. 2) The string literal 'NEVER GIVE A SUCKER AN EVEN BREAK' is assigned to the variable solvedPuzzle.](images/9781394289240-fg0420.png)

[FIGURE 4-20:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0020) Making `solvedPuzzle`.

For the `solvedPuzzle` variable, I could have written

`String solvedPuzzle = new String("NEVER GIVE A SUCKER AN EVEN BREAK");`

But a double-quoted `String` literal is already an instance of the `String` class. So, with strings, you can usually omit the `new String` part.

#### Calling a method

If you refer back to [Chapter 1](#c01.xhtml), you see that each class describes both data and code for working with the data. In [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011), the `String` instance has data (the characters `"NEVER GIVE A SUCKER AN EVEN BREAK"`). But it also has code for working with the data. Each such piece of code is actually a method.

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) For an introduction to methods, refer to [Chapter 3](#c03.xhtml).

In Java's API, the `String` class has its own method named `replace`. When you write `solvedPuzzle.replace('V', ' ')`, you're asking Java to call the `solvedPuzzle` instance's `replace` method. You're telling Java to create a new string by replacing each occurrence of the letter `'V'` in `solvedPuzzle` with a blank space. (See [Figure 4-21](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0021).)

![A Java code snippet with annotations. The code line reads String unsolvedPuzzle = solvedPuzzle.replace('V', ' ');. The annotations explain that solvedPuzzle is an instance of the String class, replace is a method of the String class, and 'V' and ' ' are the parameters passed to the method. The dot before replace indicates that Java should call the replace method on the solvedPuzzle object.](images/9781394289240-fg0421.png)

[FIGURE 4-21:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0021) Calling the `String` class's replace method.

Also, in Java's API, the `Random` class has its own `nextInt` method. Remember that the `myRandom` object is a formula that can pump out numbers that appear to be randomly generated. When you write `myRandom.nextInt(9)`, you're asking Java to use the `myRandom` formula to feed you one of the numbers 0, 1, 2, 3, …, 8\. (See [Figure 4-22](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0022).)

In [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011), you call methods by writing `solvedPuzzle.replace('V', ' ')` and `myRandom.nextInt(9)`. Do you see the pattern? In general, you call an object's method by putting a dot between the object and the method. You write something like *`someObject.doSomething(someExtraInformation)`*.

![The image shows a Java code snippet and annotations explaining its components. The code snippet is int number = 500 + 50 * myRandom.nextInt(9);. Annotations describe myRandom as an object of the Random class, nextInt(9) as a method belonging to the Random class, and the dot operator as a way to call the method using values stored in the myRandom object. The number 9 is described as extra information sent to the method.](images/9781394289240-fg0422.png)

[FIGURE 4-22:](#c04.xhtml_rc04-fig-0022) Calling the `Random` class's nextInt method.

### What did he just say?

This talk about OOP can be overwhelming, so to keep you from getting a throbbing headache, I summarize some main points:

*   A class describes both data and code for working with the data. A piece of code to work with the data is a method.
*   You can reserve a variable name so it can refer to an instance of a particular class. To do this, you write *`ClassName variableName`*.
*   You can create an object – an instance of a particular class. To do this, you sometimes use the class's name preceded by Java's `new` keyword. Literals (like `"NEVER"`, `'J'`, and `47.92`) are already objects, so you can assign them to variables without using `new`.
*   You can call a method belonging to an object. To do this, you typically put a dot between the object's name and the method's name.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Try these experiments:

Run the code in [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011) a few times on your computer. Does the winning amount seem to be randomly generated?

First, run the following code a few times and make some notes so you can keep track of the results.

`Random yourRandom;`
`yourRandom = new Random();`
`println(yourRandom.nextInt(100));`
`println(yourRandom.nextInt(100));`
`println(yourRandom.nextInt(100));`

Next modify the code by changing `new Random()` to `new Random(12345)`. Run this modified code a few times. How do the results differ from the those in the first set of runs?
