The first statement in [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011) is the declaration of the `solvedPuzzle` variable. Immediately after that statement, add a call to the `String` class's `toLowerCase` method:

`solvedPuzzle = solvedPuzzle.toLowerCase();`

What happens when you run this code?

#### I'D RATHER BE IN PHILADELPHIA

The first statement in [Listing 4-8](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0011) is the declaration of the `solvedPuzzle` variable. Immediately after that statement, add a call to one of the `String` class's `replace` methods:

`solvedPuzzle = solvedPuzzle.replace("NEVER", "ALWAYS");`

What happens when you run this code? Look online for a Javadoc page describing the `String` class and find out how many methods the `String` class has.

Chapter 5

# Controlling the Flow by Making Decisions

IN THIS CHAPTER

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Writing statements that choose between alternatives**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Forming logical conditions**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Putting statements inside one another**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Choosing among many alternatives**

The TV show *Dennis the Menace* aired on CBS from 1959 to 1963\. I remember one episode in which Mr. Wilson was having trouble making an important decision. I think it was something about changing jobs or moving to a new town. Anyway, I can still see that shot of Mr. Wilson sitting in his yard, sipping lemonade, and staring into nowhere for the whole afternoon. Of course, the annoying character Dennis was continually interrupting Mr. Wilson’s peace and quiet. That’s what made this situation funny.

What impressed me about this episode (the reason I remember it clearly, even now) was Mr. Wilson’s dogged intent in making the decision. This guy wasn’t going about his everyday business, roaming around the neighborhood while thoughts about the decision wandered in and out of his mind. He was sitting quietly in his yard, making marks carefully and logically on his mental balance sheet. How many people actually make decisions this way?

At that time, I was still pretty young. I’d never faced the responsibility of making a big decision that affected my family and me. But I wondered what such a decision-making process would be like. Would it help to sit there like a stump for hours on end? Would I make my decisions by the careful weighing and tallying of options? Or would I shoot in the dark, take risks, and act on impulse? Only time would tell.

## Making Decisions (Java if Statements)

When you’re writing computer programs, you’re constantly hitting forks in roads. Did the user correctly type the password? If yes, let the user work; if no, kick the bum out. So the Java programming language needs a way of making a program branch in one of two directions. Fortunately, the language has a way: It’s called an `if` statement*.*

### Guess the number

[Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001) illustrates the use of an `if` statement. Two runs of the program in [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001) are shown in [Figure 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0001).

![“The image shows two instances of a number guessing game. In the first instance, the player picks the number 10 but loses because the random number was 7\. In the second instance, the player picks the number 2 and wins. The winning message is surrounded by asterisks.”](images/9781394289240-fg0501.png)

[FIGURE 5-1:](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fig-0001) Two runs of the guessing game.

[LISTING 5-1](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fea-0001) A Guessing Game

`void main() {`
`String input = readln("Pick a number from 1 to 10: ");`
`int inputNumber = Integer.parseInt(input);`
`int randomNumber = new Random().nextInt(10) + 1;`

`if (inputNumber == randomNumber) {`
`println("**********");`
`println("*You win.*");`
`println("**********");`
`} else {`
`println("You lose.");`
`print("The random number was ");`
`println(randomNumber + ".");`
`}`

`println("Thank you for playing.");`
`}`

The program in [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001) plays a guessing game with the user. The program gets a number (a guess) from the user and then generates a random number between 1 and 10\. If the number the user entered is the same as the random number, the user wins. Otherwise, the user loses and the program tells the user what the random number was.

### She controlled keystrokes from the keyboard

In [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001), the line

`String input = readln("Pick a number from 1 to 10: ");`

does three things:

1.  It displays the words `Pick a number from 1 to 10:` on the user's screen.
2.  It waits for the user to type some characters and then press Enter.
3.  After the user presses Enter, it makes the variable `input` refer to the characters that the user typed.

So, what happens on the first line in [Figure 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0001)? On that line, Java makes `input` refer to `"10"`.

Wait! At the end of that last sentence, why are there quotation marks around the characters `10`? The quotation marks indicate that, as its name suggests, the variable `input` refers to a string of characters. Java's `readln` method always interprets the user's input as a string of characters. That's why [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001) starts with the words `String input`.

The third statement in [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001) generates a random number from 1 to 10\. (If you don't believe me, look at the section about Java's `Random` class in [Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml).) Anyway, the `Random` class's `nextInt` method always gives you an `int` value. Later on in the code, we have to compare two things: the stuff the user entered, which is a `String` instance, and the number that `Random` gave us, which is an `int` value.

In some languages, you can ask whether `input` equals `randomNumber`. But Java is fussy about such things. In Java, you can't compare `String` apples with `int` oranges. You have to compare strings with strings or `int` values with `int` values. So, in [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001), the line

`int inputNumber = Integer.parseInt(input);`

creates a new `int` variable named `inputNumber`. The method `Integer.parseInt` examines the string that we got from the user's input and interprets that string as an `int` value. Because `inputNumber` and `randomNumber` are both `int` values, we can compare them and find out whether they're equal to one another. Thank goodness for the `Integer.parseInt` method! It's defined in Java's API.

### Reading double values: a cautionary tale

[Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml) describes Java's primitive types, including the `int` and `double` types. To read a `double` value from the keyboard, you mimic what you do in [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001) but you change a few words. Here's how it works:

`void main() {`
`String input = readln("Enter an amount: ");`
**`double`** `inputNumber =` **`Double.parseDouble`**`(input);`
`println(inputNumber + 0.5);`
`}`

I receive plenty of emails from readers, and one of the most common questions is, “Why do I get an error message when I try to read a `double` value?” Decimal points vary from one country to another. In the United States, *10.5* (with a period) represents ten-and-a-half, but in France, *10,5* (with a comma) represents ten-and-a-half. In Switzerland, 10.50 is an amount of money, and 10,50 is an amount that's not money-related. In the Persian language, a decimal point looks like a slash (but sits a bit lower than the digit characters). Your computer's operating system stores information about the country you live in, and Java reads that information to decide what ten-and-a-half looks like. If you run a program containing a `Double.parseDouble` method call and Java responds with a `NumberFormatException`, check your input. You might have input 10.5 when your country's conventions require 10,5 (or another way of representing ten-and-a-half). For more information, see the sidebar “Where on earth do you live?” in [Chapter 9](#c09.xhtml).

### The if statement

At the core of [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001) is a Java `if` statement. This `if` statement represents a fork in the road. (See [Figure 5-2](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0002).) The computer follows one of two prongs: the prong that prints `You win` or the prong that prints `You lose`. The computer decides which prong to take by testing the truth or falsehood of a *condition.* In [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001), the condition being tested is

`inputNumber == randomNumber`

Does the value of `inputNumber` equal the value of `randomNumber`? When the condition is true, the computer does the stuff between the condition and the word *else.* When the condition turns out to be false, the computer does the stuff after the word *else.* Either way, the computer goes on to execute the last `println` call, which displays `Thank you for playing`.

![Remember](images/remember.png) The condition in an `if` statement must be enclosed in parentheses. However, a line like `if (inputNumber == randomNumber)` is not a complete statement (just as “If I had a hammer” isn't a complete sentence). So, this line `if (inputNumber == randomNumber)` shouldn't end with a semicolon.

![“A flowchart with the question 'Does inputNumber equal randomNumber?' at the top. Two arrows point downwards from this question: one labeled 'yes' leading to 'You win.', and the other labeled 'no' leading to 'You lose. The random number was...'. Below both outcomes is the text 'Thank you for playing.'”](images/9781394289240-fg0502.png)

[FIGURE 5-2:](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fig-0002) An `if` statement is like a fork in the road.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) Sometimes, when I'm writing about a condition that’s being tested, I slip into using the word *expression* instead of *condition.* That’s okay because every condition is an expression. An expression is something that has a value and, sure enough, every condition has a value. The condition’s value is either `true` or `false`. (For revealing information about expressions and values like `true` and `false`, see [Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml).)

### Equal, equal

In [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001), in the `if` statement's condition, notice the use of the double equal sign. Comparing two numbers to see whether they're the same isn’t the same as setting something equal to something else. That’s why the symbol to compare for equality isn’t the same as the symbol that’s used in an assignment or an initialization. In an `if` statement’s condition, you can’t replace the double equal sign with a single equal sign. If you do, your program just won’t work. (You almost always get an error message when you try to compile your code.)

On the other hand, if you never make the mistake of using a single equal sign in a condition, you’re not normal. Several years ago, while I was teaching an introductory Java course, I promised that I’d swallow my laser pointer if no one made the single-equal-sign mistake during any of the lab sessions. This wasn’t an idle promise. I knew I’d never have to keep it. As it turned out, even if I had ignored the first ten times anybody made the single-equal-sign mistake during those lab sessions, I would still be laser-pointer-free. Everybody mistakenly uses the single equal sign several times in a programming career.

![Tip](images/tip.png) The trick is not to avoid making the single-equal-sign mistake; the trick is to catch the mistake whenever you make it.

### Brace yourself

The `if` statement in [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001) has two halves: a top half and a bottom half. I have names for these two parts of an `if` statement. I call them the *if part* (the top half) and the *else part* (the bottom half).

The `if` part in [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001) seems to have more than one statement in it. I make this happen by enclosing the three statements of the `if` part in a pair of curly braces. When I do this, I form a block. A *block* is a bunch of statements scrunched together by a pair of curly braces.

With this block, three calls to `println` are tucked away safely inside the `if` part. With the curly braces, the rows of asterisks and the words `You win` display only when the user's guess is correct.

This business with blocks and curly braces applies to the `else` part as well. In [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001), whenever `inputNumber` doesn't equal `randomNumber`, the computer executes three `print/println` calls. To convince the computer that all three of these calls are inside the `else` clause, I put these calls into a block. That is, I enclose these three calls in a pair of curly braces.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) Strictly speaking, [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001) has only one statement between the `if` and `else` statements and only one statement after the `else` statement. The trick is that when you place a bunch of statements inside curly braces, you get a block; and a block behaves, in all respects, like a single statement. In fact, the official Java documentation lists blocks as one of the many kinds of statements. So, in [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001), the block that prints `You win` and asterisks is a single statement that has, within it, three smaller statements.

### Your intent to indent

Notice how, in [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001), the `print` and `println` calls inside the `if` statement are indented. (This includes both the `You win` and `You lose` statements. The `print` and `println` calls that come after the word `else` are still part of the `if` statement.) Strictly speaking, you don't have to indent the statements that are inside an `if` statement. For all the compiler cares, you can write your whole program on a single line or place all your statements in an artful, misshapen zigzag. The problem is that neither you nor anyone else can make sense of your code if you don't indent your statements in some logical fashion. In [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001), the indenting of the `print` and `println` statements helps your eye (and brain) see quickly that these statements are subordinate to the overall `if`/`else` flow.

In a small program, unindented or poorly indented code is barely tolerable. But in a complicated program, indentation that doesn't follow a neat, logical pattern is a big, ugly nightmare.

![Remember](images/remember.png) Many Java IDEs have tools to indent your code automatically. In your favorite IDE, look for menus such as Source ⇒   Format or Code ⇒   Reformat Code.

![Warning](images/warning.png) When you write `if` statements, you may be tempted to chuck out the window all the rules about curly braces and simply rely on indentation. This strategy works in other programming languages, such as Python and Haskell, but not in Java. If you indent three statements after the word `else` and forget to enclose those statements in curly braces, the computer thinks that the `else` part includes only the first of the three statements. What's worse, the indentation misleads you into believing that the `else` part includes all three statements. This makes it more difficult for you to figure out why your code isn't behaving the way you think it should. Watch those braces!

### Elseless in Helsinki

Okay, so the title of this section is contrived. Big deal! The idea is that you can create an `if` statement without the `else` part. Take, for instance, the code in [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001), shown earlier. Maybe you'd rather not rub it in whenever the user loses the game. The modified code in [Listing 5-2](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0003) shows you how to do this (and [Figure 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0003) shows you the result).

![The image shows two instances of a number guessing game. In the first instance, the player guessed the number 3, but the random number was 5\. In the second instance, the player guessed the number 9 and won because the random number was also 9\. Both instances include the messages: 'Pick a number from 1 to 10:', 'That was a very good guess ', 'The random number was', and 'Thank you for playing.' The second instance also includes 'You win.' after the correct guess.](images/9781394289240-fg0503.png)

[FIGURE 5-3:](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fig-0003) Two runs of the game in [Listing 5-2](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0003).

[LISTING 5-2](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fea-0003) A Kinder, Gentler Guessing Game

`void main() {`
`String input = readln("Pick a number from 1 to 10: ");`
`int inputNumber = Integer.parseInt(input);`
`int randomNumber = new Random().nextInt(10) + 1;`

`if (inputNumber == randomNumber) {`
`println("*You win.*");`
`}`
`println("That was a very good guess \uD83D\uDE00");`
`print("The random number was ");`
`println(randomNumber + ".");`

`println("Thank you for playing.");`
`}`

The `if` statement in [Listing 5-2](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0003) has no `else` part. When `inputNumber` is the same as `randomNumber`, the computer prints `You win`. When `inputNumber` is different from `randomNumber`, the computer doesn't print `You win`.

Notice the cryptic-looking code `\uD83D\uDE00` near the middle of [Listing 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004). That's Java's Unicode representation of the grinning-face emoji. You can see the result in [Figure 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0003). As of March 2024, Java's Unicode implementation includes the 65,536 characters you can represent with 16 bits, plus an additional 84,277 characters known as *surrogates*. You can even use Unicode characters in your Java code's variable names. (See [Figure 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0004).)

![“A snippet of Java code written in Hebrew. The code declares three integer variables, assigns values to two of them, and calculates the sum of the first two variables to assign to the third. It then prints a Hebrew string followed by the value of the third variable.”](images/9781394289240-fg0504.png)

[FIGURE 5-4:](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fig-0004) Right-to-left text meets left-to-right text.

![Tip](images/tip.png) You can see an official list of emojis by visiting [`https://unicode.org/emoji/charts/full-emoji-list.html`](https://unicode.org/emoji/charts/full-emoji-list.html).

*   Find out what happens when you run the code in [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001) and input the decimal representation of three-and-a-half (`3.5` or `3,5`, or something else, depending on your country's standard).

#### HOW DO YOU WRITE DECIMAL NUMBERS?

Run the following program:

`void main() {`
`String input = readln("What's the value of pi? ");`
`double pi = Double.parseDouble(input);`
`println("Thanks!");`
`}`

Does it display `Thanks!` or give you an error message? If you get an error message, your country's way of representing decimal numbers may be different from the way I represent decimals in this book. Rather than type `3.14`, try typing `3,14` or some variation that you usually use.

In [Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml), [Listing 4-5](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0007) tells you whether you can or cannot fit ten people on an elevator. A run of the listing's code looks something like this:

`True or False?`
`You can fit all ten of the`
`Brickenchicker decuplets`
`on the elevator:`

`false`

Use what you know about Java's `if` statements to make the program's output more natural. Depending on the value of the program's `elevatorWeightLimit` variable, the output should be either

`You can fit all ten of the`
`Brickenchicker decuplets`
`on the elevator.`

or

`You can't fit all ten of the`
`Brickenchicker decuplets`
`on the elevator.`

## Forming Conditions with Comparisons and Logical Operators

The Java programming language has plenty of little squiggles and doodads for your various condition-forming needs. This section tells you all about them.

### Comparing numbers; comparing characters

[Table 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-tbl-0001) shows you the operators you can use to compare one value with another.

| **Operator Symbol** | **Meaning** | **Example** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| == | is equal to | `numberOfCows == 5` |
| `!=` | is not equal to | `buttonClicked != panicButton` |
| `<` | is less than | `numberOfCows < 5` |
| `>` | is greater than | `myInitial > 'B'` |
| `<=` | is less than or equal to | `numberOfCows <= 5` |
| `>=` | is greater than or equal to | `myInitial >= 'B'` |

[TABLE 5-1](#c05.xhtml_rc05-tbl-0001) Comparison Operators

You can use all of Java's comparison operators to compare numbers and characters. When you compare numbers, things go pretty much the way you think they should go. But when you compare characters, things are a little strange. Comparing uppercase letters with one another is no problem. Because the letter *B* comes alphabetically before *H,* the condition `'B' < 'H'` is true. Comparing lowercase letters with one another is also okay. What's strange is that when you compare an uppercase letter with a lowercase letter, the uppercase letter is always smaller. So, even though `'Z' < 'A'` is `false`, `'Z' < 'a'` is `true`.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) Under the hood, the letters *A* through *Z* are stored with numeric codes 65 through 90\. The letters *a* through *z* are stored with codes 97 through 122. That's why each uppercase letter is smaller than each lowercase letter.

![Warning](images/warning.png) Be careful when you compare two numbers for equality (with `==`) or inequality (with `!=`). After you do some calculations and obtain two `double` values or two `float` values, the values that you have are seldom dead-on equal to one another. (The problem comes from those pesky digits beyond the decimal point.) For instance, the Fahrenheit equivalent of 21 degrees Celsius is 69.8, and when you calculate `9.0 / 5 * 21 + 32` by hand, you get 69.8\. But the condition `9.0 / 5 * 21 + 32 == 69.8` turns out to be false. That's because, when the computer calculates `9.0 / 5 * 21 + 32`, it gets 69.80000000000001, not 69.8.

### Comparing objects

When you start working with objects, you find that you can use `==` and `!=` to compare objects with one another. For instance, a button you see on the computer screen is an object. You can ask whether the thing that was just mouse-clicked is a particular button on your screen. You do this with Java's equality operator:

`if (click.getSource() == submitButton) {`
`Desktop.getDesktop().browse(new URI(`
`"`[`https://JavaForDummies.allmycode.com`](https://JavaForDummies.allmycode.com)`"));`
`}`

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) To find out more about responding to button clicks, read [Chapter 16](#c16.xhtml).

The big gotcha with Java’s comparison scheme comes when you compare two strings. (For a word or two about Java’s `String` type, see the section about reference types in [Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml).) When you compare two strings with one another, you don't want to use the double equal sign. Using the double equal sign would ask, “Is this string stored in exactly the same place in memory as that other string?” Usually, that's not what you want to ask. Instead, you usually want to ask, “Does this string have the same characters in it as that other string?” To ask the second question (the more appropriate question), Java’s `String` type has a method named `equals`. (Like everything else in the known universe, this `equals` method is defined in the Java API.) The `equals` method compares two strings to see whether they have the same characters in them. For an example using Java's `equals` method, see [Listing 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004). ([Figure 5-5](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0005) shows a run of the program in [Listing 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004).)

![Text on a screen humorously explaining that the typed password is correct but stored differently.](images/9781394289240-fg0505.png)

[FIGURE 5-5:](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fig-0005) The results of using `==` and using Java's `equals` method.

[LISTING 5-3](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fea-0004) Checking a Password

`void main() {`
`String password = readln("What's the password? ");`

`println("You typed >>" + password + "<<");`
`println("");`

**`if (password == "swordfish")`** `{`
`println("""`
`The word you typed is stored in the same place`
`as the real password. You must be a hacker.""");`
`} else {`
`println("""`
`The word you typed is not stored in the same place`
`as the real password, but that's no big deal.""");`
`}`
`println("");`

**`if (password.equals("swordfish"))`** `{`
`println("""`
`The word you typed has the same characters as the`
`real password. You can use our precious system.""");`
`} else {`
`println("""`
`The word you typed doesn't have the same characters as`
`the real password. You can't use our precious system.""");`
`}`
`}`

In [Listing 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004), the call `readln("What's the password? ")` grabs whatever word the user types on the computer keyboard. The code shoves the user's string input into the variable named *password.* Then the program's `if` statements use two different techniques to compare `password` with the string literal `"swordfish"`.

The more appropriate of the two techniques uses Java's `equals` method. The `equals` method looks funny because when you call it, you put a dot after one string and put the other string in parentheses. But that's the way you have to do it.

Wait! What about `Integer.parseInt`? I made a big fuss about that when I used it in [Listing 5-1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0001). Why don't you need `Integer.parseInt` here in [Listing 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004)? To answer this question, look at the variable types in [Listing 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004). Since `readln` produces a `String` instance, the `password` variable has the same type as the `"swordfish"` literal. This is reinforced by the words `String password` near the start of [Listing 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004). There's no reason to convert anything to a numeric value in [Listing 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004). (In fact, calling `Integer.parseInt` to turn `password` into a number would be a very bad idea.)

In calling Java's `equals` method, it doesn’t matter which string gets the dot and which gets the parentheses. For instance, in [Listing 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004), you could have written

`if ("swordfish".equals(password))`

The method would work just as well.

A call to Java’s `equals` method looks imbalanced, but it's not. There's a reason behind the apparent imbalance between the dot and the parentheses. The idea is that you have two objects: the `password` object and the `"swordfish"` object. Each of these two objects is of type `String`. (However, `password` is a variable of type `String`, and `"swordfish"` is a `String` literal.) When you write `password.equals("swordfish")`, you're calling an `equals` method that belongs to the `password` object. When you call that method, you're feeding `"swordfish"` to the method as the method's parameter (pun intended). You can read more about methods belonging to objects in [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml).

![Tip](images/tip.png) In addition to its `equals` method, Java has an `equalsIgnoreCase` method. Even though `"SWORDFISH".equals("swordfish")` is `false`, its close cousin `"SWORDFISH".equalsIgnoreCase("swordfish")` is `true`.

![Warning](images/warning.png) The code in [Listing 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004) does a good job with `if` statements, but it does a terrible job with real-world user authentication. First, never show a password in plain view (without asterisks to masquerade the password). Second, don't handle passwords without encrypting them. Third, don't put passwords in the code. Fourth … well, I could go on and on. The code in [Listing 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004) just isn’t meant to illustrate good username/password practices.

### When one line isn't enough

A *text block* is a bunch of text surrounded on both sides by three double quotes (`"""`).

`println(`**`"""`**
`The word you typed is stored in the same place`
`as the real password. You must be a hacker.`**`"""`**`);`

A text block starts with three double quotation marks, and the remainder of that block's first line must be blank. If you mistakenly put nonblank text after those first three quotation marks, Java becomes sick to its stomach:

**`// Don't do this:`**
`println(`**`"""The word you typed is stored`** `in the same place`
`as the real password. You must be a hacker.""");`

This rule about having only blanks after the first three quotation marks helps with indentation issues. Take a look at the following code:

`println("""`
`I. Controlling Program Flow`
`A. Introduction`
`B. Making Decisions`
`1\. Guess the Number`
`2\. Equal, equal`
`C. Forming Conditions""");`

With the enormous 21-character indentation of the `I. Controlling Program Flow` line, the output of the code looks like this:

`I. Controlling Program Flow`
`A. Introduction`
`B. Making Decisions`
`1\. Guess the Number`
`2\. Equal, equal`
`C. Forming Conditions`

Text blocks are useful because the text inside a block can straddle more than one line. Without text blocks, you may be tempted to put one quotation mark at each end, but that doesn't work. The following code, with traditional Java string notation, is forbidden:

**`// This code is incorrect:`**
`println(`**`"`**
`The word you typed is stored in the same place`
`as the real password. You must be a hacker.`**`"`**`);`

This book's seventh edition hit the shelves in 2017, before Java had text blocks. In that edition, [Listing 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004) had a truckload of `println` calls:

`println("The word you typed is stored in the same place");`
`println("as the real password. You must be a hacker.");`

That was some ugly code!

### Java's logical operators

Mr. Spock would be pleased: Java has all the operators you need for mixing and matching logical tests. The operators are shown in [Table 5-2](#c05.xhtml_c05-tbl-0002).

| **Operator Symbol** | **What It Means** | **Example** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `&&` | and | `5 < x && x < 10` |
| `&#124;&#124;` | or | `x < 5 &#124;&#124; 10 < x` |
| `!` | not | `!password.equals("swordfish")` |

[TABLE 5-2](#c05.xhtml_rc05-tbl-0002) Logical Operators

You can use these operators to form all kinds of elaborate conditions. [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005) has an example.

[LISTING 5-4](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fea-0005) Checking Username and Password

`import javax.swing.JOptionPane;`

`void main() {`
`String username = JOptionPane.showInputDialog("Username:");`
`String password = JOptionPane.showInputDialog("Password:");`

`if (`
`username != null && password != null`
`&&`
`(`
`(username.equals("bburd") && password.equals("swordfish"))`
`||`
`(username.equals("hritter") && password.equals("preakston"))`
`)`
`)`
`{`
`JOptionPane.showMessageDialog(null, "You're in.");`
`}`

`else {`
`JOptionPane.showMessageDialog(null, "You're suspicious.");`
`}`
`}`

Several runs of the program in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005) are shown in [Figure 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0006). When the username is *bburd* and the password is *swordfish* or when the username is *hritter* and the password is *preakston,* the user sees a nice message. Otherwise, the user is a bum who sees the nasty message they deserve.

![“The image shows a series of login attempts with different usernames and passwords, followed by messages indicating whether the login was successful or not. The usernames used are 'lburd,' 'hitter,' and 'lschmoe,' and the passwords used are 'swordfish' and 'freakston.' The messages displayed after the login attempts are either 'You're in.' or 'You're suspicious.'”](images/9781394289240-fg0506.png)

[FIGURE 5-6:](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fig-0006) Several runs of the code from [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005).

[Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005) illustrates a new way to get user input; namely, to show the user an input dialog box. The statement

`String password = JOptionPane.showInputDialog("Password:");`

in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005) performs more or less the same task as the statement

`String password = readln("What's the password? ");`

from [Listing 5-3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004). The big difference is, while `readln` displays dull-looking text in a console, `JOptionPane.showInputDialog` displays a fancy dialog box containing a text field and buttons. (Compare [Figures 5-5](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0005) and [5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0006).) When the user clicks OK, the computer takes whatever text is in the text field and hands that text over to a variable. In fact, [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005) uses `JOptionPane.showInputDialog` twice — once to get a value for the username variable and a second time to get a value for the password variable.

Near the end of [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005), I use a slight variation on the `JOptionPane` business:

`JOptionPane.showMessageDialog(null, "You're in.");`

With `showMessageDialog`, I show a simple dialog box — a box with no text field. (Again, see [Figure 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0006).)

![Tip](images/tip.png) If you want to look like an experienced Java programmer, don't imitate the code in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005). For one thing, the indentation and line breaks in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005) are unusual. I wrote the `if` statement this way so that you can easily see how the statement's parts relate to one another, but professional Java programmers would balk at this formatting. Aside from the strange indentation, [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005) uses the outdated `JOptionPane` class to display a window on the computer screen. Modern Java programs shun `JOptionPane` and the other `javax.swing` classes in favor of a platform called *JavaFX*. I used `JOptionPane` because it's good for small, single-window programs like the one in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005). But JavaFX is much better for elaborate desktop designs. For the skinny on JavaFX, see [Chapter 16](#c16.xhtml).

### Vive les nuls!

The French translations of *For Dummies* books are books *Pour les Nuls.* So a “dummy” in English is a “nul” in French.^([*](#c05.xhtml_c05-note-0001)) But in Java, the word `null` means “nothing.” When you see

`if (`
`username != null`

in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005), you can imagine that you see

`if (`
`username` *`isn't nothing`*

or

`if (`
`username has any value at all`

To find out how usernames can have no value, see the last row in [Figure 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0006). When you click Cancel in the first dialog box, the computer hands `null` to your program. So, in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005), the variable username becomes `null`. The comparison `username != null` checks to make sure you haven't clicked Cancel in the program's first dialog box. The comparison `password != null` performs the same kind of check for the program's second dialog box. When you see the `if` statement in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005), you can imagine that you see the conditions in [Figure 5-7](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0007).

![“The image shows a logical condition in a programming language. It checks if the user did not press 'Cancel' in both the username and password dialogs, and if either the username is 'bburd' with the password 'swordfish', or the username is 'hritter' with the password 'preekston'.”](images/9781394289240-fg0507.png)

[FIGURE 5-7:](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fig-0007) Interpreting the code in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005).

![Warning](images/warning.png) In [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005), the comparisons `username != null` and `password != null` are not optional. If you forget to include these and then click Cancel when the program runs, you get a nasty `NullPointerException` message, and the program comes crashing down before your eyes. The word `null` represents nothing, and in Java, you can't compare nothing to a string like `"bburd"` or `"swordfish"`. In [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005), the purpose of the comparison `username != null` is to prevent Java from moving on to check `username.equals("bburd")` whenever you happen to click Cancel. Without this preliminary `username != null` test, you're courting trouble.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) The last couple of `nulls` in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005) are different from the others. In the code `JOptionPane.showMessageDialog (null, "You're in.")`, the word `null` stands for “no other dialog box.” In particular, the call `showMessageDialog` tells Java to pop up a new dialog box, and the word `null` indicates that the new dialog box doesn't grow out of any existing dialog box. One way or another, Java insists that you say something about the origin of the newly popped dialog box. (For some reason, Java doesn't insist that you specify the origin of the `showInputDialog` box. Go figure!) Anyway, in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005), having a `showMessageDialog` box pop up from nowhere is quite useful.

### (Conditions in parentheses)

Keep an eye on those parentheses! When you're combining conditions with logical operators, it's better to waste typing effort and add unneeded parentheses than to goof up your result by using too few parentheses. Take, for example, this expression:

`2 < 5 || 100 < 6 && 27 < 1`

By misreading this expression, you might conclude that the expression is false. That is, you could wrongly read the expression as meaning `(`*`something-or-other`*`) && 27 < 1`. Because `27 < 1` is false, you would conclude that the whole expression is false. The fact is that, in Java, any `&&` operator is evaluated before any `||` operator. So the expression really asks whether `2 < 5 || (`*`something-or-other`*`)`. Because `2 < 5` is true, the whole expression is true.

To change the expression's value from `true` to `false`, you can put the expression's first two comparisons in parentheses, like this:

**`(`**`2 < 5 || 100 < 6`**`)`** `&& 27 < 1`

![Tip](images/tip.png) Java's `||` operator is *inclusive.* This means that you get a `true` value whenever the thing on the left side is true, the thing on the right side is true, or both things are true. For instance, the expression `2 < 10 || 20 < 30` is true.

![Warning](images/warning.png) In Java, you can't combine comparisons the way you do in ordinary English. In English, you may say, “We'll have between three and ten people at the dinner table.” But in Java, you get an error message if you write `3 <= people <= 10`. To do this comparison, you need something like `3 <= people && people <= 10`.

In [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005), the `if` statement's condition has more than a dozen parentheses. What happens if you omit two of them?

`if (`
`username != null && password != null &&`
**`// open parenthesis omitted`**
`(username.equals("bburd") && password.equals("swordfish")) ||`
`(username.equals("hritter") && password.equals("preakston"))`
**`// close parenthesis omitted`**
`)`

Java tries to interpret your wishes by grouping everything before the “or” (the `||` operator):

`if (`
`username != null && password != null &&`
`(username.equals("bburd") && password.equals("swordfish"))`

**`||`**

`(username.equals("hritter") && password.equals("preakston"))`
`)`

When the user clicks Cancel and `username` is `null`, Java says, “Okay! The stuff before the `||` operator is false, but maybe the stuff after the `||` operator is true. I'll check the stuff after the `||` operator to find out whether it's true.” (Java often talks to itself. The psychiatrists are monitoring this situation.)

Anyway, when Java finally checks `username.equals("hritter")`, your program aborts with an ugly `NullPointerException` message. You've made Java angry by trying to apply `.equals` to a `null` username. (Psychiatrists have recommended anger management sessions for Java, but Java's insurance plan refuses to pay for the sessions.)

### An import declaration

It's always good to announce your intentions upfront. Consider the following classroom lecture:

*   *Today, in our History of Film course, we'll discuss the career of actor **Lionel Herbert Blythe Barrymore***.
*   *Born in Philadelphia, **Barrymore** appeared in more than 200 films, including* It’s a Wonderful Life, Key Largo, and Dr. Kildare’s Wedding Day*. In addition, **Barrymore** was a writer, composer, and director. Barrymore did the voice of Ebenezer Scrooge every year on radio… .*

Interesting stuff, heh? Now compare these paragraphs with a lecture in which the instructor doesn’t begin by introducing the subject:

*   *Welcome once again to the History of Film.*
*   *Born in Philadelphia, **Lionel Barrymore** appeared in more than 200 films, including* It’s a Wonderful Life, Key Largo, *and* Dr. Kildare’s Wedding Day. *In addition, **Barrymore (not Ethel, John, or Drew)** was a writer, composer, and director. **Lionel Barrymore** did the voice of Ebenezer Scrooge every year on radio… .*

Without a proper introduction, a speaker may have to remind you repeatedly that the discussion is about Lionel Barrymore and not about any other Barrymore. The same is true in a Java program. Look again at the beginning of [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005):

**`import javax.swing.JOptionPane;`**

`void main() {`
`String username =` **`JOptionPane`**`.showInputDialog("Username:");`
`String password =` **`JOptionPane`**`.showInputDialog("Password:");`

The first line of [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005) is an *import declaration*. The import declaration announces that you’ll be using `JOptionPane` in this Java program. The declaration clarifies what you mean by `JOptionPane` with the class's full name `javax.swing.JOptionPane`. (Hey! Didn't the first lecturer clarify with the full name “Lionel Herbert Blythe Barrymore”?) After announcing your intentions in the import declaration, you can use the abbreviated name `JOptionPane` in the remainder of [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005).

Like thousands of other names, the name `javax.swing.JOptionPane` is defined in Java's API. So, to use the name `JOptionPane` throughout [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005), I import `javax.swing.JOptionPane` at the top of the listing. If you don't use this import declaration, you have to repeat the full `javax.swing.JOptionPane` name wherever you use the name `JOptionPane` in your code. For example, without an import declaration, the start of [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005) would look like this:

`void main() {`
`String username =` **`javax.swing.JOptionPane`**`.showInputDialog("Username:");`
`String password =` **`javax.swing.JOptionPane`**`.showInputDialog("Password:");`

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) To be precise, `javax.swing.JOptionPane` is the *fully qualified name* of a class whose *simple name* is `JOptionPane`.

Every class in Java's API has a fully qualified name. For example, the fully qualified name of the familiar `String` class is `java.lang.String`. The `Random` class's close friends call it by its simple name `Random`. But the `Random` class's birth certificate has the class's fully qualified name — `java.util.Random`.

So your next question is, “Why do I have to import `javax.swing.JOptionPane` and not `java.lang.String` or `java.util.Random`? The answer is that some classes in Java's API creep into your code without the need for explicit import declarations. Java's API has thousands of classes, and each class belongs to a group of classes known as a *package*. Going one step further, each package belongs to a group of packages known as a *module*. [Figure 5-8](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0008) shows you a few of the classes in the Java API.

![“A diagram showing the structure of Java modules and packages. It includes java.baseand java.desktopmodules. The java.basemodule contains java.io, java.lang, and java.utilpackages with classes and methods like println, equals, parseInt, and nextInt. The java.desktopmodule contains javax.swingpackage with classes and methods like JOptionPane and getPassword.”](images/9781394289240-fg0508.png)

[FIGURE 5-8:](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fig-0008) What-lives-where in Java's API.

Notice the box labeled `java.base` in [Figure 5-8](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0008). With each example in [Chapters 1](#c01.xhtml)–[6](#c06.xhtml) of this book, you don't need an import declaration for any class inside `java.base`. Java automatically imports all these classes for you. So, you don't start any of this chapter's examples by importing `String` or `Random`. But you start [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005) by importing `javax.swing.JOptionPane` because `JOptionPane` isn't inside `java.base`.

### Warning! Explicit content!

Here's an official explanation of the `import` situation in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005): A Java program has explicit elements and implicit elements.

*   **An *explicit* element corresponds to some text in the program.**

    At the start of [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005), the line `import javax.swing.JOptionPane` is an explicit import declaration. For that matter, each part of the code in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005) stands for an explicit something-or-other.

*   **An *implicit* element is something that Java adds behind the scenes, without corresponding to any text in the program.**

    Even though the line `import java.lang.String` doesn't appear in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005), Java behaves as if the listing contains that line. This comes from the rule that Java implicitly declares everything in `java.base` for programs like the one in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005).

Starting in [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml), you can find listings that don't implicitly import all the classes inside the `java.base` module. Instead, these listings implicitly import only classes inside the `java.lang` package. (Refer to [Figure 5-8](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0008).) So, in some of these listings, you don't need an import declaration for `String`, but you do need import declarations for `Random`. Sorry about that. All good things must come to an end!

There's another wrinkle in this story about import declarations. It involves `println`, `Integer`, `Double`, and many other names. My advice is for you to ignore the wrinkle until you read about `static` in [Chapter 11](#c11.xhtml).

![Remember](images/remember.png) Understanding import declarations isn't easy. But fortunately, many IDEs have convenient helper features for import declarations. In your IDE of choice, look for menus such as Code ⇒   Optimize Imports or Source ⇒   Organize Imports.

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) For more details about the use of import declarations, see [Chapters 7](#c07.xhtml), [11](#c11.xhtml), and [12](#c12.xhtml).

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Make some changes to the code in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005).

#### THE RULE OF THREE

Add a third username/password combination to the list of acceptable logins.

#### OUT, DAMN'D NOT!

In [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005), change

`username != null && password != null`

to

`!(username == null || password == null)`

Does the program still work? Why or why not?

#### EQUAL BYTES

In [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005), change

`username != null && password != null`

to

`!(username == null` **`&&`** `password == null)`

This is almost the same as the previous experiment. The only difference is the use of `&&` instead of `||` between the two `== null` tests. Does the program still work? Why or why not?

#### PASSWORDS IN PLAIN SIGHT

Run the following code and find out what it does:

`import javax.swing.JOptionPane;`
`import javax.swing.JPasswordField;`

`void main() {`
`JPasswordField passwordField = new JPasswordField();`
`JOptionPane.showMessageDialog(null, new Object[] {"Password", passwordField});`
`String userInput = new String(passwordField.getPassword());`
`if (userInput.equals("swordfish")) {`
`JOptionPane.showMessageDialog(null, "You're in.");`
`} else {`
`JOptionPane.showMessageDialog(null, "You're suspicious.");`
`}`
`}`

## The Nesting Habits of if Statements

Have you seen those cute Russian matryoshka nesting dolls? Open one, and another one is inside. Open the second, and a third one is inside it. You can do the same thing with Java's `if` statements. (Talk about fun!) [Listing 5-5](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0006) shows you how.

[LISTING 5-5](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fea-0006) Nested if Statements

`void main() {`
`String username = readln("Username: ");`
`if (username.equals("bburd")) {`
`String password = readln("Password: ");`
`if (password.equals("swordfish")) {`
`println("You're in.");`
`} else {`
`println("Incorrect password");`
`}`
`} else {`
`println("Unknown user");`
`}`
`}`

[Figure 5-9](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0009) shows several runs of the code in [Listing 5-5](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0006). The main idea is that to log on, you have to pass two tests. (In other words, two conditions must be true.) The first condition tests for a valid username; the second condition tests for the correct password. If you pass the first test (the username test), you march right into another `if` statement that performs a second test (the password test). If you fail the first test, you never make it to the second test. [Figure 5-10](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0010) shows the overall plan.

![“The image shows three login attempts with different outcomes. The first attempt with username 'bburd' and password 'swordfish' is successful, displaying 'You're in.' The second attempt with the same username 'bburd' but password 'catfish' fails, displaying 'Incorrect password.' The third attempt with username 'jschmoe' results in 'Unknown user.'”](images/9781394289240-fg0509.png)

[FIGURE 5-9:](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fig-0009) Three runs of the code in [Listing 5-5](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0006).

![“A flowchart for user authentication. The first decision point asks if the username equals 'lizard'. If no, the result is 'Unknown user'. If yes, the next decision point asks if the password equals 'swordfish'. If no, the result is 'Incorrect password'. If yes, the result is 'You're in'.”](images/9781394289240-fg0510.png)

[FIGURE 5-10:](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fig-0010) Don't eat with this fork.

#### I CHANGED MY MIND

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Modify the program in [Listing 5-4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0005) so that if the user clicks Cancel for either the username or the password, the program replies with a `Not enough information` message.

## Choosing among Many Alternatives

I'm the first to admit that I hate making decisions. If things go wrong, I would rather have the problem be someone else's fault. Writing the previous sections (on making decisions with Java’s `if` statement) knocked the stuffing right out of me. That’s why my mind boggles as I begin this section on choosing among many alternatives. What a relief it is to have that confession out of the way!

### Java's glorious switch statement

Now it’s time to explore situations in which you have a decision with many branches. Take, for instance, the popular campfire song “[Al’s All Wet](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0041).” (For a review of the lyrics, see the nearby “[Al’s All Wet](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0041)” sidebar.) You’re eager to write code that prints this song’s lyrics. Fortunately, you don’t have to type all the words over and over again. Instead, you can take advantage of the repetition in the lyrics.

To display parts of the song's lyrics, you create a variable named `verse`. The value of `verse` is `1`, `2`, or `3`, depending on which verse of “Al's All Wet” you're trying to print. You could have a big, clumsy bunch of `if` statements that checks each possible `verse` number:

`if (verse == 1) {`
`println("That's because he has no brain.");`
`}`
`if (verse == 2) {`
`println("That's because he is a pain.");`
`}`
`if (verse == 3) {`
`println("'Cause this is the last refrain.");`
`}`

But that approach seems wasteful. Why not create a statement that checks the value of `verse` just once and then takes an action based on the value it finds? Fortunately, just such a statement exists — it's called a *switch* statement. [Listing 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0008) has an example of a `switch` statement.

[LISTING 5-6](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fea-0008) A switch Statement

`void main() {`
`int verse = Integer.parseInt(readln("Which verse? "));`
`switch (verse) {`
`case 1 -> println("That's because he has no brain.");`
`case 2 -> println("That's because he is a pain.");`
`case 3 -> println("'Cause this is the last refrain.");`
`default -> println("No such verse. Please try again.");`
`}`
`println("Oh, oh, oh, oh");`
`}`

![Warning](images/warning.png) Before Oracle released Java 14 in 2020, most of the features I describe in this section weren't available. If you stumble upon a reason to use an older version of Java, check the later section titled “[Your grandparents' `switch` statement](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0045).”

[Figure 5-11](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0011) shows two runs of the program in [Listing 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0008). ([Figure 5-12](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0012) illustrates the program's overall idea.) First, the user types a number, like the number 2\. Then execution of the program reaches the top of the `switch` statement. The computer checks the value of the `verse` variable. When the computer determines that the `verse` variable's value is `2`, the computer checks each case of the `switch` statement. The value `2` doesn't match the topmost case, so the computer proceeds to the middle of the three cases. The value posted for the middle case (the number 2) matches the value of the `verse` variable, so the computer executes the statement in case 2:

`println("That's because he is a pain.");`

![“The image shows text from a conversation or script. It includes two sections where a question 'Which verse?' is followed by a number. The first section asks for verse 2 and responds with 'That's because he is a pain. Oh, oh, oh, oh.' The second section asks for verse 6 and responds with 'No such verse. Please try again. Oh, oh, oh, oh.'”](images/9781394289240-fg0511.png)

[FIGURE 5-11:](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fig-0011) Running the code from [Listing 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0008) two times.

![“A flowchart asks 'Which verse is this?' with four options: 1 (Has no brain), 2 (Is a pain), 3 (Last refrain), and other (Try again). Below the options, the text reads 'Oh, oh, oh, oh.'”](images/9781394289240-fg0512.png)

[FIGURE 5-12:](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fig-0012) The big fork in the code of [Listing 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0008).

If the pesky user asks for verse 6, the computer bypasses cases 1, 2, and 3\. The computer goes straight to the default. In the default, the computer displays `No such verse. Please try again` and then jumps out of the `switch` statement. After the computer is out of the `switch` statement, the computer displays `Oh, oh, oh, oh`.

![Tip](images/tip.png) You can put more than one value in each `case` clause of a `switch` statement. You can also use curly braces to create a block of statements inside a `case` clause. Here's some sample code:

`void main() {`
`String reply = readln("Will you pay me? ");`
`switch (reply.toLowerCase()) {`
`case "yes", "y", "ok" -> {`
`println("Thank you!");`
`println("You're very nice.");`
`}`
`case "Maybe" -> println("Wha' da' ya' mean by that?");`
`case "no", "n" -> {`
`println("You're a deadbeat.");`
`println("Thanks for nothing!");`
`}`
`}`
`}`

This code responds correctly to `yes`, `y`, and `ok`. In fact, if the user types `YES` or even `yEs`, the value of `reply.toLowerCase()` is the lowercase string `"yes"`, so the program responds with `"Thank you!"` and `"You're very nice."`

### Express yourself

If you look again at [Listing 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0008), you wonder why someone took so much delight in typing the word `println`. That word appears four times in the `switch` statement, and the only difference is the choice of a sarcastic verse. This repeated use of `println` seems wasteful. Why not have only one call to `println` for all the different answers the program can display?

Java's `switch` expression addresses this issue. In fact, runs of the code in [Listing 5-7](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0009) look exactly the same as the runs of [Listing 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0008). (Refer to [Figure 5-11](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0011).)

[LISTING 5-7](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fea-0009) Out with the Old println!

`void main() {`
`int verse = Integer.parseInt(readln("Which verse? "));`

`String line;`
`line = switch (verse) {`
`case 1 -> "That's because he has no brain.";`
`case 2 -> "That's because he is a pain.";`
`case 3 -> "'Cause this is the last refrain.";`
`default -> "No such verse. Please try again.";`
`};`

`println(line);`
`println("Oh, oh, oh, oh");`
`}`

In any computer language, the word *expression* refers to a bunch of code that stands for a value. In [Listing 5-7](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0009), the entire `switch` expression stands for a string of characters. For example, when `verse` is `2`, the code

`line = switch (verse) {`
`case 1 -> "That's because he has no brain.";`
`case 2 -> "That's because he is a pain.";`
`case 3 -> "'Cause this is the last refrain.";`
`default -> "No such verse. Please try again.";`
`};`

stands for the string `"That's because he is a pain."` So, in that scenario, [Listing 5-7](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0009) does the same thing as this code:

`line = "That's because he is a pain.";`
`out.println(line);`

In this small snippet of code, notice how the first line ends with a semicolon. That's why there's a semicolon at the end of the big `switch` expression in [Listing 5-7](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0009).

You can squeeze even more goodness out of Java's `switch` expression. For example, you don't have to assign the expression's value to a variable. You can bypass the variable and print the `switch` expression's value:

**`println(`**`switch (verse) {`
`case 1 -> "That's because he has no brain.";`
`case 2 -> "That's because he is a pain.";`
`case 3 -> "'Cause this is the last refrain.";`
`default -> "No such verse. Please try again.";`
`}`**`)`**`;`

But remember that a `switch` expression can't stand independently on its own. The following code makes no sense:

**`// Bad code!`**
`int verse = Integer.parseInt(readln("Which verse? "));`
`String line;`

**`"That's because he is a pain."; // This is not a complete statement.`**

`println(line);`

The code with a freestanding `switch` expression doesn't work, either.

**`// Bad code!`**
`int verse = Integer.parseInt(readln("Which verse? "));`
`String line;`

`switch (verse) {` **`// This`**
`case 1 -> "That's because he has no brain.";` **`// is`**
`case 2 -> "That's because he is a pain.";` **`// not`**
`case 3 -> "'Cause this is the last refrain.";` **`// a`**
`default -> "No such verse. Please try again.";` **`// complete`**
`};` **`// statement.`**

`println(line);`

### Your grandparents' switch statement

In versions of Java before Java 14, the `switch` constructs in [Listings 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0008) and [5-7](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0009) wouldn't have worked. That's because Java's `switch` statement came originally from the `switch` statement in the C/C++ language family, and the C/C++ `switch` statement was quite clunky. The code in [Listing 5-8](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0010) does the same thing as the code in [Listings 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0008) and [5-7](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0009), but [Listing 5-8](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0010) uses Java's old-style `switch` statement.

[LISTING 5-8](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fea-0010) A la recherche du temps perdu

`void main() {`
`int verse = Integer.parseInt(readln("Which verse? "));`
`switch (verse) {`
`case 1:`
`println("That's because he has no brain.");`
`break;`
`case 2:`
`println("That's because he is a pain.");`
`break;`
`case 3:`
`println("'Cause this is the last refrain.");`
`break;`
`default:`
`println("No such verse. Please try again.");`
`break;`
`}`
`println("Oh, oh, oh, oh");`
`}`

Runs of the code in [Listing 5-8](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0010) look exactly the same as runs of [Listings 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0008) and [5-7](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0009). (Refer to [Figure 5-11](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0011).) The big difference is that [Listing 5-8](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0010) uses the old form of the `switch` statement. In the old form:

*   You use colons (`:`) instead of arrows (`->`).
*   You need `break` statements to bypass any remaining cases.

When Java encounters a `break` statement, it jumps out of whatever `switch` statement it's in. For example, imagine that `verse` has the value `2`. In [Listing 5-8](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0010), Java prints `That's because he is a pain`. Then, because of the `break` statement, Java skips right past the case that would display `'Cause this is the last refrain`. In fact, Java jumps out of the entire `switch` statement and goes directly to the statement just after the end of the `switch` statement. The computer displays `Oh, oh, oh, oh` because that's what the statement after the `switch` statement tells Java to do.

![Tip](images/tip.png) You don't really need to put a `break` at the very end of an old `switch` statement. In [Listing 5-8](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0010), the last `break` (the `break` that's part of the default) is just for the sake of overall tidiness.

The need for `break` inside the old `switch` statement is a Java programmer's nightmare. Everyone forgets to add these `break` statements, and when you forget to add `break` statements, you get fall-through. With *fall-through*, execution of the code falls right through from one case to the next. Execution keeps falling through until you eventually reach a `break` statement or the end of the entire `switch` statement.

![Tip](images/tip.png) With `switch` statements from the olden days, you can sandwich as many statements as you want between two `case` clauses. For example, the following code snippet is perfectly legal:

`case 1:`
`print("That's because ");`
`println("he has no brain.");`
`println("… and because I don't like him.");`
`break;`
`case 2:`

### Free fall

Usually, when you're using a `switch` statement, you don't want fall-through, so you pepper `break` statements throughout the `switch` statements. But, occasionally, fall-through is just the thing you need. Take, for instance, the “Al's All Wet” song. (The classy lyrics are shown in the sidebar bearing the song's name.) Each verse of “[Al’s All Wet](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0041)” adds new lines in addition to the lines from previous verses. This situation (accumulating lines from one verse to another) cries out for a `switch` statement with fall-through. [Listing 5-9](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0011) demonstrates the idea.

[LISTING 5-9](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fea-0011) A switch Statement with Fall-Through

`void main() {`
`String verse = readln("Which verse? (one, two or three) ");`
`switch (verse) {`
`case "three": println("Last refrain, last refrain,");`
`case "two": println("He's a pain, he's a pain,");`
`case "one": println("Has no brain, has no brain,");`
`}`
`println("""`
`In the rain, in the rain.`
`Oh, oh, oh, oh""");`
`println("");`
`}`

[Figure 5-13](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0013) shows several runs of the program in [Listing 5-9](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0011). Because the `switch` has no `break` statements in it, fall-through happens all over the place. For instance, when the user enters the word `two`, Java starts by executing the statement in `case "two"`:

`println("He's a pain, he's a pain,");`

Then Java marches right on to execute the statement in `case "one"`:

`println("Has no brain, has no brain,");`

That's good because the song's second verse has all these lines in it.

Notice what happens when the user asks for verse six. The `switch` statement in [Listing 5-9](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0011) has no `case "six"` and no `default`, so none of the actions inside the `switch` statement is executed. Even so, with statements that print `In the rain, in the rain` and `Oh, oh, oh, oh` right after the `switch` statement, the computer displays something when the user asks for verse `"six"`.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Get some practice with `if` statements and `switch` statements!

![“The image contains four sets of lyrics, each asking 'Which verse? (one, two, or three)' followed by the corresponding verse number. The lyrics describe a character with no brain, who is a pain, in the rain, with repetitive 'Oh, oh, oh, oh' lines. The fourth set humorously asks for verse six, which does not exist, and repeats the 'In the rain, in the rain' line.”](images/9781394289240-fg0513.png)

[FIGURE 5-13:](#c05.xhtml_rc05-fig-0013) Running the code of [Listing 5-9](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0011) four times.

#### MONTH-TO-MONTH RESUSCITATION

Write a program that inputs the name of a month and outputs the number of days in that month. In this first version of the program, assume that February always has 28 days. Use Java's `switch` statement — the kind I used in [Listing 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0008).

#### LEAPING TO CONCLUSIONS

Make changes to your code from the earlier section “[Month-to-Month Resuscitation](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0049).” Have the user input a month name and also `yes` or `no` in response to the question `Is it a leap year?`

#### HOW ABOUT A DATE?

Make changes to your code from the earlier section “[Leaping to Conclusions](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0051).” Rather than mimic [Listing 5-6](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0008), use a `switch` expression of the kind you find in [Listing 5-7](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0009). Then use Java's old-style `switch` statement, like the one you see in [Listing 5-8](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0010).

#### THE YIELD APP

Take this code for a spin and see what it does:

`void main() {`
`String month = readln("Which month? ");`
`println(switch (month) {`
`case "January", "March", "May", "July",`
`"August", "October", "December" -> 31;`
`case "April", "June", "September", "November" -> 30;`
`case "February" -> {`
`boolean isLeapYear =`
`Boolean.parseBoolean(readln("Leap year (true/false)? "));`
`if (isLeapYear) {`
`yield 29;`
`} else {`
`yield 28;`
`}`
`}`
`default -> 0;`
`} + " days");`
`}`

Chapter 6

# Controlling Program Flow with Loops

IN THIS CHAPTER

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Using basic looping**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Counting as you loop**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Repeating relentlessly (until the user provides a clear answer)**

In 1966, the company that brings you Head & Shoulders shampoo made history. On the back of the bottle, the directions for using the shampoo read, “LATHER–RINSE–REPEAT.” Never before had a complete set of directions (for doing anything, let alone shampooing your hair) been summarized so succinctly. People in the directions-writing business hailed this as a monumental achievement. Directions like these stood in stark contrast to others of the time. (For instance, the first sentence on a can of bug spray read, “Turn this can so that it points away from your face.” Duh!)

Aside from their brevity, the thing that made the Head & Shoulders directions so cool was that, with three simple words, company leaders managed to capture a notion that’s at the heart of all instruction-giving: repetition. That last word, *REPEAT,* took an otherwise bland instructional drone and turned it into a sophisticated recipe for action.

The fundamental idea is that when you’re following directions, you don’t just follow one instruction after another. Instead, you take turns in the road. You make decisions (“If HAIR IS DRY, then USE CONDITIONER”) and you go into loops (“LATHER–RINSE, and then LATHER–RINSE again.”). In computer programming, you use decision-making and looping all the time. This chapter explores looping in Java.

## Repeating Instructions Over and Over Again (Java while Statements)

Here’s a guessing game for you. The computer generates a random number from 1 to 10\. The computer asks you to guess the number. If you guess incorrectly, the game continues. As soon as you guess correctly, the game is over. [Listing 6-1](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0001) shows the program to play the game, and [Figure 6-1](#c06.xhtml_c06-fig-0001) shows a round of play.

![“The image shows a text-based guessing game. The game welcomes the player and asks them to pick a number from 1 to 10\. The player makes several guesses: 2, 5, 8, and finally 3, which is the correct number. The game indicates that the player wins after 4 guesses.”](images/9781394289240-fg0601.png)

[FIGURE 6-1:](#c06.xhtml_rc06-fig-0001) Play until you drop.

[LISTING 6-1](#c06.xhtml_rc06-fea-0001) A Repeating Guessing Game

`void main() {`

`int numGuesses = 0;`
`int randomNumber = new Random().nextInt(10) + 1;`

`println("""`
`************`
`Welcome to the Guessing Game`
`************""");`
`println(""); // or println(); (See Chapter 4.)`

`String input = readln("Pick a number from 1 to 10: ");`
`int inputNumber = Integer.parseInt(input);`
`numGuesses++;`

**`while (inputNumber != randomNumber) {`**
`println("");`
`println("Try again…");`
`input = readln("Pick a number from 1 to 10: ");`
`inputNumber = Integer.parseInt(input);`
`numGuesses++;`
**`}`**
`print("You win after ");`
`println(numGuesses + " guesses.");`
`}`

In [Figure 6-1](#c06.xhtml_c06-fig-0001), the user makes four guesses. Each time around, the computer checks to see whether the guess is correct. An incorrect guess generates a request to try again. For a correct guess, the user gets a rousing `You win`, along with a tally of the number of guesses they made. The computer repeats several statements, checking each time through to see whether the user’s guess is the same as a certain randomly generated number. Each time the user makes a guess, the computer adds 1 to its tally of guesses. When the user makes the correct guess, the computer displays that tally. [Figure 6-2](#c06.xhtml_c06-fig-0002) illustrates the flow of action.

![“A black background with a simple white outline of a cat's face, including the eyes, nose, mouth, and whiskers. The design is minimalistic and striking.”](images/9781394289240-fg0602.png)

[FIGURE 6-2:](#c06.xhtml_rc06-fig-0002) Around and around you go.

When you look over [Listing 6-1](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0001), you see the code that does all this work. At the core of the code is a thing called a *while statement* (also known as a *while loop*). Rephrased in English, the `while` statement says:

`while inputNumber` *`doesn't equal`* `randomNumber`*`, repeat what's in these braces`* `{`

`}`

The stuff in curly braces (the stuff that repeats) is the code that prints `Try again` and `Pick a number…`, gets a value from the keyboard, and adds 1 to the count of the user’s guesses.

![Tip](images/tip.png) When you’re dealing with counters, like `numGuesses` in [Listing 6-1](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0001), you may easily become confused and be off by 1 in either direction. You can avoid this headache by making sure that the `++` statements stay close to the statements whose events you’re counting. For example, in [Listing 6-1](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0001), the variable `numGuesses` starts with a value of `0`. That’s because, when the program starts running, the user hasn’t made any guesses. Later in the program, right after each call to `readln`, is a `numGuesses++` statement. That’s how you do it — you increment the counter as soon as the user enters another guess.

The statements in curly braces are repeated as long as `inputNumber != randomNumber` is true. Each repetition of the statements in the loop is called an *iteration* of the loop. In [Figure 6-1](#c06.xhtml_c06-fig-0001), the loop undergoes three iterations. (If you don’t believe that [Figure 6-1](#c06.xhtml_c06-fig-0001) has exactly three iterations, count the number of `Try again` printings in the program’s output. A `Try again` appears for each incorrect guess.)

When, at long last, the user enters the correct guess, the computer goes back to the top of the `while` statement, checks the condition in parentheses, and finds itself in double double-negative land. The not equal (`!=`) relationship between `inputNumber` and `randomNumber` no longer holds. In other words, the `while` statement’s condition, `inputNumber != randomNumber`, is false. Because the `while` statement’s condition is false, the computer jumps past the `while` loop and goes on to the statements just below the `while` loop. In these two statements, the computer prints `You win` after four guesses.

![Remember](images/remember.png) With code of the kind shown in [Listing 6-1](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0001), the computer never jumps out in midloop. When the computer finds that `inputNumber` isn’t equal to `randomNumber`, the computer marches on and executes all five statements inside the loop’s curly braces. The computer performs the test again (to see whether `inputNumber` is still not equal to `randomNumber`) only after it fully executes all five statements in the loop.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) I have two things for you to try:

#### BIGGER AND BETTER

Modify the program in [Listing 6-1](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0001) so that the randomly generated number is a number from 1 through 100\. To make life bearable for the game player, have the program display a hint whenever the player guesses incorrectly. Hints such as `Try a higher number` or `Try a lower number` are helpful.

#### NO NEGATIVITY

Write a program in which the user types `int` values, one after another. The program stops looping when the user types a number that isn’t positive (for example, the number 0 or the number –17). After all the looping, the program displays the largest number that the user typed. For example, if the user types the numbers

`7`
`25`
`3`
`9`
`0`

the program displays the number 25.

## Count On Me

“Write *I will not talk in class* on the blackboard 100 times.”

What your teacher really meant was this:

*`Set the count to 0.`*
*`As long as the count is less than 100,`*
*`Write 'I will not talk in class' on the blackboard,`*
*`Add 1 to the count.`*

Fortunately, you didn’t know about loops and counters at the time. If you pointed out all this stuff to your teacher, you’d have gotten into a lot more trouble than you were already in.

One way or another, life is filled with examples of counting loops. And computer programming mirrors life — or is it the other way around? When you tell a computer what to do, you’re often telling the computer to print three lines, process ten accounts, dial a million phone numbers, or whatever. Because counting loops is so common in programming, the people who create programming languages have developed statements just for loops of this kind. In Java, the statement that repeats something a certain number of times is called a *for statement.*[Listings 6-2](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0002) and [6-3](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0003) illustrate the use of the `for` statement. [Listing 6-2](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0002) has a rock-bottom simple example and [Listing 6-3](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0003) has a more exotic example. Take your pick.

[LISTING 6-2](#c06.xhtml_rc06-fea-0002) The World’s Most Boring for Loop

`void main() {`
**`for (int count = 1; count <= 10; count++) {`**
`print("The value of count is ");`
`print(count);`
`println(".");`
**`}`**
`println("Done!");`
`}`

[Figure 6-3](#c06.xhtml_c06-fig-0003) shows you what you get when you run the program from [Listing 6-2](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0002). (You get exactly what you deserve.) The `for` statement in [Listing 6-2](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0002) starts by setting the `count` variable to 1\. Then the statement tests to make sure that `count` is less than or equal to 10 (which it certainly is). Then the `for` statement dives ahead and executes the printing statements between the curly braces. (At this early stage of the game, the computer prints `The value of count is 1`.) Finally, the `for` statement does that last thing inside its parentheses — it adds 1 to the value of `count`.

![“A text-based image displaying a sequence of statements indicating the value of a variable named 'count' incrementing from 1 to 10, followed by the word 'Done!'.”](images/9781394289240-fg0603.png)

[FIGURE 6-3:](#c06.xhtml_rc06-fig-0003) Counting to ten.

With `count` now equal to 2, the `for` statement checks again to make sure that `count` is less than or equal to 10\. (Yes, 2 is smaller than 10.) Because the test turns out okay, the `for` statement marches back into the curly-braced statements and prints `The value of count is 2` on the screen. Finally, the `for` statement does that last thing inside its parentheses — it adds 1 to the value of `count`, increasing the value of `count` to 3.

And so on. This whole business repeats until, after ten iterations, the value of `count` finally reaches 11. When this happens, the check for `count` being less than or equal to 10 fails and the loop’s execution ends. The computer jumps to whatever statement comes immediately after the `for` statement. In [Listing 6-2](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0002), the computer prints `Done!` as its output. [Figure 6-4](#c06.xhtml_c06-fig-0004) illustrates the whole process.

![“A flowchart illustrating a loop that starts by setting a variable 'count' to 1\. It checks if 'count' is less than or equal to 10\. If true, it prints 'The value of count is ...' and adds 1 to 'count', then repeats the check. If false, the process ends with 'Done'.”](images/9781394289240-fg0604.png)

[FIGURE 6-4:](#c06.xhtml_rc06-fig-0004) The action of the `for` loop in [Listing 6-2](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0002).

### The anatomy of a for statement

After the word *for,* you always put three things in parentheses. The first of these three is called an *initialization,* the second is an *expression,* and the third is an *update:*

`for (` *`initialization`* `;` *`expression`* `;` *`update`* `)`

Each of the three items in parentheses plays its own, distinct role:

*   The **initialization** is executed once, when the run of your program first reaches the `for` statement.
*   The **expression** is evaluated several times (before each iteration).
*   The **update** is also evaluated several times (at the end of each iteration).

If it helps, think of the loop as though its text is shifted all around:

*`int`* `count` *`= 1`*
*`for`* `count` *`<= 10 {`*
`print("The value of count is ");`
`print(count);`
`println(".");`
`count++;`
*`}`*

You can’t write a real `for` statement this way. Even so, this is the order in which the parts of the statement are executed.

![Warning](images/warning.png) If you declare a variable in the initialization of a `for` loop, you can’t use that variable outside the loop. For instance, in [Listing 6-2](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0002), you see an error message if you try putting `println(count)` after the end of the loop.

![Tip](images/tip.png) Anything that can be done with a `for` loop can also be done with a `while` loop. Choosing to use a `for` loop is a matter of style and convenience, not necessity.

### The world premiere of “Al’s All Wet”

[Listing 6-2](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0002) is very nice, but the program in that listing does nothing interesting. For a more eye-catching example, see [Listing 6-3](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0003). In [Listing 6-3](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0003), I make good on a promise I make in [Chapter 5](#c05.xhtml): The program in [Listing 6-3](#c06.xhtml_c06-fea-0003) prints all the lyrics of the hit single “Al’s All Wet.” (You can find the lyrics in [Chapter 5](#c05.xhtml).)

[LISTING 6-3](#c06.xhtml_rc06-fea-0003) The Unabridged “Al’s All Wet” Song

`void main() {`
`String intro = """`
`Al's all wet. Oh, why is Al all wet? Oh,`
`Al's all wet 'cause he's standing in the rain.""";`

`for (int verse = 1; verse <= 3; verse++) {`
`println(intro);`
`println("Why is Al out in the rain?");`

`println(switch (verse) {`
`case 1 -> "That's because he has no brain.";`
`case 2 -> "That's because he is a pain.";`
`case 3 -> "'Cause this is the last refrain.";`
`default -> "";`
`});`

`switch (verse) {`
`case 3:`
`println("Last refrain, last refrain,");`
`case 2:`
`println("He's a pain, he's a pain,");`
`case 1:`
`println("Has no brain, has no brain,");`
`}`

`println("""`
