*   *Local resident:* Oh, the planet! Well, then, catch the 8:19 train leaving for Cape Canaveral. No, wait — that’s the local train. That’d take you through West Virginia … .

The meaning of a name depends on where you’re using the name. Although most English-speaking people think of Mars as a place with a carbon dioxide atmosphere, some folks in Pennsylvania think about all the shopping they can do in Mars. And those folks in Pennsylvania really have two meanings for the name *Mars.* In Java, those names may look like this: `Mars` and `planets.Mars`.

### Putting a variable in its place

Your first experiment is shown in [Listings 7-12](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0012) and [7-13](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0013). The listings' code highlights the difference between two kinds of variables:

*   **Field:** A variable that's *not* declared inside of a method.
*   **Method-local variable:** A variable that *is* declared inside of a method.

[LISTING 7-12](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fea-0012) Two Meanings for Mars

`import java.io.IO;`

**`class EnglishSpeakingWorld {`**
**`String mars = " red planet"; // field`**

**`void visitPennsylvania() {`**
`IO.println("visitPA is running:");`

**`String mars = " Janine's home town"; // method-local variable`**

`IO.println(`**`mars`**`);` **`// Janine's home town`**
`IO.println(`**`this.mars`**`);` **`// red planet`**
`}`
`}`

[LISTING 7-13](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fea-0013) Calling the Code of [Listing 7-12](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0012)

**`void main() {`**
`println("main is running:");`
`var e = new EnglishSpeakingWorld();`

`//out.println(mars); cannot resolve symbol`
`println(e.mars);`
`e.visitPennsylvania();`
`}`

[Figure 7-11](#c07.xhtml_c07-fig-0011) shows a run of the code in [Listings 7-12](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0012) and [7-13](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0013). [Figure 7-12](#c07.xhtml_c07-fig-0012) shows a diagram of the code's structure. Notice that, in this example, I choose to put the `main` method *outside* of the `EnglishSpeakingWorld` class.

The `main` method creates an instance of the `EnglishSpeakingWorld` class. The variable e refers to the new instance. The new instance is an object with a variable named `mars` inside it. That `mars` variable has the value `"red planet"`. This `mars` variable is a field.

![“A console output displays the following text: 'main is running: red planet visitPA is running! Janice's home town red planet.' This output is the result of a program execution, indicating the progress and messages from different parts of the code.”](images/9781394289240-fg0711.png)

[FIGURE 7-11:](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fig-0011) A run of the code in [Listings 7-12](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0012) and [7-13](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0013).

![“A UML-like diagram showcases two main sections: EnglishSpeakingWorld and main. The EnglishSpeakingWorld section includes a field named mars with the value red planet and a method/variable named mars with the value Janine's home town inside a method called visitPennsylvania. The main section contains a reference to the mars field in EnglishSpeakingWorld. This diagram illustrates the relationship between these sections and highlights how fields and methods with the same name can exist in different contexts within a program.”](images/9781394289240-fg0712.png)

[FIGURE 7-12:](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fig-0012) The structure of the code in [Listings 7-12](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0012) and [7-13](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0013).

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) Another way to describe that `mars` field is to call it an *instance variable* because that `mars` variable (the variable whose value is `"red planet"`) belongs to an *instance* of the `EnglishSpeakingWorld` class.

Now look at the `main` method in [Listing 7-13](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0013). Inside the `main` method, you aren't permitted to write `println(mars)`. In other words, a bare-faced reference to any `mars` variable is a definite no-no. The `mars` variable that I mention in the preceding paragraph belongs to the `EnglishSpeakingWorld` class. But the `main` method lives outside of the `EnglishSpeakingWorldClass`. Too bad for that!

However, inside the main method, you can certainly write `e.mars` because the e variable refers to your `EnglishSpeakingWorld` object. That's nice.

Near the bottom of [Listing 7-13](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0013), you call the `visitPennsylvania` method. When you're inside `visitPennsylvania`, you have another declaration of a `mars` variable, whose value is `"Janine's home town"`. This particular `mars` variable is a method-local variable because it belongs to just one method: the `visitPennsylvania` method.

Now you have to worry about two variables in [Listing 7-12](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0012), both with the name *mars.* One `mars` variable, a field, has the value `"red planet"`. The other `mars` variable, a method-local variable, has the value `"Janine's home town"`. In [Listing 7-12](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0012), when you use the word *mars,* which of the two variables are you referring to?

The answer is, when you're visiting Pennsylvania, the variable with value `"Janine's home town"` wins. When in Pennsylvania, think the way the Pennsylvanians think. When you're executing code inside the `visitPennsylvania` method, resolve any variable name conflicts by going with method-local variables — variables declared right inside the `visitPennsylvania` method.

What if you're in Pennsylvania and need to refer to that 2-mooned celestial object? More precisely, how does code inside the `visitPennsylvania` method refer to the field with value `"red planet"`? The answer is, use `this.mars`. The word *this* points to whatever object contains all this code (and not to any methods inside the code). That object, an instance of the `EnglishSpeakingWorld` class, has a big, fat `mars` field, and that field's value is `"red planet"`. So that's how you can force code to see outside the method it’s in — you use the Java keyword `this`.

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) For more information on the keyword `this`, see [Chapter 10](#c10.xhtml).

### Telling a variable where to go

Years ago, when I lived in Milwaukee, Wisconsin, I made frequent use of the local bank's automatic teller machines. Machines of this kind were just beginning to become standardized. The local teller machine system was named TYME, which stood for Take Your Money Everywhere.

I remember traveling by car out to California. At one point, I got hungry and stopped for a meal, but I was out of cash. So I asked a gas station attendant, “Do you know where there's a TYME machine around here?”

So you see, a name that works well in one place could work terribly, or not at all, in another place. In [Listings 7-14](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0014) and [7-15](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0015), I illustrate this point (with more than just an anecdote about teller machines).

[LISTING 7-14](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fea-0014) Tale of Atomic City

`import java.io.IO;`

`class EnglishSpeakingWorld2 {`
**`String mars;`**

**`void visitIdaho() {`**
`IO.println("visitID is running:");`

**`mars = " red planet";`**
**`String atomicCity = " Population: 25";`**

`IO.println(mars);`
`IO.println(atomicCity);`
`}`

**`void visitNewJersey() {`**
`IO.println("visitNJ is running:");`
`IO.println(mars);`
`// IO.println(atomicCity); cannot resolve symbol`
`}`
`}`

[LISTING 7-15](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fea-0015) Calling the Code of [Listing 7-14](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0014)

`void main() {`
`var e = new EnglishSpeakingWorld2();`

`e.visitIdaho();`
`e.visitNewJersey();`
`}`

[Figure 7-13](#c07.xhtml_c07-fig-0013) shows a run of the code in [Listings 7-14](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0014) and [7-15](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0015). [Figure 7-14](#c07.xhtml_c07-fig-0014) shows a diagram of the code’s structure. The code for `EnglishSpeakingWorld2` has two variables: The `mars` variable, which isn't declared inside a method, is a field; the other variable, `atomicCity`, is a method-local variable and is declared inside the `visitIdaho` method.

![“The console output of a program shows two lines of text. The first line reads, 'visitID is running: red planet Population: 25.' The second line states, 'visitMN is running! red planet.' This output suggests that two separate processes, identified as 'visitID' and 'visitMN,' are interacting with or referencing the same 'red planet.' The 'visitID' process includes additional information about the 'red planet's' population, which is 25.”](images/9781394289240-fg0713.png)

[FIGURE 7-13:](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fig-0013) A run of the code in [Listings 7-14](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0014) and [7-15](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0015).

![“A UML class diagram for EnglishSpeakingWorld2, featuring three methods: mars(field), visitIdaho, and visitNewJersey. The mars(field) method includes a local variable named red planet. Within the visitIdaho method, there's a nested method atomicCity(method=local variable) with a local variable labeled Population: 25\. The visitNewJersey method is currently empty.”](images/9781394289240-fg0714.png)

[FIGURE 7-14:](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fig-0014) The structure of the code in [Listings 7-14](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0014) and [7-15](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0018).

In [Listing 7-14](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0014), notice where each variable can and can't be used. When you try to use the `atomicCity` variable inside the `visitNewJersey` method, you see an error message. Literally, the message says `cannot resolve symbol`. Figuratively, the message says, “Hey, buddy, Atomic City is in Idaho, not New Jersey.” Technically, the message says that the method-local variable `atomicCity` is available only in the `visitIdaho` method because that's where the variable was declared.

Back inside the `visitIdaho` method, you're free to use the `atomicCity` variable as much as you want. After all, the `atomicCity` variable is declared inside the `visitIdaho` method.

And what about Mars? Have you forgotten about your old friend, that lovely 80-degrees-below-0 planet? Well, both the `visitIdaho` and `visitNewJersey` methods can access the `mars` variable. That's because the `mars` variable is a field. That is, the `mars` variable is declared in the code for the `EnglishSpeakingWorld2` class but not inside any particular method. (In my stories about the names for things, remember that people who live in both states, Idaho and New Jersey, have heard of the planet Mars.)

The life cycle of the `mars` field has three separate steps:

1.  When the `EnglishSpeakingWorld2` class first flashes into existence, the computer sees `String mars` and creates space for the `mars` field.
2.  When the `visitIdaho` method is executed, the method assigns the value `"red planet"` to the mars field. (The `visitIdaho` method also prints the value of the `mars` field.)
3.  When the `visitNewJersey` method is executed, the method prints the mars value once again.

In this way, the `mars` field's value is passed from one method to another.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Try out these programs. See what you think.

#### WHO'S WHO?

What's the output of the following code? Why?

`import java.io.IO;`

`public class Main1 {`
`String name = "Nancy";`

`void main() {`
`IO.println(name);`
`String name = "Barry";`
`IO.println(name);`
`}`
`}`

#### WHO ELSE IS WHO?

What's the output of the following code? Why?

`import java.io.IO;`

`public class Main2 {`
`String name = "George";`

`void main() {`
`doStuff();`
`}`

`void doStuff() {`
`IO.println(name);`

`String name = "Barry";`
`IO.println(name);`

`IO.println(this.name);`
`}`
`}`

## Passing Parameters

A method can communicate with another part of your Java program in several ways. One way is by way of the method's parameter list. Using a parameter list, you pass on-the-fly information to a method as the method is being called.

Imagine that the information you pass to the method is stored in one of your program’s variables. What, if anything, does the method actually do with that variable? The following sections present a few interesting case studies.

### Pass by value

According to my web research, the town of Smackover, Arkansas, has 2,232 people in it. But my research isn’t current. Just yesterday, Dora Kermongoos celebrated a joyous occasion over at Smackover General Hospital — the birth of her healthy, brown-eyed baby girl. (The girl weighs 7 pounds, 4 ounces, and is 21 inches tall.) Now the town’s population has risen to 2,233.

[Listing 7-16](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0016) has a very bad program in it. The program is supposed to add 1 to a variable that stores Smackover’s population, but the program doesn’t work. Take a look at [Listing 7-16](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0016) to see why.

[LISTING 7-16](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fea-0016) This Program Doesn’t Work

`void main() {`
`int smackoverARpop = 2232;`

**`birth(smackoverARpop);`**
`println(smackoverARpop);`
`}`

**`void birth(int cityPop) {`**
`cityPop++;`
`}`

When you run the program in [Listing 7-16](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0016), the program displays the number 2232 onscreen. After nine months of planning and anticipation and Dora’s whopping seven hours in labor, the Kermongoos family’s baby girl wasn’t registered in the system. What a shame!

The improper use of parameter passing caused the problem. In Java, when you pass a parameter that has one of the eight primitive types, that parameter is *passed by value.*

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) For a review of Java’s eight primitive types, see [Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml).

Here’s what this means in plain English: Any changes that the method makes to the value of its parameter don’t affect the values of variables back in the calling code. In [Listing 7-16](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0016), the `birth` method can apply the ++ operator to `cityPop` all it wants — the application of `++` to the `cityPop` parameter has absolutely no effect on the value of the `smackoverARpop` variable back in the main method.

Technically, what's happening is the copying of a value. (See [Figure 7-15](#c07.xhtml_c07-fig-0015).) When the `main` method calls the `birth` method, the value stored in `smackoverARpop` is copied to another memory location — a location reserved for the `cityPop` parameter's value. During the `birth` method's execution, 1 is added to the `cityPop` parameter. But the place where the original `2232` value was stored — the memory location for the `smackoverARpop` variable — remains unaffected.

![“A diagram with two sections labeled 'main' and 'birth.' In the 'main' section, there's a box labeled 'smackoverAllPop' with the number 2232\. An arrow connects this box to another box in the 'birth' section labeled 'cityPop,' initially showing 2232 but crossed out and updated to 2231\. The diagram illustrates the relationship between these two variables, highlighting how the value of 'smackoverAllPop' (2232) in the 'main' section updates the 'cityPop' value in the 'birth' section, changing it from 2232 to 2231.”](images/9781394289240-fg0715.png)

[FIGURE 7-15:](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fig-0015) Pass by value, under the hood.

![Remember](images/remember.png) When you do parameter passing with any of the eight primitive types, the computer uses *pass by value.* The value stored in the calling code's variable remains unchanged. This happens even if the calling code's variable and the called method’s parameter happen to have exactly the same name.

### Returning a result

You must fix the problem that the code in [Listing 7-16](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0016) poses. After all, a young baby Kermongoos can’t go through life untracked. To record this baby’s existence, you have to add 1 to the value of the `smackoverARpop` variable. You can do this in plenty of ways, and the way presented in [Listing 7-17](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0017) isn’t the simplest. Even so, the way shown in [Listing 7-17](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0017) illustrates a point: Returning a value from a method call can be an acceptable alternative to parameter passing. Look at [Listing 7-17](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0017) to see what I mean.

[LISTING 7-17](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fea-0017) This Program Works

`void main() {`
`int smackoverARpop = 2232;`

**`smackoverARpop = birth(smackoverARpop);`**
`println(smackoverARpop);`
`}`

**`int birth(int cityPop) {`**
**`return cityPop + 1;`**
`}`

After running the code in [Listing 7-17](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0017), the number you see on your computer screen is the correct number: 2233.

The code in [Listing 7-17](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0017) has no new features in it (unless you call *working correctly* a new feature). The most important idea in [Listing 7-17](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0017) is the `return` statement, which also appears in Chapter 7\. Even so, [Listing 7-17](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0017) presents a nice contrast to the approach in [Listing 7-16](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0016), which had to be discarded.

### Pass by reference

In the previous section or two, I take great pains to emphasize a certain point — that when a parameter has one of the eight primitive types, the parameter is passed by value. If you read this, you probably missed the emphasis on the parameter's having one of the eight primitive types. The emphasis is needed because passing objects (reference types) doesn't quite work the same way.

When you pass an object to a method, the object is *passed by reference.* What this means to you is that statements in the called method *can* change any values that are stored in the object’s variables. Those changes *do* affect the values that are seen by whatever code called the method. [Listings 7-18](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0018) and [7-19](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0019) illustrate the point.

[LISTING 7-18](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fea-0018) What Is a City?

`class City {`
`int population;`
`}`

[LISTING 7-19](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fea-0019) Passing an Object to a Method

`void main() {`
`var smackoverAR = new City();`
`smackoverAR.population = 2232;`
**`birth(smackoverAR);`**
`println(smackoverAR.population);`
`}`

**`void birth(City aCity) {`**
**`aCity.population++;`**
`}`

When you run the code in [Listings 7-18](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0018) and [7-19](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0019), the output you see is the number 2233\. That’s good because the code has things like `++` and the word *birth* in it. The deal is, adding 1 to `aCity.population` inside the `birth` method actually changes the value of `smackoverAR.population`, as it's known in the `main` method.

To see how the `birth` method changes the value of `smackoverAR.population`, look at [Figure 7-16](#c07.xhtml_c07-fig-0016). When you pass an object to a method, the computer doesn't make a copy of the entire object. Instead, the computer makes a copy of a *reference to* that object. (Think of it the way it's shown in [Figure 7-16](#c07.xhtml_c07-fig-0016). The computer makes a copy of an arrow that points to the object.)

![“The diagram features instances of a class named City. There are two sections: smackoverAR and birth. In the smackoverAR section, the population property has a value of 2221\. In the birth section, there's an instance named aCity, which is also linked to the population property, showing how the population data is shared between these instances.”](images/9781394289240-fg0716.png)

[FIGURE 7-16:](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fig-0016) Pass by reference, under the hood.

In [Figure 7-16](#c07.xhtml_c07-fig-0016), you see just one instance of the `City` class, with a `population` variable inside it. Now keep your eye on that object as you read the following steps:

1.  Just before the `birth` method is called, the `smackoverAR` variable refers to that object — the instance of the `City` class.
2.  When the `birth` method is called and `smackoverAR` is passed to the `birth` method's `aCity` parameter, the computer copies the reference from `smackoverAR` to `aCity`. Now `aCity` refers to that same object — the instance of the `City` class.
3.  When the statement `aCity.population++` is executed inside the `birth` method, the computer adds 1 to the object's `population` field. Now the program's one-and-only `City` instance has 2233 stored in its `population` field.
4.  The flow of execution goes back to the `main` method. The value of `smackoverAR.population` is printed. But `smackoverAR` refers to that one instance of the `City` class. So `smackoverAR.population` has the value `2233`. The Kermongoos family is so proud.

### Returning an object from a method

Believe it or not, the previous sections on parameter passing left unexplored one nook-and-cranny of Java methods: When you call a method, the method can return something right back to the calling code. In previous chapters and sections, I return primitive values, such as int values, or nothing (otherwise known as *void*). In this section, I return a whole object. It's an object of type `City` from [Listing 7-18](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0018). The code that makes this happen is in [Listing 7-20](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0020).

[LISTING 7-20](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fea-0020) Here, Have a City

`void main() {`
`var smackoverAR = new City();`
`smackoverAR.population = 2232;`
**`smackoverAR = doBirth(`**`smackoverAR`**`)`**`;`
`println(smackoverAR.population);`
`}`

**`City doBirth`**`(City aCity) {`
`var myCity = new City();`
`myCity.population = aCity.population + 1;`
**`return myCity;`**
`}`

If you run the code in [Listing 7-20](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0020), you get the number 2233\. That's good. The code works by telling the `doBirth` method to create another `City` instance. In the new instance, the value of `population` is `2233`. (See [Figure 7-17](#c07.xhtml_c07-fig-0017).)

![“The UML object diagram depicts two objects: smackoverAR and myCity. Both objects have a population attribute, with smackoverAR showing a population of 2232 and myCity showing a population of 2233\. The smackoverAR object is part of the main method, and the myCity object is part of the doBirth method. Both objects are instances of the aCity class, illustrating how population values are assigned and maintained within different methods.”](images/9781394289240-fg0717.png)

[FIGURE 7-17:](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fig-0017) The `doBirth` method creates a `City` instance.

After the `doBirth` method is executed, that `City` instance is returned to the `main` method. Then, back in the `main` method, that instance (the one that `doBirth` returns) is assigned to the `smackoverAR` variable. (See [Figure 7-18](#c07.xhtml_c07-fig-0018).) Now `smackoverAR` refers to a brand-new `City` instance — an instance whose population is 2,233.

![“A diagram with three nodes labeled 'smackoverAR,' 'aCity,' and 'myCity.' Each node displays a population value: 'smackoverAR' and 'aCity' both show a population of 2232, while 'myCity' shows a population of 2233\. The nodes are connected by lines, indicating relationships, and are organized under two categories: 'main' and 'doBirth.' This visual representation showcases how population values are associated with different nodes within a hierarchical structure.”](images/9781394289240-fg0718.png)

[FIGURE 7-18:](#c07.xhtml_rc07-fig-0018) The new `City` instance is assigned to the `smackoverAR` variable.

In [Listing 7-20](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0020), notice the type consistency in the calling and returning of the `doBirth` method:

*   The `smackoverAR` variable has type `City`. The `smackoverAR` variable is passed to the `aCity` parameter, which is also of type `City`.
*   The `myCity` variable is of type `City`. The `myCity` variable is sent back in the `doBirth` method's `return` statement. That's consistent, because the `doBirth` method's header begins with `static City doBirth(blah, blah, blah`*…* — a promise to return an object of type `City`.
*   The `doBirth` method returns an object of type `City`. Back in the `main` method, the object that the call to `doBirth` returns is assigned to the `smackoverAR` variable, and (you guessed it) the `smackoverAR` variable is of type `City`.

Aside from being quite harmonious, all this type agreement is absolutely necessary. If you write a program in which your types don't agree with one another, the compiler spits out an unsympathetic `incompatible types` message.

### A final word

Dora Kermongoos and her newborn baby daughter are resting comfortably in their Smackover, Arkansas, home. Be sure to watch the upcoming reality television series, *Smackover Newborns: Secrets from Behind Closed Doors*.

Chapter 8

# Constructing New Objects

IN THIS CHAPTER

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Defining constructors**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Using Java’s default constructor**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Creating a class with one line of code**

Ms. Jennie Burd

121 Schoolhouse Lane

Anywhere, Kansas

Dear Ms. Burd,

In response to your letter of June 21, I believe I can say with complete assurance that objects are not created spontaneously from nothing. Although I’ve never actually seen an object being created (and no one else in this office can claim to have seen an object in its moment of creation), I have every confidence that some process or another is responsible for the building of these interesting and useful thingamajigs. We here at ObjectsAndClasses.com support the unanimous opinions of both the scientific community and the private sector in matters of this nature. Furthermore, we agree with the recent finding of a blue-ribbon panel that concludes, beyond any doubt, that spontaneous object creation would impede the present economic outlook.

Please be assured that I have taken all steps necessary to ensure the safety and well-being of you, our loyal customer. If you have any further questions, please do not hesitate to contact our Complaint department. The department’s manager is Mr. Blake Wholl. You can contact him by visiting our company’s website.

Once again, let me thank you for your concern, and I hope you continue to patronize ObjectsAndClasses.com.

Yours truly,

Mr. Scott Brickenchicker (the one who couldn’t get on the elevator in [Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml))

## Defining Constructors (What It Means to Be a Temperature)

Here’s a statement that creates an object:

`Account myAccount = new Account();`

I know it works — I got it from one of my own examples in [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml). Anyway, [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml) has me saying, “When Java performs the initialization `myAccount = new Account()`, Java creates a new object (a new instance of the `Account` class) and makes the variable `myAccount` refer to that new object.” What does this pithy sentence mean?

Well, the keyword `new` tells Java to create an object — an instance of a class. Java responds by performing certain actions. For starters, Java finds a place in its memory to store information about the new object. If the object has fields, the fields should eventually have meaningful values.

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) To find out about fields, see [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml).

When you ask Java to create a new object, you may want to specify what's placed in the object's fields. And what if you’re interested in doing more than filling fields? Perhaps, when Java creates a new object, you have a whole list of jobs for Java to carry out. For instance, when Java creates a new window object, you want Java to realign the sizes of all buttons in that window.

Creating a new object can involve all kinds of tasks, so in this chapter you create constructors. A *constructor* tells Java to perform a new object’s start-up tasks.

### What is a temperature?

“Good morning, and welcome to Object News. The local temperature in your area is a pleasant 73 degrees Fahrenheit.”

Each temperature consists of two parts: a number and a temperature scale. A number is just a `double` value, such as 32.0 or 70.52\. But what's a temperature scale? Is it a string of characters, like `"Fahrenheit"` or `"Celsius"`? Not really, because some strings aren't temperature scales. There's no `"Quelploof"` temperature scale, and a program that can display the temperature `"73 degrees Quelploof"` is a bad program. So how can you limit the temperature scales to the small number of scales that people use? One way to do it is with Java's `enum` type.

### What is a temperature scale? (Java's enum type)

Java provides lots of ways for you to group things together. In [Chapter 12](#c12.xhtml), you group things to form an array. And in [Chapter 12](#c12.xhtml), you group things to form a collection. In this chapter, you group things into an `enum` type. (Of course, you can't group anything unless you can pronounce `enum`. The word *enum* is pronounced “ee-NOOM,” like the first two syllables of the word *enumeration.*)

Creating a complicated `enum` type isn't easy, but to create a simple `enum` type, just write a bunch of words inside a pair of curly braces. [Listing 8-1](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0001) defines an `enum` type. The name of the `enum` type is `TempScale`.

[LISTING 8-1](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fea-0001) The TempScale Type (an enum Type)

`public enum TempScale {`
`CELSIUS, FAHRENHEIT, KELVIN, RANKINE`
`}`

(Author's note: In [Listing 8-1](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0001), I list four different temperature scales. In previous *Java For Dummies* editions, I tried to show off by listing nine temperature scales, some with fancy Unicode characters in their names. But during my research for this edition, I stumbled upon [`www.curiousnotions.com/temperature-converter`](https://www.curiousnotions.com/temperature-converter/) a website with formulas for 73 different temperature scales. I am truly humbled.)

When you define an `enum` type, two important things happen:

*   **You create values.**

    Just as `13` and `151` are `int` values, `CELSIUS` and `FAHRENHEIT` are `TempScale` values.

*   **You can create variables to refer to those values.**

    In [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002), I declare the fields `number` and `scale`. Just as

    `double number;`

    declares that a `number` variable is of type `double`,

    `TempScale scale;`

    declares variable `scale` to be of type `TempScale`.

    “To be of type `TempScale`” means that you can have values `CELSIUS`, `FAHRENHEIT`, `KELVIN`, or `RANKINE`. So, in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002), I can give the `scale` variable the value `FAHRENHEIT` (or `TempScale.FAHRENHEIT`, to be more precise).

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) An `enum` type is a Java class in disguise. That's why [Listing 8-1](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0001) contains an entire file devoted to one thing — namely, the declaration of an `enum` type (the `TempScale` type). Like the declaration of a class, an `enum` type declaration belongs in a file all its own. The code in [Listing 8-1](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0001) belongs in a file named `TempScale.java`.

### Okay, so then what is a temperature?

Each temperature consists of two things: a number and a temperature scale. The code in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) makes this fact abundantly clear.

[LISTING 8-2](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fea-0002) The Temperature Class

`public class Temperature {`
`private double number;`

`private TempScale scale;`

**`public Temperature() {`**
`number = 0.0;`
`scale = TempScale.FAHRENHEIT;`
`}`

**`public Temperature(double number) {`**
`this.number = number;`
`scale = TempScale.FAHRENHEIT;`
`}`

**`public Temperature(TempScale scale) {`**
`number = 0.0;`
`this.scale = scale;`
`}`

**`public Temperature(double number, TempScale scale) {`**
`this.number = number;`
`this.scale = scale;`
`}`

`public void setNumber(double number) {`
`this.number = number;`
`}`

`public double getNumber() {`
`return number;`
`}`

`public void setScale(TempScale scale) {`
`this.scale = scale;`
`}`

`public TempScale getScale() {`
`return scale;`
`}`

`public String toString() {`
`return String.format("%5.2f\u00B0 ", number) + scale;`
`}`
`}`

The code in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) has the usual setter and getter methods (accessor methods for the `number` and `scale` fields).

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) For some good reading on setter and getter methods (also known as accessor methods), see [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml).

In addition to its setters and getters, [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) has four other method-like-looking things. Each of these method-like things has the name `Temperature`, which happens to be the same as the name of the class. None of these `Temperature` method-like things has a `return` type of any kind — not even `void`, which is the cop-out `return` type.

Each of these method-like things is called a constructor. A *constructor* is like a method, except that a constructor has a special purpose: to create new objects.

![Remember](images/remember.png) Whenever the computer creates a new object, the computer executes the statements inside a constructor.

### What you can do with a temperature

[Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003) gives form to some of the ideas that I describe in the preceding section. In [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003), you call the constructors that are declared earlier, in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002). [Figure 8-1](#c08.xhtml_c08-fig-0001) shows what happens when you run all this code.

![A chart comparing temperatures in Fahrenheit, Celsius, and Kelvin.](images/9781394289240-fg0801.png)

[FIGURE 8-1:](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fig-0001) Running the code from [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003).

[LISTING 8-3](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fea-0003) Using the Temperature Class

`public class UseTemperature {`

`public static void main(String[] args) {`

**`var temp = new Temperature();`**
`temp.setNumber(70.0);`
`temp.setScale(TempScale.FAHRENHEIT);`
`System.out.println(temp);`

**`temp = new Temperature(32.0);`**
`System.out.println(temp);`

**`temp = new Temperature(TempScale.CELSIUS);`**
`System.out.println(temp);`

**`temp = new Temperature(2.73, TempScale.KELVIN);`**
`System.out.println(temp);`
`}`
`}`

![Remember](images/remember.png) In [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003), I use the traditional style for writing a main method with the method inside an explicit class. Code of this kind doesn't implicitly import the `java.io.IO` package's `print`, `println`, and `readln` methods. (Refer to [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml).) So, [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003) makes a long-winded `System.out.println` call.

In [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003), each statement of the kind

`temp = new Temperature(`*`blah-blah`*`);`

calls one of the constructors from [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002). So, by the time the code in [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003) finishes running, it creates four instances of the `Temperature` class. Each instance is created by calling a different constructor from [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002).

In [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003), the last of the four constructor calls has two parameters: `2.73` and `TempScale.KELVIN`. This isn't particular to constructor calls. A method call or a constructor call can have a bunch of parameters. You separate one parameter from another with a comma. Another name for “a bunch of parameters” is a *parameter list*.

The only rule you must follow is to match the parameters in the call with the parameters in the declaration. For example, in [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003), the fourth and last constructor call

`new Temperature(2.73, TempScale.KELVIN)`

has two parameters: the first of type `double` and the second of type `TempScale`. Java approves of this constructor call because [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) contains a matching declaration. That is, the header

`public Temperature(double number, TempScale scale)`

has two parameters: the first of type `double` and the second of type `TempScale`. If a `Temperature` constructor call in [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003) had no matching declaration in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002), [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003) would crash and burn. (To state things more politely, Java would display errors when you tried to compile the code in [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003).)

### What does a temperature look like?

The last item in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) is a `toString` method. When you call a `print` or `println` method, the first thing Java does is to call `toString` on your behalf. That way, whatever you're trying to display gets turned into a string of characters — a string that can show up on a computer screen. For example, when you call `println(2 + 2)`, Java adds 2 and 2 to get the `int` value 4, then it changes the `int` value `4` into the string `"4"`, and finally, Java displays the contents of that `"4"` string on the screen.

Every class has a `toString` method, whether you write code for it or not. If you remove the `toString` declaration from [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002), Java adds a `toString` method behind the scenes. I think of this added method as an implicit `toString` method. But, for reasons that become clear in [Chapter 10](#c10.xhtml), this added method is called the *inherited*`toString` method.

For most classes, the inherited `toString` method isn't user-friendly. To show you what I mean, I removed my `toString` declaration from [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002). Then I ran the code in [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003). This was the output:

`Temperature@5b480cf9`
`Temperature@6f496d9f`
`Temperature@723279cf`
`Temperature@10f87f48`

My `Temperature` class's inherited `toString` method isn't helpful at all. So, in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002), I create my own `toString` method with the format string `"%5.2f``\u00B0 "`. In this string, `\u00B0` is the Unicode representation of a temperature's degree symbol (`°`).

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) For some wise remarks about Unicode, refer to [Chapter 5](#c05.xhtml).

### Constructing a temperature; a slow-motion replay

When the computer executes one of the new `Temperature` statements in [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003), Java has to decide which of the constructors in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) to use. Java decides by looking at the parameter list — the stuff in parentheses after the words `new Temperature`. For instance, when the computer executes

`temp = new Temperature(32.0);`

from [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003), Java says to itself, “The number 32.0 in parentheses is a `double` value. One of the `Temperature` constructors in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) has just one parameter with type `double`. The constructor's header looks like this:

`public Temperature(double number)`

“So I guess I'll execute the statements inside that particular constructor.” Java goes on to execute the following statements:

`this.number = number;`
`scale = TempScale.FAHRENHEIT;`

As a result, you get a brand-new object whose `number` field has the value `32.0` and whose `scale` field has the value `TempScale.FAHRENHEIT`.

In the two lines of code, you have two statements that set values for the fields `number` and `scale`. Take a look at the second of these statements, which is a bit easier to understand. The second statement sets the new object's `scale` field to `TempScale.FAHRENHEIT`. You see, the constructor's parameter list is `(double number)`, and that list doesn't include a `scale` value. So whoever programmed this code had to make a decision about what value to use for the `scale` field. The programmer could have chosen `FAHRENHEIT` or `CELSIUS`, but they could also have chosen `KELVIN` or `RANKINE`. (This programmer happens to live in New Jersey, in the United States, where people commonly use the old Fahrenheit temperature scale.)

Marching back to the first of the two statements, this first statement assigns a value to the new object's `number` field. The statement uses a cute trick that you can see in many constructors (and in other methods that assign values to objects' fields). To understand the trick, take a look at [Listing 8-4](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0004). The listing shows you two ways that I could have written the same constructor code.

[LISTING 8-4](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fea-0004) Two Ways to Accomplish the Same Thing

`//Use this constructor …`

`public Temperature(double` **`whatever`**`) {`
**`number = whatever`**`;`
`scale = TempScale.FAHRENHEIT;`
`}`

`// … or use this constructor …`

`public Temperature(double` **`number`**`) {`
**`this.number = number`**`;`
`scale = TempScale.FAHRENHEIT;`
`}`

`// … but don't put both constructors in your code.`

[Listing 8-4](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0004) has two constructors in it. In the first constructor, I use two different names: `number` and `whatever`. In the second constructor, I don't need two names. Rather than make up a new name for the constructor's parameter, I reuse an existing name by writing `this.number`.

Here’s what’s going on in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002):

*   In the statement `this.number = number`, the name *this.number* refers to the new object's `number` field — the field that's declared near the top of [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002). (See [Figure 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fig-0002).)
*   In the statement `this.number = number`, *number* (on its own, without `this`) refers to the constructor's parameter. (Again, see [Figure 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fig-0002).)

![“Java code snippet displaying a class named 'Temperature' with fields for 'number' (a double) and 'scale' (a TempScale). The constructor initializes the 'number' field with a parameter and sets the 'scale' to TempScale.FAHRENHEIT.The code highlights the 'number' field and its assignment.”](images/9781394289240-fg0802.png)

[FIGURE 8-2:](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fig-0002) What `this.number` and `number` mean.

In general, `this.`*`someName`* refers to a field belonging to the object that contains the code. In contrast, plain old *`someName`* refers to the closest place where *`someName`* happens to be declared. In the statement `this.number = number` (refer to [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002)), that closest place happens to be the `Temperature` constructor's parameter list.

### The default constructor

The `Temperature` class declaration in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) has four constructors, each with its own parameter list. What happens if you don't include all four of them? The answer isn't as straightforward as you may think. To find out what happens, try this three-step experiment:

1.  **Delete all four constructors from** [**Listing** **8-2**](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002)**. Then try to run the code in** [**Listing** **8-3**](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003)**.**

    To get [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003) running, you have to delete three of the code's constructor calls. The only call that works is the parameterless `new Temperature()` call. When you don't declare any constructors, Java creates one for you. This implicit constructor has no parameters. It's called a *default constructor*.

    Look back at the `Account` class in [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml). In that chapter, you don't declare any constructors. And yet, you're able to call `new Account()` in each of the chapter's main methods. That's Java's default constructor at work!

    ![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) Java's default constructor is an implicit element. For some exciting reading about implicit elements, refer to [Chapter 5](#c05.xhtml).

2.  **Delete three of the four constructors from** [**Listing** **8-2**](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002)**. The only constructor that you don't delete is the parameterless constructor.**

    When you do this, you get the same result as the one in Step 1\. You don't have to let Java create an implicit default constructor for you. You can declare the parameterless constructor explicitly in your code. It's your choice.

3.  **Delete three of the four constructors from** [**Listing** **8-2**](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002)**. The only constructor that you don't delete is the 2-parameter constructor** `Temperature(double number, TempScale scale)`**.**

    I call this the class's *fully parameterized constructor*. It's the constructor whose parameters include every one of the class's fields.

    When you eliminate all but the `Temperature` class's fully parameterized constructor, you can call `new Temperature(2.73, TempScale.KELVIN)`, but you can't call `new Temperature()`. Why not? What happened to Java's wonderful default constructor?

    The rule for constructors is as follows: When you declare a class and you don't explicitly declare any constructors (as in Step 1), Java creates a parameterless default constructor on your behalf. But, when you declare any constructors in your code, Java doesn't create a default constructor. So, if you want your class to have a parameterless constructor and some other constructors, you have to declare all of them (including the parameterless constructor) in your code. Pretty strange, hey!

If you find this default constructor business confusing, you can avoid thinking about it. Instead of worrying about this section's experiments, just make sure to explicitly declare all the constructors you plan to call. If you plan to call a parameterless constructor, write code for it in your class declaration. If you plan to call any parameterized constructors (fully parameterized or not), write code for these constructors in your class declaration.

Finally, if you don't enjoy writing constructor definitions as much as I do, let your IDE do the work. From your IDE's main menu, try choosing Code ⇒   Generate ⇒   Constructor or Source ⇒   Generate Constructor.

![Remember](images/remember.png) The terminology can be confusing. Java has *default* constructors and *inherited* methods. Both of these things appear magically when you don't explicitly define them in your code. Fortunately, you can write programs without remembering that one is called *default* and the other is called *inherited*. All you have to do is understand what's going on when you read these words.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) There's always more stuff for you to try.

#### SCHOOL DAYS

Create a `Student` class with a name, an ID number, a grade point average (GPA), and a major area of study. The student's name is a `String`. The student's ID number is an `int` value. The GPA is a `double` value between 0.0 and 4.0\. The `Major` is an `enum` type, with values such as `COMPUTER`_`SCIENCE`, `MATHEMATICS`, `LITERATURE`, `PHYSICS`, and `HISTORY`.

Every student has a name and an ID number, but a brand-new student might not have a GPA or a major. Create constructors with and without GPA and `Major` parameters.

As usual, create a main method that makes use of your new `Student` class.

#### FLIGHT OF FANCY

Create an `AirplaneFlight` class with a flight number, a departure airport, the time of departure, an arrival airport, and a time of arrival. The flight number is an `int` value. The departure and arrival airport fields belong to an `Airport` enum type, with values corresponding to some of the official IATA airport codes. (For example, London Heathrow Airport's code is LHR; Los Angeles International Airport's code is LAX; check out [`www.iata.org/publications/Pages/code-search.aspx`](http://www.iata.org/publications/Pages/code-search.aspx) for a searchable database of airline codes.)

For the times of arrival and departure, use Java's `LocalTime` class. (For more on `LocalTime`, check out the `LocalTime` documents page at [`https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/17/docs/api/java.base/java/time/LocalTime.html`](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/17/docs/api/java.base/java/time/LocalTime.html).) To create a `LocalTime` object that's set to 2:15 P.m. (also known as 14:15), execute

`LocalTime twoFifteen = LocalTime.of(14, 15);`

To create a `LocalTime` object that's set to the current time (according to the computer's system clock), execute

`LocalTime currentTime = LocalTime.now();`

Every flight has a number, a departure airport, and an arrival airport. But some flights might not have departure and arrival times. Create constructors with and without departure and arrival time parameters.

Create a main method that makes use of your new `AirplaneFlight` class.

## The Easy Way to Create a Class

[Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) describes the `Temperature` class in about 40 lines of code. That's a lot of code to define something that has only two properties — a number and a temperature scale. Most of the code in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) is routine, boilerplate stuff. When my eyes rest on the statement `this.number = number`, my brain ignores it. A devilish voice inside me says, “Don't bother me with `this.number = number`. Tell me something I don't already know.” I don't expect to find anything interesting in a class declaration of this kind. So, if another programmer throws a curveball inside a class definition, I probably won't notice it. That's not good.

Maybe there's a better way to create a class. No, there isn't. I wrote this section just to complain about long class declarations.

Oops! I lied. There *is* a better way to create a class. Take a look at [Listing 8-5](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0005).

[LISTING 8-5](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fea-0005) Your First Record Class

`public` **`record`** `Temperature (double number, TempScale scale) {}`

[Listing 8-5](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0005) is about as small as any listing can be. And yet the code in [Listing 8-5](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0005) completely defines a `Temperature` class. This `Temperature` class shares many features with the class in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) and even has a few features that [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) lacks. The word `record` in [Listing 8-5](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0005) makes this `Temperature` class be a special kind of class. To no one's surprise, it's called a *record class*. Every record class has certain characteristics that make life easy for Java programmers.

The code in [Listing 8-6](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0006) creates instances of the record class in [Listing 8-5](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0005). [Figure 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fig-0003) shows the output from [Listings 8-5](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0005) and [8-6](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0006).

[LISTING 8-6](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fea-0006) Instances of a Record Class

`void main() {`

`var temp = new Temperature(70.0, TempScale.FAHRENHEIT);`
`println(String.format("%5.2f\u00B0 ",` **`temp.number()`**`) +` **`temp.scale()`**`);`

`temp = new Temperature(2.73, TempScale.KELVIN);`
`println(temp);`
`}`

![Digital readout displaying “70,000 PARSNIPPETY” with temperature range (min: -7.3, max: 20+XMAX).](images/9781394289240-fg0803.png)

[FIGURE 8-3:](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fig-0003) Running the code from [Listing 8-6](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0006).

[Listing 8-6](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0006) does more or less the same things that [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003) does. The listing creates some `Temperature` instances and displays them. But record classes march to the beat of their own drummers. So [Listing 8-6](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0006) isn't quite the same as the original [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003).

For one thing, a record class doesn't implicitly create a parameterless default constructor. Instead, a record class implicitly creates a fully parameterized constructor. That's why, in [Listing 8-6](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0006), you see these two lines:

`var temp = new Temperature(70.0, TempScale.FAHRENHEIT);`

`temp = new Temperature(2.73, TempScale.KELVIN);`

But you don't see any of these lines:

**`// Don't add any of these lines to`****`Listing 8-6.`**
`var temp = new Temperature();`
`temp.number = 21.0;`
`temp.scale = TempScale.CELSIUS;`

`temp.setNumber(70.0);`
`temp.setScale(TempScale.FAHRENHEIT);`

This section's `Temperature` class doesn't let you call `new Temperature()` and doesn't let you set the values of an existing object's `number` or `scale` fields. Once you've created an object with `new Temperature(70.0, TempScale.FAHRENHEIT)`, you can't change that object's `number` value from 70.0 to 71.5\. You can't change the object's `scale` value, either.

The word *immutability* means “unchangeable,” and instances of record classes are all immutable. Contrary to popular opinion, immutability is a good thing. At least, the immutability of an object is a good thing. If an object's field values never change, you know what those values are from the creation of the object until the end of the program's run. Other programmers can't add code that changes values and fouls up your logic.

But remember — this immutability business doesn't apply to variables that refer to record class instances. Look at the `temp` variable in [Listing 8-6](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0006). During a run of this code, the `temp` variable refers to two different objects. One is a comfortable room temperature, and the other is the temperature of our chilly cosmic microwave background. In [Listing 8-6](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0006), there's nothing to keep you from changing the object that `temp` refers to. But once you've created an object, that object's fields can't be changed.

What if you want to print a `Temperature` instance's `number` field? The expressions `temp.number` and `temp.getNumber()` won't work. But you can follow the pattern in the first `println` call of [Listing 8-6](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0006). You can refer to `temp.number()` and `temp.scale()`. You type the name of the field followed by an empty pair of parentheses. That's the way you get the value of a record class's field.

![Warning](images/warning.png) This differs from the conventions for classes that aren't record classes, so you have to be careful. (And I have to be careful. I've gotten this wrong many times.)

The last line in [Figure 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fig-0003) shows what you get when you don't explicitly format a record class instance. Java quietly concocts a decent-looking `toString` method. You don't see that `toString` method in your code. But, when you call `println(temp)` in [Listing 8-6](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0006), Java displays the `temp` object's `number` and `scale` values. (Refer to [Figure 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fig-0003).) That's good!

[Listing 8-5](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0005) shows you how to define a class with only one line of code. Impressive as that is, you may want to create a record class that includes some customized method declarations. [Listing 8-7](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0007) adds the `toString` method to a `Temperature` record class.

[LISTING 8-7](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fea-0007) Another Record Class

`public record Temperature (double number, TempScale scale) {`

`public String toString() {`
`return String.format("%5.2f\u00B0 ", number) + scale;`
`}`
`}`

With the `toString` method in [Listing 8-7](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0007), a call to `println(temp)` displays a nicely formatted Fahrenheit room temperature. [Figure 8-4](#c08.xhtml_c08-fig-0004) shows you what you get when you run the main method in [Listing 8-6](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0006) against the `Temperature` class in [Listing 8-7](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0007).

![Display showing temperatures: “70.00° FAHRENHEIT” and “2.73° KELVIN”.](images/9781394289240-fg0804.png)

[FIGURE 8-4:](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fig-0004) Two nicely formatted temperatures.

### Constructors don't have to be dull

Here's a quote from somewhere near the start of this chapter: “… what if you’re interested in doing more than filling fields? Perhaps, when the computer creates a new object, you have a whole list of jobs for the computer to carry out.” Okay, what-if?

With the `Temperature` classes in previous sections, a programmer can create temperatures that are colder than absolute zero. And, in physics, that's a definite no-no. [Listing 8-8](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0008) has a constructor that makes this science fiction scenario impossible.

[LISTING 8-8](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fea-0008) It's Cold Outside!

`public record Temperature(double number, TempScale scale) {`

`public Temperature(double number, TempScale scale) {`

**`this.number = Math.max(number,`**
**`switch (scale) {`**
**`case CELSIUS -> -273.15;`**
**`case FAHRENHEIT -> -459.6;`**
**`case KELVIN, RANKINE -> 0.0;`**
**`}`**
**`);`**
`this.scale = scale;`
`}`

`public String toString() {`
`return String.format("%5.2f\u00B0 ", number) + scale;`
`}`
`}`

When you use [Listing 8-8](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0008) and run the following main method, you get the result shown in [Figure 8-5](#c08.xhtml_c08-fig-0005).

`void main() {`
`println(new Temperature(-1000.0, TempScale.FAHRENHEIT));`

`println(new Temperature(-1000.0, TempScale.CELSIUS));`

`println(new Temperature(-1000.0, TempScale.KELVIN));`

`println(new Temperature(2.73, TempScale.KELVIN));`
`}`

![Text showing temperature conversion between Fahrenheit, Celsius, and Kelvin.](images/9781394289240-fg0805.png)

[FIGURE 8-5:](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fig-0005) You're getting warmer!

In [Listing 8-8](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0008), the `max` method (a member of Java's `Math` class) returns the larger of two numbers. For example, when `number` is –1000.00 and `scale` is `FAHRENHEIT`, the `switch` expression in [Listing 8-8](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0008) returns –459.6\. So `this.number` becomes `Math.max(-1000.00, -459.6)`, which is `-459.6`. The new object's temperature is the lowest it can possibly be, and no lower. (Just writing about it makes me want to bundle up!)

### Equality for all!

This chapter's “[What does a temperature look like?](#c08.xhtml_c08-sec-0012)” section tells you that every class has a `toString` method. If you don't write a `toString` method, Java creates an implicit `toString` method for you. It's called an inherited `toString` method.

The same is true of Java's `equals` methods. Every class has an `equals` method. An example in [Chapter 5](#c05.xhtml) shows how nice Java's inherited `equals` method works for `String` values. In fact, to find out if `myName` and `yourName` contain the same characters, you shouldn't write

`if (myName == yourName)` **`// No!`**

Instead, you should write

`if (myName.equals(yourName))` **`// Yes`**

The `String` class's `equals` method is very useful, but the `String` class's `equals` method isn't typical. Inherited `equals` methods don't work well for arbitrary Java classes. Look at the main method in [Listing 8-9](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0009). [Figure 8-6](#c08.xhtml_c08-fig-0006) shows you the output when I run this main method along with the code in [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003).

[LISTING 8-9](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fea-0009) Comparing Temperatures

`void main() {`
`var freezing1 = new Temperature(0.0, TempScale.CELSIUS);`
`var freezing2 = new Temperature(0.0, TempScale.CELSIUS);`
`println(freezing1 + " equals " + freezing2 + " ?");`
`println(freezing1.equals(freezing2));`
`println("");`

`var freezing3 = new Temperature(32.0, TempScale.FAHRENHEIT);`
`println(freezing1 + " equals " + freezing3 + " ?");`
`println(freezing1.equals(freezing3));`
`}`

![Text comparing Celsius to itself and to Fahrenheit with the results marked as false.](images/9781394289240-fg0806.png)

[FIGURE 8-6:](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fig-0006) You've got to be kidding!

In [Listing 8-9](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0009), the `freezing1` and `freezing2` variables refer to two different objects. So, with the class [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003), Java's inherited `equals` method says they're not equal. That's disappointing, but what else can you expect from the `Temperature` class in [Listing 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0003)? It's not a record class.

Record classes behave better than other kinds of classes. When you define `Temperature` to be a record class, the inherited `toString` method gives something like `Temperature[number=2.73, scale=KELVIN]`. (See [Figure 8-3](#c08.xhtml_c08-fig-0003).) That's good! A record class's inherited `toString` method returns a reasonable representation of an object and its fields.

So, maybe the record class in [Listing 8-7](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0007) has a nice inherited `equals` method. Wha' da' ya' think? [Figure 8-7](#c08.xhtml_c08-fig-0007) shows what you get when you run the main method in [Listing 8-9](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0009) along with the record class in [Listing 8-7](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0007).

![Text comparing 0 degrees Celsius to itself (true) and to 32 degrees Fahrenheit (false).](images/9781394289240-fg0807.png)

[FIGURE 8-7:](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fig-0007) That's a little bit better.

If you define `Temperature` to be a record class, Java's `equals` method does an “okay” job. With two 0°-Celsius objects, the `equals` method returns `true`. That's better than what you get with a class that's not a record class. But it's not the best. The inherited `equals` method doesn't know how to convert between temperature scales.

To solve this problem, you write your own `equals` method. [Listing 8-10](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0010) shows you how.

[LISTING 8-10](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fea-0010) Comparing Temperatures

`public record Temperature (double number, TempScale scale) {`

`public boolean equals(Temperature other) {`
`var number1 = this.toCelsius();`
`var number2 = other.toCelsius();`
`return number1 == number2;`
`}`

`public double toCelsius() {`
`return switch (scale) {`
`case CELSIUS -> number;`
`case FAHRENHEIT -> (number - 32) * 5.0 / 9.0;`
`case KELVIN -> number - 273.15;`
`case RANKINE -> (number - 491.67) * 5.0 / 9.0;`
`};`
`}`

`public String toString() {`
`return String.format("%5.2f\u00B0 ", number) + scale;`
`}`
`}`

Combining the main method in [Listing 8-9](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0009) with the `Temperature` class in [Listing 8-10](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0010), you get the output shown in [Figure 8-8](#c08.xhtml_c08-fig-0008).

![Text comparing 0 degrees Celsius with 0 degrees Fahrenheit and stating both comparisons are true.](images/9781394289240-fg0808.png)

[FIGURE 8-8:](#c08.xhtml_rc08-fig-0008) Perfect!

Lo and behold! The output in [Figure 8-8](#c08.xhtml_c08-fig-0008) is correct in both cases. It helps to write your own `equals` method.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) So, you thought you were finished with this chapter about constructors! Well, there's more to do if you want to practice coding.

#### IT ISN'T WHAT IT ISN'T

Modify the code in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) so that none of the constructors lets you create a less-than-absolute-zero temperature. While you're at it, modify the setter methods, too.

#### KEEP IT SHORT AND SWEET

Change the code you wrote in this chapter's “School days” and “[Flight of fancy](#c08.xhtml_c08-sec-0019)” sections. Turn both classes into record classes.

Chapter 9

# Piles of Files: Dealing with Information Overload

IN THIS CHAPTER

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Using data on your hard drive**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Writing code to access the hard drive**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Troubleshooting input/output behavior**

Consider these scenarios:

*   You’re a business owner who handles hundreds of invoices each day. You store invoice data in a file on your hard drive. You need customized code to sort and classify the invoices.
*   You’re an astronomer with data from scans of the night sky. When you’re ready to analyze a chunk of data, you load the chunk onto your computer’s hard drive.
*   You’re the author of a popular self-help book. Last year’s fad was the Self-Mirroring Method. This year’s craze is the Make Your Cake System. You can’t modify your manuscript without converting it to the publisher’s new specifications. You need software to make the task bearable.

Each situation calls for a new computer program, and each program reads from a large data file. On top of all of that, each program creates a brand-new file containing bright, shiny results.

In previous chapters, the examples get input from the keyboard and send output to a text window. That’s fine for small tasks, but you can’t have the computer prompt you for each bit of night sky data. For big problems, you need lots of data, and the best place to store the data is on a computer’s hard drive.

## What It Means to Be an Employee

When you write an object-oriented program, you start by thinking about the data. You’re writing about accounts. So, what’s an account? You’re writing code to handle button clicks. So, what’s a button? You’re writing a program to send payroll checks to employees. What’s an employee?

In this chapter’s first example, an employee is someone with a name and a job title. Sure, employees have other characteristics, but for now, I stick to the basics. The code in [Listing 9-1](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0001) defines what it means to be an employee.

[LISTING 9-1](#c09.xhtml_rc09-fea-0001) What Is an Employee?

`public class Employee {`
`private String name;`
`private String jobTitle;`

`public void setName(String name) {`
`this.name = name;`
`}`

`public String getName() {`
`return name;`
`}`

`public void setJobTitle(String jobTitle) {`
`this.jobTitle = jobTitle;`
`}`

`public String getJobTitle() {`
`return jobTitle;`
`}`

`public void cutCheck(double amountPaid) {`
`System.out.print("Pay to the order of " + name);`
`System.out.print(" (");`
`System.out.print(jobTitle);`
`System.out.print(") ***$");`
`System.out.println(String.format("%,.2f", amountPaid));`
`}`
`}`

The `Employee` class in [Listing 9-1](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0001) has seven members. Two of these members are fairly simple: Each employee has a `name` field and a `jobTitle` field.

The other five `Employee` members are methods. For example, each employee has four methods to handle the values of the employee's name and job title. These methods are `setName`, `getName`, `setJobTitle`, and `getJobTitle`. I explain methods like these (getter and setter methods) in [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml).

On top of all of that, each employee has a `cutCheck` method. The idea is that the method that writes payroll checks has to belong to one class or another. Because most of the information in the payroll check is customized for a particular employee, you may as well put the `cutCheck` method inside the `Employee` class.

![Remember](images/remember.png) The `print`, `println`, and `readln` methods that I use in many examples didn't become part of Java until September 2024, and then only as a preview feature. Until then, the easiest way to display text on the screen was to call the `System.out.println` method. (See [Listing 9-1](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0001).)

### Putting your class to good use

The `Employee` class in [Listing 9-1](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0001) has no `main` method, so there's no starting point for executing code. To fix this deficiency, the programmer writes a separate program with a `main` method and uses that program to create `Employee` instances. [Listing 9-2](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0002) shows a class with a `main` method — one that puts the code in [Listing 9-1](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0001) to the test.

[LISTING 9-2](#c09.xhtml_rc09-fea-0002) The Check Is in the Mail

`import java.util.Scanner;`

`public class PayOneEmployee {`

`public static void main(String[] args) {`
`var keyboard = new Scanner(System.in);`

`var name = keyboard.nextLine();`
`var jobTitle = keyboard.next();`

`var anEmployee = new Employee();`
`anEmployee.setName(name);`
`anEmployee.setJobTitle(jobTitle);`

`var pay = keyboard.nextDouble();`

`anEmployee.cutCheck(pay);`
`}`
`}`

For a run of the code in [Listing 9-1](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0001), see [Figure 9-1](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0001).

![“Logo of the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) with the text 'National Institute of Standards and Technology' in black and 'NIST' in green.”](images/9781394289240-fg0901.png)

[FIGURE 9-1:](#c09.xhtml_rc09-fig-0001) Barry gets paid.

![Tip](images/tip.png) Back in [Listing 9-1](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0001), notice the comma inside the `%,.2f` placeholder. The comma tells the program to use *grouping separators.* That's why, in [Figure 9-1](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0001), you see `5,000.00` instead of `5000.00`. If you run the same program with the statement

`java.util.Locale.setDefault(new java.util.Locale("kl", "GL"));`

you get the Greenland Kalaallisut version of five thousand, which is `5.000,00`.

### She reads keystrokes from the keyboard

In [Listing 9-2](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0002), the code

`import java.util.Scanner;`

`var keyboard = new Scanner(System.in);`

`int name = keyboard.nextLine();`

performs almost the same function as this single statement:

`var name = IO.readln(""); // or, in Java 24 and beyond, IO.readln();`

The code gets whatever text the user types on a single line on the computer's keyboard. In addition, the code stores this text in the program's `name` variable. Here are some facts about this use of Java's `Scanner` class:

*   **Typically (on a desktop or laptop computer), the name *System.in* stands for the keyboard.**

    To get characters from someplace other than the keyboard, you can type something other than `System.in` inside the parentheses.

    ![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) What else can you put inside the `new Scanner(…)` parentheses? For some ideas, see this chapter's later section “[Working with Disk Files](#c09.xhtml_c09-sec-0012).”

    In [Listing 9-2](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0002), I make the arbitrary decision to give one of my variables the name `keyboard`. The name `keyboard` reminds you, the reader, that this variable refers to a bunch of plastic buttons in front of your computer. Naming something `keyboard` doesn't tell Java anything about plastic buttons or about user input. On the other hand, the name `System.in` always tells Java about those plastic buttons. The code `var keyboard = new Scanner(System.in)` in [Listing 9-2](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0002) connects the name `keyboard` with the plastic buttons that we all know and love.

*   **Java's** `Scanner`**class has several different** `next`*`SomethingOrOther`***methods**.

    Here are some examples:

    *   The `nextDouble` method reads a `double` value (a number containing a decimal point). [Listing 9-2](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0002) calls `nextDouble` to get a payment amount.

        ![Warning](images/warning.png) The representation of decimal points varies from one country to another. For details (and some interesting trivia), see this chapter's later sidebar “[Where on Earth do you live?](#c09.xhtml_c09-sec-0014)”

    *   The `nextInt` method reads an `int` value (a whole number of some kind).
    *   The `next` method reads a single word — a bunch of text up to, but not including, some white space. In [Figure 9-1](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0001), the call `keyboard.next()` gets the word `CEO` and nothing else.

        By the way, the term *white space* means a blank space, a tab character, the end of a line, and several other oddities.

    *   The `nextLine` method gets everything up to the end of a line. In [Figure 9-1](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0001), a call to `nextLine` scoops up everything on one line of input (both words `Barry` and `Burd`).

        ![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) Some surprising things happen when you combine `nextLine` with other `Scanner` methods. For details, see this chapter's later section “[Reading a line at a time](#c09.xhtml_c09-sec-0020).”

*   **Whenever you use Java's `Scanner`, you should call the `close` method after your last `next`*`Whatever`* call.**

    In [Listing 9-2](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0002), the `main` method's last statement is

    `keyboard.close();`

    This statement does some housekeeping to disconnect the Java program from the computer keyboard. (The amount of required housekeeping is more than you might think!) If I omit this statement from [Listing 9-2](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0002), nothing terrible happens. Java usually cleans up after itself very nicely. But using `close()` to explicitly detach from the keyboard is good practice, and some IDEs display warnings if you omit the `keyboard.close()` statement. In this book's example, I always remember to close my `Scanner` variables.

    In [Chapter 14](#c14.xhtml), I show you a more reliable way to incorporate the `keyboard.close()` statement in your Java program.

The listings in previous chapters use Java's `readln` method to get input from the keyboard. So, why not use `readln` here in [Listing 9-2](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0002)? As of September 2024, with the release of Java 23, the newly-created `readln` method works only with keyboard input. If you want to get data from a disk file, you can't use the `readln` method. And no one wants to go through life without reading from a disk file. Relying exclusively on keyboard input is too confining. If you have several employees, you store employee information on a disk.

Java has many classes and methods for reading input of various kinds. The `Scanner` class from [Listing 9-2](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0002) dates back to the release of Java 5.0 in 2004\. A more versatile input package, `java.nio`, was born in 2002 as part of Java 1.4\. And the venerable `InputStreamReader`/`BufferedReader` classes came with Java 1.0 in 1996.

In any programming language, with input of any kind, there's a lot going on under the hood. Your program sets up a connection between the code and the storage device, prepares for possible trouble reading from the device, does the reading, converts the characters it reads into the type of value that you want and, finally, breaks your connection with the storage device. And the underlying operating system does even more work. It's a big mess.

Java's `Scanner` class doesn't offer all the options you get with the old Java 1.0 classes or with `java.nio`. In exchange for its lack of versatility, the `Scanner` class makes disk input fairly simple. Even so, disk input with `Scanner` can confuse novice programmers. So, the next section translates [Listing 9-2](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0002) into a program that reads from a disk file using the `Scanner` class.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) There are always interesting things for you to try:

#### GO, SCANNER, GO

Write a program that reads two double values separated by an arithmetic operator sign and uses a `switch` structure to do the arithmetic. For example, if the user types `2.0 * 5.1`, the program outputs `10.2`.

#### LOCALE, LOCALE, LOCALE

A `PlaceToLive` has an address, a number of bedrooms, and an area (in square feet or square meters). Write the `PlaceToLive` class's code. Write code for a main method that creates a few `PlaceToLive` instances by assigning values to their `address`, `numberOfBedrooms`, and `area` fields. The main method also reads (from the keyboard) the cost of living in each place. For each place, your code displays the cost per square foot (or square meter) and the cost per bedroom.

## Working with Disk Files

Your company is growing. In [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003), you had only one employee. In the next listing, you have three!

[LISTING 9-3](#c09.xhtml_rc09-fea-0003) Writing Payroll Checks

`import java.io.File; // These imports are optional for`
`import java.io.IOException; // a simple source file`
`import java.util.Scanner; // (like this file, for example).`

`void main() throws IOException {`
`var diskScanner = new Scanner(new File("EmployeeInfo.txt"));`

`for (int empNum = 1; empNum <= 3; empNum++) {`
`var anEmployee = new Employee();`

`anEmployee.setName(diskScanner.nextLine());`
`anEmployee.setJobTitle(diskScanner.nextLine());`
`anEmployee.cutCheck(diskScanner.nextDouble());`
`diskScanner.nextLine();`
`}`

`diskScanner.close();`
`}`

The `for` loop in [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003) performs three iterations. Here's how the variable name *anEmployee* is reused and recycled during those iterations:

*   During the first iteration, the statement `anEmployee = new Employee()` makes `anEmployee` refer to a new object.
*   During the second iteration, the computer executes the same statement again. This second execution creates a new incarnation of the `anEmployee` variable that refers to a brand-new object.
*   During the third iteration, all the same stuff happens again. A new `anEmployee` variable ends up referring to a third object.

The whole story is pictured in [Figure 9-2](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0002).

![“The image illustrates three instances of the Employee class, each with attributes 'name' and 'jobTitle'. The instances are named Barry (CEO), Harriet (Captain), and You (Exec). Below these instances, a for loop iterates through each Employee instance, assigning them to a variable 'anEmployee' with empNum values 1, 2, and 3 respectively.”](images/9781394289240-fg0902.png)

[FIGURE 9-2:](#c09.xhtml_rc09-fig-0002) Three employees.

To run the code in [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003), your hard drive must contain a file named `EmployeeInfo.txt`. Fortunately, the stuff that you download from this book's website ([`http://javafordummies.allmycode.com`](http://javafordummies.allmycode.com/)) comes with an `EmployeeInfo.txt` file. Just copy the `EmployeeInfo.txt` file to a place where [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003) can find it.

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) For help deciding where [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003) can find the file, see this chapter's “[Where's your file?](#c07.xhtml_c07-sec-0018)” section.

[Figure 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0003) shows the `EmployeeInfo.txt` file you get when you visit this book's website and download the book's code.

![“A list showing names, titles, and corresponding amounts of money. The names and titles are Barry Burd (CEO) with $5000.00, Harriet Ritter (Captain) with $7000.00, and 'Your Name Here' (Honorary Exec of the Day) with $10000.00.”](images/9781394289240-fg0903.png)

[FIGURE 9-3:](#c09.xhtml_rc09-fig-0003) An `EmployeeInfo.txt` file.

You can create your own `EmployeeInfo.txt` file with Windows Notepad or TextEdit on a Mac. (If you use TextEdit, be sure to choose Settings ⇒   Format ⇒   Plain Text.) When you type the characters in [Figure 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0003), you must finish up by typing `10000.00` and then pressing Enter. (Look again at [Figure 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0003) and notice how the cursor is at the start of a brand-new line.) If you forget to finish by pressing Enter, the code in [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003) will crash when you try to run it.

And remember: Grouping separators vary from one country to another. The file shown in [Figure 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0003) works on a computer configured in the United States where *5000.00* means “five thousand.” But the file doesn't work on a computer that's configured in what I call a “comma country” — a country where *5000,00* means “five thousand.” If you live in a comma country, be sure to read this chapter's nearby sidebar “[Where on Earth do you live?](#c09.xhtml_c09-sec-0014)”

[Figure 9-4](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0004) shows the output when you run [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003) using `EmployeeInfo.txt` file in [Figure 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0003).

![“The image shows three lines of text, each indicating a payment order. The first line reads, 'Pay to the order of Barry Burd (CEO) ***$5,000.00.' The second line reads, 'Pay to the order of Harriet Ritter (Captain) ***$7,000.00.' The third line reads, 'Pay to the order of Your Name Here (Honorary Exec of the Day) ***$10,000.00.'”](images/9781394289240-fg0904.png)

[FIGURE 9-4:](#c09.xhtml_rc09-fig-0004) Everybody gets paid.

### Cutting a check

In previous chapters, programs read characters from the computer's keyboard. But the code in [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003) reads characters from a specific file. The file (named `EmployeeInfo.txt`) lives on your computer's hard drive.

This `EmployeeInfo.txt` file is like a word processing document. The file can contain letters, digits, and other characters. But unlike a word processing document, the `EmployeeInfo.txt` file contains no formatting — no italics, no bold, no font sizes, nothing of that kind.

The `EmployeeInfo.txt` file contains only ordinary characters — the kinds of keystrokes that you type while you play a guessing game from [Chapters 5](#c05.xhtml) and [6](#c06.xhtml).
