Of course, getting guesses from a user's keyboard and reading employee data from a disk file aren't exactly the same. In a guessing game, the program displays prompts, such as `Pick a number from 1 to 10`. The game program conducts a back-and-forth dialogue with the person sitting at the keyboard. In contrast, [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003) has no dialogue. This `DoPayroll` program reads characters from a hard drive and doesn't prompt or interact with anyone.

### Repeat after me

In almost any computer programming language, reading data from a file can be tricky. You add extra lines of code to tell the computer what to do. Sometimes you can copy and paste these lines from other people's code. For example, you can follow this pattern (extracted from [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003)):

**`/*`**
**`* The pattern in`****`Listing 9-3`**
**`*/`**
**`import java.io.File;`**
**`import java.io.IOException;`**
**`import java.util.Scanner;`**

`void main()` **`throws IOException`** `{`

`var` ***`scannerName`*** `= new Scanner(`**`new File("`*`SomeFileName`*`"`**`));`

`//`*`Some code goes here`*

***`scannerName`***`.nextInt();`
***`scannerName`***`.nextDouble();`
***`scannerName`***`.next();`
***`scannerName`***`.nextLine();`
`//`*`Some code goes here`*

***`scannerName`***`.close();`
`}`

You want to read data from a file. You start by imagining that you're reading from the keyboard. Put the usual `Scanner` and `next` codes into your program. Then add some extra items from the [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003) pattern:

*   Add three new `import` declarations: one for `java.io.File`, another for `java.io.IOException`, and a third for `java.util.Scanner`. (With a file that has an implicitly declared class, like the file in [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003), these imports are optional.)
*   Type **throws IOException** in your method's header.
*   Type **new File(" ")** in your call to `new Scanner`.
*   Take a file that's already on your hard drive. Type that filename inside the quotation marks.
*   Take the word that you use for the name of your scanner. Reuse that word in calls to `next`, `nextInt`, `nextDouble`, and so on.
*   Take the word that you use for the name of your scanner. Reuse that word in a call to `close`.

Occasionally, copying and pasting code can get you into trouble. Maybe you're writing a program that doesn't fit the simple [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003) pattern. You need to tweak the pattern a bit. But to tweak the pattern, you need to understand some of the ideas behind the pattern.

That’s how the next section comes to your rescue. It covers some of these ideas.

### Reading from a file

To read characters from a file, you can use the `Scanner` class and methods like `nextLine` and `nextDouble`. But in addition to these goodies, you have a few extra hurdles to jump. Here's a list:

*   **You need a new** `File`**object.** To be more precise, you need a new instance of the API's `File` class. You get this new instance with code like

    `new File("EmployeeInfo.txt")`

    The stuff in quotation marks is the name of a file — a file on your computer’s hard drive. The file contains characters like those shown previously in [Figure 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0003).

    At this point, the terminology makes mountains out of molehills. Sure, I use the phrases *new File object* and *new File instance,* but all you’re doing is making `new File("EmployeeInfo.txt")` stand for a file on your hard drive. After you shove `new File("EmployeeInfo.txt")` into `new Scanner`,

    `var` **`diskScanner`** `= new Scanner(`**`new File("EmployeeInfo.txt")`**`);`

    you can forget all about the `new File` business. From that point on in the code, `diskScanner` stands for the `EmployeeInfo.txt` file on your computer's hard drive. (The name `diskScanner` stands for a file on your hard drive just as, in previous examples, the name `keyboard` stands for those buttons you press day in and day out.)

    ![Remember](images/remember.png) Creating a `new File` object in [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003) is like creating a `new Employee` object later in the same listing. It's also like creating a `new Account` object in the examples of [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml). The only difference is that the `Employee` and `Account` classes are defined in this book's examples. The `File` class is defined in Java's API.

    ![Warning](images/warning.png) When you connect to a disk file with `new Scanner`, don't forget the `new File` part. If you write `new Scanner("EmployeeInfo.txt")` without `new File`, Java's compiler doesn't mind. (You don't get any warnings or error messages before you run the code.) But when you run the code, you don't get anything like the results that you expect to get.

*   **You need a** `throws IOException`**clause.** Lots of things can go wrong when your program connects to `EmployeeInfo.txt`. For one thing, your hard drive may not have a file named `EmployeeInfo.txt`. For another, the file `EmployeeInfo.txt` may be in the wrong directory. To brace for this kind of calamity, the Java programming language takes certain precautions. The language insists that when a disk file is involved, you acknowledge the possible dangers of calling `new Scanner`.

    You can acknowledge the hazards in several possible ways, but the simplest way is to use a `throws` clause. In [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003), the `main` method's header ends with the words *throws IOException.* By adding these two words, you appease the Java compiler. It's as if you’re saying “I know that calling `new Scanner` can lead to problems. You don’t have to remind me.” And, sure enough, adding `throws IOException` to your `main` method keeps Java from complaining. (Without this `throws` clause, you get an `unhandled exception` error message.)

    ![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) For the full story on Java exceptions, read [Chapter 14](#c14.xhtml). In the meantime, add `throws IOException` to the header of any method that calls `new Scanner(new File( … ))`.

### Where's your file?

If Java can't find an `EmployeeInfo.txt` file, the whole project doesn't run properly. Instead, you get a `FileNotFoundException`.

In general, when you get a `FileNotFoundException`, some file that your program needs isn't available to it. This is an easy mistake to make. It can be frustrating because, to you, a file such as `EmployeeInfo.txt` may look like it's available to your program. But remember: Computers are stupid. If you make a tiny mistake, the computer can’t read between the lines for you. If the filename is spelled incorrectly, the computer chokes when it tries to run your code.

Sometimes you know darn well that an `EmployeeInfo.txt` (or `whatever.xyz`) file exists on your hard drive. But when you run your program, you still get a mean-looking `FileNotFoundException`. When this happens, the file may be in the wrong directory on your hard drive. (Of course, it depends on your point of view. Maybe the file is in the right directory but your Java program is looking for the file in the wrong directory.) To diagnose this problem, add the following code to [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003):

`var employeeInfo = new File("EmployeeInfo.txt");`
`System.out.println("Looking for " + employeeInfo.getCanonicalPath());`

When you run the code, Java tells you where, on your hard drive, the `EmployeeInfo.txt` file should be.

### You can decide where to put your file

With code like `new File("EmployeeInfo.txt")`, your IDE decides where to look for a file named `EmployeeInfo.txt`. But you can override this behavior by naming a file's exact location in your Java code. Code like `new File("C:\\Users\\bburd\\Documents\\EmployeeInfo.txt")` on Windows or `File("/Users/bburd/Documents/EmployeeInfo.txt")` on a Mac looks really ugly, but it works.

In the preceding paragraph, did you notice the double backslashes in`"C: \\Users\\bburd\\Documents …"`? If you're a Windows user, you'd be tempted to write `C:\Users\bburd\Documents …` with single backslashes. But in Java, the single backslash has its own, special meaning. (For example, `print("\n\nBoo!")` means, “Skip two lines and then print `Boo!`.”) So, in Java, to indicate a backslash inside a quoted string, you use a double backslash instead.

### Reading a line at a time

[Listings 9-2](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0002) and [9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003) illustrate some useful tricks for reading data. In particular, every scanner that you create has a `nextLine` method. (You might not use this `nextLine` method, but the method is available nonetheless.) When you call a scanner's `nextLine` method, the method grabs everything up to the end of the current line of text.

In the previous paragraph, notice my careful choice of words: `nextLine` reads everything “up to the end of the current line.” Unfortunately, what it means to read up to the end of the current line isn't always what you think it means. Intermingling `nextInt`, `nextDouble`, and `nextLine` calls can be messy. You have to watch what you're doing and check your program's output carefully.

To understand all of this, you need to be painfully aware of a data file’s line breaks. Think of a line break as an extra character, stuck between one line of text and the next. Then imagine that calling `nextLine` means to read everything up to and including the next line break.

[Figure 9-5](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0005) contains an annotated version of the first several lines in [Figure 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0003). Notice what you do to read `5000.00` followed by `Harriet Ritter`:

*   You call `nextDouble` to get `5000.00` and then,
*   You call `nextLine`, thinking that you'll get `Harriet Ritter` from the call. But, instead, you get the line break after `5000.00`.

![“The image is a flowchart of a Java program that processes employee data using a disk scanner. It depicts the sequence of steps for reading and assigning values to employee attributes such as name, title, and paycheck amount. The flowchart includes code snippets, starting with 'empNum = 1;' and continuing with operations like setting the employee's name to 'Barry Burd' (CEO) and 'Harriet Ritter' (Captain).”](images/9781394289240-fg0905.png)

[FIGURE 9-5:](#c09.xhtml_rc09-fig-0005) Calling `nextDouble` and `nextLine`.

So, after reading the number `5000.00`, you need *two* calls to `nextLine` in order to scoop up the name `Harriet Ritter`. The mistake that I usually make is to forget the first of those two calls.

![Warning](images/warning.png) Look again at the file in [Figure 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0003). For this section's code to work correctly, you must have a line break after the last `10000.00`. If you don't, a final call to `nextLine` makes your program crash and burn. The error message reads `NoSuchElementException: No line found`.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) I'm always surprised by the number of quirks that I find in each programming language's scanning methods. For example, the first `nextLine` that reads from the file in [Figure 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fig-0003) devours `Barry Burd[LineBreak]` from the file. But that `nextLine` call delivers `Barry Burd` (with no line break) to the running code. So `nextLine` looks for a line break, and then `nextLine` loses the line break. Yes, this is a subtle point. And no, this subtle point hardly ever causes problems for anyone.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) If this business about `nextDouble` and `nextLine` confuses you, please don't put the blame on Java. Mixing input calls is delicate work in any computer programming language. And the truly nasty thing is that each programming language approaches the problem a little differently. What you find out about `nextLine` in Java helps you understand the issues when you get to know C++ or Visual Basic, but it doesn't tell you all the details. Each language’s details are unique to that language. (Yes, it’s a big pain. But because all computer programmers become rich and famous, the pain eventually pays off.)

### Clean up after yourself

To the average computer user, a keyboard doesn't feel anything like a file stored on a computer's hard drive. But disk files and keyboard input have a lot in common. In fact, a basic principle of computer operating systems dictates that any differences between two kinds of input be, for the programmer, as blurry as possible. As a Java programmer, you should treat disk files and keyboard input almost the same way. That's why [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003) contains a `diskScanner.close()` call.

When you run a Java program, you normally execute the `main` method's statements, starting with the first statement in the method body and ending with the last statement in the method body. You take detours along the way, skipping past `else` parts and diving into method bodies, but basically you finish executing statements at the end of the `main` method. That's why, in [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003), the call to `close` is at the end of the `main` method's body. When you run the code in [Listing 9-3](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0003), the last thing you do is disconnect from the disk file. And, fortunately, that disconnection takes place after you've executed all the `nextLine` and `nextDouble` calls.

#### ON THE RECORD

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Previously in this chapter, you create instances of your own `PlaceToLive` class and display information about those instances. Modify the text-based version of your code so that it gets each instance's characteristics (address, number of bedrooms, and area) from a disk file.

Chapter 10

# Saving Time and Money: Reusing Existing Code

IN THIS CHAPTER

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Adding new life to old code**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Tweaking your code**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Making changes without spending a fortune**

r**euse** /ree-YOOSS/ *noun* The act of using something for the *n*th time, where *n* is greater than 1. Example: “Reuse of the material in *Java For Dummies,* 9th Edition, is strictly prohibited.”

Reuse is good but, in many situations, reuse is a rarity. For example, in the United States, the Department of Agriculture estimates that 30 to 40 percent of the nation's food goes to waste. And, according to one source, only 9 percent of the world's plastics are recycled.

Information goes to waste too. When I revised this chapter for the eighth edition, I decided not to reuse the seventh edition's chapter introduction. (That introduction's made-up story isn't amusing in the least.) In desperation, I looked at some of my other books to find an introduction that I could reuse for this chapter. No luck! I came up empty-handed.

I can't even reuse paragraphs to explain common concepts. My description of Java classes from another book wouldn't work well in this book. I've even experienced times when I had to scrap sections in several chapters because of a small update in [Chapter 3](#c03.xhtml).

No doubt about it! Reuse is a precious commodity, and it's in very short supply. Wouldn’t it be nice if every piece of software did just what you wanted it to do? In an ideal world, you could buy a program, make it work right away, plug it seamlessly into new situations, and update it easily whenever your needs change. Unfortunately, software of this kind doesn’t exist. (Nothing of this kind exists.) The truth is that no matter what you want to do, you can find software that does some of it, but not all of it.

This is one of the reasons why object-oriented programming has been successful. For years, companies were buying prewritten code, only to discover that the code didn’t do what they wanted it to do. So, what did the companies do about it? They started messing with the code. Their programmers dug deep into the program files, changed variable names, moved subprograms around, reworked formulas, and generally made the code worse. The reality was that if a program didn’t already do what you wanted it to do (even if it did something ever so close to what you wanted), you could never improve the situation by mucking around inside the code. The best option was always to chuck the whole program (expensive as that was) and start all over again. What a sad state of affairs!

With object-oriented programming, a big change has come about. At its heart, an object-oriented program is made to be modified. With correctly written software, you can take advantage of features that are already built-in, add new features of your own, and override features that don’t suit your needs. And the best part is that the changes you make are clean — no clawing and digging into other people’s brittle program code. Instead, you make nice, orderly additions and modifications without touching the existing code’s internal logic. It’s the ideal solution.

## Defining Subclasses (What It Means to Be a Full-Time or Part-Time Employee)

This time last year, your company paid $10 million for a piece of software. (Too bad! You didn't know you could find that software in [Chapter 9](#c09.xhtml) of *Java For Dummies,* 9th Edition.)

The software came in the form of an `Employee.class` file. People at Burd Brain Consulting (the company that created the software) didn't want you to know about the innards of the software. (They didn't want you to steal their ideas.) So, you don't have the Java program file that the software came from. In other words, you *don’t* have the `Employee.java` file in [Listing 9-1](#c09.xhtml_c09-fea-0001) (over in [Chapter 9](#c09.xhtml)) and [Listing 10-1](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0001).

[LISTING 10-1](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fea-0001) What Is an Employee?

`public class Employee {`
`private String name;`
`private String jobTitle;`

`public void setName(String name) {`
`this.name = name;`
`}`

`public String getName() {`
`return name;`
`}`

`public void setJobTitle(String jobTitle) {`
`this.jobTitle = jobTitle;`
`}`

`public String getJobTitle() {`
`return jobTitle;`
`}`

`public void cutCheck(double amountPaid) {`
`System.out.print("Pay to the order of " + name);`
`System.out.print(" (");`
`System.out.print(jobTitle);`
`System.out.print(") ***$");`
`System.out.println(String.format("%,.2f", amountPaid));`
`}`
`}`

You can run the bytecode in the `Employee.class` file. You can also read the documentation in a web page named *Employee.html.* But you can't see the statements inside the `Employee.java` program, and you can't change any of the program’s code.

![Remember](images/remember.png) [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml) describes the difference between filenames ending with `.java` and those ending with `.class`. To write code that uses the `Employee` class, you don't need the `Employee.java` file on your hard drive. All you need is the file `Employee.class`.

Since this time last year, your company has grown. Unlike in the old days, your company now has two kinds of employees: full-time and part-time. Each full-time employee is on a fixed, weekly salary. (If the employee works nights and weekends, then in return for this monumental effort, the employee receives a hearty handshake.) In contrast, each part-time employee works for an hourly wage. Your company deducts an amount from each full-time employee's paycheck to pay for the company’s benefits package. Part-time employees, however, don’t get benefits.

The question is whether the software that your company bought last year can keep up with the company’s growth. You invested in a helpful program to handle employees and their payroll, but the program doesn’t differentiate between your full-time and part-time employees. You have several options:

*   **Call your next-door neighbor, whose 12-year-old child knows more about computer programming than anyone in your company.** Get this uppity little brat to take the employee software apart, rewrite it, and hand it back to you with all the changes and additions your company requires.

    On second thought, you can’t do that. No matter how smart that kid is, the complexities of the employee software will probably confuse the kid. By the time you get the software back, it’ll be filled with bugs and inconsistencies. Besides, you don’t even have the `Employee.java` file to hand to the kid. All you have is the `Employee.class` file, which is bytecode. And bytecode can't be read or modified with a text editor. (See [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml).) Besides, your kid just beat up the neighbor's kid. You don’t want to give your neighbor the satisfaction of seeing you beg for the whiz kid’s help.

*   **Scrap the $10 million employee software.** Persuade someone in your company to rewrite the software from scratch.

    In other words, say goodbye to your time and money.

*   **Write a new front end for the employee software.** That is, build a piece of code that does some preliminary processing on full-time employees and then hands the preliminary results to your $10 million software. Do the same for part-time employees.

    This idea could be decent or spell disaster. Are you sure that the existing employee software has convenient *hooks* in it? (That is, does the employee software contain entry points that allow your front-end software to easily send preliminary data to the expensive employee software?) *Remember:* This plan treats the existing software as one big, monolithic lump, which can become cumbersome. Dividing the labor between your front-end code and the existing employee program is difficult. And, if you add layer upon layer to existing black box code, you’ll probably end up with a fairly inefficient system.

*   **Call Burd Brain Consulting, the company that sold you the employee software.** Tell Dr. Burd that you want the next version of his software to differentiate between full-time and part-time employees.

    “No problem,” says Dr. Burd. “It’ll be ready by the start of the next fiscal quarter.” That evening, Dr. Burd makes a discreet phone call to his next-door neighbor… .

*   **Create two new Java classes named *FullTimeEmployee* and *PartTimeEmployee.*** Have each new class extend the existing functionality of the expensive `Employee` class, but have each new class define its own, specialized functionality for certain kinds of employees.

    Way to go! [Figure 10-1](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0001) shows the structure that you want to create.

![“An image of a flowchart from a Java program processing employee data using a disk scanner. The sequence of steps includes reading and assigning values to employee attributes, such as name, title, and paycheck amount. Specific examples include 'empNum = 1;' and setting employee names to 'Barry Burd' (CEO) and 'Harriet Ritter' (Captain).”](images/9781394289240-fg1001.png)

[FIGURE 10-1:](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fig-0001) The `Employee` class family tree.

### Creating a subclass

[Listing 10-1](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0001) defines an `Employee` class. You may not have access to the `Employee.java` file, but if you have the compiled `Employee.class` file and some documentation about the code, you can extend the definition of an `Employee` to create new, more specialized classes. So, in [Listing 10-2](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0002), I define a new class: a `FullTimeEmployee` class.

[LISTING 10-2](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fea-0002) What Is a FullTimeEmployee?

`public class FullTimeEmployee` **`extends Employee`** `{`
`private double weeklySalary;`
`private double benefitDeduction;`

`public void setWeeklySalary(double weeklySalary) {`
`this.weeklySalary = weeklySalary;`
`}`

`public double getWeeklySalary() {`
`return weeklySalary;`
`}`

`public void setBenefitDeduction(double benefitDeduction) {`
`this.benefitDeduction = benefitDeduction;`
`}`

`public double getBenefitDeduction() {`
`return benefitDeduction;`
`}`

`public double findPaymentAmount() {`
`return weeklySalary - benefitDeduction;`
`}`
`}`

Looking at [Listing 10-2](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0002), you can see that each instance of the `FullTimeEmployee` class has two fields: `weeklySalary` and `benefitDeduction`. But are those the only fields that each `FullTimeEmployee` instance has? No, they're not. The first line of [Listing 10-2](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0002) says that the `FullTimeEmployee` class extends the existing `Employee` class. This means that in addition to having a `weeklySalary` and a `benefitDeduction`, each `FullTimeEmployee` instance has two other fields: `name` and `jobTitle`. These two fields come from the definition of the `Employee` class, which you can find in [Listing 10-1](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0001).

In [Listing 10-2](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0002), the magic word is *extends.* When one class extends an existing class, the extending class implicitly inherits functionality that's defined in the existing class. So, the `FullTimeEmployee` class *inherits* the `name` and `jobTitle` fields. The `FullTimeEmployee` class also inherits all the methods that are declared in the `Employee` class: `setName`, `getName`, `setJobTitle`, `getJobTitle`, and `cutCheck`.

The `FullTimeEmployee` class is a *subclass* of the `Employee` class. That means the `Employee` class is the *superclass* of the `FullTimeEmployee` class. You can also talk in terms of blood relatives: The `FullTimeEmployee` class is the *child* of the `Employee` class, and the `Employee` class is the *parent* of the `FullTimeEmployee` class.

Several names from [Listing 10-1](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0001) are implicitly defined in [Listing 10-2](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0002). It's almost (but not quite) as if the `FullTimeEmployee` class were defined by the code in [Listing 10-3](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0003).

[LISTING 10-3](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fea-0003) Fake (but Informative) Code

`public class FullTimeEmployee {`
`private String name;`
`private String jobTitle;`
`private double weeklySalary;`
`private double benefitDeduction;`

`public void setName(String name) {`
`this.name = name;`
`}`

`public String getName() {`
`return name;`
`}`

`public void setJobTitle(String jobTitle) {`
`this.jobTitle = jobTitle;`
`}`

`public String getJobTitle() {`
`return jobTitle;`
`}`

`public void setWeeklySalary(double weeklySalary) {`
`this.weeklySalary = weeklySalary;`
`}`

`public double getWeeklySalary() {`
`return weeklySalary;`
`}`

`public void setBenefitDeduction(double benefitDeduction) {`
`this.benefitDeduction = benefitDeduction;`
`}`

`public double getBenefitDeduction() {`
`return benefitDeduction;`
`}`

`public double findPaymentAmount() {`
`return weeklySalary - benefitDeduction;`
`}`

`public void cutCheck(double amountPaid) {`
`System.out.print("Pay to the order of " + name);`
`System.out.print(" (");`
`System.out.print(jobTitle);`
`System.out.print(") ***$");`
`System.out.println(String.format("%,.2f", amountPaid));`
`}`
`}`

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) Why does the title for [Listing 10-3](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0003) call that code “fake”? (Should the code feel insulted?) Well, the main difference between [Listing 10-3](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0003) and the inheritance situation in [Listings 10-1](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0001) and [10-2](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0002) is this: A child class can't directly reference the private fields of its parent class. To do anything with the parent class’s private fields, the child class has to call the parent class’s getter and setter methods. Back in [Listing 10-2](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0002), calling `setName("Rufus")` would be legal, but the code `name="Rufus"` wouldn't be. If you believe everything you read in [Listing 10-3](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0003), you'd think that code in the `FullTimeEmployee` class can do `name="Rufus"`. Well, it can't. (My, what a subtle point this is!)

### Creating subclasses is habit-forming

After you're accustomed to extending classes, you can get extend-happy. If you created a `FullTimeEmployee` class, you might as well create a `PartTimeEmployee` class, as shown in [Listing 10-4](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0004).

[LISTING 10-4](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fea-0004) What Is a PartTimeEmployee?

`public class PartTimeEmployee` **`extends Employee`** `{`
`private double hourlyRate;`

`public void setHourlyRate(double hourlyRate) {`
`this.hourlyRate = hourlyRate;`
`}`

`public double getHourlyRate() {`
`return hourlyRate;`
`}`

`public double findPaymentAmount(int hours) {`
`return hourlyRate * hours;`
`}`
`}`

Unlike the `FullTimeEmployee` class, `PartTimeEmployee` has no salary or deduction. Instead, `PartTimeEmployee` has an `hourlyRate` field. (Adding an `hoursWorked` field would also be a possibility. I chose not to do this, figuring that the number of hours a part-time employee works may change from week to week.)

## Using Subclasses

The preceding section tells a story about creating subclasses. It's a good story, but it's incomplete. Creating subclasses is fine, but you gain nothing from these subclasses unless you write code to use them.

[Listing 10-5](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0005) contains the simplest possible example of a program that uses the subclasses `FullTimeEmployee` and `PartTimeEmployee`. (For a look at some more interesting examples, visit this book's website).

[LISTING 10-5](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fea-0005) Putting Subclasses to Good Use

`void main() {`

`var ftEmployee = new FullTimeEmployee();`

`ftEmployee.setName("Barry Burd");`
`ftEmployee.setJobTitle("CEO");`
`ftEmployee.setWeeklySalary(5000.00);`
`ftEmployee.setBenefitDeduction(500.00);`
`ftEmployee.cutCheck(ftEmployee.findPaymentAmount());`
`println("");`

`var ptEmployee = new PartTimeEmployee();`

`ptEmployee.setName("Steve Surace");`
`ptEmployee.setJobTitle("Driver");`
`ptEmployee.setHourlyRate(7.53);`
`ptEmployee.cutCheck(ptEmployee.findPaymentAmount(10));`
`}`

[Figure 10-2](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0002) shows the output of the code in [Listing 10-5](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0005).

To understand [Listing 10-5](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0005), you need to keep an eye on three classes: `Employee`, `FullTimeEmployee`, and `PartTimeEmployee`. (For a look at the code that defines these classes, see [Listings 10-1](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0001), [10-2](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0002), and [10-4](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0004).)

![“The image shows two lines of text, each representing a payment order. The first line reads 'Pay to the order of Barry Burd (CEO) ***$4,500.00'. The second line reads 'Pay to the order of Steve Surace (Driver) ***$75.30'.”](images/9781394289240-fg1002.png)

[FIGURE 10-2:](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fig-0002) I earn a lot more than Steve. Na, na, na!

The first half of [Listing 10-5](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0005) deals with a full-time employee. Notice the methods that are available for use with the `ftEmployee` variable. You can call `ftEmployee.setWeeklySalary` because `ftEmployee` has type `FullTimeEmployee`. You can also call `ftEmployee.setName` because the `FullTimeEmployee` class extends the `Employee` class.

Because `cutCheck` is declared in the `Employee` class, you can call `ftEmployee.cutCheck`. But you can also call `ftEmployee.findPaymentAmount` because a `findPaymentAmount` method is in the `FullTimeEmployee` class.

Subclasses embody what computer scientists call the *is a* relationship. Every `FullTimeEmployee` instance *is an*`Employee` instance too. To make sure I'm not lying to you, run this code:

`void main() {`
`var ftEmployee = new FullTimeEmployee();`
`println(ftEmployee instanceof FullTimeEmployee);`
`println(ftEmployee instanceof Employee);`
`}`

The output from both `println` calls is the word `true`.

### Making types match

Look again at the first half of [Listing 10-5](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0005). Take special notice of the statement that cuts a check for `ftEmployee`. The statement forms a nice, long chain of values and their types. You can see this by reading the statement from the inside out:

*   Method `ftEmployee.findPaymentAmount` is called with an empty parameter list. (Refer to [Listing 10-5](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0005).) That's good because a full-time employee's `findPaymentAmount` method takes no parameters. (Refer to [Listing 10-2](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0002).)
*   The `findPaymentAmount` method returns a value of type `double`. (Again, refer to [Listing 10-2](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0002).)
*   The `double` value that `ftEmployee.findPaymentAmount` returns is passed to method `ftEmployee.cutCheck`. (Refer to [Listing 10-5](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0005).) That's good because the `Employee` class's `cutCheck` method takes one parameter of type `double`. (Refer to [Listing 10-1](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0001).)

For a fanciful graphical illustration of this type-matching business, see [Figure 10-3](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0003).

![Three puzzle pieces with “findPaymentAmount” and “cutCheck” functions connected by arrows indicating data flow.](images/9781394289240-fg1003.png)

[FIGURE 10-3:](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fig-0003) Matching parameters.

![Remember](images/remember.png) Always feed a method the value types that it wants in its parameter list.

### The second half of the story

In the second half of [Listing 10-5](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0005), the code creates an object of type `PartTimeEmployee`. A variable of type `PartTimeEmployee` can do some of the same things a `FullTimeEmployee` variable can do. But the `PartTimeEmployee` class doesn't have the `setWeeklySalary` and `setBenefitDeduction` methods. Instead, the `PartTimeEmployee` class has the `setHourlyRate` method. (See [Listing 10-4](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0004).) So, in [Listing 10-5](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0005) the next-to-last line is a call to the `setHourlyRate` method.

The last line of [Listing 10-5](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0005) is by far the most interesting. On that line, the code hands the number `10` (the number of hours worked) to the `findPaymentAmount` method. Compare this with the earlier call to `findPaymentAmount` — the call for the full-time employee in the first half of [Listing 10-5](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0005). Between the two subclasses, `FullTimeEmployee` and `PartTimeEmployee`, are two different `findPaymentAmount` methods. The two methods have two different kinds of parameter lists:

*   The `FullTimeEmployee` class's `findPaymentAmount` method takes no parameters (refer to [Listing 10-2](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0002)).
*   The `PartTimeEmployee` class's `findPaymentAmount` method takes one `int` parameter (refer to [Listing 10-4](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0004)).

This is par for the course. Finding the payment amount for a part-time employee isn't the same as finding the payment amount for a full-time employee. A part-time employee's pay changes each week, depending on the number of hours the employee works in a week. The full-time employee’s pay stays the same each week. So, the `FullTimeEmployee` and `PartTimeEmployee` classes both have `findPaymentAmount` methods, but each class's method works quite differently.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Yes, I have some things for you to try:

#### BUY OR RENT

In [Chapter 9](#c09.xhtml), you create instances of your own `PlaceToLive` class and display information about those instances. Create two subclasses of your `PlaceToLive` class: a `House` class and an `Apartment` class. Each `House` object has a mortgage cost (a monthly amount) and a property tax cost (a yearly amount). Each `Apartment` object has a rental cost (a monthly amount).

A main method creates some houses and some apartments. For each house or apartment, your main method displays the total cost per square foot (or square meter) and the total cost per bedroom, both calculated monthly.

#### TAX BREAKS

In [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml), you create an `Organization` class. Each instance of your `Organization` class has a name, an annual revenue amount, and a `boolean` value indicating whether the organization is or is not a profit-making organization.

Create a new `Organization2` class. Each instance of this new class has only a name and an annual revenue amount. Create two subclasses: Call them the `ProfitMakingOrganization` class and a `NonProfitOrganization` class. A profit-making organization pays 10 percent of its revenue in tax, but a nonprofit organization pays only 2 percent of its revenue in tax.

Make a main method that creates `ProfitMakingOrganization` instances as well as `NonProfitOrganization` instances while also displaying information about each instance, including the amount of tax the organization pays.

## Changing the Payments for Only Some of the Employees

Wouldn't you know it! Some knucklehead in the human resources department offered double pay for overtime to one of your part-time employees. Now word is getting around, and some of the other part-timers want double pay for their overtime work. If this keeps up, you'll end up in the poorhouse, so you need to send out a memo to all the part-time employees, explaining why earning more money is not to their benefit.

In the meantime, you have two kinds of part-time employees — the ones who receive double-pay-for-overtime hours and the ones who don’t — so you need to modify your payroll software. What are your options?

*   Well, you can dig right into the `PartTimeEmployee` class code, make a few changes, and hope for the best. (Not a good idea!)
*   You can follow the previous section’s advice and create a subclass of the existing `PartTimeEmployee` class. “But wait,” you say. “The existing `PartTimeEmployee` class already has a `findPaymentAmount` method. Do I need some tricky way of bypassing this existing `findPaymentAmount` method for each double-pay-for-overtime employee?”

    At this point, you can thank your lucky stars that you're doing object-oriented programming in Java. With object-oriented programming, you can create a subclass that overrides the functionality of its parent class. [Listing 10-6](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0006) has just such a subclass.

[LISTING 10-6](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fea-0006) Yet Another Subclass

`public class PartTimeWithOver` **`extends PartTimeEmployee`** `{`

**`@Override`**
**`public double findPaymentAmount(int hours)`** `{`

`if (hours <= 40) {`
`return getHourlyRate() * hours;`
`} else {`
`return getHourlyRate() * 40 + getHourlyRate() * 2 * (hours - 40);`
`}`
`}`
`}`

[Figure 10-4](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0004) shows the relationship between the code in [Listing 10-6](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0006) and other pieces of code in this chapter. In particular, `PartTimeWithOver` is a subclass of a subclass. In object-oriented programming, a chain of this kind is not the least bit unusual. In fact, as subclasses go, this chain is rather short.

![“A class diagram illustrating inheritance relationships between different types of employees. The diagram shows a top-level class labeled 'Employee,' which has two subclasses: 'FullTimeEmployee' and 'PartTimeEmployee.' Additionally, 'PartTimeEmployee' has a further subclass labeled 'PartTimeWithOver.' The diagram depicts the hierarchical structure of these employee types in an object-oriented programming context.”](images/9781394289240-fg1004.png)

[FIGURE 10-4:](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fig-0004) A tree of classes.

The `PartTimeWithOver` class extends the `PartTimeEmployee` class, but the `PartTimeWithOver` picks and chooses what it wants to inherit from the `PartTimeEmployee` class. Because `PartTimeWithOver` has its own declaration for the `findPaymentAmount` method, the `PartTimeWithOver` class doesn't inherit a `findPaymentAmount` method from its parent. (See [Figure 10-5](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0005).)

According to the official terminology, the `PartTimeWithOver` class *overrides* its parent class's `findPaymentAmount` method. If you create an object from the `PartTimeWithOver` class, that object has the `name`, `jobTitle`, `hourlyRate`, and `cutCheck` of the `PartTimeEmployee` class, but the object has the `findPaymentAmount` method that's defined in [Listing 10-6](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0006).

### A Java annotation

The word `@Override` in [Listing 10-6](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0006) is an example of an *annotation*. A Java annotation tells your computer something about your code. In particular, the `@Override` annotation in [Listing 10-6](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0006) tells the Java compiler to be on the lookout for a common coding error. The annotation says, “Make sure that the method immediately following this annotation has the same stuff (the same name, the same parameters, and so on) as one of the methods in the superclass. If not, then display an error message.”

![“The image shows a class hierarchy diagram for an employee management system. The top class is 'Employee' (Listing 10-1) with attributes 'name' and 'jobTitle' and a method 'cutCheck'. The 'Employee' class is inherited by 'PartTimeEmployee' (Listing 10-4) which adds an attribute 'hourlyRate' and a method 'findPaymentAmount'. The 'PartTimeEmployee' class is further inherited by 'PartTimeWithOver' (Listing 10-6) which overrides the 'findPaymentAmount' method.”](images/9781394289240-fg1005.png)

[FIGURE 10-5:](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fig-0005) Method `findPaymentAmount` isn't inherited.

So, in [Listing 10-6](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0006), if I accidentally type

`@Override`
`public double findPaymentAmount(`**`double`** `hours) {`

with `double hours` instead of `int hours`, the compiler reminds me that my new `findPaymentAmount` method doesn't really override anything that's in [Listing 10-4](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0004).

Java has other kinds of annotations. For a list, visit [`https://docs.oracle.com/javase/tutorial/java/annotations/predefined.html`](https://docs.oracle.com/javase/tutorial/java/annotations/predefined.html).

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) Java's `@Override` annotation is optional. If you remove the word `@Override` from [Listing 10-6](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0006), your code still runs correctly. But the `@Override` annotation gives your code some added safety. With `@Override`, the compiler checks to make sure that you're doing something you intend to do (namely, overriding one of the superclass's methods). And with apologies to George Orwell, some types of annotations are less optional than others. You can omit certain annotations from your code only if you're willing to replace the annotation with lots and lots of unannotated Java code.

### Using methods from classes and subclasses

If you need clarification on this notion of overriding a method, look at the code in [Listing 10-7](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0007). A run of that code is shown in [Figure 10-6](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0006).

![“The image shows three lines of text indicating payments to different individuals with their roles and amounts. The payments are: Barry Budd (CEO) for $4,500.00, Chris Apelian (Computer Book Author) for $3,376.50, and Steve Stance (Driver) for $827.10.”](images/9781394289240-fg1006.png)

[FIGURE 10-6:](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fig-0006) Running the code of [Listing 10-7](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0007).

[LISTING 10-7](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fea-0007) Paying Three Kinds of Employees

`void main() {`
`var ftEmployee = new FullTimeEmployee();`

`ftEmployee.setName("Barry Burd");`
`ftEmployee.setJobTitle("CEO");`
`ftEmployee.setWeeklySalary(5000.00);`
`ftEmployee.setBenefitDeduction(500.00);`
**`ftEmployee.cutCheck(ftEmployee.findPaymentAmount());`**

`var ptEmployee = new PartTimeEmployee();`

`ptEmployee.setName("Chris Apelian");`
`ptEmployee.setJobTitle("Computer Book Author");`
`ptEmployee.setHourlyRate(7.53);`
**`ptEmployee.cutCheck(ptEmployee.findPaymentAmount(50));`**

`var ptoEmployee = new PartTimeWithOver();`

`ptoEmployee.setName("Steve Surace");`
`ptoEmployee.setJobTitle("Driver");`
`ptoEmployee.setHourlyRate(7.53);`
**`ptoEmployee.cutCheck(ptoEmployee.findPaymentAmount(55));`**
`}`

The code in [Listing 10-7](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0007) writes checks to three employees. The first employee is a full-timer. The second is a part-time employee who hasn't yet gotten wind of the overtime payment scheme. The third employee knows about the overtime payment scheme and demands a fair wage.

With the subclasses, all three of these employees coexist in [Listing 10-7](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0007). Sure, one subclass extends the old `PartTimeEmployee` class, but that doesn't mean you can’t create an object from the `PartTimeEmployee` class. In fact, Java is smart about this. [Listing 10-7](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0007) has three calls to the `findPaymentAmount` method, and each call reaches out to a different version of the method:

*   In the first call, `ftEmployee.findPaymentAmount`, the `ftEmployee` variable is an instance of the `FullTimeEmployee` class. So the method that's called is the one in [Listing 10-2](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0002).
*   In the second call, `ptEmployee.findPaymentAmount`, the `ptEmployee` variable is an instance of the `PartTimeEmployee` class. So the method that's called is the one in [Listing 10-4](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0004).
*   In the third call, `ptoEmployee.findPaymentAmount`, the `ptoEmployee` variable is an instance of the `PartTimeWithOver` class. So the method that's called is the one in [Listing 10-6](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0006).

This code is fantastic. Using subclasses makes the code clean and elegant. With all the money that you save on software, you can afford to pay everyone double for overtime hours. (Whether you do that or keep the money for yourself is another story.)

#### VIRTUAL METHODS

Create a project with four Java source files. Each file defines a particular Java class:

`void main() {`
`MyThing myThing, myThing2;`

`myThing = new MySubThing();`
`myThing2 = new MyOtherThing();`

`myThing.value = 7;`
`myThing2.value = 44;`
`myThing.display();`
`myThing2.display();`
`}`

`public class MyThing {`

`}`
`}`

`public void display() {`

`}`
`}`

`public class MyOtherThing extends MyThing {`

What output do you see when you run the `Main.java` file's code? What does this output tell you about variable declarations and method calling in Java?

To find the number of hours between two `LocalTime` objects (such as `twoFifteen` and `currentTime`), execute

To find the number of minutes between two `LocalTime` objects (such as `twoFifteen` and `currentTime`), execute

## Yet Another switch

From one version of Java to the next, the `switch` structure becomes more and more versatile. With Java 23, a `switch` does almost anything you want it to do. The folks at Wiley don't know it yet, but someday, they'll have to publish a book titled *Java's switch Statements and Expressions For Dummies*.

[Listing 10-8](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0008) demonstrates the `switch` structure's *pattern-matching* feature.

[LISTING 10-8](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fea-0008) What Will They Think of Next?

`void main() {`
`var ptEmployee = new PartTimeEmployee();`
`ptEmployee.setName("Chris Apelian");`
`ptEmployee.setJobTitle("Computer Book Author");`
`ptEmployee.setHourlyRate(7.53);`
`payEmployee(ptEmployee);`

`var ptoEmployee = new PartTimeWithOver();`
`ptoEmployee.setName("Steve Surace");`
`ptoEmployee.setJobTitle("Driver");`
`ptoEmployee.setHourlyRate(7.53);`
`payEmployee(ptoEmployee);`

`var employee = new Employee();`
`payEmployee(employee);`
`}`

`void payEmployee(Employee employee) {`
`employee.cutCheck(`**`switch (employee) {`**
**`case FullTimeEmployee fte -> fte.findPaymentAmount();`**
**`case PartTimeWithOver pto -> pto.findPaymentAmount(55);`**
**`case PartTimeEmployee pte -> pte.findPaymentAmount(50);`**
**`case Employee e -> 0.00;`**
**`}`**`);`
`}`

When you run the code in [Listing 10-8](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0008), you get the output shown in [Figure 10-7](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0007). In [Listing 10-8](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0008), the `employee` object has no name and no job title. So, on the last line in [Figure 10-7](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0007), that person's name and title are both `null`. In Java, `null` means “no value.”

![“The image shows a list of payments to different individuals. The first payment is to Chris Apelian (Computer Book Author) for $376.50\. The second payment is to Steve Surace (Driver) for $277.10\. The third payment is to null (null) for $2.00.”](images/9781394289240-fg1007.png)

[FIGURE 10-7:](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fig-0007) The `switch` statement does what it's supposed to do.

The main method in [Listing 10-8](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0008) calls `payEmployee` three times. Each time, the `payEmployee` method dives into a `switch` expression. Like the `switch` structures in earlier examples, this `switch` divides into cases. But unlike those earlier `switch` structures, this expression's cases are the names of classes. The `switch` expression asks, “What subclass does the `employee` parameter belong to?” Depending on the answer, the `switch` expression calls one of the subclasses' `findPaymentAmount` methods (or gives up and returns `0.00`).

If you mess with the cases in [Listing 10-8](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0008), you may run into trouble. For example, try changing the order of the middle two cases, like this:

**`// Bad code with cases reversed!`**
`case` **`PartTimeEmployee`** `pte -> pte.findPaymentAmount(50);`
`case` **`PartTimeWithOver`** `pto -> pto.findPaymentAmount(55);`

Java absolutely despises this code, and lets you know by displaying a harsh message. “Label is dominated by a preceding case label,” says Java. Why does Java say this? I'm glad you asked.

Think about the `ptoEmployee` object in [Listing 10-8](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0008). It's an instance of the `PartTimeWithOver` class. But the `PartTimeWithOver` class is a subclass of `PartTimeEmployee`. So `ptoEmployee` is also an instance of the `PartTimeEmployee` class. In fact, `ptoEmployee` is an instance of all three classes — `PartTimeWithOver`, `PartTimeEmployee`, and `Employee`. Java's `switch` expression looks for a match from the topmost case downward. (See [Figure 10-8](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0008).) So, in the Cases Reversed code, the `ptoEmployee` object matches the `PartTimeEmployee` case and never reaches the `PartTimeWithOver` case. In fact, nothing ever reaches the `PartTimeWithOver` case. The `PartTimeWithOver` case is useless, so Java refuses to accept the code.

Here's another thing you can do to ruin the case expression in [Listing 10-8](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0008). Try removing the last `Employee e` case. Java's `switch` expression must deliver a value no matter what kind of `Employee` instance you feed to it. But the `employee` object near the end of the listing's main method matches none of the first three case clauses. So, without the `Employee e` case, Java makes an unpleasant fuss.

The cases in a `switch` expression of this kind must cover all possibilities. Even if you remove the last two statements in the `main` method in [Listing 10-8](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0008), Java insists on having a `case` clause that can catch a `new Employee()` object.

![“A flowchart diagram illustrates a switch statement in a programming context, specifically dealing with different types of employees. The switch statement checks the type of an employee object. The first case checks if the employee is a FullTimeEmployee and does not match. The second case checks if the employee is a PartTimeEmployee and matches, executing the findPaymentAmount(50) method. The third case, which checks for PartTimeWithOver, is marked as unreachable. The diagram highlights that the second case catches the object of type PartTimeWithOver.”](images/9781394289240-fg1008.png)

[FIGURE 10-8:](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fig-0008) Nothing good happens.

![Tip](images/tip.png) Rather than list every possibility, you can get lazy and end a `switch` expression with a `default` clause. Just don't put both the `Employee` case and a `default` clause in [Listing 10-8](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0008). Java would rebel against that, too.

## Doing Something about the Weather

Here's some feedback I got from a reader:

*   Hi Barry,
*   In [Chapter 8](#c08.xhtml), the way you display a temperature bothers me. Instead of displaying `70.00° FAHRENHEIT`, you should display `70 °F`. What gives with your crazy temperature notation?
*   All the best,
*   Persnickety Percy

And I replied thusly:

*   Hi Persnickety,
*   To display temperatures your way, create a subclass of my `Temperature` class. You can find the code in [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009).
*   Regards,
*   Barry

[LISTING 10-9](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fea-0009) Persnickety Percy Gets His Way

`public class TemperatureForPercy extends Temperature {`

`public TemperatureForPercy() {`
**`super();`** `// This super call is optional. (See this`
`// chapter's "Constructor madness" section.)`
`}`

`public TemperatureForPercy(double number) {`
**`super(number);`**
`}`

`public TemperatureForPercy(TempScale scale) {`
**`super(scale);`**
`}`

`public TemperatureForPercy(double number, TempScale scale) {`
**`super(number, scale);`**
`}`

`@Override`
`public String toString() {`
`return String.format("%2.0f \u00B0", getNumber()) +`
`switch (getScale()) {`
`case CELSIUS -> "C";`
`case FAHRENHEIT -> "F";`
`case KELVIN -> "K";`
`case RANKINE -> "R";`

`};`
`}`

`public double toCelsius() {`
`return switch (getScale()) {`
`case CELSIUS -> getNumber();`
`case FAHRENHEIT -> (getNumber()- 32) * 5.0 / 9.0;`
`case KELVIN -> getNumber()- 273.15;`
`case RANKINE -> (getNumber()- 491.67) * 5.0 / 9.0;`
`};`
`}`
`}`

In the `toString` and `toCelsius` methods of [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009), notice the calls to the `Temperature` class's `getNumber` and `getScale` methods. Why do I do this? Well, inside the `TemperatureForPercy` class's code, any direct references to the `number` and `scale` fields would generate error messages. It's true that every `TemperatureForPercy` instance has its own `number` and `scale` fields. (After all, `TemperatureForPercy` is a subclass of the `Temperature` class, and the code for the `Temperature` class defines the `number` and `scale` fields.) But because `number and scale` are declared to be private inside the `Temperature` class, only code that's right inside the `Temperature` class can directly use these fields.

![Warning](images/warning.png) Don't put additional declarations of the `number` and `scale` fields inside the `TemperatureForPercy` class's code. If you do, you inadvertently create four different variables (two called `number` and another two called `scale`). You'll assign values to one pair of variables. Then you'll be shocked that when you display the other pair of variables, those values seem to have disappeared.

![Remember](images/remember.png) When an object’s code contains a call to one of the object’s own methods, you don’t need to preface the call with a dot. For instance, in the `toString` method of [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009), the object calls its own methods with `getNumber()` and `getScale()`, not with `someObject.getNumber()` and `somethingOrOther.getScale()`. If going dotless makes you queasy, you can compensate by taking advantage of yet another use for the `this` keyword: Just write `this.getNumber()` and `this.getScale()` in the `toString` method in [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009).

### Constructors for subclasses

By far, the biggest news in [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009) is the way the code declares constructors. The `TemperatureForPercy` class has four of its own constructors. If you've gotten in gear thinking about subclass inheritance, you may wonder why these constructor declarations are necessary. Doesn't `TemperatureForPercy` inherit the parent `Temperature` class's constructors? No, subclasses don't inherit constructors.

![Remember](images/remember.png) Subclasses don’t inherit constructors.

That’s right. Subclasses don’t inherit constructors. The default constructors in [Chapter 8](#c08.xhtml) may look as if they're inherited, but they're not. In general, when you define a subclass, you declare new constructors to go with the subclass.

So the code in [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009) has four constructors. Each constructor has the name `TemperatureForPercy`, and each constructor has its own uniquely identifiable parameter list. That’s the boring part. The interesting part is that each constructor makes a call to something named `super`, which is a Java keyword.

In [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009), `super` stands for a constructor in the parent class:

*   The statement `super()` in [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009) calls the parameterless `Temperature()` constructor that's in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002). (Yes, you'll need to flip back to [Chapter 8](#c08.xhtml) to find it.) That parameterless constructor assigns `0.0` to the `number` field and `TempScale.FAHRENHEIT` to the `scale` field.
*   The statement `super(number, scale)` in [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009) calls the constructor `Temperature(double number, TempScale scale)` that's in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002). In turn, the constructor assigns values to the `number` and `scale` fields.
*   In a similar way, the statements `super(number)` and `super(scale)` in [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009) call constructors from [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002).

The computer decides which of the `Temperature` class's constructors is being called by looking at the parameter list after the word `super`. For instance, when the computer executes

`super(number, scale);`

from [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009), the computer says to itself, “The `number` and `scale` fields in parentheses have types `double` and `TempScale`. And only one of the `Temperature` constructors in [Listing 8-2](#c08.xhtml_c08-fea-0002) has two parameters with types `double` and `TempScale`. The constructor's header looks like this:

`public Temperature(double number, TempScale scale)`

“So I guess I'll execute the statements inside that particular constructor.”

### Using all this stuff

In [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009), I define what it means to be in the `TemperatureForPercy` class. Now it’s time to put this `TemperatureForPercy` class to good use. [Listing 10-10](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0010) has code that uses `TemperatureForPercy`.

[LISTING 10-10](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fea-0010) Using Percy's Class

`void main() {`
`println(new TemperatureForPercy(2.73, TempScale.KELVIN));`
`println(new TemperatureForPercy(TempScale.CELSIUS));`
`println(new TemperatureForPercy(32.0));`

`var temperature = new TemperatureForPercy();`
`temperature.setNumber(70);`
`println(temperature);`
`}`

[Listing 10-10](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0010) creates instances of the `TemperatureForPercy` class, so the listing's `println` calls access the `toString` method in the `TemperatureForPercy` class, not the parent `Temperature` class.

[Figure 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0009) shows a run of the code in [Listing 10-10](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0010).

![“A table with four rows and two columns. The left column contains the numbers 3, 6, 32, and 70\. The right column contains the letters K, C, Z, and Z.”](images/9781394289240-fg1009.png)

[FIGURE 10-9:](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fig-0009) At last! Percy is happy.

Read the comment after the first `super` call in [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009). Try removing that `super` call and notice that you still get the output shown in [Figure 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0009). What does this tell you? It tells you that, under certain circumstances, Java adds a parameterless `super()` call to your constructor.

And what are those “certain circumstances”? The next section reveals the answer.

#### Constructor madness

The big message in the previous section is that subclasses don't inherit constructors. When you combine this with some other rules concerning constructors, you get some surprising outcomes. Here are some facts about constructors:

1.  A subclass doesn't inherit any constructors from its parent class.
2.  When you define a class, and you don't explicitly type the code for any constructors, Java generates a parameterless default constructor for you.

    This rule goes both ways. If you explicitly type the code for a constructor, Java *doesn't* generate a default constructor for you.

3.  When a class contains a constructor, and that constructor doesn't contain a statement of this kind:

    `super(`*`some parameters or no parameters`*`);`

    then Java generates a parameterless `super()` call for you. In other words, Java calls the parent class's parameterless constructor on your behalf.

To see these rules in action, consider the code in [Listings 10-11](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0011) through [10-13](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0013).

[LISTING 10-11](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fea-0011) Getting Help in the Kitchen

`public class KitchenItem {`
`public String purpose;`

`public KitchenItem() {`
`System.out.println("""`
`You've called the parameterless`
`KitchenItem constructor.""");`
`}`
`}`

[LISTING 10-12](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fea-0012) Don't Eat with Your Hands

`enum Material {METAL, PLASTIC, WOOD}`

`public class Utensil extends KitchenItem {`
`public Material material;`

`public Utensil(String purpose, Material material) {`
`this.purpose = purpose;`
`this.material = material;`
`}`

**`// Some code is missing.`**
`}`

[LISTING 10-13](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fea-0013) You May Have to Google the Word *Tine*

`public class Fork extends Utensil {`
`public int numberOfTines;`
`}`

If you type all this code, [Listing 10-13](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0013) gives you an error message such as, `There is no default constructor available in 'Utensil'`. Why is Java complaining to you? Have you done something wrong? [Figure 10-10](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0010) illustrates the situation.

![“A Java code snippet demonstrating class inheritance and constructor behavior. It includes three classes: Utensil, Fork, and KitchenItem. The Utensil class extends KitchenItem and has a two-parameter constructor. The Fork class extends Utensil and includes a default constructor calling the parent constructor using super(). Comments explain the absence of a default constructor in Utensil and the use of super().”](images/9781394289240-fg1010.png)

[FIGURE 10-10:](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fig-0010) Implicitly generated code makes trouble for you.

With Rules 2 and 3, an implicit default constructor inside the `Fork` class tries to call a parameterless `Utensil` constructor. But Rule 2 tells Java not to create a parameterless `Utensil` constructor. So the `super()` in the `Fork` class's default constructor has nothing to call. Java recognizes this difficulty and insists that you repair your code.

You can fix this by adding an explicit parameterless constructor to the `Utensil` class in [Listing 10-12](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0012):

`public class Utensil extends KitchenItem {`
`public Material material;`

`public Utensil(String purpose, Material material) {`
`this.purpose = purpose;`
`this.material = material;`
`}`

**`public Utensil() {`**
**`}`**
`}`

[Figure 10-11](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0011) shows you what happens when you add this constructor.

![“A Java class diagram demonstrating inheritance and constructor chaining. The diagram includes three classes: KitchenItem, Utensil, and Fork. KitchenItem has a constructor that prints a message. Utensil extends KitchenItem and has its own constructor calling super(). Fork extends Utensil and also calls the parent constructor using super(). Annotations explain the flow of constructor calls in the hierarchy, with a main method creating an instance of Fork to demonstrate the sequence.”](images/9781394289240-fg1011.png)

[FIGURE 10-11:](#c10.xhtml_rc10-fig-0011) One constructor call after another.

In [Figure 10-11](#c10.xhtml_c10-fig-0011), a call to the `Fork` class's default constructor sets off a chain of calls that ends with a call to the `KitchenItem` constructor. Normally, you don't put `println` calls of any kind inside a constructor declaration. But in this example, the `println` I've added helps to convince you that Java obeys Rules 2 and 3.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Here's a neat little program for you to write:

#### A CONVERSION DIVERSION

You may have noticed that the `TemperatureForPercy` class in [Listing 10-9](#c10.xhtml_c10-fea-0009) has a `toCelsius` method. Create a subclass of `TemperatureForPercy` named `TemperatureCelsius`. The `TemperatureCelsius` class has only three constructors. When you run this main method:

`void main() {`
`println(new TemperatureCelsius());`

`println(new TemperatureCelsius(100.00));`

`var temp = new TemperatureForPercy(70.0, TempScale.FAHRENHEIT);`
`println(new TemperatureCelsius(temp));`
`}`

the output is

`0 °C`
`100 °C`
`21 °C`

Part 4

# Smart Java Techniques

IN THIS PART …

*   Decide where declarations belong in your Java program.
*   Deal with bunches of things (bunches of rooms, bunches of sales, and even bunches of bunches).
*   Keep cool even when something goes wrong with your program.
*   Fully embrace Java’s object-oriented features.
*   Create a windowed app and respond to mouse-clicks.

Chapter 11

# Putting Variables and Methods Where They Belong

IN THIS CHAPTER

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Making something belong to an entire class**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Putting variables inside and outside methods**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Improving your batting average**

Our family's neighborhood elementary school has a yearly tradition: Each spring, the students in fifth grade take a daylong field trip to Philadelphia. When my son was in fifth grade, I went along on the trip to help supervise 4 of the 20 children. While the 4 kids and I were touring the city's historical sites, I misplaced the preplanned itinerary. I didn't know where we were supposed to go next. So, what could I do? I did what any good Philadelphia native would do — I ignored the itinerary and took the kids to the nearest cheap restaurant. I treated them all to Philadelphia's signature dish — the fat-laden, greasy, Philadelphia cheesesteak.

What could this story possibly have to do with Java? That's simple. This chapter is about access — how one class's code may gain access to another class's code. My Philadelphia anecdote is about access, too. Shortly after the school trip, my son's fifth-grade teacher got wind of my shenanigans during the tour. Later, when my daughter was in fifth grade, the teacher said that I wasn’t welcome to take part in that year's junket to Philadelphia. The teacher had revoked my access to those Philadelphia cheesesteaks.

## Making a Package

Today, our local baseball team plays against the neighboring town's team. Our team is the Hankies; the other town's team is the Socks. As the team's official recordkeeper, I've created code to keep track of the players' names and batting averages. (See [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001).)

[LISTING 11-1](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fea-0001) The Player Record Class

`package com.example.baseball;`

`public record Player(String name, double average) {`
`}`

Wow! That's a tiny piece of code! (If you have doubts about what's going on in [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001), refer to the section on record classes in [Chapter 8](#c08.xhtml).) To sum it up, each `Player` instance has a `name` field and an `average` field. There's only one way to create a `Player` instance, and that's by calling `new Player(`*`a_name`*`,` *`an_average`*`)`. Once you've created a player, you can't change that player's name or average. To get a particular player's name and average, you call the record class's `name()` and `average()` methods. That's all there is to it.

For comparison, the `Player` class in previous editions of *Java For Dummies* looked something like this:

`public class Player {`
`String name;`
`double average;`

`public Player(String name, double average) {`
`this.name = name;`
`this.average = average;`
`}`
`}`

This older code did mostly what the code in [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001) does.

### Package deal

The `Player` class in [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001) belongs to a package that I named `com.example.baseball`. The first line in [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001) makes it so. That first line is called a *package declaration*. That line says, “This file is one of possibly many files that are grouped inside a package named `com.example.baseball`.”

In Java, every class belongs to a package. Imagine a file named `Hello.java` containing only this text:

`void main() {`
`println("Hello");`
`}`

Because of the file's name, this `main` method belongs to an implicit class named `Hello`. And that `Hello` class belongs to an implicit package. Java developers call it an *unnamed package*.

None of the listings in previous chapters starts with a package declaration, so each of those listings belongs to an unnamed package. But professional Java programmers avoid using unnamed packages. So, in [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001), I decided to straighten up and start flying right.

The Java API defines thousands of classes, each belonging to one of several hundred packages. When you write `import java.util.Scanner`, you're referring to a class named `Scanner`, which belongs to the package `java.util`.

In the Java world, programmers customarily give packages long, dot-filled names. For example, I've registered the domain name [`allmycode.com`](http://allmycode.com), so I may decide to name a package `com.allmycode.dummiesframe`. In truth, I can name a package almost anything I want — like `com.example.baseball`, `fish.and.chips`, or simply `hamburger`. In any case, informative package names like `com.allmycode.dummiesframe` are better than silly names like `midnight.in.akron` or `do.not.run.this.code`.

![Tip](images/tip.png) In this book, I start most of my package names with `com.example`. The Internet Assigned Numbers Authority has set aside the [`example.com`](http://example.com) domain for anyone to use in writing documentation, illustrating concepts, describing ideas, and so on.

How many classes can you define? The `Player` class in [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001) is one of them. You can also create a `Game` class, a `Season` class, and classes named `Inning`, `Bat`, `Ball`, `Strike`, `HomeRun`, `Foul`, `Base`, `Pitcher`, `Catcher`, `Umpire`, `Manager`, `Infielder`, `Outfielder`, `DoublePlay`, and many more. Of course, you may want to separate these classes from your other classes named `Song`, `Novel`, `Film`, `Painting`, and `Video`. So you might create two different packages: one named `com.example.baseball` and another named `com.example.arts`.

You can start any Java file with a package declaration, but you have to remember a few things:

*   **A Java file's package name dictates the names of some folders and subfolders.**

    The code in [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001) begins with `package com.example.baseball`. So your hard drive must have a folder named `com`, which has a subfolder named `example`, which in turn has its own subfolder named `baseball`. Your `Player.java` file must be inside this `baseball` folder. For example, on my computer, I have a project whose root folder is named `11-01`. My copy of [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001) is inside the `11-01/src/com/example/baseball` folder.

*   **Many IDEs have to be told explicitly to create the needed folders.**

    Before you copy the code from [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001) into your IDE, you probably have to tell the IDE to create a package named `com.example.baseball`. When you do, your IDE will create the `com/example/baseball` folders for you. Then you can copy the `Player.java` file into the `com/example/baseball` folder.

    How do you tell your IDE to create a package? When you start a new project, your IDE might prompt you for a new package name. The IDE creates the package as part of the new project. When you work on an existing project, creating a package might mean fishing around for New ⇒   Package among your IDE's menus. If you can't find a way to do that, look for some hints on this book's website ([`http://javafordummies.allmycode.com`](http://javafordummies.allmycode.com/)).

After all this fuss about packages, you may wonder what difference it makes that a class is in one package or another. For some insight, see this chapter's later section “[It's All About Access](#c11.xhtml_c11-sec-0014).”

### Using the Player class

[Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002) has code that uses the `Player` class — the class that's defined earlier, in [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001).

[LISTING 11-2](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fea-0002) Using the Player Class

`import com.example.baseball.Player;`

`import java.io.*;`
`import java.util.Scanner;`

`public class ShowPlayers {`

`public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {`
`var hankiesData = new Scanner(new File("Hankies.txt"));`

`IO.print("Player");`
`IO.println(String.format("%45s", "Batting Average"));`
`IO.print("------");`
`IO.println(String.format("%45s", "---------------"));`

`for (int num = 1; num <= 9; num++) {`
`var player = new Player(hankiesData.nextLine(),`
`hankiesData.nextDouble());`
`hankiesData.nextLine();`
`IO.print(String.format("%-40s", player.name()));`
`IO.println(String.format("%.3f", player.average()).substring(1));`
`}`

`hankiesData.close();`
`}`
`}`

In a typical Java application, the application's classes belong to several different packages. And the code in those packages depends on code from other organizations' packages. Examples like the ones in this book, with only one or two packages, are rare indeed.

For my baseball example, I put the `Player` class in a package that I name `com.example.baseball` and put the `ShowPlayers` class in Java's ever-present unnamed package. Since the `ShowPlayers` class isn't in the same package as my `Player` class, I start [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002) with an import declaration. Without the import declaration at the very top of [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002), I'd have to write

`var player = new` **`com.example.baseball.Player`**`(hankiesData.nextLine(),`
`hankiesData.nextDouble());`

inside the listing's `for` loop.

In [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002), notice how `import java.io.*;` ends with an asterisk. The asterisk means “import every class that's in the `java.io` package.” [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002) abbreviates three of those classes: `IOException`, `File`, and `IO`. But, with an asterisk in the import declaration, the listing could abbreviate `BufferedReader`, `FileInputStream`, or any other classes in the `java.io` package.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) A Java package can have one or more *subpackages*. For example, the `javax.swing.text` package is for displaying text of any kind in a graphical window, and the `javax.swing.text.html` subpackage is for displaying HTML text in particular. But don't let the name *subpackage* fool you. The relationship between a package and its subpackage is flimsy. If you declare `import javax.swing.text.*`, you can abbreviate the names of classes in the `javax.swing.text` package — classes like `Caret`, `Highlighter`, `Position`, and `Style`. But you can't abbreviate the names of classes in the `javax.swing.text.html` package. For that, you declare `import javax.swing.text.html.*`.

### Running the code

To run this program, you need a file containing data on your favorite baseball players. Fortunately, the stuff you download from this book's website comes with a `Hankies.txt` file. (See [Figure 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0001).)

![“The image shows a diagram of class inheritance in object-oriented programming. There are four classes depicted: 'class B extends A', 'class M extends L' with a 'protected field', 'class Y extends X', and 'class N extends M'. This diagram illustrates the relationships between these classes, highlighting inheritance and access modifiers.”](images/9781394289240-fg1101.png)

[FIGURE 11-1:](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fig-0001) My `Hankies.txt` file.

For a run of the code in [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002), see [Figure 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0002).

![“A table listing baseball players and their batting averages. The players are Barry Burd (.101), Marrie Ritter (.200), Weelie J. Katz (.030), Harry 'The Crazyman' Spoonwagler (.124), Pilecia 'Pishy' Katz (.175), Mia, Just 'Mia' (.111), Jeremy Plooflong Jones (.102), I. M. D'Arthor (.001), and Hugh R. DaReader (.212).”](images/9781394289240-fg1102.png)

[FIGURE 11-2:](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fig-0002) What a team!

The program's `Hankies.txt` file can't be in just any old folder on your computer's hard drive. The file has to be in a place where Java can find it, which is most likely your project's root folder. If Java can't find the file, then a run of this section's example gives you an unpleasant `FileNotFoundException` message. For advice on choosing locations for files like `Hankies.txt`, refer to [Chapters 6](#c06.xhtml) and [9](#c09.xhtml).

![Warning](images/warning.png) You may live in a country where the value of π is approximately 3,14159 (with a comma) instead of 3.14159 (with a period). If you do, the file shown in [Figure 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0001) won't work for you. The program will crash with an `InputMismatchException`. To run this section's example, you have to change the periods in the `Hankies.txt` file into commas. Alternatively, you can add a statement such as `Locale.setDefault(Locale.US)` to your code. For details, see [Chapter 9](#c09.xhtml).

![Remember](images/remember.png) For this section's code to work correctly, you must have a line break after the last `.212` in [Figure 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0001). For details about line breaks, see [Chapter 9](#c09.xhtml).

### One class; nine objects

The code in [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002) calls the `Player` constructor nine times. The result is that the code creates nine instances of the `Player` class. The first time through the loop, the code creates an instance with the name `Barry Burd`. The second time through the loop, the code abandons the `Barry Burd` instance and creates another instance with name `Harriet Ritter`. The third time through, the code abandons poor `Harriet Ritter` and creates an instance for `Weelie J. Katz`. The code has only one instance at a time but, all in all, the code creates nine instances.

Each `Player` instance has its own `name` and `average` fields. Each instance also has its own `Player` constructor and, since it's a record class, its own `name()` and `average()` methods. Look at [Figure 11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0003) and think of the `Player` class with its nine incarnations.

![“A diagram showing the Player class with instances of players. Each instance includes a player's name, ID, contractor name, and average. The players listed are Danny (.173), Harriet (.260), Maxilla (.300), Harry (.154), Flashy (.275), Mike (.240), Conway (.192), E. M. (.401), and Hope (.212).”](images/9781394289240-fg1103.png)

[FIGURE 11-3:](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fig-0003) A class and its objects.

### Some notes on formatting

[Figure 11-4](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0004) illustrates the work of the first two printing statements in [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002). In `"%45s"`, the letter `s` says, “Expect the next parameter to be a string.” And the next parameter, `"Batting Average"`, is indeed a string. In that same code, the number `45` says “Use 45 characters to display the string.” So Java pads the 15-character `"Batting Average"` string with 30 blank spaces.

![“A diagram illustrating the formatting of a printed line in Java. The line 'IO.print('Player')' prints the word 'Player'. The line 'IO.println(String.format('%45s', 'Batting Average'))' prints the phrase 'Batting Average' after 30 blank spaces, making the total length 45 characters. The diagram visually represents the spacing and alignment of the text output.”](images/9781394289240-fg1104.png)

[FIGURE 11-4:](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fig-0004) Printing a header row.

[Figure 11-5](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0005) shows how a `print` call in [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002) formats my favorite player's name.
