In `"%-40s"`, the `40s` says, “Use 40 characters to display the second parameter, which is a string.” The minus sign calls for left-justification, so the name is to the left of the 30-character padding.

![“The image illustrates a Java code snippet for left-justifying a name within a formatted string. The name 'Barry Burd' is shown, followed by 30 blank spaces, and then the number '101'. The code snippet used is IO.print(String.format(“%-40s”, player.name()));, which formats the name to be left-justified within a 40-character wide field.”](images/9781394289240-fg1105.png)

[FIGURE 11-5:](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fig-0005) Printing a player's name.

[Figure 11-6](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0006) describes the formatting of a player's batting average. In `"%.3f"`, the `.3f` part asks for a number with three digits to the right of the decimal point. For numbers less than 1, Java puts a digit `0` on the left side of the decimal point. That's usually good. (My high-school teacher told me to do that so that the decimal point would be harder to miss.)

But the convention for displaying batting averages is to not have this leading `0`. So, to get rid of the leading `0`, I call Java's `substring` method. In particular,

*   The value of `"0.101".substring(`**`0`**`)` is `"0.101"`,
*   The value of `"0.101".substring(`**`1`**`)` is `".101"`,
*   The value of “0.101”.substring(**2**) is “101”,

… and so on.

![“The image illustrates a sequence diagram for a Java program. It starts with the name 'Harry Burd' followed by 80 blank spaces leading to a box labeled 'v_101'. Below the diagram is a line of code: IO.println(String.format('%.3f', Player.average().substring(1)));. This demonstrates formatting a string to three decimal places.”](images/9781394289240-fg1106.png)

[FIGURE 11-6:](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fig-0006) Printing a player's awful batting average.

![Tip](images/tip.png) Calling `String.format` isn't the only way to format numbers in Java. Other ways include the `System.out.printf` method, the `DecimalFormat` class, and the `NumberFormat` class's `getCurrencyInstance` method. For more information, see [Chapter 12](#c12.xhtml).

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Would you like some practice with the material in this section? If so, try this:

#### COURTING DISASTER

The code in [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002) reads from a file named `Hankies.txt`. Delete that `Hankies.txt` file from your computer's hard drive, or temporarily move the file to a different directory. Then try to run the program in [Listings 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001) and [11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002). What horrible things happen when you do this?

#### MEN'S CLOTHING

A line of men's clothing features shirts, pants, jackets, overcoats, neckties, and shoes. Create an `enum` to represent the six kinds of items. Then create a `MensClothingItem` class. Each instance of the class has a kind (one of the six `enum` values) and a name (such as *Casual Summer Design #7*).

Write code to display a table with six rows that describe one complete men's wardrobe.

#### DEAL ME IN

Create an `enum` to represent the suits in a deck of playing cards (`CLUBS`, `DIAMONDS`, `HEARTS`, and `SPADES`). Create a `PlayingCard` class. Each playing card has a number (from 1 to 13) and a suit. In the numbering scheme, 11 stands for a Jack, 12 stands for a Queen, and 13 stands for a King. Write code that creates several cards and displays them on the screen.

## It's All About Access

Many examples in this book throw around the words `public` and `private` as if they're no big deal at all. That's okay because the English-language meanings of the words describe, more or less, what they mean in a Java program. But it's nice to be precise. So this section zooms in on the words `public` and `private`. These words are two of Java's *access modifiers*. The word `protected` is another Java access modifier. And, not using any word (in a place where you can use `public`, `private`, or `protected`) is also a kind of access modifier.

For starters, what does *access* mean? Imagine that you have two fields, two methods, a class with a separate method, or two things of any kind. Does the name of the first thing mean anything to the second thing? Here are two pieces of code:

`class First {`
`int number = 0;`
`}`

`class Second {`
`void main() {`
**`var first = new First();`**
**`first.number = 99;`**
`}`
`}`

Is this code okay? Can the `Second` class's main method refer to the `First` class and to its `number` field? If so, the `Second` class has access to these things.

An *access modifier* helps to determine what kind of access is available. To describe Java's access modifiers, I start with the big picture — modules.

### Access for Java modules

There's an old poem that goes something like this:

`As I was going to high school,`
`I ran some code with seven modules,`
`Each module had seven packages,`
`Each package had seven classes,`
`Each class had seven members:`
`Members, classes, packages, and modules,`
`How many were there going to high school?`

A *module* is a group of packages. Each module is a world unto itself. If you have two modules and you don't do anything to connect the two modules, then none of the code in either module can make reference to code in the other module.

Imagine that you have two modules: module1 and module2\. If you want some code in module2 to make reference to any of the code in module1, then module1 must explicitly *export* the code, and module2 must explicitly *require* the code. You must create two files, both named `module-info.java`, like so:

`// One module-info.java file:`
`module module1 {`
`exports` *`some_package_in_module1`*`;`
`}`

`// Another module-info.java file:`
`module module2 {`
`requires` *`some_package_in_module1`*`;`
`}`

The details can be complicated. But that's okay because most Java programmers never have to create modules. In other words, you're off the hook.

![On the web](images/ontheweb.png) For more information about modules, visit [`https://openjdk.org/projects/jigsaw/quick-start`](https://openjdk.org/projects/jigsaw/quick-start).

From this point on in the chapter, wherever you read about “two packages” or “two classes,” imagine that they're both in the same module.

### Access modifiers for classes

In Java, the `public` keyword has two slightly different meanings: one meaning for the members of a class and another meaning for the classes themselves. This section deals with the classes themselves.

A class can be either public or nonpublic. If you see something like

`public class Drawing`

you're looking at the declaration of a public class. But if you see plain old

`class OnlyMyDrawing`

the class that's being declared isn’t public.

#### Public classes

If a class is public, you can refer to the class from anywhere in your code. Of course, some restrictions apply. You must obey all the rules in this chapter’s earlier sections “[Package deal](#c11.xhtml_c11-sec-0004)” and “Access for Java modules.” You must also refer to a packaged class properly. For example, in [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002), you write

`import com.example.baseball.Player;`

`var player = new Player(hankiesData.nextLine(),`
`hankiesData.nextDouble());`

Alternatively, you can do without the import declaration and write

`var player = new com.example.baseball.Player(hankiesData.nextLine(),`
`hankiesData.nextDouble());`

One way or another, your code must acknowledge that the `Player` class isn't in the same package as the code in [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002).

#### Nonpublic classes

If a class isn't public, you can refer to the class only from code within the class’s package.

I tested this rule. I went back to [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001) and deleted the word *public.* At that point, [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002) went poof! To be more precise, my IDE displayed these messages for [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002):

`'com.example.baseball.Player' is not public in 'com.example.baseball'.`
`Cannot be accessed from outside package.`
`Cannot access 'name()' in 'com.example.baseball.Player.`
`Cannot access 'average()' in 'com.example.baseball.Player."`

I guess I got what was coming to me.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) Things are never as simple as they seem. The rules I describe in this section apply to almost every class in this book. But Java has fancy things called *sealed classes* and *inner classes,* and these classes follow a different set of rules. Fortunately, a typical novice programmer has little contact with sealed or inner classes. So, for now, you can live quite happily with the rules that I describe in this section.

Enough about access rules for classes! It's time to explore other avenues.

### A word or two about method-local variables

Consider this chunk of Java code:

`public class MyClass {`
`int myField; // This is a` **`field`** `(a member of MyClass).`
**`//`** `myVariable++; //` **`Illegal. No access.`**

`void myMethod() { // This is a` **`method`** `(another member of MyClass).`
`int myVariable; // This is a` **`method-local variable`** `(NOT a member).`
`}`
`}`

The comments on the right side of the code tell the whole story. This class has three kinds of members: a field, a method, and a method-local variable. There's not much to say about method-local variables. A method-local variable can't be accessed from any code outside of the method. Period. If you want more details, refer to [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml).

### Access for members (fields and methods)

Imagine for a moment that you're a member of a class. In particular, you're a field named `identificationNumber`. From somewhere in the code, you hear the words “Hey, `identificationNumber`! What's your current value?” Your first response should be, “Who wants to know?” Depending on who's asking for the `identificationNumber`, you may or may not be willing to answer.

#### Members in the same class

Two members in the same class have free and easy access to one another. For example, the following code is right and proper:

`class MyClass {`
`private int x = 7;`
`private int y = addOne();`

`private int addOne() {`
`return x + 1;`
`}`

`void main() {`
`System.out.println(y);`
`}`
`}`

The output of this silly program is `8`. In this program, every name is available to the rest of the program's code. The `addOne` method uses the value of `x`, the initialization of `y` calls `addOne`, and `main` uses the value of `y`. Almost everything is declared to be private. But because everything is in the same class, the word `private` doesn't matter.

#### Members in different classes

The ability to access a member of a different class depends on that member's access modifier. Here's the whole story:

#### PRIVATE ACCESS

If a member is private, only code that's inside the member’s class can refer directly to that member’s name. Here's some sample code:

**`class SomeClass {`**
**`private int myField = 10;`**
`}`

`///////////////////////////////`

**`class SomeOtherClass {`**

`void main() {`
`var someObject = new SomeClass();`
`System.out.println(someObject.`**`myField`**`);` **`//This doesn't work.`**
`}`
`}`

For a picture that describes private access, see [Figure 11-7](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0007).

![“A diagram showing a class named 'M' that extends another class named 'L' and contains a private field.”](images/9781394289240-fg1107.png)

[FIGURE 11-7:](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fig-0007) The range of code in which a private field or method can be used (shaded).

#### PUBLIC ACCESS

If a member is public, any code can refer directly to that member’s name. Here's some sample code:

**`class SomeClass {`**
**`public int myField = 10;`**
`}`

`///////////////////////////////`

**`class SomeOtherClass {`**

`void main() {`
`var someObject = new SomeClass();`

`System.out.println(someObject.`**`myField`**`);` **`//This works.`**
`}`
`}`

(Remember that both classes have to be in the same module. Otherwise, all bets are off.) For a picture that describes public access, see [Figure 11-8](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0008).

![“The image shows a diagram of class inheritance in object-oriented programming. There are nine boxes arranged in a 3x3 grid. The top row contains 'class A,' 'class L,' and 'class X.' The middle row contains 'class B extends A,' 'class M extends L' with a note 'public field,' and 'class Y extends X.' The bottom row contains 'class C extends B,' 'class N extends M,' and 'class Z extends Y.' This diagram illustrates the hierarchical relationships between different classes.”](images/9781394289240-fg1108.png)

[FIGURE 11-8:](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fig-0008) The range of code in which a public field or method can be used (shaded).

![Warning](images/warning.png) Public fields are easy to use and even easier to misuse. The best way to engineer your code is to make access to each field as restrictive as possible. If a field doesn't absolutely need to be public, try making it private. If other classes have to get or set the field's values, provide public getter and setter methods.

#### DEFAULT ACCESS

A member declared without any of the words *public, private,* or *protected* in front of it has *default access*, also known as *package-wide access*. A member with default access is accessible only in the package in which its class resides. [Figure 11-9](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0009) illustrates the point.

![“The image shows three boxes, each containing a Java class declaration. The first box contains 'class B extends A', the second box contains 'class M extends L' with a note 'default access field', and the third box contains 'class Y extends X'.”](images/9781394289240-fg1109.png)

[FIGURE 11-9:](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fig-0009) The range of code in which a field or method with default access can be used (shaded).

![Warning](images/warning.png) A subpackage has no more access than any other package. Think about two packages: `com.burdbrain.drawings` and `com.burdbrain.drawings.rectangles`. One is a subpackage of the other. One lives in a `com/burdbrain/drawings` folder with the other in the `com/burdbrain/drawings/rectangles` folder. Can these packages access each other's default members? You might guess that they can. But no, they can't. A package and its subpackage have the same privileges as two unrelated packages. That's the shocking truth.

#### PROTECTED ACCESS

When I was first getting to know Java, I thought the word *protected* meant “nice and secure” or something like that. “Wow, that field is protected. It must be hard to get at.” Well, this notion turned out to be wrong. In Java, a member that's protected is less hidden, less secure, and available for use in more classes than one that has default access. In other words, protected access is more permissive than default access. For me, the terminology is misleading. But that's the way it is.

Think of protected access this way: You start with a field that has default access (a field without the word `public`, `private`, or `protected` in its declaration). That field can be accessed only inside its own package. Now add the word `protected` to the front of the field's declaration. Suddenly, some classes outside that field's package have access to the field. You can now reference the field from a subclass of the class in which the field is declared. You can also reference the field from a sub-subclass, a sub-sub-subclass, and so on. Any descendant class will do. [Figure 11-10](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0010) has the story.

![“The image shows a diagram of class inheritance in object-oriented programming. There are four classes depicted: 'class B extends A', 'class M extends L', 'class Y extends X', and 'class N extends M'. The class 'M' has a protected field.”](images/9781394289240-fg1110.png)

[FIGURE 11-10:](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fig-0010) The range of code in which a protected field or method can be used (shaded).

Don't stress your brain trying to understand protected access. You can read about it again if you ever need to know about it.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Here are some things for you to try:

#### NAME THAT TUNE

A `Work` has a title (a `String`) and a release year (an `int`).

The `Song` and `Painting` classes are subclasses of the `Work` class. Each `Song` instance has a genre (a value from an `enum` named `Genre`). The values of `Genre` are `ROCK`, `POP`, `BLUES`, and `CLASSICAL`. Each `Painting` instance has a style (a value from an `enum` named `Style`). The values of `Style` are `REALISTIC`, `ABSTRACT`, `IMPRESSIONISM`, and `EXPRESSIONISM`.

Each `Artist` instance has a name (a `String`) and a `Work` instance.

Create these classes. Devise a plausible scenario in which these classes would be spread over more than one package. In a separate class, construct instances of each class, and display information about these instances on the screen.

#### WHAT'S MINE IS YOURS

The following four classes live in four different `.java` files. Without typing these classes in an IDE's editor, decide which statements will cause the IDE to display error messages. For each such statement, decide on the least permissive access change that would eliminate the error message:

**`// THIS CODE DOES NOT COMPILE:`**

**`package com.mypackage.things;`**

`import com.yourpackage.stuff.Stuff;`
`import com.yourpackage.stuff.morestuff.MoreStuff;`

`public class Things {`
`protected int i = 0;`
`private int j = 0;`
`int k = 0;`

`public static void main(String[] args) {`
`var stuff = new Stuff();`
`System.out.println(stuff.i);`
`var moreStuff = new MoreStuff();`
`System.out.println(moreStuff.i);`
`}`
`}`

**`package com.yourpackage.stuff;`**

`import com.yourpackage.stuff.morestuff.MoreStuff;`

`public class Stuff {`
`protected int i = 0;`

`void aMethod() {`
`new MoreStuff().myMethod();`
`}`
`}`

**`package com.yourpackage.stuff.morestuff;`**

`import com.mypackage.things.Things;`

`public class MoreStuff extends Things {`
`protected void myMethod() {`
`System.out.println(i);`
`}`
`}`

**`package com.mypackage.things;`**

`public class MoreThings extends Things {`

`public void anotherMethod() {`
`System.out.println(i);`
`System.out.println(j);`
`System.out.println(k);`
`}`
`}`

## Making Static (Finding the Team Average)

Thinking about the code in [Listings 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001) and [11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002), you decide that you want to find the team's overall batting average. Rather than pore over game logs and do a careful analysis, you settle on finding the average of the numbers in [Figure 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0002). That's the average of the players' averages. Using this strategy, the team in [Figure 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0002) has an average of about .106\. No doubt about it: The Hankies need some intensive training.

While the players are out practicing on the ball field, you have a philosophical question to answer. Where should the variables storing your overall, team-average tally go? Should you add more fields to the `Player` class in [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001)? After considering this possibility, you have some reservations. An instance of the `Player` class represents just one player on the team. What business does a single player have storing overall team data? Sure, you could make the code work, but it wouldn't be an elegant solution to the problem.

Luckily for you, Java has its `static` keyword. Anything that's declared to be `static` belongs to the whole class, not to any particular instance of the class. When you create the `static` field, `totalOfAverages`, you create just one copy of the field. This copy stays with the entire class. No matter how many instances of the class you create — one, nine, or none — you have just one `totalOfAverages` field. And, while you're at it, you create another `static` field (`playerCount`) and a `static` method (`findTeamAverage`). To see what I mean, look at [Figure 11-11](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0011).

![“The image is a completely black square with no visible content, text, or distinguishable features. It represents a blank or empty space, which could be used for various purposes such as a placeholder, background, or to convey a sense of emptiness or absence.”](images/9781394289240-fg1111.png)

[FIGURE 11-11:](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fig-0011) Some static and nonstatic fields and methods.

The `Player` class in [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001) does what it was originally supposed to do. And you remember the old saying, “If something's not broken, don't fix it.” With that in mind, you create a brand-new `Player` class and put it in [Listing 11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0003). The new `Player` class has all the features of your old `Player` class plus a few more.

[LISTING 11-3](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fea-0003) Creating a Team Batting Average

`package com.example.sport;`

`public record Player(String name, double average) {`

`private` **`static`** `int playerCount = 0;`
`private` **`static`** `double totalOfAverages = .000;`

**`public Player {`**
**`playerCount++;`**
**`totalOfAverages += average;`**
**`}`**

`public` **`static`** `double findTeamAverage() {`
`return totalOfAverages / playerCount;`
`}`
`}`

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) Full disclosure: In previous editions of *Java For Dummies*, the `Player` class in [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001) wasn't a record class. But in this edition, I decided to make it a record class. I did this because a 4-line record class saves space on the page. (I also wanted to show off Java's new record class feature.) Anyway, one consequence of this decision is that the code in [Listing 11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0003) can't extend the `Player` class in [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001). Record classes are *final*. That means you can't create a subclass of a record class. So [Listing 11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0003) has a completely new `Player` class. This new `Player` class has all the features of the old `Player` class (see [Listing 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001)) plus the ability to calculate the overall team average. The two `Player` classes (see [Listings 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0001) and [11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0003)) are in different packages, so Java won't mistake one of them for the other.

### So much static!

In [Listing 11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0003), I declare `playerCount` to be `static`. That's good because something like `playerCount` (the number of team members) shouldn't belong to individual team members, and having each `Player` instance keep track of its own count would be silly. (“I know how many team members I am. I'm just one team member!”) If you had nine individual `playerCount` fields, either each field would store the number 1 (which is useless) or you would have nine different copies of the count, which is wasteful and prone to error. By making `playerCount` static, you're keeping the `playerCount` in just one place, where it belongs.

The same kind of reasoning holds for the `totalOfAverages`. Eventually, the `totalOfAverages` field will store the sum of the players' batting averages. For all nine members of the Hankies, this adds up to .956.

It's not until someone calls the `findTeamAverage` method that the computer actually finds the overall Hankies team batting average. You want this `findTeamAverage` method to be static. Without the word `static`, there would be nine `findTeamAverage` methods — one for each instance of the `Player` class. This wouldn't make much sense. Each instance would have the code to calculate `totalOfAverages` `/` `playerCount` on its own, and each of the nine calculations would yield the same answer.

In general, any task that all instances have in common (and that yields the same result for each instance) should be coded as a `static` method.

### Meet the compact constructor

To find the average of the team members' batting averages, you divide the total of all team members' averages by the number of team members. In [Listing 11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0003), that's `totalOfAverages / playerCount`. The `playerCount` and `totalOfAverages` fields in [Listing 11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0003) are static. Whether you have one team member, nine team members, or a thousand team members, there's only one copy of the `playerCount` field and one copy of the `totalOfAverages` field.

How do you keep tallies of `playerCount` and `totalOfAverages` values? One way is to update these fields whenever you call the `Player` constructor. If `Player` wasn't a record class, you'd code a constructor like this:

**`// A four-statement constructor`**

`public Player(String name, double average) {`
`this.name = name;`
`this.average = average;`
**`playerCount++;`**
**`totalOfAverages += average;`**
`}`

You could do this in [Listing 11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0003). But since [Listing 11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0003) is a record class, there's a better way. Remember: When you don't code a constructor in a record class, Java quietly adds its own fully parameterized implicit constructor. In other words, the code in [Listing 11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0003) automatically gets a constructor of the following kind:

`public Player(String name, double average) {`
`this.name = name;`
`this.average = average;`
`}`

To this implicit constructor, you want to add a `playerCount++` statement and a `totalOfAverages += average` statement.

Lo and behold! The wizards who design Java thought about your needs! They said you can put a *compact constructor* in your record class. In [Listing 11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0003), the compact constructor looks like this:

`public Player {`
`playerCount++;`
`totalOfAverages += average;`
`}`

A record class's compact constructor has no parentheses after the class's name. When Java sees this, it *adds* the statements in the compact constructor to the statements in its own implicit constructor. It's as if you had coded this section's 4-statement constructor. That's pretty handy.

### Displaying the overall team average

[Listing 11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0003), shown previously, contains code that defines a `Player` class, and [Listing 11-4](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0004) contains code that uses this `Player` class.

[LISTING 11-4](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fea-0004) Using the Code from [Listing 11-3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0003)

`import com.example.sport.Player;`

`import java.io.File;`
`import java.io.IOException;`
`import java.util.Scanner;`

**`import static java.lang.System.out;`**

`void main() throws IOException {`
`var hankiesData = new Scanner(new File("Hankies.txt"));`

`printTableHeader();`

`for (int num = 1; num <= 9; num++) {`
`var player = new Player(hankiesData.nextLine(),`
`hankiesData.nextDouble());`
`hankiesData.nextLine();`
`printTableRow(player);`
`}`
`hankiesData.close();`

`printTableSummary();`
`}`

`void printTableHeader() {`
**`out.print`**`("Team Member");`
**`out.println`**`(String.format("%40s", "Batting Average"));`
`out.print("-----------");`
`out.println(String.format("%40s", "---------------"));`
`}`

`void printTableRow(Player player) {`
`out.print(String.format("%-40s", player.name()));`
`out.println(String.format("%.3f", player.average()).substring(1));`
`}`

`void printTableSummary() {`
`out.println(String.format("%44s", "----"));`
`var teamAverageString =` **`String.format`**`("%.3f",`
**`Player.findTeamAverage()`**`).substring(1);`
`out.println(String.format("%44s", teamAverageString));`
`}`

[Figure 11-12](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0012) shows a run of the code from [Listing 11-4](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0004). This run depends on the availability of the `Hankies.txt` file from [Figure 11-1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0001).

![Text list of fictional team members with their low batting averages.](images/9781394289240-fg1112.png)

[FIGURE 11-12:](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fig-0012) A run of the code in [Listing 11-4](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0004).

[Listing 11-4](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0004) is rather long, but most of it is the kind of code you see in previous examples.

### All about static import

Here's one new and exciting thing in [Listing 11-4](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0004). It's the listing's `import static` declaration:

`import` **`static`** `java.lang.System.out;`

When I add this `import static` declaration, I can abbreviate the long-winded `System.out.print` and `System.out.println` calls.

Other import declarations begin with only the word `static`. So, why do I need the two words `import static` for a declaration in [Listing 11-4](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0004)?

The answer is that you can import two kinds of things — classes and static members. The `java.util` package has a *class* named `Scanner`. So, to abbreviate `java.util.Scanner` with plain old `Scanner`, you write

`import java.util.Scanner;`

But the `System` class (inside the `java.lang` package) has a static member named `out`:

`public class System {`
`// …`
`public static PrintStream out;`
`}`

So, to abbreviate `java.lang.System.out` with plain old `out`, you write

`import` **`static`** `java.lang.System.out;`

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) You can always abbreviate `java.lang.System` with the simple name `System`. Java adds an implicit `import java.lang.*;` to every program.

### All about static references

Notice the code

`Player.findTeamAverage()`

near the end of [Listing 11-4](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0004).

That code has the following form:

*`ClassName`*`.methodName()`

In earlier chapters, I say that you normally preface a method call with an object's name, not a class’s name. So why does this dot notation use a class name? The answer: When you call a `static` method, you preface the method’s name with the name of the class that contains the method. The same holds true whenever you reference another class’s `static` field. This makes sense because a static member belongs to an entire class, not to any particular instance of the class.

This rule about calling static methods isn't new. [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml) introduces `String.format` as a way of making output look nice. When I wrote that chapter, I hoped you wouldn't notice that `String` is the name of a class, not the name of an object. But now, I can come clean. The `format` method is a static member of the `String` class.

`public class String {`
`public static String format( … ) {`
`// …`
`}`
`// …`
`}`

So there! To refer to the static `format` method, you start with the name of the method's class. You write `String.format`.

### Why static members don't always play well with others

In Java, every method must be a member of a class, even if that class is declared implicitly. And before Java 21, every `main` method had to be static. To honor that tradition, the `main` method in [Listing 11-2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fea-0002) is a static member of a class named `ShowPlayers`.

`public class ShowPlayers {`

`public` **`static`** `void main(String[] args) throws IOException {`

As you travel through Javaland, you'll see many static `main` methods. The thing to remember about static main methods is what other methods they can and cannot call. Take, for example, the following bad code:

**`// Kids, don't try this at home (or anywhere else).`**

`public class Attendee {`

`public` **`static`** `void main(String args[]) {`
`var person1 = new Attendee();`
`var person2 = new Attendee();`
`var person3 = new Attendee();`
**`areYouHere();`**
**`startMeeting();`**
`}`

**`public void areYouHere() {`**
`System.out.println("I'm here.");`
`}`

**`public void startMeeting() {`**
`System.out.println("Welcome, everyone.");`
`}`
`}`

In this bad code, the main method creates three people, tries to ask whether they're attending the meeting, and then tries to start the meeting. The error message I get with this code is “Non-static method cannot be referenced from a static context.” When I first started writing Java programs, I had recurring dreams about seeing this error message. So often did I see this message, so thoroughly was I perplexed, that the memory of this error message became burned into my brain.

In this `Attendee` class, the main method is static, but the `youAreHere` and `startMeeting` methods aren't static. (See [Figure 11-13](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0013).) So there's only one main method, but there are three versions of the `areYouHere` method and three versions of the `startMeeting` method. When the one-and-only main method tries to call `areYouHere()`, Java doesn't know which instance's `areYouHere` method to call. The same thing happens when the static main method tries to call `startMeeting()`. In both cases, Java says “Non-static method cannot be referenced from a static context.”

![“The image shows a class diagram for 'The Attendee class' with three instances: person1, person2, and person3\. Each instance has two methods: areYouHere() and startMeeting(). Below the instances, there is a static main method that calls areYouHere() and startMeeting(), with comments questioning which person is being referred to.”](images/9781394289240-fg1113.png)

[FIGURE 11-13:](#c11.xhtml_rc11-fig-0013) This is no good.

To find out who's present at a meeting, you don't stare into space and ask “Are you here?” of no one in particular. Instead, you go down your list of people on the roster and ask each of them individually “Are you here?” So each `areYouHere` call should refer to a specific attendee.

In contrast, starting the formal proceedings is a one-person job. Your code should have only one copy of the `startMeeting` method, so `startMeeting` should be static.

Here's some code with the appropriate fixes:

`public class Attendee {`

`public static void main(String args[]) {`
`var person1 = new Attendee();`
`var person2 = new Attendee();`
`var person3 = new Attendee();`
**`person1.areYouHere();`**
**`person2.areYouHere();`**
**`person3.areYouHere();`**
`startMeeting();`
`}`

`public void areYouHere() {`
`System.out.println("I'm here.");`
`}`

`public` **`static`** `void startMeeting() {`
`System.out.println("Welcome, everyone.");`
`}`
`}`

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) The `startMeeting` method is a static member of the `Attendee` class. So, instead of calling `startMeeting()`, you have the option of calling `Attendee.startMeeting()`. This `Attendee` prefix is necessary when you want to call the `Attendee` class's `startMeeting` method from code that's not inside the `Attendee` class itself. (Oddly enough, a call like `person1.startMeeting()` also works, although I've never understood why that way of calling a static method makes any sense.)

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) I don't know about you, but I can always use some practice with static variables and methods:

#### DESIGNER DUMMIES

In a previous section, you create a class to represent items in a line of men's clothing. Add fields for the name of the designer and the cost of the item. The designer's name is static because all items in the line come from the same designer — *Dummies House of Fashion*.

Write code to display eight rows. The first row displays the designer's name. The next six rows describe one complete men's wardrobe. The last row shows the wardrobe's total cost.

#### COUNT ME IN

In a previous section, you create a class to represent a playing card. Add a static field to your `PlayingCard` class. The field keeps track of the number of times the class's constructor has been called, and thus has a count of the number of playing cards.

#### ATTACK OF THE MUTANTS

What's the output of the following code? Make some predictions, and then run the code to see whether your predictions are correct:

`package com.example.mutants;`

`import static java.lang.System.out;`

`public class Main {`

`public static void main(String[] args) {`

`out.println("bigValue: " + MutableInteger.bigValue);`
`// out.println("bigValue: " + IntegerHolder.value); ILLEGAL`

`var holder1 = new MutableInteger(42);`
`var holder2 = new MutableInteger(7);`

`out.println("holder1: " + holder1.value);`
`out.println("holder2: " + holder2.value);`
`out.println();`
`holder1.value++;`
`holder2.value++;`
`MutableInteger.bigValue++;`

`out.println("bigValue: " + MutableInteger.bigValue);`
`out.println("holder1: " + holder1.value);`
`out.println("holder2: " + holder2.value);`

`out.println();`
`holder1.bigValue++;`
`out.println("bigValue according to holder1: " + holder1.bigValue);`
`out.println("bigValue according to holder2: " + holder2.bigValue);`
`}`
`}`

`class MutableInteger {`
`int value;`
`static int bigValue = 1_000_000;`

`public MutableInteger(int value) {`
`this.value = value;`
`}`
`}`

Chapter 12

# Using Collections and Streams

IN THIS CHAPTER

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Dealing with a bunch of objects at one time**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Creating a dictionary**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Using Java's cool functional programming features**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Developing code for multicore processors**

The English word *venery* means “the act of hunting for wild animals.” In olden times, hunters made up words to refer to collections of animals. For example, a group of birds is a *flock*, and an assemblage of wolves is a *pack*. Collective nouns of this kind are called *terms of venery*. Other examples include a *pride* of lions, *gaggle* of geese, *school* of fish, *swarm* of bees, *herd* of cattle, *murder* of crows, *quiver* of cobras, *convocation* of eagles, and *litter* of cats. (Why anyone would want to go hunting for a bunch of house cats, I'll never know!)

Anyway, Java has many of its own terms of venery. But Java's terms differ from English animals' terms in an important way. Java's terms don't differ from one another by the kinds of things they describe. Instead, they differ in the way the underlying collection is organized. It's like the difference between a *committee* of people and a *mob* of people. A mob can be scary, but a committee is usually very boring.

The first part of this chapter is about Java's terms of venery, known to everyone else in the world as Java's *collection classes*. Once you have collection classes, you can take full advantage of Java's functional programming features. Those functional programming features dominate this chapter's second half.

## Using an ArrayList

One of the most commonly used collection classes is the `ArrayList`. [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001) shows you how it works.

[LISTING 12-1](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fea-0001) Working with a Java Collection

**`import module java.base;`**

`public class ShowAllWords {`

`public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {`

**`ArrayList<String> vocabulary = new ArrayList<>();`**

`var diskScanner = new Scanner(new File("AllWords.txt"));`
`while (diskScanner.hasNext()) {`
**`vocabulary.add(diskScanner.nextLine());`**
`}`
`diskScanner.close();`

**`vocabulary.remove(0);`**
**`vocabulary.add(2, "paradox");`**

**`for (String word : vocabulary) {`**
**`System.out.println(word);`**
**`}`**
`}`
`}`

The code in [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001) reads an `AllWords.txt` file and loads the file's values in an `ArrayList` named `vocabulary`. [Figure 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0001) shows you a sample `AllWords.txt` file. (In case anyone cares, I got the words in this file by asking ChatGPT.) With that particular `AllWords.txt` file, the output of [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001) is what you see in [Figure 12-2](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0002).

![A list of words in a rectangular box. The words are: Luminous, harbor, zephyr, enigma, serendipity, quicksilver, echo, velvet, mirage, driftwood.](images/9781394289240-fg1201.png)

[FIGURE 12-1:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0001) Several words in a file.

According to [Figure 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0001), the word at the start of the `AllWords.txt` file is `luminous`. So, you may be tempted to say the following: “Immediately after reading the contents of `AllWords.txt`, the first value in the `vocabulary` list is the word `luminous`.” If you say this, you're *mostly* correct.

![A list of words in a vertical column: harbor, zephyr, paradox, enigma, serendipity, quicksilver, echo, velvet, mirage, driftwood.](images/9781394289240-fg1202.png)

[FIGURE 12-2:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0002) The output of the code in [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001).

Why do I say “mostly correct”? It's because the word *first* suggests “number 1,” but `luminous` isn't the “number 1” value in the vocabulary list. Computer languages number everything starting with 0, not 1\. Because of this, these statements are true:

*   The *index* of `luminous` in the vocabulary list is 0.
*   The index of the next word, `harbor`, in the vocabulary list is 1.
*   The index of the next word, `zephyr`, in the vocabulary list is 2.
*   And so on.

See [Figure 12-3](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0003).

![A diagram of an array with indices from 0 to 9\. Each index points to a word: 0 - luminous, 1 - harbor, 2 - zephyr, 3 - enigma, 4 - serendipity, 5 - quicksilver, 6 - echo, 7 - velvet, 8 - mirage, 9 - driftwood.](images/9781394289240-fg1203.png)

[FIGURE 12-3:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0003) Immediately after reading the `AllWords.txt` file.

You can guess what happens when Java executes `vocabulary.remove(0)` in [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001). The element with index 0 (the word `luminous`) gets taken from the list. As a result, the word `harbor` moves to the 0-index slot. In fact, all remaining values move leftward to fill in the gap. After removing `luminous` from the list, you have the `vocabulary` list shown in [Figure 12-4](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0004).

![A diagram of an array with indices from 0 to 8\. Each index points to a word: 0 - harbor, 1 - zephyr, 2 - enigma, 3 - serendipity, 4 - quicksilver, 5 - echo, 6 - velvet, 7 - mirage, 8 - driftwood.](images/9781394289240-fg1204.png)

[FIGURE 12-4:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0004) After removing the entry with index 0.

The next statement in [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001), `vocabulary.add(2, "paradox")`, inserts the word `paradox` into the list and gives that word the index 2\. Once again, some values in the list get new indices. In particular, the words from `enigma` onward move over to accommodate the insertion. (See [Figure 12-5](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0005).)

![A diagram of an array with indices from 0 to 9\. Each index points to a word: 0 - harbor, 1 - zephyr, 2 - Paradox, 3 - enigma, 4 - serendipity, 5 - quicksilver, 6 - echo, 7 - velvet, 8 - dirge, 9 - driftwood.](images/9781394289240-fg1205.png)

[FIGURE 12-5:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0005) After inserting an entry at index 2.

Notice that an `ArrayList` instance has two different `add` methods.

*   The first `add` method in [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001) has only one parameter:

    `vocabulary.add(diskScanner.nextLine());`

    This statement takes whatever word it finds on a line of the input file and appends that word to the *end* of the list.

*   The second `add` method in [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001) has two parameters:

    `vocabulary.add(2, "paradox")`

    Rather than append a word to the end of the file, this method inserts the word at a specific index in the file.

### A new way to do looping

The last few lines of [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001) contain an *enhanced*`for`*loop* — a `for` loop that doesn't use counters or indices. An enhanced `for` loop has three parts:

`for (`***`variableType variableName`* `:` *`rangeOfValues`***`)`

In [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001), the *variableName* is `word`, and the *variableType* is `String`. During each loop iteration, the `word` variable takes on a new `String` value. The loop's *rangeOfValues* is `vocabulary`. So, during the initial loop iteration, the value of `word` is the `vocabulary` list's initial entry, which is `"harbor"`. (Refer to [Figure 12-5](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0005).) During the next iteration, the value of `word` is the list's next entry, which is `"zephyr"`. After that comes `"paradox"`. And so on.

![Warning](images/warning.png) Java's enhanced `for` loop requires a word of caution: Each time through the loop, the variable that steps through the range of values stores a *copy* of the value in the original range. The variable does *not* point to the range itself.

For example, if you add an assignment statement that changes the value of `word` in [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001), this statement has no effect on any of the values stored in the `vocabulary` array. To drive this point home, imagine filling the `vocabulary` list with five words: `"Air"`, `"Hi"`, `"More"`, `"A"`, and `"I"`. Then you execute the following code:

`for (var word: vocabulary) {`
**`word += "s";`**
`print(word);`
`print(" ");`
`}`

`println("");`

`for (var word: vocabulary) {`
`print(word);`
`print(" ");`
`}`

The `word += "s"` statement changes each `word` value *inside* the topmost `for` loop. But that statement *doesn't* change any of the `vocabulary` list's entries. So the code's output looks like this:

`Airs His Mores As Is`
`Air Hi More A I`

![Tip](images/tip.png) Java has some shortcuts for filling a new `ArrayList` with values. For example, you can write

`ArrayList<String> list =`
`new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList("harbor", "zephyr", "enigma", "echo"));`

Or, if you want 100 copies of the word *Dummies* in a list, you can write

`ArrayList<String> dummiesList =`
`new ArrayList<>(Collections.nCopies(100, "Dummies"));`

### Import a module in one fell swoop

The code in [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001) uses several classes from Java's API. My original draft of [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001) started with all these import declarations:

`import java.io.File;`
`import java.io.IOException;`
`import java.util.ArrayList;`
`import java.util.Collections;`
`import java.util.Scanner;`

Eventually, I replaced these five lines with only one line: the `import module java.base` declaration at the top of [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001).

That `import module` line can replace most of the other import declarations in this book. In particular, all the declarations that begin with `import java.util` and `import java.io` can be replaced with this single `import module` declaration. The reason is that the packages whose names start with `java.util` and `java.io` are all inside the `java.base` module.

You can import the `java.base` module at the start of any file, even a file that doesn't need any imports at all. (In rare cases, you can stumble into a class name conflict. That's when you need a class in some other module that has the same name as a class in `java.base`. In that case, Java tells you that you have a problem, and you can take time to work it all out.)

The `import module` declaration is a win-win. So why don't you see it in earlier chapters? I held off using it until Chapter 12 for three reasons:

*   **The import module feature is new.**

    In fact, it's a preview feature in Java 23\. I don't think it will disappear in later versions of Java, but I can't be sure of it.

*   **Old-fashioned import declarations (without the word** `module`**) occur in zillions of Java programs.**

    It's good to know how those traditional import declarations work. And finally,

*   **I didn't think of using import module declarations until after I wrote** [**Chapters** **1**](#c01.xhtml)**–**[**11**](#c11.xhtml)**.**

    Don't tell anybody about that.

### Using generics

Look again at [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001), shown earlier, and notice the funky `ArrayList` declaration:

`ArrayList`**`<String>`** `vocabulary = new ArrayList`**`<>`**`();`

Each collection class is *generified.* That ugly-sounding word means that every collection declaration should contain some angle-bracketed stuff, such as `<String>`. The thing that's sandwiched between `<` and `>` tells Java what kinds of values the new collection may contain. For example, in [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001), the words `ArrayList<String> vocabulary` tell Java that `vocabulary` is a bunch of strings. That is, the `vocabulary` list contains `String` objects (not `Room` objects, not `Account` objects, not `Employee` objects — nothing other than `String` objects). So, from that point on, any reference to an item from the `vocabulary` collection is treated exclusively as a `String`. If you write

`vocabulary.add(new` **`Account`**`());`

the compiler coughs up your code and spits it out because an `Account` (created in [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml)) isn't the same as a `String`. (This coughing and spitting happens even if the compiler has access to the `Account` class's code — the code in [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml).) But the statement

`vocabulary.add(`**`"George Gow"`**`);`

is just fine. Because `"George Gow"` has type `String`, the compiler smiles happily.

#### You have options

For historical reasons, there are a few different ways to define a new collection. For example, if you love typing, you can repeat the generic type name, like so:

`ArrayList<String> vocabulary = new ArrayList<`**`String`**`>();`

In the right situations, you can use the word `var`:

**`var`** `vocabulary = new ArrayList<`**`String`**`>();`

You may even see old code that's not generified, like this:

`ArrayList vocabulary = new ArrayList();`

But I don't recommend writing new declarations this way. Without generics, Java won't know for sure what type of values you're putting into your collection. So the rest of your code may require some special care. For example, you may need extra code to make sure you don't accidentally add an `Employee` instance or `Account` instance to your `vocabulary` list.

### Wrap it up

In [Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml), I point out that Java has two kinds of types: primitive types and reference types. (If you didn't read those sections, or if you don't remember them, don't feel guilty. You'll be okay.) Things like `int`, `double`, `char`, and `boolean` are primitive types, and things like `String`, `Employee`, `ArrayList`, and `Account` are reference types.

The distinction between primitive types and reference types has been a source of contention since Java's birth in 1995\. Even now, Oracle's wizards are hatching plans to work around the stickier consequences of having two kinds of types. One of those consequences is that collections, such as the `ArrayList`, can't contain values of a primitive type. For example, it's okay to write

`ArrayList<`**`String`**`> vocabulary = new ArrayList<>();`

but it's not okay to write

`ArrayList<`**`int`**`> numbers = new ArrayList<>();` **`// BAD! BAD!`**

because `int` is a primitive type. So, if you want to store values like `3`, `55`, and `21` in an `ArrayList`, what do you do? Rather than store `int` values in the `ArrayList`, you store Java's `Integer` values:

`ArrayList<`**`Integer`**`> list = new ArrayList<>();`

In previous chapters, you see the `Integer` class in connection with the `parseInt` method:

`int numberOfCows = Integer.parseInt("536");`

The `Integer` class has many methods, such as `parseInt`, for dealing with `int` values. The class also has fields such as `MAX_VALUE` and `MIN_VALUE`, which stand for the largest and smallest values that `int` variables may have.

The `Integer` class is an example of a *wrapper class.* Each of Java's eight primitive types has a corresponding wrapper class. You can use methods and fields in Java's `Double`, `Character`, `Boolean`, `Long`, `Float`, `Short`, and `Byte` wrapper classes. For example, the `Double` class has methods named `parseDouble`, `compareTo`, `toHexString`, and fields named `MAX_VALUE` and `MAX_EXPONENT`.

Notice the pattern. All eight of Java's primitive types (such as `int`, `double`, and `char`) have names that start with lowercase letters. In contrast, all the wrapper classes (`Integer`, `Double`, `Character`, and so on) have names that begin with uppercase letters.

![Tip](images/tip.png) Java programmers like to begin the names of classes with uppercase letters. You can declare `public class account` or `public class employee`. But if you do, other programmers will think you're a rube.

The `Integer` class wraps the primitive `int` type with useful methods and values. In addition, you can create an `Integer` instance that wraps a single `int` value:

`Integer myInteger = Integer.valueOf(42);`

In this line of code, the `myInteger` variable has one `int` value inside it: the `int` value `42`. In Paul's words, wrapping the `int` value `42` into an `Integer` object `myInteger` is “something like putting lots of extra breading on okra — it makes `42` more digestible for finicky eaters like collections.”

Instances of the other wrapper classes work the same way. For example, an instance of the `Double` class wraps up a single primitive `double` value:

`Double averageNumberOfTomatoes = Double.valueOf(1.41421356237);`

Here's a program that stores five `Integer` values in an `ArrayList`:

`void main() {`
`ArrayList<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>();`
`fillTheList(list);`
`for (Integer n : list) {`
`println(n);`
`}`
`}`

`public static void fillTheList(ArrayList<Integer> list) {`
`list.add(85);`
`list.add(19);`
`list.add(0);`
`list.add(103);`
`list.add(13);`
`}`

In the code, notice calls like `list.add(85)` that have `int` value parameters. At this point, little Billy gets excited and says, “Look, Mom! I added the primitive `int` value `85` to my `ArrayList`!” No, Billy. That's not what's going on.

In this code, the `list` collection contains `Integer` values, not `int` values. A primitive `int` value is a lot like an instance of the `Integer` class. But a primitive `int` value isn't exactly the same as an `Integer` instance.

What's going on is called *autoboxing*. Before Java 5.0, you had to write

`list.add(new Integer(85));`

if you wanted to add an `Integer` to an `ArrayList`. But Java 5.0 and later Java versions can automatically wrap an `int` value inside a box. An `int` value in a parameter list becomes an `Integer` in `ArrayList`. Java's autoboxing feature makes programs easier to read and write.

### Are we done yet?

Here's a pleasant surprise. When you write a program like the one shown previously, in [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001), you don't have to know how many names are in the input file. Having to know the number of names may defeat the purpose of using the easily expandable `ArrayList` class. Rather than loop until you read exactly nine names, you can loop until you run out of data.

The `Scanner` class has several nice methods, such as `hasNextInt`, `hasNextDouble`, and plain old `hasNext`. Each of these methods checks for more input data. If there's more data, the method returns `true`. Otherwise, the method returns `false`.

[Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001) uses the general-purpose `hasNext` method. This `hasNext` method returns `true` as long as there's anything more to read from the program's input. After the program scoops up that last `driftwood` line in [Figure 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0001), the subsequent `hasNext` call returns `false`. This `false` condition ends execution of the `while` loop and plummets the computer toward the remainder of the [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001) code.

The `hasNext` method is quite handy. In fact, `hasNext` is so handy that it's part of a bigger concept known as an *iterator,* and iterators are baked into all of Java's collection classes.

### Once and again

An iterator spits out a collection's values, one after another. To obtain a value from the collection, you call the iterator's `next` method. To find out whether the collection has any more values in it, you call the iterator's `hasNext` method. For example, you can replace the loop at the end of [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001) with the following code:

`Iterator<String> iterator = vocabulary.iterator();`
`while (iterator.hasNext()) {`
`System.out.println(iterator.next());`
`}`

The first line of code creates an iterator from the `vocabulary` collection. The second and third lines call the iterator's `hasNext` and `next` methods to grab all objects stored in the `vocabulary` collection — one for each iteration of the loop. These lines display each of the `vocabulary` collection's values. When you make this code replacement, you get the same output as before. (You get the output in [Figure 12-2](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0002).)

![Remember](images/remember.png) To run this code, you may need an `import java.util.Iterator` declaration.

Which is better? An enhanced `for` loop or an iterator? Java programmers prefer the enhanced `for` loop because the `for` loop involves less baggage — no `iterator` object to carry from one line of code to the next. But as you can see later in this chapter, the most programming-enhanced feature can be upgraded, streamlined, tweaked, and otherwise reconstituted. There's no end to the way you can improve upon your code.

## Sorting and Searching

Every introductory computer programming book has sections featuring two important problems:

*   **Sorting:** Reorder the values in a list from increasing to decreasing, or from decreasing to increasing.
*   **Searching:** Find a particular value that's one of many values in a list. Report the location of that value in the list.

Java has methods to solve both of these problems easily. You can see them in [Listing 12-2](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0002).

[LISTING 12-2](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fea-0002) Where in the World Is the Word “Enigma”?

`void main() throws IOException {`
`ArrayList<String> vocabulary = new ArrayList<>();`

`var diskScanner = new Scanner(new File("AllWords.txt"));`
`while (diskScanner.hasNext()) {`
`vocabulary.add(diskScanner.nextLine());`
`}`
`diskScanner.close();`

**`Collections.sort(vocabulary);`**
`for (String word : vocabulary) {`
`println(word);`
`}`

`println("");`
`print("Enigma's index: ");`
`println(`**`vocabulary.indexOf("enigma")`**`);`
`}`

[Figure 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0006) shows a run of the code in [Listing 12-2](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0002).

![A list of words with corresponding numerical values. The words and values are: luminous 1521841, harbor 14117675, zephyr 1134961, enigma 1579665, serendipity 830226, quicksilver 804862, echo 19416462, velvet 5591498, mirage 2493561, driftwood 536980.](images/9781394289240-fg1206.png)

[FIGURE 12-6:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0006) Sorted and searched.

In [Listing 12-2](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0002), the call `Collections.sort(vocabulary)` rearranges the values in the `vocabulary` list. This call uses the default arrangement, which is alphabetical ordering.

Later in [Listing 12-2](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0002), the call `vocabulary.``indexOf("enigma")` returns the index of the first occurrence of the word `"enigma"` in the sorted `vocabulary` list. In [Figure 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0006), `driftwood` has index 0, `echo` has index 1, and `enigma` has index 2. And, sure enough, a call to `indexOf("enigma")` returns the `2` that you see at the end of [Figure 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0006).

![Tip](images/tip.png) Java's sorting methods are versatile. You can arrange values in any order you want. For example, the call `Collections.reverse(vocabulary)` arranges an alphabetically sorted list in reverse alphabetical order. And the call

`Collections.sort(vocabulary, Comparator.comparingInt(w -> w.charAt(w.length() - 1)));`

arranges values based on the *last* letter in each word.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png)[Chapter 11](#c11.xhtml) describes Java's `static` keyword and reveals some of the things you do when you bump into a static field or method. In [Listing 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0001), you write `Collections.sort` because `sort` is a `static` method in Java's `Collections` class. My college students freeze up when I use the word `static`. So, if you don't understand this paragraph, don't worry about it. You can learn about `static` as you write more Java programs.

## Another Kind of Collection Class

I tried to find out how many collection classes the Java 23 API has, but I gave up after a while. It has too many for a simple tally. Anyway, I decided to show off a class whose structure differs significantly from the `ArrayList` class. It's Java's `HashMap` class.

A `HashMap` instance stores a lookup table. For example, Dr. Rachael Tatman counted the number of occurrences of 333,333 words on Internet pages and published her results at [`www.kaggle.com/datasets/rtatman/english-word-frequency `](http://www.kaggle.com/datasets/rtatman/english-word-frequency). I copied ten of her values to create a file named `WordFrequencies.txt`. (In retrospect, I should have asked her to count the number of collection classes in Java's API!) My `WordFrequencies.txt` file is shown in [Figure 12-7](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0007).

The code in [Listing 12-3](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0003) reads the `WordFrequencies.txt` file into a hash map. Then the program tries to find two words in the hash map and output those numbers' frequencies.

![A list of words each paired with a sequence of numbers, displayed in a monospaced font.](images/9781394289240-fg1207.png)

[FIGURE 12-7:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0007) The `WordFrequencies.txt` file.

[LISTING 12-3](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fea-0003) Using a HashMap

`void main() throws IOException {`
`var diskScanner = new Scanner(new File("WordFrequencies.txt"));`

**`var map = new HashMap<String, Integer>();`**

`while (diskScanner.hasNext()) {`
**`map.put(diskScanner.next(), diskScanner.nextInt());`**
`}`
`diskScanner.close();`

**`println(map.get("quicksilver"));`**
**`println(map.get("dummies"));`**
`}`

For a run of the code in [Listing 12-3](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0003), see [Figure 12-8](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0008).

![Image of a number sequence “808642” above a word “null” enclosed in a border.](images/9781394289240-fg1208.png)

[FIGURE 12-8:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0008) Running the code in [Listing 12-3](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0003).

[Listing 12-3](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0003) creates a variable whose type is `HashMap<String, Integer>`. So the map is shaped like a 2-column table, with `String` values in the first column and `Integer` values in the second column. It's the same shape as the text in [Figure 12-7](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0007). (If you're not sure why the type declaration says `<String, Integer>` instead of `<String, int>`, refer to this chapter's earlier section “[Wrap it up](#c12.xhtml_c12-sec-0008).”)

The most important methods in the `HashMap` class are `put` and `get`. You call `put` to add a row to the map and `get` to fetch a value from the map.

For example, the loop in [Listing 12-3](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0003) fills the map by calling `put` several times. Each time, the call's parameters are a `String` value (like the word `"luminous"`), and an `Integer` value (like the number 1521841). When the loop is finished reading the file in [Figure 12-7](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0007), the `map` variable's `HashMap` instance has ten word/number pairs.

To find out how many times Dr. Tatman found the word *quicksilver,* you call `map.get("quicksilver")` near the end of [Listing 12-3](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0003). According to [Figure 12-8](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0008), she found *quicksilver* 808,862 times.

The last statement in [Listing 12-3](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0003) looks for the frequency of the word *dummies*. In fact, Dr. Tatman's research saw the word *dummies* 3,392,894 times, but I didn't put that information in my `WordFrequencies.txt` file. So [Listing 12-3](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0003) doesn't add `"dummies"` to the program's hash map. As a result, the call to `map.get("dummies")` returns nothing at all. And, in Java, the word `null` means “nothing at all.”

### So many collection classes!

The `ArrayList` class that I use in many of this chapter's examples is only the tip of the Java collections iceberg. The Java library contains many collections classes, each with its own advantages. [Table 12-1](#c12.xhtml_c12-tbl-0001) contains an abbreviated list.

| **Class Name** | **Characteristic** |
| --- | --- |
| `ArrayList` | A resizable list. |
| `LinkedList` | A list of values, each having a field that points to the next one in the list. |
| `Stack` | A structure that grows from bottom to top. The structure is optimized for access to the topmost value. You can easily push a value onto the top or pop off the topmost value. |
| `Queue` | A structure that grows at one end. The structure is optimized for adding values to one end (the rear) and removing values from the other end (the front). |
| `PriorityQueue` | A structure, like a queue, that lets certain (higher-priority) values move toward the front. |
| `HashSet` | A collection containing no duplicate values in no particular order. |
| `HashMap` | A collection of key/value pairs. |

[TABLE 12-1:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-tbl-0001) Some Collection Classes

![On the web](images/ontheweb.png) To find out which collection classes best meet your needs, visit [`https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/java.base/java/util/Collection.html`](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/java.base/java/util/Collection.html). (Warning: This page is difficult to read!)

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Once again, I'd like to put you to work:

#### OUR BEST PLAYERS

Use the `Player` class from [Chapter 11](#c11.xhtml) to make an `ArrayList` of `Player` objects. Write a program to display the names of the players whose average is .100 or higher.

#### OUR BIGGEST NUMBER

Create an `ArrayList` containing `Integer` values. Then step through the values in the list to find the largest value among all values in the list. For example, if the list contains the numbers 85, 19, 0, 103, and 13, display the number 103.

#### INSERT IN ORDER

Create an `ArrayList` containing `String` values in alphabetical order. When the user types an additional word on the keyboard, the program inserts the new word into the `ArrayList` in the proper (alphabetically ordered) place.

For example, imagine that the list starts off containing the words `"cat"`, `"dog"`, `"horse"`, and `"zebra"` (in that order). After the user types the word `fish` on the keyboard (and presses Enter), the list contains the words `"cat"`, `"dog"`, `"fish"`, `"horse"`, and `"zebra"` (in that order).

To write this program, you may find the `String` class's `compareToIgnoreCase` method and the `ArrayList` class's `size` method useful. You can find out about these methods by visiting [`https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/java.base/java/lang/String.html#compareToIgnoreCase(java.lang.String)`](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/java.base/java/lang/String.html#compareToIgnoreCase(java.lang.String)) and [`https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/java.base/java/util/ArrayList.html#size()`](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/java.base/java/util/ArrayList.html#size()).

#### A REAL DICTIONARY

Modify the code in [Listing 12-3](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0003) so that the map contains ten words and their definitions. When the user types one of the ten words, the program displays that word's definition.

## Functional Programming

From 1953 to 1957, John Backus and others developed the FORTRAN programming language, which contained the basic framework for thousands of today's programming languages. The framework has come to be known as *imperative programming* because of its do-this-then-do-that nature.

A few years after the rise of FORTRAN, John McCarthy created another language, named *Lisp*. Unlike FORTRAN, the underlying framework for Lisp is *functional programming.* In a purely functional program, you avoid writing “do this, then do that.” Instead, you write things like “Here's how you'll be transforming this into that when you get around to doing the transformation.”

For one reason or another, imperative programming became the dominant mode. But in recent years, functional programming has emerged as a powerful and useful way of thinking about code.

### Problem-solving the old-fashioned way

Congratulations! Your license to sell goods over the Internet has been approved. You've set up an e-commerce site and paid for some advertising. Your inventory includes joke books and novelty prank toys. One of the toys is a can with a label that says Cashew Nuts. When someone opens it, a fake, spring-loaded snake pops out. Another of your toys is a small buzzer. When you shake someone's hand, you surprise them with a fast vibration. You also sell traditional Groucho eyeglasses. Whoever wears them appears to have bushy eyebrows and a big mustache.

In your world, the sale of a single item looks something like the stuff in [Listing 12-4](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0004). Each sale has an item and a price.

[LISTING 12-4](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fea-0004) The Sale Class

`package com.example.sales;`

`public record Sale(String item, double price) {`
`}`

To make use of the `Sale` class, you create a small program. The program totals up the sales on books. The program is shown in [Listing 12-5](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0005).

[LISTING 12-5](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fea-0005) Total Sales of Books

`import com.example.sales.Sale;`

`void main() {`
`ArrayList<Sale> sales = new ArrayList<>();`
**`var currency = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance();`**

`fillTheList(sales);`

**`double runningTotal = 0;`**
**`for (Sale sale : sales) {`**
**`if (itemIsBook(sale)) {`**
**`runningTotal += sale.price();`**
**`}`**
**`}`**
`println(`**`currency.format(runningTotal)`**`);`
`}`

**`boolean itemIsBook(Sale sale) {`**
**`return sale.item().equals("Book");`**
**`}`**

`void fillTheList(ArrayList<Sale> sales) {`
`sales.add(new Sale("Book", 10.95));`
`sales.add(new Sale("Book", 30.00));`
`sales.add(new Sale("Toy", 9.67));`
`sales.add(new Sale("Book", 20.00));`
`}`

The output of the program in [Listing 12-5](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0005) is `$60.95`. That's the total revenue from all books sold. On my computer, the combination of

`var currency = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance();`

and

`currency.format(runningTotal)`

makes that total look like a US dollar amount.

You can customize the display for your own country's currency. For example, with the code

`var currency = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance(`**`Locale.JAPAN`**`);`

the program's output is `¥61`.

### A familiar problem

The scenario in [Listing 12-5](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0005) isn't unusual. You have a collection of items (a collection of sales, perhaps). You step through the items in the collection, finding the items that meet certain criteria (the sale of a book, for example). You grab a certain value (such as the sale price) of each item that meets your criteria. Then you do something useful with the values you've grabbed (for example, adding the values together).

Here are some other examples:

*   **Step through your list of employees.** Find each employee whose performance evaluation scored 3 or higher. Give each such employee a $100 bonus and then determine the total amount of money you'll pay in bonuses.
*   **Step through your list of customers.** For each customer who has shown interest in buying a smartphone, send the customer an email about this month's discount plans.
*   **Step through the list of planets that have been discovered.** For each M-class planet, find the probability of finding intelligent life on that planet. Then find the average of all such probabilities.

This scenario is so common that it's worth finding better and better ways to deal with the scenario. One way to deal with it is to use some of the functional programming features in Java.

### Lambda expressions

In [Chapter 3](#c03.xhtml) of *Java For Dummies,* the author says,

> *In Java, a method is a list of things to do. Every method has a name, and you tell the computer to do the things in the list by using the method's name in your program.*

He's a clever fellow because, in this sentence, he's careful to use the word *method.* Yes, a method is a list of things to do, but not all to-do lists are methods, and not all to-do lists have names.

A *lambda expression* is a to-do list with no name. Here's an example:

`sale -> sale.item().equals("Book")`

When you put this expression in just the right place, the expression does the work of the `itemIsBook` method in [Listing 12-5](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0005). [Figure 12-9](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0009) brings this point home.

A lambda expression is a concise way of creating a list of things to do. The list may include only one to-do item (as in [Figure 12-9](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0009)) or any number of items:

`sale -> {`
`var compareTo = "Book";`
`return sale.item().equals(compareTo);`
`}`

![A flowchart and a code snippet. The flowchart starts with a box labeled 'sale' pointing to another box that reads 'sale.item().equals('Book')'. The code snippet defines a boolean method named 'itemIsBook' that takes a 'Sale' object as a parameter and returns the result of 'sale.item().equals('Book')'.](images/9781394289240-fg1209.png)

[FIGURE 12-9:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0009) Does the item that's being sold happen to be a book?

When you put more than one to-do item in a lambda expression, the expression must have a complete method body with curly braces, semicolons, and the `return` keyword. Only the stuff before the arrow (`->`) is abbreviated.

The lambda expression in [Figure 12-9](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0009) has only one parameter: the `sale` parameter. Can a lambda expression have more than one parameter? Of course, it can. Here's an example:

`(price1, price2) -> price1 + price2`

When you have more than one parameter, you have to surround the parameters with parentheses and separate them with commas.

[Figure 12-10](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0010) describes the new lambda expression's meaning.

![A diagram illustrating a simple function. Step 1: Take two values, call them price1 and price2\. Step 2: Return the sum of price1 and price2.](images/9781394289240-fg1210.png)

[FIGURE 12-10:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0010) Add two prices.

The lambda expression in [Figure 12-10](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0010) does (roughly) what the following method does:

`double sum(double price1, double price2) {`
`return price1 + price2;`
`}`

### The interpretation of streams

The earlier section “[Once and again](#c12.xhtml_c12-sec-0010)” introduces iterators. An iterator's `next` method spits out a collection's values, one by one. That's helpful, but Java takes this concept a step further with the notion of a stream. A *stream* is like an iterator except that, with a stream, you don't have to call a `next` method. A stream spits out a collection's values automatically. To get values from a stream, you don't call a stream's `next` method. Instead, you create a pipeline that pulls values from the stream. This pipeline solution uses concepts from functional programming.

A pipeline consists of several methods. Each method takes the data, transforms the data in some way or another, and hands its results to the next method in line. [Figure 12-11](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0011) illustrates the pipeline for this chapter's sales problem.

![A flowchart showing the process of filtering and summing book prices. The process starts with a list of items and their prices: ('Book', 10.95), ('Book', 30.00), ('Toy', 5.67), ('Book', 20.00). The first step filters out non-book items, leaving: ('Book', 10.95), ('Book', 30.00), ('Book', 20.00). The second step extracts the prices: 10.95, 30.00, 20.00\. The final step sums these prices to get a total of 60.95.](images/9781394289240-fg1211.png)

[FIGURE 12-11:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0011) A functional programming pipeline.

In [Figure 12-11](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0011), imagine a bunch of raw materials as they're transformed along an assembly line. Each downward-pointing arrow in the figure represents a method (or, metaphorically, a worker on the assembly line).

For example, in the transition from the figure's second box to the third box, a worker method (the `filter` method) sifts out sales of items that aren't books. Imagine Lucy Ricardo (in the classic “I Love Lucy” episode at the chocolate factory) standing between the second and third boxes, removing each toy from the assembly line and tossing it carelessly onto the floor.

Taken as a whole, the entire pipeline shown in [Figure 12-11](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0011) adds up the prices of books sold. [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006) contains a complete program using the streams and lambda expressions of [Figure 12-11](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0011).

[LISTING 12-6](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fea-0006) Living the Functional Way of Life

`import com.example.sales.Sale;`

`void main() {`
`ArrayList<Sale> sales = new ArrayList<>();`
`var currency = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance();`

`fillTheList(sales);`

**`double total = sales.stream()`**
**`.filter(sale -> sale.item().equals("Book"))`**
**`.map(sale -> sale.price())`**
**`.reduce(0.0, (price1, price2) -> price1 + price2);`**

`println(currency.format(total));`
`}`

`void fillTheList(ArrayList<Sale> sales) {`
`sales.add(new Sale("Book", 10.95));`
`sales.add(new Sale("Book", 30.00));`
`sales.add(new Sale("Toy", 9.67));`
`sales.add(new Sale("Book", 20.00));`
`}`

The output of the program in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006) is `$60.95` (the same as the output of [Listing 12-5](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0005)).

The boldface code in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006) is one big Java assignment statement. The stuff after the equal sign is a sequence of method calls. Each method call passes items to the next method call in line. That's how you form the pipeline.

The next few sections describe each of the method calls.

#### Getting a stream from a collection

When you do functional programming in Java, you start with a collection and call the `stream` method on that collection. This gives you a stream that feeds items from the collection to the rest of the code's pipeline. For example, in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006), the name `sales` refers to an `ArrayList`, and `sales.stream()` refers to a stream. (See [Figure 12-12](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0012).)

![A flowchart with three boxes connected by arrows. The first box contains a list of tuples: ('Book', 10.95), ('Book', 30.00), ('Toy', 9.67), ('Book', 20.00). The second box, connected to the first by an arrow, contains the same list of tuples. The third box, connected to the second by an arrow, also contains the same list of tuples. A speech bubble from the second box states, 'The stream method has no parameters](images/9781394289240-fg1212.png)

[FIGURE 12-12:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0012) Yet another streaming service.

#### Filtering the items in a stream

A `filter` method takes items from one stream and passes some of them to another stream. The `filter` method's parameter is a condition, and the items that get passed are the ones that satisfy the condition. For example, in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006), the lambda expression

`sale -> sale.item().equals("Book")`

returns `true` for a particular sale item as long as that sale happens to be a book.

[Figure 12-13](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0013) illustrates the work of the `filter` method in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006).

![A flowchart illustrating a filtering process for items with prices. The flowchart starts with a list of tuples representing items and their prices: ('Book', 10.95), ('Book', 30.00), ('Toy', 9.67), ('Book', 20.00). The first decision point filters out non-book items, removing ('Toy', 9.67). The second decision point shows the resulting list after filtering: ('Book', 10.95), ('Book', 30.00), ('Book', 20.00).](images/9781394289240-fg1213.png)

[FIGURE 12-13:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0013) We don't care about the toys.

#### Getting a value from each item in a stream

A `map` method takes each item in a stream, does something with that item, and then passes the “something” to the next stream in the pipeline. For example, with each sale, the `map` method in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006) gets the price of the sale and passes that price onward.
