[Figure 12-14](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0014) illustrates the work of the `map` method in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006).

#### Summarizing the items in a stream

A `reduce` method combines a stream's items. The reduce call in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006) adds all the values from its incoming stream — the stream that comes from the `map` method. That incoming stream has the prices of all the books from the original `sales` list. (See [Figure 12-15](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0015).)

![A flowchart with two sections. The top section contains a box labeled '3' with text listing book prices: (“Book\”, 10.95), (“Book\”, 30.00), (“Book\”, 20.00). The bottom section contains a box labeled '4' with text listing just the prices: 10.95, 30.00, 20.00.](images/9781394289240-fg1214.png)

[FIGURE 12-14:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0014) Getting the price from each book sale.

![A schematic diagram of a mechanical or structural system with labeled components and dimensions. The diagram includes two rectangular sections connected by a narrow passage. The upper section has dimensions labeled as 10.95, 30.00, and 20.00\. The lower section has a dimension labeled as 60.95\. There are also two circular labels with numbers 4 and 5, indicating specific points or components.](images/9781394289240-fg1215.png)

[FIGURE 12-15:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0015) Getting the total price of all book sales.

Java's `reduce` method does more than just scrunch numbers together. The method takes two parameters:

*   The first parameter is an initial value.

    In [Figure 12-15](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0015), the initial value is `0.0`.

*   The second parameter is a function (usually, a lambda expression) that takes two of its own parameters and returns yet another value. For example, in [Figure 12-15](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0015), the lambda expression takes two parameters, `price1` and `price2`, and returns the sum:

    `(price1, price2) -> price1 + price2`

The lambda expression combines the values from the incoming stream. The initial value serves as the starting point for all the combining. So, in [Figure 12-15](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0015), the `reduce` method performs three additions. (See [Figure 12-16](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0016).)

![The image shows three white circles with black plus signs in the center, arranged diagonally from the top left to the bottom right on a black background. This arrangement is interesting as it might represent a pattern or a specific design choice, potentially used in various contexts such as diagrams, user interfaces, or symbolic representations.](images/9781394289240-fg1216.png)

[FIGURE 12-16:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0016) The reduce method adds two values at a time.

For comparison, imagine calling the method

`reduce(10.0, (value1, value2) -> value1 * value2)`

with the stream whose values include `3.0`, `2.0`, and `5.0`. The resulting action is shown in [Figure 12-17](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0017).

![The image consists of three white circles with black asterisks (*) in their centers. These circles are arranged in a diagonal line from the top left to the bottom right against a black background. This visual arrangement might be used in various contexts, such as design patterns, user interfaces, or symbolic representations.](images/9781394289240-fg1217.png)

[FIGURE 12-17:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0017) The reduce method multiplies values from an incoming stream.

The `reduce` method plays a special role in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006). Every method in a stream performs a kind of operation: either an intermediate operation or a terminal operation. An *intermediate* operation is one that returns another stream. A *terminal* operation is one that either returns something other than a stream or returns nothing (`void`).

A chain of calls like the one in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006) begins with one or more intermediate operations and ends with a single terminal operation. For example, in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006), `filter` and `map` perform intermediate operations, and `reduce` performs a terminal operation. In a sense, the terminal operation pulls values from all the intermediate operations. When Java tries to find the value of `total`, the terminal `reduce` operation eagerly asks for `price1` and `price2`. At that moment, the lazy, intermediate `filter` and `map` operations wake up and start pumping out values.

![Remember](images/remember.png) If you don't put a terminal operation call at the end of your call chain, Java doesn't execute any of the intermediate calls. For an example, see the Try It Out paragraph at the end of this chapter.

### Why bother?

The chain of method calls in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006) accomplishes everything that the loop in [Listing 12-5](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0005) accomplishes. But the code in [Figure 12-14](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0014) uses concepts from functional programming. What's the big deal? Are you better off with [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006) than with [Listing 12-5](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0005)?

You are. For the past several years, the big trend in chip design has been *multicore* processors. With several cores, a processor can execute several statements at the same time, speeding up a program's execution by a factor of 2 or 4 or 8 or even more. Programs run much faster if you divide the work among several cores. But how do you divide the work?

You can modify the imperative code in [Listing 12-5](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0005). For example, with some fancy features, you can hand different loop iterations to different cores. But the resulting code is messy. For the code to work properly, you have to micromanage the loop iterations, checking carefully to make sure that the final `runningTotal` value is correct.

In contrast, the functional code is easy to modify. To take advantage of multicore processors, you change *only one word* in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006)!

`double total = sales.`**`parallelStream`**`()`
`.filter(sale -> sale.item().equals("Book"))`
`.map(sale -> sale.price())`
`.reduce(0.0, (price1, price2) -> price1 + price2);`

In [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006), the `stream()` method call creates a serial stream. With a *serial* stream, Java does its processing one sale at a time. But a call to `parallelStream()` creates a slightly different kind of stream: a parallel stream. With a *parallel* stream, Java divides the work among the number of cores in the computer's processor (or according to some other useful measure of computing power). If you have 4 million sales and four cores, each core processes 1 million of the sales.

Each core works independently of the others, and each core dumps its result into a final `reduce` method. The `reduce` method combines the cores' results into a final tally. In the best possible scenario, all the work gets done in one-fourth the time it would take with an ordinary serial stream.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) When you read the preceding paragraph, don't gloss over the phrase *best possible scenario.* Parallelism isn't magic. And sometimes, parallelism isn't your friend. Consider the situation in which you have only 20 sale amounts to tally. The time it takes to divide the problem into four groups of 5 sales each far exceeds the amount of time you save in using all four cores. In addition, some problems don't lend themselves to parallel processing. Imagine that the price of an item depends on the number of similar items being sold. In that case, you can't divide the problem among four independently operating cores. If you try, each core has to know what the other cores are doing. You lose the advantage of having four threads of execution.

### Method references

Take a critical look at the last lambda expression in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006):

`(price1, price2) -> price1 + price2)`

This expression does roughly the same work as a `sum` method. (In fact, you can find a `sum` method's declaration in the earlier section “[Lambda expressions](#c12.xhtml_c12-sec-0026).”) If your choice is between typing a 3-line `sum` method and typing a 1-line lambda expression, you'll probably choose the lambda expression. But what if you have a third alternative? Rather than type your own `sum` method, you can refer to an existing `sum` method. Using an existing method is the quickest and safest thing to do.

As luck would have it, Java's `Double` class contains a static `sum` method. You don't have to create your own `sum` method. If you run the following code:

`double i = 5.0, j = 7.0;`
`println(Double.sum(i, j));`

the computer displays `12.0`. So, rather than type the `price1 + price2` lambda expression in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006), you can use two colons to create a *method reference* — an expression that refers to an existing method.

`sales.stream()`
`.filter(sale -> sale.item().equals("Book"))`
`.map(sale -> sale.price())`
`.reduce(0.0,` **`Double :: sum`**`);`

The expression `Double :: sum` refers to the `sum` method belonging to Java's `Double` class. When you use this `Double :: sum` method reference, you do the same thing that the last lambda expression does in [Listing 12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006). Everybody is happy.

For information about static methods, see [Chapter 11](#c11.xhtml).

### Some black sheep among the lambdas

In this chapter, I've saved the worst for last. I refuse to give the following code a listing number. Instead, I refer to it as the “[Ugly Code](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0007).”

[UGLY CODE:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fea-0007) Lambda Expressions with Side Effects

**`// This code works correctly, but its style is terrible.`**

`import com.example.sales.Sale;`

**`double runningTotal = 0.0;`**

`void main() {`
`ArrayList<Sale> sales = new ArrayList<>();`
`var currency = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance();`

`fillTheList(sales);`

`var kindOfItem = "Book";`

`sales.stream()`
`.filter(sale -> sale.item().equals(`**`kindOfItem`**`))`
`.map(sale -> sale.price())`
**`.forEach(price -> addToRunningTotal(price));`**

`println(currency.format(runningTotal));`
`}`

**`void addToRunningTotal(double price) {`**
**`runningTotal += price;`**
**`}`**

`void fillTheList(ArrayList<Sale> sales) {`
`sales.add(new Sale("Book", 10.95));`
`sales.add(new Sale("Book", 30.00));`
`sales.add(new Sale("Toy", 9.67));`
`sales.add(new Sale("Book", 20.00));`
`}`

The `forEach` method in this ugly code does what its name suggests. It executes its lambda expression for every sale that comes its way. The ugly code uses `forEach` to tally up the prices of all book sales.

The output of this code is the same as the code in [Listings 12-5](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0005) and [12-6](#c12.xhtml_c12-fea-0006). But there's more to writing code than getting the correct output. [Figure 12-18](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0018) compares the three lambda expressions in the ugly code's stream.

Functional programming enthusiasts like the middle expression in [Figure 12-18](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0018) — the `sale -> sale.price()` expression. That expression gets information from its parameter (`sale`) and returns a value (the `price` of that `sale`) to the rest of the code.

![A diagram with two sections labeled 'Parameters' on the left and 'Return values' on the right. The 'Parameters' section lists 'sale,' 'sale,' and 'price.' The 'Return values' section lists 'true or false' and 'a double value.'](images/9781394289240-fg1218.png)

[FIGURE 12-18:](#c12.xhtml_rc12-fig-0018) Functions with and without side effects.

Functional programmers don't like the other two expressions in [Figure 12-18](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0018). That's because the other two expressions have side effects. A *side effect* takes place when a method or lambda expression refers to a value other than a parameter or its return value.

Side effects make programs difficult to understand. Programs without side effects have fewer bugs than programs with side effects. So, in functional programming, a side effect is considered a second-class citizen, a last resort, a tactic you use when you can't simply take in a parameter and return a result.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) You can always try the programming challenges that you dream up on your own. If you have no ideas to give you practice with functional programming, I have a couple of suggestions for you:

#### BE GENEROUS

Each employee has a name and a performance evaluation score. Find the total amount of money you'll pay in bonuses if you give a $100 bonus to each employee whose score is 3 or higher. In your code, make optimal use of lambda expressions and method references.

#### SAVE TIME

Each recipe has a name, a list of ingredients (some of which involve meat products), and an estimated preparation time. Find the average time estimate for cooking one of the vegetarian recipes.

#### INTERMEDIATE METHODS ARE JUST PLAIN LAZY

In the earlier section “[The interpretation of streams](#c12.xhtml_c12-sec-0027),” I warn that Java does nothing when you end a chain without a terminal operation. To double-check that I'm not lying about this, run the following code:

`void main() {`
`ArrayList<String> words =`
`new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList("I", "am", "happy."));`

`var sentence = words.stream()`
`.peek(word -> println(word))`
`.reduce("", (word1, word2) -> word1 + " " + word2);`

`println(sentence);`
`}`

The lambda expression in Java's `peek` method never returns a value. So, aside from executing the lambda expression, `peek` doesn't modify the stream in any way. The `peek` method simply passes the stream's values on to the next method in line.

But `peek` performs an intermediate operation — not a terminal operation. So rerun the code with the following changes. You'll find out what happens when you end a chain with an intermediate operation:

`var sentence = words.stream()`
`.peek(word -> println(word))`**`;`**
**`//`** `.reduce("", (word1, word2) -> word1 + " " + word2);`

**`//`** `println(sentence);`

#### STREAMLESS VARMINT

You don't need streams to make use of Java's lambda expressions. Run this code for a glimpse at the possibilities:

`void main() {`
`println(change("Hello", string -> {return string + "!";}));`
`}`

`String change(String str, Function<String, String> func) {`
`return func.apply(str);`
`}`

Chapter 13

# Using Arrays to Juggle Values

IN THIS CHAPTER

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Dealing with several values at a time**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Writing to disk files**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Running a program with different values each time**

Welcome to the Java Motel! No haughty bellhops, no overpriced room service, none of the silly jokes that I write in other chapters. Just a nice room at a darn good price!

## Getting Your Ducks All in a Row

The Java Motel, with its ten comfortable rooms, sits in a quiet place off the main highway. Aside from a small, separate office, the motel is just one long row of ground-floor rooms. Each room is easily accessible from the spacious front parking lot.

Oddly enough, the motel’s rooms are numbered 0 through 9\. I could say that the numbering is a fluke — something to do with the builder’s original design plan. But the truth is that starting with 0 makes the examples in this chapter easier to write. Computer programming structures start counting from 0 onward. That's all there is to it.

Anyway, you’re trying to keep track of the number of guests in each room. To store all the numbers, you use an array.

An *array* is a row of values, like the row of rooms in a 1-floor motel. To picture the array, look at the Java Motel in [Figure 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0001).

![“A visual representation of an array with indices from 0 to 9\. The elements of the array are 0, 1, 4, 2, 0, 1, 4, 3, 2, 1\. The element at index 6 is highlighted, indicating it holds the value 4\. Labels are present pointing to the value 4 and index 6, demonstrating the array's element accessing method.”](images/9781394289240-fg1301.png)

[FIGURE 13-1:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fig-0001) An abstract snapshot of rooms in the Java Motel.

In the lingo of this chapter, the entire row of rooms is called an *array.* Each room in the array is called a *component* of the array (also known as an array *element*). Each component has two numbers associated with it:

*   The room number (a number from 0 to 9), which is called an *index* of the array
*   A number of guests, which is a *value* stored in a component of the array

To declare an array with ten `int` values in it, you can write one fairly short statement:

`int[] guests = new int[10];`

If your declaration is inside of a method, you can use `var`, like so:

`var guests = new int[10];`

In some situations, it's handy to create an array in two separate steps, like this:

`class MyClass {`

`int[] guests; // The var keyword isn't permitted here.`

`void myMethod() {`
`guests = new int[10]; // This must be inside a method body.`
`// …`
`}`

`// …`
`}`

In each of these code snippets, notice the use of the number 10\. This number tells the computer to make the `guests` array have ten components. Each component of the array has a name of its own. The starting component is named *guests[0],* the next is named *guests[1],* and so on. The last of the ten components is named *guests[9].*

![Remember](images/remember.png) A declaration like

`int[] guests;`

doesn't reserve any space to store `int` values. It only reserves the variable name `guests` to eventually refer to an array of `int` values;

![Tip](images/tip.png) In an array declaration, you can put the square brackets before or after the variable name. In other words, you can write `int[] guests` or `int guests[]`. Java creates the same `guests` variable no matter which form you use.

### How to book hotel guests

Business is booming at the Java Motel. (Most people prefer the Java Motel over its competitor — the C-Side Resort and Motor Lodge.) As the Java Motel's owner, manager, and system administrator, you have a file containing the number of guests in each room. A sample file named `GuestList.txt` is shown in [Figure 13-2](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0002).

![“A sequence of numbers displayed in a rectangular box: 1, 4, 2, 0, 2, 1, 4, 3, 8, 2.”](images/9781394289240-fg1302.png)

[FIGURE 13-2:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fig-0002) The `GuestList.txt` file.

After you've made a file, you can call on the `Scanner` class to get values from the file. The code is shown in [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001), and the resulting output is in [Figure 13-3](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0003).

![“A table displaying two columns labeled 'PROCESS' and 'COUNTER'. The 'PROCESS' column lists numbers from 0 to 9, while the 'COUNTER' column lists values 5, 4, 2, 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 0, and 2 respectively.”](images/9781394289240-fg1303.png)

[FIGURE 13-3:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fig-0003) Running the code from [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001).

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) For tips on reading from disk files, refer to [Chapter 9](#c09.xhtml).

[LISTING 13-1](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fea-0001) Filling an Array with Values

`import module java.base;`

`public class ShowGuests {`

`public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {`
**`int[] guests = new int[10];`**
`var diskScanner = new Scanner(new File("GuestList.txt"));`

**`for (int roomNum = 0; roomNum < 10; roomNum++) {`**
**`guests[roomNum] =`** `diskScanner.nextInt();`
`}`

`diskScanner.close();`

`System.out.println("Room\tGuests");`
**`for (int roomNum = 0; roomNum < 10; roomNum++) {`**
`System.out.print(roomNum);`
`System.out.print("\t");`
**`System.out.println(guests[roomNum]);`**
`}`
`}`
`}`

The code in [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001) has two `for` loops: The first loop reads numbers of guests, and the second loop writes numbers of guests.

### Tab stops and other special things

In [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001), some calls to `print` and `println` use the `\t` escape sequence. It's called an *escape sequence* because you escape from displaying the letter `t` on the screen. Instead, the characters `\t` stand for a tab. The computer moves forward to the next tab stop before printing any more characters. Java has a few of these handy escape sequences. Some of them are shown in [Table 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-tbl-0001).

| **Sequence** | **Meaning** |
| --- | --- |
| `\b` | Backspace |
| `\t` | Horizontal tab |
| `\n` | Line feed |
| `\f` | Form feed |
| `\r` | Carriage return |
| `\"` | Double quote `"` |
| `\'` | Single quote `'` |
| `\\` | Backslash `\` |

[TABLE 13-1:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-tbl-0001) Escape Sequences

[Figure 13-3](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0003) looks good because I ran the program in a Mac Terminal window. In the Windows command prompt and Macintosh Terminal, tab stops are eight spaces apart. But in some environments, tab stops are only four spaces apart. With four-space stops, you don't see the sensible alignment that appears earlier, in [Figure 13-3](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0003). If you want the columns to align no matter where your program runs, replace the tab characters with blank spaces in [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001). (Of course, if you're fussy, you have to check for numbers with more than one digit. But that's another story.)

### Make life easy for yourself

Besides what you see in [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001), you have another way to fill an array in Java: with an *array initializer.* When you use an array initializer, you don't even have to tell the computer how many components the array has. The computer figures it out for you.

Here's another trick: You can also use Java's enhanced `for` loop (from [Chapter 12](#c12.xhtml)) to step through an array. [Listing 13-2](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0002) has an array initializer and an enhanced `for` loop.

[LISTING 13-2](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fea-0002) Short and Sweet Code!

`void main() {`
`int[] guests =` **`{1, 4, 2, 0, 2, 1, 4, 3, 0, 2}`**`;`

`println("Room\tGuests");`

`int roomNum = 0;`
`for (int numGuests : guests) {`
`print(roomNum++);`
`print("\t");`
`println(numGuests);`
`}`
`}`

[Listing 13-2](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0002) produces the same output as [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001), and [Listing 13-2](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0002) is much easier to read. So why would anyone bother with the code in [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001)? There are several reasons:

*   **Putting data in a program, as I do in** [**Listing** **13-2**](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0002)**, is seldom a good idea.**

    Time goes by. Hotel guests come and go. In this chapter's later “[Writing to a file](#c13.xhtml_c13-sec-0016)” section, you find out how to change the numbers in the `GuestList.txt` file. That's okay because people write to files all the time. But changing the numbers in [Listing 13-2](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0002) would be another story. No one wants to edit a Java program whenever a hotel guest arrives or leaves.

*   **Array initializers aren't versatile.**

    There's a good reason for calling them *initializers*. You can use this feature to initialize an array (as in [Listing 13-2](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0002)), but you can't use it to assign values to an existing array variable. For example, the initialization in [Listing 13-2](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0002) is fine and dandy. But the following code is worthy of a slap on the wrist:

    **`// Bad code:`**
    `int[] guests;`
    `guests = {1, 4, 2, 0, 2, 1, 4, 3, 0, 2};`

*   **An enhanced** `for`**loop, like the one in** [**Listing** **13-2**](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0002)**, doesn't track the array's indices.**

    In [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001), both loops have a counter variable named `roomNum`. That makes it easy to print a room number on each line in the output. (Refer to [Figure 13-3](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0003).)

    But an enhanced `for` loop doesn't have its own counter variable. To print the value of `roomNum` in [Listing 13-2](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0002), you have to create your own `roomNum` variable and remember to update it each time through the loop. It's only a tiny amount of extra work. But, in a complicated program, this extra work makes it easier to make a mistake. (I know. I've made those mistakes.)

*   **The code in** [**Listing** **13-1**](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0002) **is a traditional Java `main` method. In comparison, the code in** [**Listing** **13-2**](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0002) **is a simple source file.**

    That makes [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001) more verbose than [Listing 13-2](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0002).

    So, why should you have to wade through the `public class ShowGuests { public static` *`blah, blah`* `…` business in [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001)? One reason is that most Java main methods are written that way. In fact, before Java 21, all of Java's main methods were written that way. That's almost 30 years of Java code with lengthy, long-winded main methods. I can't write a Java book without highlighting some of those main methods.

#### SPELL IT BACKWARD

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Modify the code in [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001) so that it displays the rooms in reverse room-number order (starting with Room 9 and working down to Room 0).

#### MAKE YOUR MARKS

First, the user types five numbers on the keyboard. Then the program displays five lines of characters, each line having the same number of asterisks as one of the user's numbers. A run of the program might look like this:

`5`
`3`
`9`
`0`
`10`
`*****`
`***`
`*********`

`**********`

#### FIND THE BIGGEST

Add code to the end of [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001) so that the program finds the room(s) with the largest number of guests. The program's new output may look something like this:

`4 guests in Room 1`
`4 guests in Room 6`

To do this, have a variable named `largestSoFar`. Examine the array's elements one by one and then update the value of `largestSoFar` whenever you find a new, record-high number.

#### FIND THE TOTAL

Add code to the end of [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001) so that the program reports the total number of people staying at the motel. To do this, have a variable named `runningTotal`. Examine the array's values one by one, adding each such value to the big `runningTotal` value.

## Array versus ArrayList: The Ultimate Showdown

This chapter's arrays are similar to the `ArrayList` collection in [Chapter 12](#c12.xhtml). So, if you need a structure that stores several values, which structure do you choose? An array or an `ArrayList`? It all depends on what you're trying to do.

*   **Reasons for choosing an** `ArrayList`**:**
    *   An `ArrayList` can grow and shrink.

        After creating an `ArrayList` instance, you can add as many values to it as you want. The only limit is the amount of your Java application's memory. That's a very high limit.

        In contrast, every array has a fixed size. When you declare an array into existence, you tell Java how many elements the array has. You can't change your mind later in the program's code. After executing the line

        `int[] guests = new int[10];`

        in [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001), you can't put more than ten values into the `guests` array. Java simply won't allow it.

    *   An `ArrayList` has a rich set of helpful methods.

        For example, in [Chapter 12](#c12.xhtml), you insert `"paradox"` into position 2 of the `vocabulary` list by executing only one statement: `vocabulary.add(2, "paradox")`. But, to insert a value into an array, you need several loop iterations. (This chapter's later “[Array Madness](#c13.xhtml_c13-sec-0018)” section explains all of it.)

*   **Reasons for choosing an array:**
    *   Arrays are more efficient. The more you're able to do, the longer it takes to do anything at all. Sure, an `ArrayList` is more versatile than an array, but that (unfortunately) means operations on an `ArrayList` tend to be slower than the corresponding array operations. If you can live with having a fixed number of elements, an array may be the better choice.
    *   Arrays can store primitive types.

        [Chapter 12](#c12.xhtml) says that an `ArrayList` can't store a plain, old `int` value. Instead, you have to put big, fat instances of the `Integer` class in an `ArrayList`, like so:

        `ArrayList<`**`Integer`**`> list = new ArrayList<>();`

        But the array in [Listing 13-1](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0001) has no problem storing primitive `int` values:

        `int[] guests = new` **`int`**`[10];`

        This can make an array less bulky than the corresponding `ArrayList`.

*   **Other ways that an array differs from an** `ArrayList`**:**
    *   The notation for handling an array is different from the notation for handling `ArrayList` instances.

        In particular, arrays use square brackets — something you don't see with classes like `ArrayList`.

    *   Arrays are built into the Java programming language.

        The `ArrayList` class definition is part of Java's API, which (technically) is separate from the Java language itself. That's one reason some programs start with import `java.util.ArrayList`. In contrast, arrays are Java language features.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) What makes `ArrayList` more versatile than an array? Under the hood, Java stores `ArrayList` values in a fixed-size array. When you execute code to add elements beyond the array's size, Java automatically creates a larger array and copies the old array's values into it. That's nice of Java. Isn't it?

## Do You Have a Room?

You're sitting behind the desk at the Java Motel. Look! Here comes a party of five. These people want a room, so you need software that checks whether a room is vacant. If one is, the software modifies the `GuestList.txt` file (refer to [Figure 13-2](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0002)) by replacing the number 0 with the number 5\. As luck would have it, the software is on your hard drive. The software is shown in [Listing 13-3](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0003).

[LISTING 13-3](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fea-0003) Do You Have a Room?

`void main() throws IOException {`
`int[] guests = new int[10];`
`int roomNum;`

`var keyboard = new Scanner(System.in);`
**`var diskScanner = new Scanner(new File("GuestList.txt"));`**
`for (roomNum = 0; roomNum < 10; roomNum++) {`
`guests[roomNum] = diskScanner.nextInt();`
`}`
**`diskScanner.close();`**

**`roomNum = 0;`**
**`while (roomNum < 10 && guests[roomNum] != 0) {`**
**`roomNum++;`**
**`}`**

`if (roomNum == 10) {`
`println("Sorry, no v cancy");`
`} else {`
`print("How many people for room ");`
`print(roomNum);`
`print("? ");`

`guests[roomNum] = keyboard.nextInt();`

**`var listOut = new PrintStream("GuestList.txt");`**
`for (roomNum = 0; roomNum < 10; roomNum++) {`
`listOut.print(guests[roomNum]);`
`listOut.print(" ");`
`}`
**`listOut.close();`**
`}`
`}`

[Figure 13-4](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0004) shows three runs of the code in [Listing 13-3](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0003). The motel starts with two vacant rooms: Rooms 3 and 8\. (Remember that the room numbers start with Room 0.) The first time you run the code, you put five people into Room 3\. The second time you run the code, you put a party of ten in Room 8\. (What a party!) The third time you run the code, you have no more vacant rooms. When the program discovers this, it displays the message `Sorry, no v cancy`, omitting at least one letter, in the tradition of all motel neon signs.

![A sequence of three program runs with a file named GuestList.txt containing numbers representing the number of people in different rooms. The first run shows the sequence: 1, 4, 2, 0, 2, 1, 4, 3, 0, 2\. The program responds to the query 'How many people for room 3?' with '5.' The second run displays the sequence: 1, 4, 2, 5, 2, 1, 4, 3, 0, 2\. The program answers the question 'How many people for room 8?' with '10.' The third run lists the sequence: 1, 4, 2, 5, 2, 1, 4, 3, 10, 2, and the program responds with 'Sorry, no very fancy.'](images/9781394289240-fg1304.png)

[FIGURE 13-4:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fig-0004) Filling vacancies.

![On the web](images/ontheweb.png) In [Listing 13-3](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0003), the condition `roomNum < 10 && guests[roomNum] != 0` can be tricky. If you move things around and write **guests[roomNum] != 0 && roomNum < 10**, you can get yourself into lots of trouble. For details, see this book's website ([`https://javafordummies.allmycode.com`](http://javafordummies.allmycode.com/)).

![Tip](images/tip.png) Every array has a built-in `length` field. An array's *length* is the number of components in the array. For example, in [Listing 13-3](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0003), you can replace the conditions in the `for` statements as follows:

`for (roomNum = 0;` **`roomNum < guests.length`**`; roomNum++)`

### Writing to a file

The code in [Listing 13-3](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0003) uses tricks from other chapters and sections of this book. The code's only brand-new feature is the use of `PrintStream` to write to a disk file. Think about any example in this book that calls `System.out.print` or `out.println` or their variants. What's *really* going on when you call one of these methods?

The thing called `System.out` is an object. The object is defined in the Java API. In fact, `System.out` is an instance of a class named `java.io.PrintStream` (or just `PrintStream`, to its close friends). Now each object created from the `PrintStream` class has methods named `print` and `println`. Just as each `Account` instance in [Listing 7-4](#c07.xhtml_c07-fea-0004) (over in [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml)) has a `display` method, so the `PrintStream` instance named `out` has `print` and `println` methods. When you call `System.out.println`, you're calling a method that belongs to a `PrintStream` instance.

Okay, so what of it? Well, `System.out` always stands for some text area on your computer screen. If you create your own `PrintStream` object and you make that object refer to a disk file, that `PrintStream` object refers to the disk file. When you call that object's `print` method, you write text to a file on your hard drive.

In [Listing 13-3](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0003), when you say

`var listOut = new PrintStream("GuestList.txt");`

`listOut.print(guests[roomNum]);`
`listOut.print(" ");`

you're telling Java to write text to a file on your hard drive: the `GuestList.txt` file.

That's how you update the count of guests staying in the hotel. When you call `listOut.print` for the number of guests in Room 3, you may print the number 5\. So, in [Figure 13-4](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0004), a number in the `GuestList.txt` file changes from 0 to 5\. Then in [Figure 13-4](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0004), you run the program a second time. When the program gets data from the newly written `GuestList.txt` file, Room 3 is no longer vacant. This time, the program suggests Room 8.

![Tip](images/tip.png) This is more of an observation than a tip. Say that you want to *read* data from a file named `Employees.txt`. To do this, you make a scanner. You call `new Scanner(new File("Employees.txt"))`. If you accidentally call `new Scanner("Employees.txt")` without the `new File` part, the call doesn't connect to your `Employees.txt` file. But notice how you prepare to *write* data to a file. You make a `PrintStream` instance by calling `new PrintStream("GuestList.txt")`. You don't use `new File` anywhere in the call. If you goof and accidentally include `new File`, the Java compiler becomes angry, jumps out, and bites you.

### What else can go wrong?

In any programming language, connecting to a file is a tricky business. You never know for sure whether the file exists. So, what happens when one of your constructors refers to a particular file? The answer depends partly on which constructor you call:

*   **If you call** `new Scanner(new File("GuestList.txt"))`**and Java can't find the** `GuestList.txt`**file, Java says** `FileNotFoundException`**.**

    You can create workarounds for things like the `FileNotFoundException`. To read all about it, see [Chapter 14](#c14.xhtml).

*   **If you call** `new PrintStream("GuestList.txt")`**and Java can't find a** `GuestList.txt`**file, Java makes a new** `GuestList.txt`**file on your computer's hard drive.**

    The new file starts off being empty. It's up to you to put text (or some other kind of content) into it.

    Here's something that may surprise you.

*   **If you call** `new PrintStream("GuestList.txt")`**and Java *can* find a** `GuestList.txt`**file on your computer's hard drive, Java removes all content from that file.**

    One way or another, after calling `new PrintStream("GuestList.txt")`**,** your hard drive has an empty `GuestList.txt` file.

[Listing 13-3](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0003) reads from a `GuestList.txt` file and writes to the `GuestList.txt` file. It's important to make sure that these two operations don't collide with one another. [Figure 13-5](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0005) shows what goes wrong if you call `new PrintStream("GuestList.txt")` before you've read all the values you need.

![“A Java code snippet demonstrating file handling. The code uses a Scanner object to read from a file named GuestList.txt and a PrintStream object to write to the same file. It initializes a for loop that iterates through indices 0 to 9, but since the file is empty, no integers are read into the guests array.”](images/9781394289240-fg1305.png)

[FIGURE 13-5:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fig-0005) Calling `new PrintStream` too soon.

In [Listing 13-3](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0003), I'm careful to separate the reading of values in `GuestList.txt` from the writing of new values to `GuestList.txt`. Now you know why.

## Array Madness

Near the start of this chapter, I write, “… to insert a value into an array, you need several loop iterations.” This section's example ([Listing 13-4](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0004)) brings that point home.

[LISTING 13-4](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fea-0004) Squeezing in a Number

`void main() {`
`int[] scores = {75, 80, 87, 88, 90, 95, 99, -1, -1, -1};`
`println(Arrays.toString(scores));`

`for (int i = 6; i >= 2; i--) {`
`scores[i + 1] = scores[i];`
`}`
`scores[2] = 85;`

`println(Arrays.toString(scores));`
`}`

A run of the program in [Listing 13-4](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0004) is shown in [Figure 13-6](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0006).

![“Two identical sequences of numbers are displayed: 75, 80, 87, 88, 90, 95, 99, -1, -1, followed by 75, 80, 87, 88, 90, 95, 99, -1\. This repetition may indicate a pattern or regularity in the data.”](images/9781394289240-fg1306.png)

[FIGURE 13-6:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fig-0006) Inserting a number: Before and After.

The code in [Listing 13-4](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0004) keeps track of test scores in my Java programming college course. I have ten students in the course. But, because I absolutely despise grading tests, I start the program in [Listing 13-4](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0004) with only seven tests graded. In the array initializer, the last three `–1` values are placeholders. I'll overwrite these values when I finally muster the energy to grade the last three students' tests. Without these placeholders, I would never be able to grow the array beyond the first seven entries. (I could create a loop to copy the `scores` array's values into a larger array, but that would be cumbersome.)

In the middle of [Listing 13-4](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0004), I drag my reluctant carcass to my desk and grade one more test. The eighth student scores an 85\. I want to keep the array's values in increasing order. So, before inserting that 85 into the array, I have to move all the higher scores over by one position. That's what happens in the loop in [Listing 13-4](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0004) — the loop that goes from index 6 down to index 2.

Moving five numbers from one position to another in an array is no big deal. But imagine an array with 945,847,523 elements. Inserting a value near the beginning of the array may require hundreds of millions of loop iterations. That's awful!

If you have to do lots of insertions into a bunch of values, you probably want to use Java's `LinkedList` collections class or something like it. For more information, visit [`https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/java.base/java/util/LinkedList.html`](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/java.base/java/util/LinkedList.html).

![Tip](images/tip.png) In [Listing 13-4](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0004), Java's `Arrays.toString` method saves you from having to print array values one by one in a loop. That's quite convenient. But, for nicer-looking output, you probably don't want to use `Arrays.toString`. Instead, you go back to printing values one by one.

## Return to Sanity

If you read the previous section (the one titled “[Array Madness](#c13.xhtml_c13-sec-0018)”), you know how much whining and complaining I do about grading tests. In this section, I quiet down and run another program to record test scores. This program (the one in [Listing 13-5](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0005)) illustrates a few more features of arrays.

[LISTING 13-5](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fea-0005) A Few Tricks Using Arrays

`void main() {`
`var keyboard = new Scanner(System.in);`

`print("How many students? ");`
**`int arraySize = keyboard.nextInt();`**
**`int[] scores = new int[arraySize];`**

`println("Enter " + arraySize + " students' scores:");`
`for (int i = 0; i < arraySize; i++) {`
`scores[i] = keyboard.nextInt();`
`}`

**`Arrays.sort(scores);`**

`println(Arrays.toString(scores));`
`}`

An array's size never changes. But that doesn't mean you have to know the array's size when you write the code. In [Listing 13-5](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0005), the array's size is a variable whose value isn't known until a run of the program. In [Figure 13-7](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0007), I set `arraySize` to 10.

![“A text-based interaction prompting for the number of students, with the user inputting '10'. Subsequently, the scores entered are: 75, 60, 87, 88, 90, 95, 59, 85, 98, and 77\. The scores are then sorted and displayed in ascending order: [75, 77, 80, 85, 87, 88, 90, 95].”](images/9781394289240-fg1307.png)

[FIGURE 13-7:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fig-0007) Entering students' test scores.

The program prompts me for ten test scores. I type seven of them and press Enter. Then I stall for several hours. While the program waits patiently in a window on my computer screen, I drink some coffee, watch a few videos, and call a long-lost friend. After all that, I grade another student's test. I type the student's grade (85) and press Enter. Sometime the next day, I finally grade the last two students' tests. With much relief, I type the numbers 98 and 77 and then press Enter.

The program responds by calling the `Arrays` class's `sort` method. At the start of this chapter, I write that the `ArrayList` class has many more useful methods than Java's arrays have. That's true, but there's a `java.util.Arrays` class, and that class has some goodies of its own. For a look at some of these methods, visit [`https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/java.base/java/util/Arrays.html`](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/java.base/java/util/Arrays.html).

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) The `sort` method is a static member of the `Arrays` class. That's why you write `Arrays.sort` and not `scores.sort`.

#### USING THE ARRAYS CLASS

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Run this code to discover some useful methods in the `java.util.Arrays` package:

`void main() {`
`int[] guests = {1, 4, 2, 0, 2, 1, 4, 3, 0, 2};`
`println(guests); // Not very useful!`
`println(Arrays.toString(guests));`

`int[] cSideGuests = new int[10];`
`Arrays.fill(cSideGuests, 0);`
`println(Arrays.toString(cSideGuests));`

`Arrays.fill(cSideGuests, 5, 7, 2);`
`println(Arrays.toString(cSideGuests));`

`println(Arrays.equals(guests, cSideGuests));`

`println(Arrays.binarySearch(guests, 3));`

`Arrays.setAll(cSideGuests, i -> i + 1);`
`println(Arrays.toString(cSideGuests));`
`}`

## Arrays of Objects

The Java Motel is open for business, now with improved guest registration software! The people who brought you this chapter's first section are always scratching their heads, looking for the best ways to improve their services. With some ideas from object-oriented programming, they've started thinking in terms of a `Room` class.

“And what,” you ask, “would a `Room` instance look like?” That's easy. A `Room` instance has three properties: the number of guests in the room, the room rate, and a smoking/nonsmoking stamp. [Figure 13-8](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0008) illustrates the situation.

![“A series of nine vertical panels, each labeled with a number from 0 to 9 at the bottom. Every panel features a number at the top, a value in the middle, and an icon of a hand pointing to a pie chart. The values displayed in the middle of the panels are 60.00 for panels 0 to 6, and 100.00 for panels 7 to 9.”](images/9781394289240-fg1308.png)

[FIGURE 13-8:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fig-0008) Another abstract snapshot of rooms in the Java Motel.

[Listing 13-6](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0006) shows the code that describes the `Room` class. As promised, each instance of the `Room` class has three fields: the `guests`, `rate`, and `smoking` fields. (A `false` value for the `smoking` field indicates a nonsmoking room.) In addition, the entire `Room` class has a `static` field named `currency`. On my computer in the United States, this `currency` object makes room rates look like dollar amounts.

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) To find out what *static* means, see [Chapter 11](#c11.xhtml). For information about the `currency` field, see [Chapter 12](#c12.xhtml).

[LISTING 13-6](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fea-0006) So This Is What a Room Looks Like!

`package com.example.hotel;`

`import java.text.NumberFormat;`
`import java.util.Scanner;`

`import static java.lang.System.out;`

`public class Room {`
`private int guests;`
`private double rate;`
`private boolean smoking;`
`private static NumberFormat currency =`
`NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance();`

`public void readRoom(Scanner diskScanner) {`
`guests = diskScanner.nextInt();`
`rate = diskScanner.nextDouble();`
`smoking = diskScanner.nextBoolean();`
`}`

`public void writeRoom() {`
`out.print(guests);`
`out.print("\t");`
`out.print(currency.format(rate));`
`out.print("\t");`
`out.println(smoking ? "yes" : "no");`
`}`
`}`

[Listing 13-6](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0006) has a few interesting quirks, but I'd rather not describe them until after you see all the code in action. That's why, at this point, I move right on to the code that calls the [Listing 13-6](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0006) code. After you read about arrays of rooms (shown in [Listing 13-7](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0007)), check out my description of the [Listing 13-6](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0006) quirks.

![Warning](images/warning.png) This warning is a deliberate repeat of an idea from [Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml), [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml), and from who-knows-what-other chapter: *Be careful* when you use type `double` or type `float` to store money values. Calculations with `double` or `float` can be inaccurate. For more information (and more finger-wagging), see [Chapters 4](#c04.xhtml) and [7](#c07.xhtml).

![Tip](images/tip.png) This tip has absolutely nothing to do with Java. If you're the kind of person who prefers a smoking room (with `boolean` field `smoking = true` in [Listing 13-6](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0006)), find someone you like — someone who can take off three consecutive days from work. Have that person sit with you and comfort you for 72 straight hours while you refrain from smoking. You might become temporarily insane while the nicotine leaves your body, but eventually you'll be okay. And your friend will feel like a real hero.

### Using the Room class

Now you need an array of rooms. The code to create such a thing is in [Listing 13-7](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0007). The code reads data from the `RoomList.txt` file. ([Figure 13-9](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0009) shows the contents of the `RoomList.txt` file.)

![“A vertical list of numbers and text, including coordinates and labels, arranged in a repeating pattern. The image shows alternating numbers 3 and 4, each followed by coordinates (00,00) or (100,00), and the text 'calue'.”](images/9781394289240-fg1309.png)

[FIGURE 13-9:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fig-0009) A file of `Room` data.

[Figure 13-10](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0010) shows a run of the code in [Listing 13-7](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0007).

![“A table listing rooms, the number of guests, the price, and whether the room is booked. The table has 10 rows, each representing a different room. Column headers include 'Room,' 'Guests,' 'Price,' and 'Booking?' with details such as the number of guests, price in dollars, and booking status, relevant for hotel management.”](images/9781394289240-fg1310.png)

[FIGURE 13-10:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fig-0010) What's happening at the Java Motel.

[LISTING 13-7](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fea-0007) Would You Like to See a Room?

`import com.example.hotel.Room;`

`void main() throws IOException {`

**`Room[] rooms;`**
**`rooms = new Room[10];`**
`var diskScanner = new Scanner(new File("RoomList.txt"));`

`for (int roomNum = 0; roomNum < 10; roomNum++) {`
**`rooms[roomNum] = new Room();`**
**`rooms[roomNum].readRoom(diskScanner);`**
`}`

`println("Room\tGuests\tRate\tSmoking?");`

`for (int roomNum = 0; roomNum < 10; roomNum++) {`
`print(roomNum);`
`print("\t");`
**`rooms[roomNum].writeRoom();`**
`}`

`diskScanner.close();`
`}`

Say what you want about the code in [Listing 13-7](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0007). As far as I'm concerned, only one issue in the whole listing should concern you. And what, you ask, is that issue? Well, to create an array of *objects* — as opposed to an array made up of primitive values — you have to do three things: Make the array variable, make the array itself, and then construct each individual object in the array. This is different from creating an array of `int` values or an array containing any other primitive type values. When you create an array of primitive type values, you do only the first two of these three things.

To help make sense of all this, follow along in [Listing 13-7](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0007) and [Figure 13-11](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0011) as you read the following points:

*   `Room[] rooms;`: This declaration creates a `rooms` variable. This variable is destined to refer to an array (but doesn't yet refer to anything).
*   `rooms = new Room[10];`: This statement reserves ten slots of storage in the computer's memory. The statement also makes the `rooms` variable refer to the group of storage slots. Each slot is destined to refer to an object (but doesn't yet refer to anything).
*   `rooms[roomNum] = new Room();`: This statement is inside a `for` loop. The statement is executed once for each of the ten room numbers. For example, the first time through the loop, this statement says `rooms[0] = new Room()`. That first time around, the statement makes the slot `rooms[0]` refer to an actual object (an instance of the `Room` class).

![A four-panel diagram explaining the process of creating and initializing an array of Room objects in Java. Panel (a) declares a Room array named 'rooms'. Panel (b) shows the array 'rooms' instantiated with a size of 10, which initially contains 10 null elements. Panel (c) assigns a new Room object to the first element of the array 'rooms[0]'. Panel (d) assigns a Room object created by the 'roomSmith' method to the third element of the array 'rooms[2]'. This visual provides a step-by-step guide for understanding array creation and object instantiation in Java programming.](images/9781394289240-fg1311.png)

[FIGURE 13-11:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fig-0011) Steps in creating an array of objects.

Although it's technically not considered a step in array making, you still have to fill each object's fields with values. For instance, the first time through the loop, the `readRoom` call says `rooms[1].readRoom(diskScanner)`, which means, “Read data from the `RoomList.txt` file into the `rooms[1]` object's fields (the `guests`, `rate`, and `smoking` fields).” Each time through the loop, the program creates a new object and reads data into that new object's fields.

![Tip](images/tip.png) In [Listing 13-7](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0007), I illustrate a point by separating parts in the creation of the `rooms` array. You don't have to do it this way. You can squeeze the steps together just as you do when creating arrays of primitive values. For instance, you can complete the first two steps in [Listing 13-7](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0007) in one fell swoop, like this:

`Room[] rooms = new Room[10];`

![Remember](images/remember.png) From [Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml) onward, I issue gentle warnings against using types such as `double` and `float` for storing currency values. For the most accurate currency calculations, use `int`, `long`, or — best of all — `BigDecimal`. You can read more about the dangers of the `double` type and currency values in [Chapter 7](#c07.xhtml).

### The conditional operator

[Listing 13-6](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0006) uses an interesting doodad called the *conditional operator.* This conditional operator takes three expressions and returns the value of just one of them. It's like a mini `if` statement. When you use the conditional operator, it looks something like this:

*`conditionToBeTested`* `?` *`expression1`* `:` *`expression2`*

The computer evaluates the *conditionToBeTested* condition. If the condition is true, the computer returns the value of *expression1*. But, if the condition is false, the computer returns the value of *expression2*.

So, in the code

`smoking ? "yes" : "no"`

the computer checks to see whether `smoking` has the value `true`. If so, the whole 3-part expression stands for the first string, `"yes"`. If not, the whole expression stands for the second string, `"no"`.

In [Listing 13-6](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0006), the call to `println` causes either `"yes"` or `"no"` to display. Which string gets displayed depends on whether `smoking` has the value `true` or `false`.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) How do you learn Java? You learn it the same way you get to Carnegie Hall: Practice! Practice! Practice!

Supercharge this chapter's examples:

*   Modify the code in [Listing 13-3](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0003) so that it uses the `Room` class from [Listing 13-6](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0006).
*   Add to your work so that when you specify a number of incoming guests, you also enter the guests' preference for a smoking or nonsmoking room.

#### DOUBLE YOUR PRESSURE

In [Chapter 8](#c08.xhtml), you create a `Student` class. Each student has a name and an ID number. For this programming challenge, imagine that each student has five grades — one for each of the five courses the student takes. Each grade is a `double` value from `0.0` to `4.0` (`4.0` is the best). A student's grade point average (GPA) is the average of the student's five grade values.

In this chapter's `Student` class, one of the fields is an array of five `double` values. Your program finds the student's GPA and displays it (along with the student's name and ID number) on the screen.

## How to Argue with Your Code

Take a method header from [Listing 13-6](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0006):

`public void readRoom(Scanner diskScanner) {`

This method has a parameter named `diskScanner`. The first time you call the method, you can make `diskScanner` read from the `RoomList.txt` file. The second time, you can make `diskScanner` read from the `wellNourishedKittens` file. The third time, `diskScanner` may represent the `underAppreciatedAuthors.md` file. This flexibility comes from the fact that `diskScanner` gets its value at the moment you call the method — and not a nanosecond before then.

The same kind of thing can be true of your program's `main` method. Every `main` method can have a `String[] args` parameter. When you launch a program, you can send one or more words to the `args` parameter. For example, if you send

`Java For Dummies`

to the program, then `args[0]` is `"Java"`, `args[1]` is `"For"`, and `args[2]` is `"Dummies"`. If you send

`"Java For Dummies" Great!`

to the program, then, because of the added quotation marks, `args[0]` is `"Java For Dummies"` and `args[1]` is `"Great!"`.

The way you send words to the `args` parameter depends on your IDE, but the general idea depends on two factors:

*   **There are many ways to run a program.**

    Each of these ways is called a *run configuration*. Without knowing it, you've probably been using a default, one-size-fits-most run configuration to test this book's sample programs. That default configuration served you well.

    But now it's time for a change. A run configuration contains answers to several different questions: Which file contains the `main` method? Which version of Java will you run? Which of your drive's folders will be used? Where will the `System.out` text appear? And so on.

*   **One of these answers is named *program arguments*.**

    Most IDEs have some kind of Modify Run Configuration menu option. When you select this option, you see a dialog box with places for all these answers. In that dialog box, one text field is labeled Program Arguments. Your IDE takes whatever you type in that text field and sends it to `args` when your code runs.

    You can change a configuration's program arguments before each run or create several different configurations, each with its own program arguments. For help setting up program arguments in Eclipse and IntelliJ IDEA, visit this book's website ([`https://javafordummies.allmycode.com`](http://javafordummies.allmycode.com/)).

### Settling the argument

[Listing 13-8](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0008) shows you how to use program arguments in your code.

[LISTING 13-8](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fea-0008) Generate a File of Numbers

`void main(`**`String[] args`**`) throws IOException {`

`var generator = new Random();`

`if (args.length < 2) {`
`println("Usage: MakeRandomNumsFile filename number");`
`System.exit(1);`
`}`

**`var printOut = new PrintStream(args[0]);`**
**`int numLines = Integer.parseInt(args[1]);`**

`for (int count = 1; count <= numLines; count++) {`
`printOut.println(generator.nextInt(10) + 1);`
`}`

`printOut.close();`
`}`

At the top of [Figure 13-12](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0012), I create a configuration for a run of [Listing 13-8](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0008). Before I run the program, I set the program arguments to `RandomNumbers.txt 5`.

![“The image shows a Run/Debug Configurations window for a program named 'MakeRandomNumbersFile' set to run on the local machine. The build and run section specifies the output file as 'RandomNumbers.txt'. Below this window, there is an arrow pointing to a list of numbers (9, 7, 2, 4, 10), which are presumably the contents of 'RandomNumbers.txt'.”](images/9781394289240-fg1312.png)

[FIGURE 13-12:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fig-0012) A run of the code in [Listing 13-8](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0008).

![Warning](images/warning.png) The look of the configuration dialog box depends on which IDE you use, and which version of that IDE. Your IDE's configuration dialog box may look different from the one in [Figure 13-12](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0012).

When I run the program, the configuration in [Figure 13-12](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0012) makes the value of `args[0]` be `"RandomNumbers.txt"` and the value of `args[1]` be `"5"`. So the program's assignment statements end up having the following meaning:

`var printOut = new PrintStream("RandomNumbers.txt");`
`int numLines = Integer.parseInt("5");`

The program creates a file named `RandomNumbers.txt` and sets `numLines` to `5`. So, later in the code, the program randomly generates five values and puts those values into `RandomNumbers.txt`. (Refer to the bottom of [Figure 13-12](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0012).)

Notice how each program argument in [Listing 13-8](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0008) is a `String` value. When you look at `args[1]`, you don't see the number 5 — you see the string `"5"` with a digit character in it. Unfortunately, you can't use that `"5"` to do any counting. To get an `int` value from `"5"`, you have to apply the `parseInt` method. (Again, see [Listing 13-8](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0008).)

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) You don't need an IDE to feed arguments to a program. Imagine that you have a Java class named `ThreeLittleWords`. Depending on your setup, you may be able to open a Command Prompt or Terminal window and type `java ThreeLittleWords.java Java For Dummies`. If all goes well, the `args` array will contain the words `Java For Dummies`.

### Checking for the right number of program arguments

What happens if the user makes a mistake? What if the user omits the number `5` in the text field in [Figure 13-12](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0012)?

Then the computer assigns `"RandomNumbers.txt"` to `args[0]` but assigns nothing to `args[1]`. This is bad. If the computer ever reaches the statement

`int numLines = Integer.parseInt(args[1]);`

the program crashes with an unfriendly `ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException`.

What do you do about this? In [Listing 13-8](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0008), you check the length of the `args` array. You compare `args.length` with `2`. If the `args` array has fewer than two components, you display a message on the screen and exit from the program. [Figure 13-13](#c13.xhtml_c13-fig-0013) shows the resulting output.

![“A text box with the label 'Username: MakeStudentNameVia filename number.' The image appears to be a placeholder or instruction for generating a username based on a student's name, file name, and a number.”](images/9781394289240-fg1313.png)

[FIGURE 13-13:](#c13.xhtml_rc13-fig-0013) The code in [Listing 13-8](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0008) tells you how to run it.

![Warning](images/warning.png) Despite the checking of `args.length` in [Listing 13-8](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0008), the code still isn't crashproof. If the user types `five` instead of `5`, the program takes a nosedive with a N`umberFormatException`. The program's `for` loop can't go from the `int` value `1` to the `String` value `"five"`. So this program's second `args` value can't be a word. It has to be a number (and a whole number, at that). I can add `if` statements to [Listing 13-8](#c13.xhtml_c13-fea-0008) to make the code more bulletproof, but checking for the `NumberFormatException` is better done in [Chapter 14](#c14.xhtml).

![On the web](images/ontheweb.png) The sun is about to set on this book's discussion of arrays. But before you leave the subject of arrays, think about this: An array is a row of things, and not every kind of thing fits into just one row. Take the first few examples in this chapter involving the motel. The motel rooms, numbered 0 through 9, are in one long line. But what if you move up in the world? You buy a big hotel with 50 floors and with 100 rooms on each floor. Then the data starts looking square-shaped. You have 50 rows, and each row contains 100 items. Sure, you can think of the rooms as though they're all in one long row, but why should you have to do that? How about having a 2-dimensional array? It’s a square-shaped array in which each component has two indices: a row number and a column number. Alas, I have no space in this book to show you a 2-dimensional array (and I can’t afford a big hotel’s prices, anyway). But if you visit this book’s website ([`https://javafordummies.allmycode.com`](http://javafordummies.allmycode.com/)), you can read all about it.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) You can never get too much practice:

#### MINE IS THE BIGGEST

Write a program whose program arguments include three `int` values. As its output, the program displays the largest of the three `int` values.

Chapter 14

# Looking Good When Things Take Unexpected Turns

IN THIS CHAPTER

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Recovering from bad input and other nasty situations**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Making your code (more or less) crash-proof**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Defining your own exception class**

S*eptember 9, 1945:* A moth flies into one of the relays of the Harvard Mark II computer and gums up the works. This becomes the first recorded case of a real-life computer bug.

*April 19, 1957:* Herbert Bright, manager of the data processing center at Westinghouse in Pittsburgh, receives an unmarked deck of computer punch cards in the mail (which is like receiving an unlabeled thumb drive in the mail today). Mr. Bright guesses that this deck comes from the development team for FORTRAN — the first computer programming language. He’s been waiting a few years for this software. (No web downloads were available at the time.)

Armed with nothing but this good guess, Bright writes a small FORTRAN program and tries to compile it on his IBM 704\. (The IBM 704 lives in its own, specially built, 2,000-square-foot room. With vacuum tubes instead of transistors, the machine has a whopping 32K of RAM. The operating system has to be loaded from tape before the running of each program, and a typical program takes between two and four hours to run.) After the usual waiting time, Bright’s attempt to compile a FORTRAN program comes back with a single error: a missing comma in one of the statements. Bright corrects the error, and the program runs like a charm.

*July 22, 1962: Mariner I,* the first US spacecraft aimed at another planet, is destroyed when it behaves badly 4 minutes after launch. The bad behavior is attributed to a missing bar (like a hyphen) in the formula for the rocket’s velocity.

Around the same time, orbit computation software at NASA is found to contain the incorrect statement `DO 10 I=1.10` (instead of the correct `DO 10 I=1,10)`. In modern notation, this is like writing `do10i = 1.10` in place of `for (int i=1; i<=10; i++)`. The change from a comma to a period turns a loop into an assignment statement.

*January 1, 2000:* Experts believe that computer programs will mistake `01/01/00` for January 1, 1900\. Prophets of doom predict worldwide disasters and unparalleled destruction. For most systems, this Y2K problem causes negligible damage. Most of the doomsayers are rightfully embarrassed.

Any historically accurate facts in these notes were borrowed from the following sources: the Computer Folklore newsgroup ([`https://groups.google.com/forum/#!forum/alt.folklore.computers`](https://groups.google.com/forum/#!forum/alt.folklore.computers)), the Free On-line Dictionary of Computing ([`https://foldoc.org`](http://foldoc.org)), *Computer* magazine ([`www.computer.org/computer-magazine`](https://www.computer.org/computer-magazine/)), and other web pages of the IEEE ([`www.computer.org`](http://www.computer.org)). All inaccuracies stem from this author's lunatic musings.

## Garbage In

You're taking inventory. This means counting item after item, box after box, and marking the numbers of such things on log sheets, in little handheld gizmos, and into forms on computer keyboards. A particular part of the project involves entering the number of boxes you find on the shelf labeled Big Dusty Boxes That Haven't Been Opened Since Year One. Rather than break the company’s decades-old habit, you decide not to open any of these boxes. You arbitrarily assign the value $3.25 to each box.

[Listing 14-1](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0001) shows the software to handle this bit of inventory. The software has a flaw, which is revealed in [Figure 14-1](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0001). When the user enters a whole number value, things are okay. But when the user enters something else (like the number 3.5), the program comes crashing to the ground. Surely something can be done about this. Computers are stupid, but they’re not so stupid that they should fail royally whenever a user enters an improper value.

[LISTING 14-1](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fea-0001) Counting Boxes

`import java.text.NumberFormat;`
`import java.util.Scanner;`

`import static java.lang.System.out;`

`public class InventoryA {`

`public static void main(String[] args) {`
`final double boxPrice = 3.25;`
`var keyboard = new Scanner(System.in);`
`var currency = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance();`

`out.print("How many boxes do we have? ");`
`String numBoxesIn = keyboard.next();`
`int numBoxes = Integer.parseInt(numBoxesIn);`

`out.print("The value is ");`
`out.println(currency.format(numBoxes * boxPrice));`
`}`
`}`

![The image shows three code snippets with their respective outputs and error messages. The first code snippet prints: 'How many boxes do we have? 3' and 'The value is 59.75'. The second snippet tries to print: 'How many boxes do we have? 3.5' but results in a NumberFormatException due to the input string '3.5'. The third snippet attempts to print: 'How many boxes do we have? three' but throws a NumberFormatException due to the input string 'three'.](images/9781394289240-fg1401.png)

[FIGURE 14-1:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0001) Three separate runs of the code in [Listing 14-1](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0001).

The key to fixing a program bug is examining the message that appears when the program crashes. The inventory program’s message says `java.lang.NumberFormatException`. That means a class named *NumberFormatException* is in the `java.lang` API package. Somehow, the call to `Integer.parseInt` brought this `NumberFormatException` class out of hiding.

Well, here's what's going on. The Java programming language has a mechanism called exception handling. With *exception handling,* a program can detect that something is about to go wrong and respond by creating a brand-new object. In the official terminology, the program is said to be *throwing* an exception. That new object, an instance of the `Exception` class, is passed like a hot potato from one piece of code to another until some piece of code decides to *catch* the exception. When the exception is caught, the program executes some recovery code, buries the exception, and moves on to the next normal statement as though nothing had ever happened. The process is illustrated in [Figure 14-2](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0002).

![“A flowchart diagram illustrating the sequence of method calls in Java and how exceptions are propagated through nested method calls. The diagram begins with a method that calls another method within a try-catch block. Each subsequent method in the chain throws an exception, demonstrating the flow of exception handling in Java. The final method in the chain throws a new exception.”](images/9781394289240-fg1402.png)

[FIGURE 14-2:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0002) Throwing, passing, and catching an exception.

The whole thing is done with the aid of several Java keywords. These keywords are described in this list:

*   `throw`: Creates a new exception object.
*   `throws`: Indicates that a method may pass the exception up to whatever code called the method.
*   `try`: Encloses code that has the potential to create a new exception object. In the usual scenario, the code inside a `try` clause contains calls to methods whose code can create one or more exceptions.
*   `catch`: Deals with the exception, buries it, and then moves on.

So the truth is out. By some chain of events like the one shown in [Figure 14-2](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0002), the method `Integer.parseInt` can throw a `NumberFormatException`. When you call `Integer.parseInt` with `3.5` or `three`, Java generates a `NumberFormatException` and passes it up the chain of method calls. The passing continues until some Java code catches the exception. But, in [Listing 14-1](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0001), there's no code to catch a `NumberFormatException`. So, when you input `3.5` or `three`, the program crashes and you get embarrassing messages like the ones in [Figure 14-1](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0001).

![Tip](images/tip.png) The Java application programming interface (API) documentation for the `parseInt` method says, “Throws: `NumberFormatException` — if the string does not contain a parsable integer.” Once in a while, reading the documentation actually pays off.

### Making things right

The code in [Listing 14-1](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0001) may throw an exception. You'd better catch the exception to keep the program from crashing. [Listing 14-2](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0002) shows the catching of an exception.

[LISTING 14-2](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fea-0002) A Safer Way to Count Boxes

`void main() {`
`final double boxPrice = 3.25;`
`var currency = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance();`

`String numBoxesIn = readln("How many boxes do we have? ");`

`try {`
`int numBoxes = Integer.parseInt(numBoxesIn);`
`print("The value is ");`
`println(currency.format(numBoxes * boxPrice));`
`} catch (NumberFormatException e) {`
`print(e.getMessage());`
`println(" … Cannot interpret the input.");`
`}`
`println("Have a nice day!");`
`}`

[Figure 14-3](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0003) shows three runs of the code from [Listing 14-2](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0002). When a misguided user types **three** instead of **3**, the program maintains its cool by displaying `For input string: "three" … Cannot interpret the input`. The trick is to enclose the call to `Integer.parseInt` inside a `try` clause. If you do this, the computer watches for exceptions when any statement inside the `try` clause is executed. If an exception is thrown, the computer jumps from inside the `try` clause to a `catch` clause below it. In [Listing 14-2](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0002), the computer jumps directly to the `catch (NumberFormatException e)` clause. The computer executes the two `print` statements inside the clause and then marches on with normal processing. In [Listing 14-2](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0002), this “normal processing” means printing `Have a nice day!`.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) An entire `try-catch` assembly — complete with a `try` clause, `catch` clause, and what-have-you — is called a *try statement.* Sometimes, for emphasis, I call it a *try-catch statement.*

### The parameter in a catch clause

Take a look at the `catch` clause in [Listing 14-2](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0002) and pay particular attention to the words `(NumberFormatException e)`. This looks a lot like a method's parameter list, doesn't it? In fact, every `catch` clause is like a little mini-method with its own parameter list.

![The image displays three different outputs of a program that calculates a value based on the number of boxes entered by the user: When the user inputs '3', the program responds with 'The value is $9.75' and ends with 'Have a nice day!'. With the input 'three', the program returns an error message indicating it cannot interpret the input string 'three' but still wishes the user 'Have a nice day!'. For the input '-25', the program calculates a negative value of '-$81.25' and ends with 'Have a nice day!'.](images/9781394289240-fg1403.png)

[FIGURE 14-3:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0003) Three runs of the code in [Listing 14-2](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0002).

The parameter list always has an exception type name and then a parameter. The parameter is an object — an instance of the `NumberFormatException` class. When an exception is caught, Java makes the `catch` clause's parameter refer to that exception object. In other words, the name *e* in [Listing 14-2](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0002) stores a bunch of information about the exception. To take advantage of this, you can call some of the exception object's methods.

In [Listing 14-2](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0002), I call the exception's `getMessage` method. Lo and behold! The `getMessage` call returns the text `For input string: "three"`. That's helpful information. Java hands this information to the `e` variable whenever it can't make sense of the user's `three` input.

In addition to the `getMessage` method, each exception has a handy `printStackTrace` method. To see how this works, rewrite the `catch` clause in [Listing 14-2](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0002) as follows:

`} catch (NumberFormatException e) {`
**`e.printStackTrace()`**`;`
`}`

With this new `catch` clause, a run of the `inventory` program may look like the run shown in [Figure 14-4](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0004). When you call `printStackTrace`, you see a list of methods that were running when the exception was thrown, along with line numbers in each of the methods. In [Figure 14-4](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0004), the display includes `Integer.parseInt` and the `main` method.

![Text containing Java code snippets and a handwritten note saying “Too many hours do we have? cheers Love x alice day”.](images/9781394289240-fg1404.png)

[FIGURE 14-4:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0004) Java's stack trace.

Notice the similarity between the run in [Figure 14-4](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0004) and two of the runs in [Figure 14-1](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0001).

*   **In** [**Figure** **14-4**](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0004)**:** Java throws a `NumberFormatException`. The code catches the exception and calls a method that prints a stack trace. Then the program keeps running (to print `Have a nice day`, for example).
*   **In** [**Figure** **14-1**](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0001)**:** Java throws a `NumberFormatException`. None of the code in [Listing 14-1](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0001) catches the exception, so Java does what it always does for any uncaught exceptions: It prints a stack trace and then abruptly ends the run of the program. In other words, your program crashes. Nothing else gets calculated, printed, or processed in any way.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) In [Figure 14-4](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0004), notice that `Have a nice day` appears before the exception's stack trace. That’s peculiar. Doesn’t Java call `e.printStackTrace()` before it prints `Have a nice day`? It does. But the stack trace and `nice day` outputs follow different paths to get to your computer screen. So they don't always appear in the expected order.

The `print` and `println` calls in [Listing 14-2](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0002) go to your old friend, `System.out`. But `printStackTrace` doesn't write directly to `System.out`. The `printStackTrace` method writes to something called `System.err`, which, by default, shows up in the same place as `System.out`. When you mix `System.out.println` calls with `printStackTrace` calls, the order in which Java displays the information isn't predictable. There are ways to tweak the code so that `System.out` and `System.err` go to different places on your screen, but that's not normally something you’d be anxious to do.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) I can't think of a clever way to connect the *Try* in *TryItOut* with the `try` in `try-catch` statements. If you think of something, scribble it in the margin on this page. Then try this little challenge:

#### DON'T BE A QUITTER

Add `try-catch` statements to keep the following code from crashing:

`void main() {`
`var words = new String[5];`

`int i = 0;`
`do {`
`words[i] = readln("");`
`} while (!words[i++].equals("Quit"));`

`for (int j = 0; j < 5; j++) {`
`println(words[j].length());`
`}`
`}`

### Do it yourself

In the previous sections, you confuse your inventory program by typing `three` instead of `3`. Nice work! What else can go wrong today? Are there other kinds of exceptions — ones that have nothing to do with the `NumberFormatException`? Sure, plenty of different exception types are out there. You can even create one of your own. You wanna try? If so, look at [Listings 14-3](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0003) and [14-4](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0004).

[LISTING 14-3](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fea-0003) Making Your Own Kind of Exception

`package com.example.inventory;`

`public class OutOfRangeException extends RuntimeException {`
`public OutOfRangeException(String message) {`
`super("A value is out of range.\n" + message);`
`}`
`}`

[LISTING 14-4](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fea-0004) Using Your Custom-Made Exception

`import com.example.inventory.OutOfRangeException;`

`void main() {`
`final double boxPrice = 3.25;`
`var currency = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance();`

`String numBoxesIn = readln("How many boxes do we have? ");`

`try {`
