`int numBoxes = Integer.parseInt(numBoxesIn);`
`if (numBoxes < 0) {`
`throw new OutOfRangeException("You typed " + numBoxes +`
`". There's no such thing as a negative box.");`
`}`
`print("The value is ");`
`println(currency.format(numBoxes * boxPrice));`
`} catch (NumberFormatException e) {`
`print(e.getMessage());`
`println(" … Cannot interpret the input.");`
`} catch (OutOfRangeException e) {`
`println(e.getMessage());`
`}`
`}`

[Listings 14-3](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0003) and [14-4](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0004) remedy a problem that quietly cropped up earlier. Look at the last of the three runs in [Figure 14-3](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0003). The user reports that the shelves have –25 boxes, and the computer takes this value without blinking an eye. The truth is that you would need a black hole (or some other exotic, space-time-warping phenomenon) to have a negative number of boxes on any shelf in your warehouse. So the program should become upset if the user enters a negative number of boxes, which is what the code in [Listing 14-4](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0004) does. To see what I mean, look at [Figure 14-5](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0005).

![“A sequence of three text interactions with a computer program that handles user input about the number of boxes. The first input is the number '3', resulting in the output 'The value is $9.75'. The second input is the word 'three', which causes the program to generate an error message: 'For input string: “three” . . . Cannot interpret the input.' The third input is '-25', and the program responds with another error message: 'A value is out of range. You typed -25\. There's no such thing as a negative box.' The image highlights the importance of input validation and error handling in programming.”](images/9781394289240-fg1405.png)

[FIGURE 14-5:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0005) Three runs of the code from [Listings 14-3](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0003) and [14-4](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0004).

The code in [Listing 14-3](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0003) declares a new kind of exception class: `OutOfRangeException`. In many situations, typing a negative number would work just fine, so `OutOfRangeException` isn't built-in to the Java API. However, in the inventory program, a negative number of boxes should be flagged as an anomaly.

How do you know that the class in [Listing 14-3](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0003) is an exception of some kind? You know because the class declaration's first line says `extends RuntimeException`. The class in [Listing 14-3](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0003) is a particular kind of exception called a *runtime exception*. To learn more about runtime exceptions, see the section “[The Buck Stops Here, Except When It Doesn't](#c14.xhtml_c14-sec-0018),” later in this chapter.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) When you call

`throw new OutOfRangeException("You typed " + numBoxes +`
`". There's no such thing as a negative box.");`

in [Listing 14-4](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0004), you pass the string `"You typed " + numBoxes + …` to the `message` parameter in [Listing 14-3](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0003). The constructor in [Listing 14-3](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0003) doesn't do much on its own with that `message` value. Instead, the constructor combines the message with its own `"A value is out of range.\n"` string. Then the constructor uses `super` to pass the combined string on to its superclass — the `RuntimeException` class. By the time you're done, the call `e.getMessage()` gives you a whole bunch of text: `A value is out of range … blah, blah, … no such thing … blah blah`.

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) To read more about Java's `super` keyword, refer to [Chapter 10](#c10.xhtml).

### Who will catch the exception?

Take one more look at [Listing 14-4](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0004). Notice that more than one `catch` clause can accompany a single `try` clause. When an exception is thrown inside a `try` clause, Java starts reviewing the accompanying list of `catch` clauses. It starts at whatever `catch` clause comes immediately after the `try` clause and works its way down the program's text.

For each `catch` clause, Java asks itself, “Is the exception that was just thrown an instance of the class in this clause's parameter list?”

*   If not, Java skips this `catch` clause and moves on to the next `catch` clause in line.
*   If so, Java executes this `catch` clause and then skips past all other `catch` clauses that come with this `try` clause. Java goes on and executes whatever statements come after the whole `try-catch` statement.

For some concrete examples, see [Listings 14-5](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0005) and [14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006).

[LISTING 14-5](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fea-0005) Yet Another Exception

`package com.example.inventory;`

`public class NumberTooLargeException extends OutOfRangeException {`
`public NumberTooLargeException(String message) {`
`super("A value is too large.\n" + message);`
`}`
`}`

[LISTING 14-6](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fea-0006) Catch Me If You Can

`import com.example.inventory.*;`

`void main() {`
`final double boxPrice = 3.25;`
`final int maxBoxes = 1000;`
`NumberFormat currency = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance();`

`String numBoxesIn = readln("How many boxes do we have? ");`

**`try {`**
`int numBoxes = Integer.parseInt(numBoxesIn);`

`if (numBoxes < 0) {`
`throw new OutOfRangeException("You typed " + numBoxes +`
`". There's no such thing as a negative box.");`
`}`
`if (numBoxes > maxBoxes) {`
`throw new NumberTooLargeException(numBoxes +`
`" is larger than the maximum of " + maxBoxes);`
`}`
`print("The value is ");`
`println(currency.format(numBoxes * boxPrice));`

**`} catch (NumberFormatException e) {`**
`print(e.getMessage());`
`println(" … Cannot interpret the input.");`

**`} catch (OutOfRangeException e) {`**
`println(e.getMessage());`

**`} catch (Exception e) {`**
`println(e.getMessage());`
**`}`**

`println("That's that.");`
`}`

To run the code in [Listings 14-5](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0005) and [14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006), you need one additional Java program file. You need the `OutOfRangeException` class in [Listing 14-3](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0003).

[Listing 14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006) addresses the scenario in which you have limited shelf space. You don't have room for more than 1,000 boxes, but once in a while the program asks how many boxes you have, and somebody enters the number *1001* accidentally. In cases like this, [Listing 14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006) performs a quick reality check: Any number of boxes over 1,000 is tossed out as being unrealistic.

[Listing 14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006) watches for a `NumberTooLargeException`, but to make life more interesting, [Listing 14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006) has no `catch` clause for the `NumberTooLargeException`. In spite of this, everything still works out just fine. It's fine because `NumberTooLargeException` is declared to be a subclass of `OutOfRangeException`, and [Listing 14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006) has a `catch` clause for the `OutOfRangeException`.

You see, because the `NumberTooLargeException` class is a subclass of the `OutOfRangeException` class, any instance of `NumberTooLargeException` is just a special kind of `OutOfRangeException`. So, in [Listing 14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006), Java may start looking for a clause to catch a `NumberTooLargeException`. When Java stumbles upon the `OutOfRangeException` catch clause, Java says, “Okay, I’ve found a match. I’ll execute the statements in this `catch` clause.”

To keep from having to write this whole story over and over again, I introduce some new terminology. I say that the `catch` clause with parameter is`OutOfRangeException`*matches* the `NumberTooLargeException` that's been thrown. I call this `catch` clause a *matching catch clause.*

The following list describes different things the user may do and how the computer responds. As you read, you can follow along by looking at the runs shown in [Figure 14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0006):

*   **The user enters an ordinary whole number, like the number *3.*** All statements in the `try` clause are executed. Then Java skips past all the catch clauses and executes the code that comes immediately after all the catch clauses. (See [Figure 14-7](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0007).)
*   **The user enters something that's not a whole number, like the word *fish.*** The code throws a `NumberFormatException`. Java skips past the remaining statements in the `try` clause. Java executes the statements inside the first `catch` clause — the clause with parameter `NumberFormatException`. Then Java skips past the second and third `catch` clauses and executes the code that comes immediately after all the `catch` clauses. (See [Figure 14-8](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0008).)
*   **The user enters a negative number, like the number –25.** The code throws an `OutOfRangeException`. Java skips past the remaining statements in the `try` clause. Java even skips past the statements in the first `catch` clause. (After all, an `OutOfRangeException` isn't any kind of a `NumberFormatException`. The `catch` clause with parameter `NumberFormatException` isn't a match for this `OutOfRangeException`.) Java executes the statements inside the second `catch` clause — the clause whose parameter is of type `OutOfRangeException`. Then Java skips past the third `catch` clause and executes the code that comes immediately after all the `catch` clauses. (See [Figure 14-9](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0009).)![A series of outputs from a program that calculates a value based on the number of boxes entered by the user. The program demonstrates different responses for various types of input: The input '3' results in the output: 'The value is $9.75'. The input 'fish' generates an error message: 'Cannot interpret the input string: “fish”'. The input '-25' produces an error message: 'A value is out of range. No such thing as a negative box.' The input '1001' results in an error message: 'A value is out of range. The value is too large, larger than the maximum of 1000.'](images/9781394289240-fg1406.png)

    [FIGURE 14-6:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0006) Four runs of the code from [Listing 14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006).

    ![“A code snippet in Java demonstrating exception handling with try-catch blocks. The code includes three catch blocks for NumberFormatException, OutOfRangeException, and a general Exception. Each catch block prints the exception message and an additional message for NumberFormatException. After the try-catch blocks, there is a println statement that outputs 'That's that.'”](images/9781394289240-fg1407.png)

    [FIGURE 14-7:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0007) No exception is thrown.

    ![“A Java code snippet showcasing exception handling with multiple catch blocks. The code throws a NumberFormatException, which is caught and handled by printing the exception message and a custom message: '... Cannot interpret the input.' Other catch blocks for OutOfRangeException and a generic Exception also print their respective messages. Finally, a println statement outputs 'That's that.'”](images/9781394289240-fg1408.png)

    [FIGURE 14-8:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0008) Java throws a `NumberFormatException`.

    ![“A Java code snippet showcasing exception handling with try-catch blocks. The code attempts to throw an OutOfRangeException within the try block. There are three catch blocks to handle different exceptions: NumberFormatException, OutOfRangeException, and a general Exception. Each catch block prints the exception message using e.getMessage() and additional text for the NumberFormatException. The code concludes with a println statement outside the try-catch blocks.”](images/9781394289240-fg1409.png)

    [FIGURE 14-9:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0009) Java throws an `OutOfRangeException`.

*   **The user enters an unrealistically large number, like the number 1001.** The code throws a `NumberTooLargeException`. Java skips past the remaining statements in the `try` clause. Java even skips past the statements in the first `catch` clause. (After all, a `NumberTooLargeException` isn't any kind of `NumberFormatException`.)

    But, according to the code in [Listing 14-5](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0005), `NumberTooLargeException` is a subclass of `OutOfRangeException`. When Java reaches the second `catch` clause, Java says, “Hmm! A `NumberTooLargeException` is a kind of `OutOfRangeException`. I'll execute the statements in this `catch` clause — the clause with parameter of type `OutOfRangeException`.” It's a match.

    Java executes the statements inside the second `catch` clause. Then Java skips the third `catch` clause and executes the code that comes immediately after all the `catch` clauses. (See [Figure 14-10](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0010).)

*   **Something else, something quite unpredictable, happens. (I don't know what.)** With my unending urge to experiment, I reached into the `try` clause of [Listing 14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006) and added a statement that commits a serious crime. The statement divides a number by zero:

    `try {`
    **`out.println(1 / 0);`**
    `int numBoxes = Integer.parseInt(numBoxesIn);`

    When Java encounters this statement, it throws an `ArithmeticException`. Java skips past the remaining statements in the `try` clause. Then Java skips past the statements in the first and second `catch` clauses. When Java reaches the third `catch` clause, I can hear Java say, “Hmm! An `ArithmeticException` is a kind of `Exception`. I've found a matching `catch` clause — a clause with a parameter of type `Exception`. I'll execute the statements in this `catch` clause.”

    So Java executes the statements inside the third `catch` clause. Then Java executes the code that comes immediately after all the `catch` clauses. (See [Figure 14-11](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0011).)

When Java looks for a matching `catch` clause, Java latches on to the topmost clause that fits one of the following descriptions:

*   The clause's parameter type is the same as the type of the exception that was thrown.
*   The clause's parameter type is a superclass of the exception’s type.

![“A Java code snippet demonstrating exception handling using multiple catch blocks. The try block contains code that throws a NumberTooLargeException. There are three catch blocks: one for NumberFormatException, one for OutOfRangeException, and a general Exception. Each catch block prints the exception message using e.getMessage(), with an additional message for NumberFormatException. The code ends with a println statement outside the try-catch blocks that outputs 'That's that.'”](images/9781394289240-fg1410.png)

[FIGURE 14-10:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0010) Java throws a `NumberTooLargeException`.

![“A Java code snippet demonstrating exception handling with try-catch blocks. The code attempts to print the result of dividing 1 by 0, which will cause an ArithmeticException. The code includes three catch blocks to handle different exceptions: NumberFormatException, OutOfRangeException, and a generic Exception. Each catch block prints the exception message. After the try-catch blocks, there is a print statement that outputs 'That's that.' This image is relevant for understanding how Java handles exceptions and the flow of control when an exception occurs.”](images/9781394289240-fg1411.png)

[FIGURE 14-11:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0011) Java throws an `ArithmeticException`.

If a better match appears farther down the list of `catch` clauses, that’s just too bad. Imagine that you added a `catch` clause with a parameter of type `NumberTooLargeException` to the code in [Listing 14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006). Imagine also that you put this new `catch` clause *after* the `catch` clause with a parameter of type `OutOfRangeException`. Then, because `NumberTooLargeException` is a subclass of the `OutOfRangeException` class, the code in the new `NumberTooLargeException` clause would never be executed. That's just the way the cookie crumbles.

### Catching two or more exceptions at a time

You can catch more than one kind of exception in a single `catch` clause. For example, in a particular inventory program, you might want the same text to accompany a `NumberFormatException` and your own `OutOfRangeException`. In that case, you can rewrite part of [Listing 14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006) this way:

`} catch (`**`NumberFormatException | OutOfRangeException e`**`) {`
`println(e.getMessage());`
`println("Input a number from 0 to " + maxBoxes + ".");`
`} catch (Exception e) {`
`println(e.getMessage());`
`}`

The pipe symbol, `|`, tells Java to catch either a `NumberFormatException` or an `OutOfRangeException`. If you throw an exception of either type, the program adds `Input a number from 0 to 1000` to its helpful output. If you throw an exception that's neither a `NumberFormatException` nor an `OutOfRangeException`, the program jumps to the last `catch` clause where you don't get that `Input a number …` output.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) Imagine that a user runs [Listing 14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006) and types the word `three` instead of the number `3`. You'd be tempted to call that an error. Right? Well, Java calls that a `NumberFormatException`. It’s an `Exception`, not an `Error`. Java has things called *errors*, but they're not the same as this chapter’s exceptions. The basic difference is that an exception is something your program may be able to deal with. Your program can catch the exception, respond appropriately, and then move on with its processing life. On the other hand, an *error* is something so heinous that your program should stop in its tracks. For example, an `OutOfMemoryError` occurs when your program runs out of space to store values. If that happens, there’s no sense in trying to catch the error and recover. Your program terminates, and it’s “back to the drawing board.”

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Try your hand at this coding task:

#### LOOSEN UP A BIT

In [Listing 14-6](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0006), `boxPrice` and `maxBoxes` have fixed values. Make improvements to the code so that the user enters both those values. Remember that some values for these quantities make no sense. For example, a negative number of boxes is never too many boxes. Use `try-catch` statements to handle inappropriate user input.

## The Buck Stops Here, Except When It Doesn't

[Listing 14-7](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0007) has a scaled-down version of an example from [Chapter 9](#c09.xhtml). The code tries to read values from a disk file and then write some results to the screen, but the code doesn't work.

[LISTING 14-7](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fea-0007) Not Writing Payroll Checks

**`/*`**
**`* This code does not compile.`**
**`*/`**

`void main() {`
`println("Starting payroll … ");`
`cutCheck();`
`println("Payroll completed.");`
`}`

`void cutCheck() {`
`var diskScanner = new Scanner(new File("EmployeePayment.txt"));`
`String name = diskScanner.nextLine();`
`double amountPaid = diskScanner.nextDouble();`

`print("Pay to the order of " + name);`
`println(String.format(" ***$%,.2f", amountPaid));`

`diskScanner.close();`
`}`

When you construct a `Scanner` with `System.out`, nothing much can go wrong. But when you construct a `Scanner` with a `File`, you have to be careful. Maybe you misspelled the filename (`EnployeePaymant.txt`). Maybe your file is in the cloud and the file is unreadable because the network is down. Maybe you lack permission to access *any* files. Who knows?

Java doesn't let you call `new Scanner(new File(`*`anything_at_all`*`))` unless you acknowledge that there's some risk. The problem is that the code deep inside the `Scanner` constructor's body can throw an exception. This kind of exception is an instance of the `FileNotFoundException` class. When you try to compile the code in [Listing 14-7](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0007), you see an unwelcome message such as

`unreported exception java.io.FileNotFoundException;`
`must be caught or declared to be thrown`

The Java programming language has two kinds of exceptions. They're called *checked* and *unchecked* exceptions:

*   The potential throwing of a checked exception must be acknowledged in the code.
*   The potential throwing of an unchecked exception doesn't need to be acknowledged in the code.

A `FileNotFoundException` is one of Java’s checked exception types. When you call a constructor or method that has the potential to throw a `FileNotFoundException`, you need to acknowledge that exception in the code.

Now, when I say that an exception is *acknowledged in the code,* what do I really mean?

`/*`
`* The programmer wishes to thank the FileNotFoundException,`
`* without which this code could not have been written.`
`*/`

No, that's not what it means to be acknowledged in the code. Acknowledging an exception in the code means one of two things:

*   The statements (including any constructor or method calls) that can throw the exception are inside a `try` clause. That `try` clause has a `catch` clause with a matching exception type in its parameter list.
*   The statements (including any constructor or method calls) that can throw the exception are inside a method that has a `throws` clause in its header. The `throws` clause contains a matching exception type.

If you're confused by the wording of these two bullets, don't worry. The next two listings illustrate the points made in the bullets.

### Catch it soon

In [Listing 14-8](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0008), the `new Scanner` call is inside a `try` clause. That `try` clause has a `catch` clause with exception type `FileNotFoundException`.

[LISTING 14-8](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fea-0008) Acknowledging with a try-catch Statement

`void main() {`
`println("Starting payroll … ");`
`cutCheck();`
`println("Payroll completed.");`
`}`

`void cutCheck() {`
**`try {`**
`var diskScanner = new Scanner(new File("EmployeePayment.txt"));`
`String name = diskScanner.nextLine();`
`double amountPaid = diskScanner.nextDouble();`

`print("Pay to the order of " + name);`
`println(String.format(" ***$%,.2f", amountPaid));`

`diskScanner.close();`
**`} catch (FileNotFoundException e) {`**
**`println(e.getMessage());`**
**`}`**
`}`

[Figure 14-12](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0012) shows two runs of the code in [Listing 14-8](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0008). For the first run, an `EmployeePayment.txt` file contains the lines `Barry Burd` and `5000.00`. For the second run, the user's hard drive contains no `EmployeePayment.txt` file.

![“Two sets of text outputs from a payroll system. The first output shows a successful payroll process, paying $5,000.00 to Barry Burd and indicating that the payroll is completed. The second output shows an error message stating that the file 'EmployeePayment.txt' could not be found, followed by a message that the payroll process is completed, despite the error.”](images/9781394289240-fg1412.png)

[FIGURE 14-12:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0012) With and without an `EmployeePayment.txt` file.

When I look at [Listing 14-8](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0008), my heart is filled with pride. I've acknowledged the `FileNotFoundException`, prevented the code from printing `Pay to the order of`, and avoided a nasty program crash. In fact, I handled the exception as soon as it arose. I put out that fire in the `cutCheck` method before it could spread and burn up the `main` method.

But wait! Did I do the right thing? Here's an important principle in software design: Don't be too hasty to catch an exception. Looking back at [Figure 14-12](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0012), the second run ends with the words `Payroll completed`. Is that true? Will Barry actually receive the $5000.00 that he so rightfully deserves? No. That second run in [Figure 14-12](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0012) throws a `FileNotFoundException`. The `cutCheck` method in [Listing 14-8](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0008) catches the exception and doesn't tell the `main` method that anything went wrong. So the `main` method, gleeful in its ignorance of the `FileNotFoundException`, prints `Payroll completed`.

I shouldn't have shielded the code's main method from the troublesome`FileNotFoundException`. Instead, I should have relayed the exception from the `cutCheck` method up to the `main` method. The example in the next section shows you how.

### Catch it later

In [Listing 14-9](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0009), the `cutCheck` method says “This `FileNotFoundException` isn't my problem. Let `main` deal with it.”

[LISTING 14-9](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fea-0009) A Method Admits Its Shortcomings

`void main() {`
`println("Starting payroll … ");`
**`try {`**
`cutCheck();`
`println("Payroll completed.");`
**`} catch (FileNotFoundException e) {`**
**`println(e.getMessage());`**
**`}`**
`}`

`void cutCheck()` **`throws FileNotFoundException`** `{`
`var diskScanner = new Scanner(new File("EmployeePayment.txt"));`
`String name = diskScanner.nextLine();`
`double amountPaid = diskScanner.nextDouble();`

`print("Pay to the order of " + name);`
`println(String.format(" ***$%,.2f", amountPaid));`

`diskScanner.close();`
`}`

[Figure 14-13](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0013) shows two runs of the code in [Listing 14-9](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0009). For the first run, an `EmployeePayment.txt` file contains the lines `Barry Burd` and `5000.00`, and the run ends with the words `Payroll completed`. For the second run, the user's hard drive contains no `EmployeePayment.txt` file, and the program doesn't display those misleading words.

![“Console output showing two payroll processing attempts. The first attempt successfully processes a payment of $5,000.00 to Barry Burd and indicates that the payroll is completed. The second attempt results in an error due to the missing file 'EmployeesPayment.txt', showing a 'No such file or directory' message.”](images/9781394289240-fg1413.png)

[FIGURE 14-13:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0013) Payroll completed? Maybe yes, maybe no.

The important part of [Listing 14-9](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0009) is in the `cutCheck` method's header. That header ends with `throws FileNotFoundException`. By announcing that it throws a `FileNotFoundException`, method `cutCheck` passes the buck. What this `throws` clause really says is, “I realize that a statement inside this method has the potential to throw a `FileNotFoundException`, but I'm not acknowledging the exception in a `try-catch` statement. Java compiler, please don't bug me about this. Instead of having a `try-catch` statement, I'm passing the responsibility for acknowledging the exception to the `main` method (the method that called the `cutCheck` method).”

Indeed, in the `main` method, the call to `cutCheck` is inside a `try` clause. That `try` clause has a `catch` clause with a parameter of type `FileNotFoundException`. So everything is okay. Method `cutCheck` passes the responsibility to the `main` method, and the `main` method accepts the responsibility with an appropriate `try-catch` statement. Everybody's happy. Even the Java compiler is happy.

To better understand the `throws` clause, imagine a volleyball game in which the volleyball is an exception. When a player on the other team serves, that player is throwing the exception. The ball crosses the net and heads directly toward you. If you pound the ball back across the net, you're catching the exception. But if you pass the ball to another player, you’re using the `throws` clause. In essence, you're saying, “Here, other player — *you* deal with this exception.”

![Remember](images/remember.png) A statement in a method can throw an exception that’s not matched by a `catch` clause. This includes situations in which the statement throwing the exception isn't even inside a `try` block. When this happens, execution of the program jumps out of the method that contains the offending statement. Execution jumps back to whatever code called the method in the first place.

![Tip](images/tip.png) A method can name more than one exception type in its `throws` clause. Just use commas to separate the names of the exception types, as in the following example:

`throws FileNotFoundException, NumberFormatException, ArithmeticException`

### Checked or unchecked?

Sneak a peek at [Listing 14-3](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0003) and notice that your newly declared `OutOfRangeException` class extends Java's `RuntimeException` class. Why would you want to extend `RuntimeException`? Here's the story:

*   **Any subclass of** `RuntimeException`**is unchecked.**

    In fact, any descendant (sub-subclass, sub-sub-subclass, and so on) of `RuntimeException` is unchecked. The unchecked exceptions represent errors that can crop up almost anywhere in your program. If your code crashes because of an unchecked exception, you should think about rewriting the code.

    Some common unchecked exceptions include the `NumberFormatException` (of [Listings 14-2](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0002), [14-4](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0004), and others), the `ArithmeticException`, the `IndexOutOfBoundsException`, the infamous `NullPointerException`, and many others. When you write Java code, much of your code is susceptible to these exceptions, but enclosing the code in `try` clauses (or passing the buck with `throws` clauses) is completely optional.

*   **Any exception that's not a descendant of** `RuntimeException`**is checked.**

    Checked exceptions represent challenges that the outside world may impose on your program. Like it or not, some files simply aren't where they should be, so you had better acknowledge this in your program and write code to make the best of it. Java's checked exceptions include the `FileNotFoundException`, (See [Listings 14-8](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0008) and [14-9](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0009).) the `PrinterException`, the `SQLException`, and a gang of other interesting exceptions.

#### READER'S ACKNOWLEDGMENTS

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) The following code doesn't compile, because the code throws an unacknowledged `FileNotFoundException`:

**`// BAD CODE:`**
`void main() {`
`var diskScanner = new Scanner(new File("numbers.txt"));`

`int[] numerators = new int[5];`
`int[] denominators = new int[5];`

`int i = 0;`

`while (diskScanner.hasNextInt()) {`
`numerators[i] = diskScanner.nextInt();`
`denominators[i] = diskScanner.nextInt();`
`i++;`
`}`

`for (int j = 0; j < numerators.length; j++) {`
`println(numerators[j] / denominators[j]);`
`}`

`diskScanner.close();`
`}`

Fix the unacknowledged `FileNotFoundException` so that the code compiles. Then notice that, depending on the values in the `numbers.txt` file, some other exceptions may be thrown during a run of the program. Add one or more `try-catch` statements to display messages about these exceptions without letting the program crash.

#### IF IT'S BROKEN, FIX IT

Add `try-catch` statements or `throws` clauses (or a mixture of these two things) to fix the following broken code:

**`// BAD CODE:`**
`void main() {`
`var fileIn = new File("input");`
`var fileInStrm = new FileInputStream(fileIn);`
`var dataInStrm = new DataInputStream(fileInStrm);`

`var fileOut = new File("output");`
`var fileOutStrm = new FileOutputStream(fileOut);`
`var dataOutStrm = new DataOutputStream(fileOutStrm);`

`int numFilesCopied = 0;`

`try {`
`while (true) {`
`dataOutStrm.writeByte(dataInStrm.readByte());`
`}`
`} catch (EOFException e) {`
`numFilesCopied = 1;`
`}`
`}`

When you've gotten the code to compile, create a file named `input` and run the code to see whether it creates the file named `output`.

## Try, Try Again!

Once upon a time, I was a young fellow living with my parents in Philadelphia and just starting to drive a car. I was heading toward a friend's house and thinking about who-knows-what when another car came from nowhere and bashed my car's passenger door. This kind of thing is called a RunARedLightException*.*

Anyway, both cars were still drivable, and we were squat in the middle of a busy intersection. To avoid causing a traffic jam, we both pulled over to the nearest curb. I fumbled for my driver’s license and opened the door to get out of my car.

And that’s when the second accident happened. As I was getting out of my car, a city bus was coming by. The bus hit me and rolled me against my car a few times. This kind of thing is called a DealWithLawyersException*.*

The truth is that everything came out just fine. I was bruised but not battered. My parents paid for the damage to the car, so I never suffered any financial consequences. (I managed to pass on the financial burden by putting the RunARedLightException into my `throws` clause.)

This incident helps to explain why I think the way I do about exception handling. In particular, I wonder, “What happens if, while the computer is recovering from one exception, a second exception is thrown?” After all, the statements inside a `catch` clause aren't immune to calamities.

Well, the answer to this question is anything but simple. For starters, you can put a `try` statement inside a `catch` clause. This protects you against unexpected, potentially embarrassing incidents that can crop up during the execution of the `catch` clause. But when you start worrying about cascading exceptions, you open up a slimy can of worms. The number of scenarios is large, and things can become complicated quickly. The program in [Listing 14-10](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0010) helps you sort things out.

[LISTING 14-10](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fea-0010) Using Two Files

`void main() {`
`println("Starting payroll … ");`
`try {`
`cutCheck();`
`println("Payroll completed.");`
`} catch (FileNotFoundException e) {`
`println(e.getMessage());`
`}`
`}`

`void cutCheck() throws FileNotFoundException {`
**`var diskScanner = new Scanner(new File("EmployeePayment.txt"));`**
**`var diskScanner2 = new Scanner(new File("LegalInfo.txt"));`**

`String name = diskScanner.nextLine();`
`double amountPaid = diskScanner.nextDouble();`
`String disclaimer = diskScanner2.nextLine();`

`print("Pay to the order of " + name);`
`println(String.format(" ***$%,.2f", amountPaid));`
`println(disclaimer);`

**`diskScanner.close();`**
**`diskScanner2.close();`**
`}`

The output of a run from [Listing 14-10](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0010) might look like the text in [Figure 14-14](#c14.xhtml_c14-fig-0014).

![“A text box with a payroll message stating: 'Starting payroll... Pay to the order of Barry Burd ***$5,000.00 We’re not responsible for anything at all. Payroll completed.'”](images/9781394289240-fg1414.png)

[FIGURE 14-14:](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fig-0014) You'll hear from our lawyers!

But what happens if, after successfully opening the first file (`EmployeePayment.txt`), Java can't find the second file (`LegalInfo.txt`)? Then Java throws a `FileNotFoundException` and jumps immediately out of the `cutCheck` method. Java never calls `diskScanner.close()`, and the connection to `EmployeePayment.txt` lives on in limbo. With a small program like the one in [Listing 14-10](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0010), this is no big deal. But if your code repeatedly fails to close such resources, you could be in big trouble.

That's why Java has a try-with-resources feature. A *try-with-resources statement* keeps track of all the things you've opened and closes them automatically when they're no longer needed. [Listing 14-11](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0011) has the full story.

[LISTING 14-11](#c14.xhtml_rc14-fea-0011) Making Sure to Close Resources

`void main() {`
`println("Starting payroll … ");`
`try {`
`cutCheck();`
`println("Payroll completed.");`
`} catch (FileNotFoundException e) {`
`println(e.getMessage());`
`}`
`}`

`void cutCheck() throws FileNotFoundException {`
**`try (var diskScanner = new Scanner(new File("EmployeePayment.txt"));`**
**`var diskScanner2 = new Scanner(new File("LegalInfo.txt"))) {`**

`String name = diskScanner.nextLine();`
`double amountPaid = diskScanner.nextDouble();`
`String disclaimer = diskScanner2.nextLine();`

`print("Pay to the order of " + name);`
`println(String.format(" ***$%,.2f", amountPaid));`
`println(disclaimer);`

**`} catch (FileNotFoundException e) {`**
**`println("Abnormal return from the cutCheck method … ");`**
**`throw e;`**
**`}`**
`}`

In [Listing 14-11](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0011), the declarations of `diskScanner1` and `diskScanner2` are in parentheses after the word `try`. The parenthesized declarations tell Java to close `diskScanner1` and `diskScanner2` automatically after execution of the statements in the `try` clause. Java closes these connections whether you throw an exception or not.

Look at all the statements in the body of the `cutCheck` method. Not one of these statements contains the word `close`. You don't need any explicit `close` calls. The try-with-resources statement does all the closing for you.

You can access all kinds of resources (files, databases, connections to servers, and others) and have peace of mind knowing that Java will sever the connections automatically when necessary. Life is good!

Before we bid a fond farewell to [Listing 14-11](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0011), let's take a moment to look at the `cutCheck` method's `catch` clause. As in [Listing 14-9](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0009), we want the code's `main` method to do something about a missing `LegalInfo.txt` file. We could add lots of clunky `if` statements to make `main` bend to our will, but it's better to have `main` respond to the original `FileNotFoundException`. In the `cutCheck` method's `catch` clause, the variable `e` stands for that unsavory `FileNotFoundException`. So the statement `throw e` does the trick. That statement says, “Pass this despicable `FileNotFoundException` on to whichever method called me.” And the “method that called me” is `main`.

![Tip](images/tip.png) In a try-with-resources statement, the `catch` clause is optional. In [Listing 14-11](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0011), if you remove the `catch` clause, the program behaves almost the same way. The only thing you lose is the possibility of getting the `Abnormal return from the cutCheck method` output.

#### DON'T MISS A TRICK

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Modify the code in [Listing 14-11](#c14.xhtml_c14-fea-0011) so that it checks for sensible values in the two disk files. Does the `EmployeePayment.txt` file have a name and a double value? Does the `LegalInfo.txt` file contain any text?

Chapter 15

# Fancy Reference Types

IN THIS CHAPTER

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Writing and using a Java interface**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Working with abstract classes**

In previous chapters, you may have read about the things that full-time and part-time employees have in common. In particular, both the `FullTimeEmployee` and `PartTimeEmployee` classes can extend the `Employee` class. That's nice to know if you're running a small business, but what if you're not running a business? What if you're taking care of house pets?

This chapter explores the care of house pets and other burning issues.

## Java's Types

[Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml) explains that Java has these two kinds of types:

*   **Primitive types:** Java has a total of eight primitive types: The four you use most often are `int`, `double`, `boolean`, and `char`.
*   **Reference types:** Java's API has thousands of reference types, and, when you write a Java program, you define new reference types.

    Java's `String` type is a reference type. So are Java's `Scanner`, `Random`, `ArrayList`, and `File` types. In [Chapter 10](#c10.xhtml), you create your own `Employee`, `FullTimeEmployee`, and `PartTimeEmployee` reference types. When you write

    `public class NiceExample {`

    `public static void main(String[] args) {`

    you define a reference type named `NiceExample`. When you put

    `void main() {`

    in a file named `EvenBetterExample.java`, you implicitly define a reference type named `EvenBetterExample`. You may not realize it, but every array belongs to a reference type. The list of reference types goes on and on.

In Java, reference types are everywhere. But until this point in the book, the only reference types you see are classes and arrays. Java has other kinds of reference types, and this chapter explores the possibilities.

## The Java Interface

Think about a class (such as an `Employee` class) and a subclass (such as a `FullTimeEmployee` class). The relationship between a class and its subclass is one of inheritance. In many real-life families, a child inherits assets from a parent. And in [Chapter 10](#c10.xhtml), the `FullTimeEmployee` class inherits `name` and `jobTitle` fields from the `Employee` class. That's the way it works.

But consider the relationship between an editor and an author. The editor says, “By signing this contract, you agree to submit a completed manuscript by the ninth of September.” Despite any excuses that the author gives before the deadline date (and, believe me, authors make plenty of excuses), the relationship between the editor and the author is one of obligation. The author agrees to take on certain responsibilities; and, in order to continue being an author, the author must fulfill those responsibilities. (By the way, there's no subtext in this paragraph — none at all.)

Now consider Barry Burd. Who? Barry Burd — that guy who writes *Java For Dummies* and certain other *For Dummies* books (all from Wiley Publishing). He's a college professor, and he's also an author. You want to mirror this situation in a Java program, but Java doesn't support multiple inheritance. You can't make Barry extend both a `Professor` class and an `Author` class at the same time.

Fortunately for Barry, Java has interfaces. An interface is a kind of reference type. In fact, the code to create an interface looks a lot like the code to create a class:

`public interface` *`MyInterfaceName`* `{`
`// blah, blah, blah`
`}`

An interface is a lot like a class, but an interface is different. (What else is new? A cow is like a planet, but it's quite a bit different. Cows moo; planets hang in space.)

Anyway, when you read the word *interface,* you can start by thinking of a class. Then, in your head, note that

*   A class can extend only one parent class, but a class can implement many interfaces.
*   A parent class is a bunch of stuff that a class inherits. But an *interface* is a bunch of stuff that an implementing class is *obligated to provide*.

What about poor Barry? He can be an instance of a `Person` class with all the fields that any person has — `name`, `address`, `age`, `height`, `weight`, and so on. He can also implement more than one interface:

*   Because Barry implements a `Professor` interface, he must have methods named `teachStudents`, `adviseStudents`, and `gradePapers`.
*   Because he implements an `Author` interface, he must have methods named `writeChapters`, `reviewChapters`, `answerEmail`, and so on.

### Declaring two interfaces

Imagine two different kinds of data. One is a column of numbers that comes from an array. Another is a table (with rows and columns) that comes from a disk file. What might these two things have in common?

I don't know about you, but I may want to display both kinds of data. So, I can write code to create a contract. The contract says, “Whoever signs this contract agrees to have a `display` method.” In [Listing 15-1](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0001), I declare a `Displayable` interface.

[LISTING 15-1](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fea-0001) Behold! An Interface!

`package com.example.data;`

`public interface Displayable {`
`void display();`
`}`

Wait just a darn minute! The `display` method declaration in [Listing 15-1](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0001) has a header but no body. No curly braces appear after `display()` — only a lonely-looking semicolon. What's going on here?

To answer the question, I'll let the code in [Listing 15-1](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0001) speak for itself. If the code in the listing could talk, here's what the code would say:

*   “As an interface, my `display` method has a header but no body. A class that claims to implement me (the `Displayable` interface) must provide (either directly or indirectly) a body for the `display` method. That is, a class that claims to implement `Displayable` must, in one way or another, provide its own code of the following kind:

    `public void display() {`
    `// Some statements go here`
    `}`

*   To implement me (the interface in [Listing 15-1](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0001)), the new code's `display` method must take no parameters and return nothing (also known as `void`).”

The `Displayable` interface is like a legal contract. The `Displayable` interface doesn't tell you what an implementing class already has. Instead, the `Displayable` interface tells you what an implementing class must declare in its own code.

In addition to displaying columns of numbers and tables, I may also want to summarize both kinds of data. How do you summarize a column of numbers? I don't know. Maybe you display the total of all the numbers. And how do you summarize a table? Maybe you display the table's column headings. How you summarize the data isn't my concern. All I care about is that you have some way to summarize the data.

So, I create code containing a second Java contract. The second contract says, “Whoever signs this contract agrees to have a `summarize` method.” In [Listing 15-2](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0002), I declare a `Summarizable` interface.

[LISTING 15-2](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fea-0002) Another Interface

`package com.example.data;`

`public interface Summarizable {`
`String summarize();`
`}`

Any class claiming to implement the `Summarizable` interface must, by hook or by crook, provide an implementation of a `summarize` method — a method with no parameters that returns a `String` value.

![Remember](images/remember.png) In the declaration of an interface, a particular method might have no body of its own. A method with no body is called an *abstract method.*

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) An interface's abstract method is automatically public whether you use the word `public` or not. For example, in [Listing 15-2](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0002), the line

`String summarize();`

has the same meaning as

`public String summarize();`

If you enjoy typing the word `public`, feel free to do so. If not, leave that word out.

### Implementing interfaces

[Listing 15-3](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0003) implements the `Displayable` and `Summarizable` interfaces for a class named `ColumnOfNumbers`. To `display` a column of numbers, the listing prints all the numbers. To `summarize` a column of numbers, the listing prints the total of all the numbers.

[LISTING 15-3](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fea-0003) Implementing Two Interfaces

`package com.example.data;`

`public class ColumnOfNumbers` **`implements Displayable, Summarizable`** `{`
`private double numbers[];`

`public ColumnOfNumbers(double[] numbers) {`
`this.numbers = numbers;`
`}`

**`@Override`**
**`public void display() {`**
`for (double d : numbers) {`
`System.out.println(d);`
`}`
`}`

**`@Override`**
**`public String summarize() {`**
`double total = 0.0;`
`for (double d : numbers) {`
`total += d;`
`}`
`return Double.toString(total);`
`}`
`}`

![Remember](images/remember.png) When you implement an interface, you provide bodies for the interface's abstract methods.

Java's compiler is serious about the use of the `implements` keyword. If you remove either of the two method declarations from [Listing 15-3](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0003) without removing the `implements` clause, you see some frightening error messages in your IDE's editor. Java expects you to honor the contract that the `implements` keyword implies. If you don't honor the contract, Java refuses to compile your code. So there!

![Tip](images/tip.png) You can use Java's error messages to your advantage. Start by typing some code containing the clause `implements Displayable``,``Summarizable`. Because of the `implements` clause, the editor displays an error mark and lists the names of the methods that you should have declared but didn't. In this section's example, those method names are `display` and `summarize`. After a few more mouse-clicks, the IDE generates simple `display` and `summarize` methods for you.

One final word about the code in [Listing 15-3](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0003): The fact that the `display` method prints all the numbers and the `summarize` method prints the total is my choice alone, and it doesn't have to be your choice. If you want the `display` method to print “Hello, World!” and the `summarize` method to print today's news, that's up to you. The only demand that Java makes is as follows:

> Because the `ColumnOfNumbers` code begins with `implements Displayable, Summarizable`, you must tell Java what to do if and when someone calls this class's `display` and `summarize` methods.

[Listing 15-4](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0004) contains another class that implements the `Displayable` and `Summarizable` interfaces. To `display` a table, the listing prints all the rows in the table. To `summarize` a table, the listing prints the table's header row.

[LISTING 15-4](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fea-0004) Another Class Implements the Interfaces

`package com.example.data;`

`import module java.base;`

`public class Table` **`implements Displayable, Summarizable`** `{`
`private Scanner diskFile;`
`private ArrayList<String> lines = new ArrayList<>();`

`public Table(String fileName) {`
`try {`
`diskFile = new Scanner(new File(fileName));`
`} catch (FileNotFoundException e) {`
`e.getMessage();`
`}`

`while (diskFile.hasNextLine()) {`
`lines.add(diskFile.nextLine());`
`}`
`}`

**`@Override`**
**`public void display()`** `{`
`for (String line : lines) {`
`System.out.println(line);`
`}`
`}`

**`@Override`**
**`public String summarize()`** `{`
`return lines.get(0);`
`}`
`}`

In [Listings 15-3](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0003) and [15-4](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0004), notice several uses of the `@Override` annotation. In [Chapter 10](#c10.xhtml), you use `@Override` to signal the replacement of a method that's already been declared in a superclass. But you can also use `@Override` to signal an interface method's implementation. That’s what I do in [Listings 15-3](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0003) and [15-4](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0004).

### Putting the pieces together

The code in [Listing 15-5](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0005) makes use of all the stuff in [Listings 15-1](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0001) to [15-4](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0004).

[LISTING 15-5](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fea-0005) Getting the Most Out of Your Interfaces

`import com.example.data.*;`

`public class UseColumnAndTable {`

`public static void main(String[] args) {`
`double numbers[] = {21.7, 68.3, 5.5};`
`var column = new ColumnOfNumbers(numbers);`

`displayMe(column);`
`summarizeMe(column);`

`var table = new Table("MyTable.txt");`

`displayMe(table);`
`summarizeMe(table);`
`}`

`static void displayMe(Displayable displayable) {`
`displayable.display();`
`System.out.println();`
`}`

`static void summarizeMe(Summarizable summarizable) {`
`System.out.println(summarizable.summarize());`
`System.out.println();`
`}`
`}`

With the `MyTable.txt` file shown in [Figure 15-1](#c15.xhtml_c15-fig-0001), the output from [Listing 15-5](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0005) is shown in [Figure 15-2](#c15.xhtml_c15-fig-0002).

![A table listing names, IDs, and balances for three individuals: Barry (ID 01, Balance 19.51) Carol (ID 02, Balance 100.35) Myrna (ID 03, Balance 10.07). The table displays information useful for tracking individual balances associated with specific IDs.](images/9781394289240-fg1501.png)

[FIGURE 15-1:](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fig-0001) The `MyTable.txt` file.

Feast your eyes on the `displayMe` method in [Listing 15-5](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0005). What kind of parameter does the `displayMe` method take? Is it a `ColumnOfNumbers`? No. Is it a `Table`? No.

![“A list of numerical values followed by a table containing names, IDs, and balances. The numbers listed are 21.7, 68.3, 5.5, and 95.5\. The table includes three rows with the following data: Barry (ID 01, Balance 19.51), Carol (ID 02, Balance 100.35), and Myrna (ID 03, Balance 10.07). There is also a repeated header 'Name ID Balance' at the bottom of the table.”](images/9781394289240-fg1502.png)

[FIGURE 15-2:](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fig-0002) Running the code in [Listing 15-5](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0005).

The `displayMe` method knows nothing about `ColumnOfNumbers` instances or `Table` instances. All the `displayMe` method knows about is things that implement `Displayable`. That's what the `displayMe` method's parameter list says. When you hand something that implements the `Displayable` interface to the `displayMe` method, the `displayMe` method knows what it can do. The `displayMe` method can call the parameter's `display` method, because that parameter object is guaranteed to have a `display` method.

The same kind of thing is true about the `summarizeMe` method in [Listing 15-5](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0005). How do you know that you can call `summarizable.summarize()` inside the body of the `summarizeMe` method? You can make this call because `summarizable` has to have a `summarize()` method. The rules about Java interfaces guarantee it.

That's the real power behind Java's interfaces.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) In this section, the `ColumnOfNumbers` and `Table` classes implement the `Displayable` and `Summarizable` interfaces. What about a `Deletable` interface? Any class implementing the `Deletable` interface must have its own `delete` method.

#### CONTROL ALT DELETE

Create the `DeletableColumnOfNumbers` class — a subclass of the `ColumnOfNumbers` class. In addition to all the things `ColumnOfNumbers` does, the `DeletableColumnOfNumbers` class also implements the `Deletable` interface. When you `delete` a column of numbers, you set the values of each of its entries to `0.0`.

Create the `DeletableTable` class — a subclass of the `Table` class. In addition to all the things `Table` does, the `DeletableTable` class also implements the `Deletable` interface. When you `delete` a table, you remove all rows except the first (table heading) row. (***Hint:*** If you call the `lines` list's `remove` method starting from the `1` row and going to the `lines.size()` row, you won't be happy with the results. A call to the `remove` method modifies the list immediately, and that can mess up your loop.)

## Abstract Classes

Consider the wondrous variety of life on planet earth:^([*](#c15.xhtml_c15-note-0001))

*   A gelada monkey spends the day on a grassy plateau. But at night the gelada goes for a snooze on the rocky, perilous edge of a mountain cliff. With any luck, the sleeping monkey doesn't toss and turn much.
*   A Pompeii worm lives in an underwater tube. The temperature by the worm's head is about 72 degrees Fahrenheit (22 degrees Celsius). But at the other end of the worm, the water temperature is normally 176 degrees Fahrenheit (80 degrees Celsius). If you know one of these worms personally, don't buy any warm socks for it.
*   A sea squirt lives part of its life as an animal. At a certain point in its life cycle, the sea squirt attaches itself permanently to a rock and then digests its own brain, effectively turning itself into a plant.
*   A tiny water bear can survive 12 days (and maybe more) with no atmosphere in the vacuum of outer space. Even the cosmic radiation in outer space doesn't harm a water bear. In 2017, a team of scientists concluded that water bears would survive most mass extinction events, including nearby supernova blasts and large asteroid impacts. So that's what I want to be in my next life — a water bear.

With so much biological diversity, there's very little I can say that applies to every animal on earth. But, I can certainly point to two properties: Every animal has a particular weight (measured in pounds, kilograms, or even picograms), and every animal makes (or, possibly, doesn't make) a characteristic sound. [Listing 15-6](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0006) has the complete scoop.

[LISTING 15-6](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fea-0006) What a Programmer Knows about Animals

`package com.example.species;`

`public class Animal {`
`private double weight;`
`private String sound;`

`public Animal(double weight, String sound) {`
`this.weight = weight;`
`this.sound = sound;`
`}`

`public String getSound() {`
`return sound;`
`}`

`public double getWeight() {`
`return weight;`
`}`
`}`

While I was typing the code for the `Animal` class, I had to stop and correct several typing mistakes. The mistakes weren't really my fault: My cat was walking back and forth across my computer keyboard. And that brings me from the subject of all animals to the topic of house pets.

A house pet is an animal. But every house pet has a name — like Fluffy, Spot, or Princess. And every house pet has a recommended routine for taking care of the pet.

Of course, the care routines differ greatly from one kind of pet to another. If I had a dog, I'd have to walk the dog. But I'd never try to walk a cat. In fact, I don't even let our cat out of the house. So, when I define my `HousePet` class, I want to be vague about pet care instructions. And in Java, a class that's somewhat vague is called an *abstract class*. [Listing 15-7](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0007) has an example.

[LISTING 15-7](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fea-0007) What It Means to Be a House Pet

`package com.example.species;`

`import static java.lang.System.out;`

`public` **`abstract`** `class HousePet extends Animal {`
`private String name;`

`public HousePet(String name, double weight, String sound) {`
`super(weight, sound);`
`this.name = name;`
`}`

`public String getName() {`
`return name;`
`}`

**`abstract public void howToCareFor();`**

`public void about() {`
`out.print(name + " weighs " + getWeight() + " pounds");`
`out.print(getSound() != null ? (" and says '" + getSound() + "'") : "");`
`out.println(".");`
`}`
`}`

On the first line of [Listing 15-7](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0007), the keyword `abstract` tells Java that `HousePet` is an abstract class. Because `HousePet` is an abstract class, `HousePet` can have an abstract method. And in [Listing 15-7](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0007), `howToCareFor` is an abstract method. An abstract method has a header but no body. In an abstract method's declaration, there are no curly braces — only a semicolon where curly braces would normally appear.

So, when you try to execute the `howToCareFor` method, what happens? Well, you can't really execute the `howToCareFor` method in [Listing 15-7](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0007). In fact, you can't even create an instance of the abstract class declared in [Listing 15-7](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0007). The following lines of code are illegal:

**`// VERY BAD CODE:`**
`HousePet myPet = new HousePet("Boop", 12.0, "Meow");`
`var yourPet = new HousePet("Pawz", 22.5, "Woof");`

An abstract class has no life of its own. To use an abstract class, you have to create an ordinary (non-abstract) class that extends the abstract class. In the ordinary class, all methods have bodies. So, everything works out.

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) Before you walk away from [Listing 15-7](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0007), notice the `super(weight, sound)` call in that listing. As in [Chapter 10](#c10.xhtml), the keyword `super` triggers a call to the superclass's constructor. In [Listing 15-7](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0007), calling `super(weight, sound)` is like calling the `Animal(double weight, String sound)` constructor from [Listing 15-6](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0006). The constructor assigns values to the new object's `weight` and `sound` fields.

### Caring for your pet

Here's a quotation from the book *Java For Dummies,* 9th Edition:

> “To use an abstract class, you have to create an ordinary (non-abstract) class that extends the abstract class.”

So, to use the `HousePet` class in [Listing 15-7](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0007), you have to create a class that extends the `HousePet` class. The code in [Listing 15-8](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0008) extends the abstract `HousePet` class and provides a body for the method named `howToCareFor`.

[LISTING 15-8](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fea-0008) It's a Dog's Life

`package com.example.species;`

`import static java.lang.System.out;`

`public class Dog` **`extends HousePet`** `{`
`private int walksPerDay;`

`public Dog(String name, double weight, int walksPerDay) {`
`super(name, weight, "Woof");`
`this.walksPerDay = walksPerDay;`
`}`

**`@Override`**
**`public void howToCareFor() {`**
`out.print("Walk " + getName());`
`out.println(" " + walksPerDay + " times each day.");`
`}`
`}`

In addition to having a `name`, a `weight`, and a `sound`, every dog gets walked a certain number of times per day. And now, because of the `howToCareFor` method's body, you know what caring for a dog means: It means walking the dog a certain number of times each day. It's a good thing the `howToCareFor` method is abstract in the `HousePet` class. You wouldn't necessarily want to walk some other kind of pet.

Take, for example, a domestic cat. “Caring” for a cat may mean not bothering it too often. And cats have other characteristics — characteristics that don't apply to dogs. For example, some cats go outdoors; others don't. You can make `walksPerDay` be 0 for an indoor cat, but that feels like cheating. Instead, each cat can have a `boolean` value representing the cat's indoor/outdoor status. [Listing 15-9](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0009) has the code.

[LISTING 15-9](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fea-0009) How to Be a Cat

`package com.example.species;`

`public class Cat` **`extends HousePet`** `{`
`private boolean isOutdoor;`

`public Cat(String name, double weight, boolean isOutdoor) {`
`super(name, weight, "Meow");`
`this.isOutdoor = isOutdoor;`
`}`

**`@Override`**
**`public void howToCareFor() {`**
`System.out.println`
`(isOutdoor ? "Let " : "Do not let " + getName() + " outdoors.");`
`}`
`}`

Both the `Dog` and `Cat` classes are subclasses of the `HousePet` class. And, because of the abstract method declaration in [Listing 15-7](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0007), both the `Dog` and `Cat` classes must have `howToCareFor` methods. But the `howToCareFor` methods in the two classes are quite different. One method refers to a `walksPerDay` field; the other method refers to an `isOutdoor` field. And because the `HousePet` class's `howToCareFor` method is abstract, there's no default behavior. Either the `Dog` and `Cat` classes implement their own `howToCareFor` methods or the `Dog` and `Cat` classes can't claim to extend `HousePet`.

![Technical Stuff](images/technicalstuff.png) This paragraph describes a picky detail, and you should ignore it if you have any inclination to do so: The `Dog` and `Cat` classes must implement the `howToCareFor` method because the `Dog` and `Cat` classes aren't abstract. If the `Dog` and `Cat` classes were abstract (that is, if they were abstract classes extending the abstract `HousePet` class), then the `Dog` and `Cat` classes would not have to implement the `howToCareFor` method. The `Dog` and `Cat` classes could pass the implementation buck to their own subclasses. For that matter, an abstract class that implements an interface doesn't have to provide bodies for all the interfaces' abstract methods. Abstract classes can take advantage of many little loopholes. But, to use these loopholes, you have to create some exotic programming examples. So, in this chapter, I simplify the story and write that (a) a class that extends an abstract class must provide bodies for the abstract class's abstract methods and (b) a class that implements an interface must provide bodies for the interface's abstract methods. It's not exactly true, but it's good enough for now.

If you live in a very small apartment, you may not have room for a dog or a cat. In that case, [Listing 15-10](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0010) is for you.

[LISTING 15-10](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fea-0010) You May Grow Up to Be a Fish

`package com.example.species;`

`public class Fish` **`extends HousePet`** `{`

`public Fish(String name, double weight) {`
`super(name, weight, null);`
`}`

**`@Override`**
**`public void howToCareFor() {`**
`System.out.println("Feed " + getName() + " daily.");`
`}`
`}`

I could go on and on creating subclasses of the `HousePet` class. Many years ago, our daughter had some pet mice. Caring for the mice meant keeping the cat away from them.

In Java, subclasses multiply like rabbits.

### Using all your classes

Your work isn't finished until you've tested your code. Most programs require hours, days, and even months of testing. But, for this chapter's `HousePet` example, I do only one test. The test is in [Listing 15-11](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0011).

[LISTING 15-11](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fea-0011) The Class Menagerie

`import com.example.species.*;`

`void main() {`
`var dog1 = new Dog("Fido", 54.7, 3);`

`var dog2 = new Dog("Rover", 15.2, 2);`

`var cat1 = new Cat("Felix", 10.0, false);`

`var fish1 = new Fish("Bubbles", 0.1);`

`dog1.howToCareFor();`
`dog2.howToCareFor();`
`cat1.howToCareFor();`
`fish1.howToCareFor();`

`dog1.about();`
`dog2.about();`
`cat1.about();`
`fish1.about();`
`}`

When you run the code in [Listing 15-11](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0011), you get the output shown in [Figure 15-3](#c15.xhtml_c15-fig-0003).

![The image shows a list of instructions and details about four pets: Fido, Rover, Felix, and Bubbles. It provides information on their walking schedules, feeding instructions, weights, and the sounds they make. Walk Fido 3 times each day. Walk Rover 2 times each day. Do not let Felix outdoors. Feed Bubbles daily. Fido weighs 51.7 pounds and says 'Woof'. Rover weighs 15.2 pounds and says 'Woof'. Felix weighs 10.0 pounds and says 'Meow'. Bubbles weighs 0.1 pounds.](images/9781394289240-fg1503.png)

[FIGURE 15-3:](#c15.xhtml_rc15-fig-0003) Please don't pet the Pompeii worm.

Notice how the code in [Listing 15-11](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0011) seamlessly and effortlessly calls many versions of the `howToCareFor` method. With the `dog1.howToCareFor()` and `dog2.howToCareFor()` calls, Java executes the method in [Listing 15-8](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0008). With the `cat1.howToCareFor()` call, Java executes the method in [Listing 15-9](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0009). And, with the `fish1.howToCareFor()` call, Java executes the method in [Listing 15-10](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0010) — it's like having a big `if` statement without writing the `if` statement's code. When you add a new class for a pet mouse, you don't have to enlarge an existing `if` statement. There's no `if` statement to enlarge.

Notice also how the `about` method in the abstract `HousePet` class keeps track of the object that called it. For example, when you call `dog1.about()` in [Listing 15-11](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0011), the `HousePet` class's nonspecific `about` method knows that the sound `dog1` makes is `Woof`. Everything falls into place very nicely.

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Do you like abstract art? You can use abstract classes to create abstract art!

#### ASCII ART

Create an abstract class named `Shape`. The `Shape` class has a `size` field (of type `int`) and an abstract `show` method. Extend the abstract `Shape` class with two other classes: a `Square` class and a `Triangle` class. In the bodies of the `Square` and `Triangle` classes' `show` methods, place the code that creates a text-based rendering of the shape in question. For example, a `Square` of size 5 looks like this:

`--------`
`| |`
`| |`
`| |`
`--------`

A `Triangle` of size 2 looks like this:

`/\`
`/ \`
`----`

## Relax! You're Not Seeing Double!

If you've read this chapter's earlier sections on interfaces and abstract methods, your head might be spinning. Both interfaces and abstract classes have abstract methods. But the abstract methods play slightly different roles in these two kinds of reference types. How can you keep it all straight in your mind?

The first thing to do is to remember that no one learns about object-oriented programming concepts without getting lots of practice in writing code. If you've read this chapter and you're confused, that may be a good thing. It means you've understood enough to know how complicated this stuff is. The more code you write, the more comfortable you'll become with classes, interfaces, and all these other ideas.

The next thing to do is to sort out the differences in the way you declare abstract methods. [Table 15-1](#c15.xhtml_c15-tbl-0001) has the story.

|  | **In an Ordinary (Non-Abstract) Class** | **In an Interface** | **In an Abstract Class** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Are abstract methods allowed? | No | Yes | Yes |
| Can a method declaration contain the `abstract` keyword? | No | Yes | Yes |
| Can a method declaration contain the `default` keyword (meaning “not abstract”)? | No | Yes | No |
| With neither the `abstract` nor the `default` keyword, a method is: | Not abstract | Abstract | Not abstract |

[TABLE 15-1](#c15.xhtml_rc15-tbl-0001) Using (or Not Using) Abstract Methods

Both interfaces and abstract classes have abstract methods. So you may be wondering how you should choose between declaring an interface and declaring an abstract class. In fact, you might ask three professional programmers how interfaces and abstract classes differ from one another. If you do, you may get five different answers. (Yes, five answers; not three answers.)

Interfaces and abstract classes are similar beasts. But the basic idea is about the relationships among things.

*   **Extending a subclass represents an *is a* relationship.**

    Think about the relationships in this chapter's earlier section “[Abstract Classes](#c15.xhtml_c15-sec-0015).” A house pet is an animal. A dog is a house pet. A cat is a house pet. A fish is a house pet.

*   **Implementing an interface represents a *can do* relationship.**

    Think about the relationships in this chapter's earlier section “[The Java Interface](#c15.xhtml_c15-sec-0003).” The class declaration in [Listing 15-3](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0003) says `implements Displayable, Summarizable`. With these words, the code promises that each `ColumnOfNumbers` object can be displayed and summarized. Later in the same listing, you make good on the promise by declaring a `display` method and a `summarize` method.

If you want more tangible evidence of the difference between an interface and an abstract class, consider this: A class can implement many interfaces, but a class can extend only one other class, even if that one class is an abstract class. So, after you've declared

`public class Dog extends HousePet`

you can't also make `Dog` extend a `Friend` class. But you can make `Dog` implement a `Befriendable` interface. And then you can make the same `Dog` class implement a `Trainable` interface. (By the way, I've tried making my `Cat` class implement a `Trainable` interface but, for some reason, it never works.)

And, if you want an even *more* tangible difference between an interface and an abstract class, I have one for you: An interface can't contain any nonstatic, nonfinal fields. For example, if the `HousePet` class in [Listing 15-7](#c15.xhtml_c15-fea-0007) were an interface, it couldn't have a `name` field. That simply wouldn't be allowed.

So there. Interfaces and abstract classes are different from one another. But if you're new at the game, you shouldn't worry about the difference. Just read as much code as you can, and don't get scared when you see an abstract method. That's all there is to it.

Chapter 16

# Oooey GUI Was a Worm

IN THIS CHAPTER

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Displaying a window**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Using buttons and text fields**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Responding to mouse-clicks**

There’s a wonderful old joke about a circus acrobat jumping over mice. Unfortunately, I’d get sued for copyright infringement if I included the joke in this book.

Anyway, the joke is about starting small and working your way up to bigger things. That’s what you do when you read *Java For Dummies,* 9th Edition.

Most of the programs in this book are text-based. A *text-based* program has no windows, no dialog boxes — nothing of that kind. In a text-based program, the user types characters in a command window, and the program displays output in the same command window.

These days, very few publicly available programs are text-based. Almost all programs use a *GUI* — a *g*raphical *u*ser *i*nterface*.* So if you’ve read every word of this book so far, you’re probably saying to yourself, “When am I going to find out how to create a GUI?”

Well, now's the time! This chapter introduces you to the world of GUI programming in Java.

## Getting Started with GUI Applications

Java comes with three sets of classes for creating GUI applications:

*   **The Abstract Window Toolkit (AWT):** The original set of classes, dating back to JDK 1.0.

    Classes in this set belong to packages whose names begin with `java.awt`. Components in this set have names like `Button`, `Frame`, `TextField`, and so on.

    The AWT implements only the kinds of components that were available on all common operating systems in the mid-1990s. So, using AWT, you can add a button to your application, but you can't easily add a table or a tree.

*   **Java Swing:** A set of classes created to fix some of the difficulties posed by the use of the AWT. Swing was introduced with Java 1.1 in 1997.

    Classes in this set belong to packages whose names begin with `javax.swing`. Components in this set have names like `JButton`, `JTextField`, `JFrame`, and so on.

    A Swing program looks the same on every operating system. But Swing components aren't pretty. A `JButton` looks primitive and crude compared to a Mac button or a Windows button.

*   **JavaFX:** The newest set of GUI classes in Oracle standard Java.

    Classes in this set belong to packages whose names begin with `javafx`.

    JavaFX supports over 60 kinds of components. (Sure, you want a `Button` component. But do you also want an `Accordion` component? JavaFX has one.) In addition, JavaFX supports multitouch operations and takes advantage of each processor's specialized graphics capabilities.

This chapter introduces JavaFX.

### Setting up your computer

[Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml) tells you to create Java programs using any IDE that works for you. In most chapters, you can use Eclipse, VSCode, BlueJ, or even Notepad with the Windows MS-DOS prompt. But in this chapter, I guide you through the creation of a JavaFX app using IntelliJ IDEA.

Don't get me wrong. IntelliJ isn’t the only IDE that lets you create JavaFX apps. But a typical JavaFX app consists of several folders, each with several files. You want an IDE to help you keep track of the moving parts. In this chapter, I have to pick one IDE to write about. So I pick IntelliJ.

![Tip](images/tip.png) Names can be confusing. The letters *IDE* stand for *Integrated Development Environment*. The most commonly used IDE for Java program development is *IntelliJ IDEA*, sometimes known simply as *IntelliJ* and occasionally known as *IDEA*. The company that makes IntelliJ IDEA used to be called *IntelliJ*. But in 2003, the company changed its name to *JetBrains*.

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) If you don't have IntelliJ IDEA on your computer, you can find installation instructions in Appendix A. You can find that appendix by visiting [`http://www.dummies.com/go/javafd9e`](http://www.dummies.com/go/javafd9e).

#### Installing the JavaFX plug-in

To make sure that IntelliJ can create a JavaFX app, you need IntelliJ's JavaFX plug-in. To get the plug-in, do the following:

1.  **Go to IntelliJ's main menu. On Windows, choose File ⇒   Settings ⇒ Plugins. On a Mac, choose IntelliJ IDEA ⇒   Settings ⇒   Plugins.**

    After doing this, you may notice two tabs: one labeled Marketplace and another labeled Installed. (See [Figure 16-1](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0001).)

2.  **Select the Marketplace tab.**

    At the top of the Marketplace list, there's a search field. (Refer to [Figure 16-1](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0001).)

3.  **In the search field, type** JavaFX**.**

    As a result, IntelliJ shows you a list of relevant plug-ins. (Yup! Refer to [Figure 16-1](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0001).) Each item in the list has an Install button or a selected check box.

    One of the list's items has the one-word name *JavaFX*. (At least, that's the plug-in's name in late 2024.) The item's fine print says *JetBrains s.r.o.*, which stands for JetBrains Společnost s ručením omezeným. If that item has a check box that's been selected, you already have the JavaFX plug-in.

4.  **If the JavaFX item has an Install button, click that button.**

    When you do, IntelliJ installs its JavaFX plug-n.

![Tip](images/tip.png) If you can't decide which plug-in you want, install more than one of them. Having too many plug-ins is usually better than having no plug-ins.

![Tip](images/tip.png) The JetBrains company makes two versions of IntelliJ IDEA: the free Community Edition and the paid Ultimate Edition. If you can't install the JavaFX plug-in on the Community Edition, try the Ultimate Edition's 30-day free trial.

In addition to IntelliJ's JavaFX plug-in, you need an application named Scene Builder.

![“The 'Settings' window of an Integrated Development Environment (IDE) showing the 'Plugins' section. The user has searched for 'JavaFX' in the plugin marketplace, displaying several related plugins including 'JavaFX,' 'JavaFX Tools,' 'JavaFX Runtime D,' 'TornadoFX,' 'FXMLHelper,' and 'ValueEdge.' The 'JavaFX' plugin by JetBrains s.r.o. is selected and installed, indicated by the blue checkmark and the 'Disable' button. The right panel provides details about the selected 'JavaFX' plugin, including its version (bundled 242.21829.142), features, and integration capabilities. Options to 'Cancel,' 'Apply,' or 'OK' are available at the bottom of the window.”](images/9781394289240-fg1601.png)

[FIGURE 16-1:](#c16.xhtml_rc16-fig-0001) The Plugins option of IntelliJ's Settings dialog box.

#### Installing Scene Builder

Imagine you're at the Louvre, looking at the *Mona Lisa.* You don't think, “da Vinci added a face, and then he put a smile on the face, and then he added a body and then a background.” The painting doesn't progress from one action to another. Instead, the painting simply *is*.

In the same way, you don't have to add text fields and buttons, one by one, to a window in a GUI application. Instead, you can simply describe a window’s elements. You can say, “Here's how the window should look when it’s all finished being rendered.” Then you can let Java decide what steps to take to display the window’s elements.

So, how do you describe an application window's elements? You can do what programmers usually do — type a bunch of text in a code editor. But there’s a better way. The most natural way for you to describe visual elements is to “draw” them.

What if there was a way for you to draw an app's window and have the computer turn your drawing into code that Java can interpret?

Yes! There's a way to do this. Using an app called Scene Builder, you draw a window’s components with your mouse. Scene Builder automatically turns your visual description into code.

Installing Scene Builder is like installing most software. Here's how you do it:

1.  **Visit** [`http://gluonhq.com/products/scene-builder`](http://gluonhq.com/products/scene-builder/)**.**
2.  **Click the Download button.**

    When you do, a list of download options appears.

3.  **Click the button corresponding to your computer's operating system (Windows, Mac, or Linux).**

    As a result, the download begins. On a Windows computer, you get an `.msi` file. Double-click the file to begin the installation.

    On a Mac, you get a `.dmg` file. Depending on your Mac web browser's setting, the browser may or may not expand the `.dmg` file automatically. If not, double-click the `.dmg` file to begin the installation.

4.  **Follow the installation routine's instructions.**
