    On a Windows computer, you accept a bunch of defaults. One of those defaults specifies the location on your hard drive where Scene Builder is to be installed. (On my Windows computer, that location is `C:\Users\bburd\AppData\Local\SceneBuilder`. Don't ask me why.) You’ll need to know this location, so be sure to make a note of it.

    On a Mac, you drag the Scene Builder's icon to the `/Applications` folder.

That's it! You've installed Scene Builder.

### Creating a bare-bones JavaFX project

After installing the JavaFX plug-in and Scene Builder, you can create a GUI application with clicks and keystrokes. Here's what you do:

1.  **Follow the instructions in the earlier section “**[**Setting up your computer**](#c16.xhtml_c16-sec-0003)**.”**
2.  **From IntelliJ's menu bar, choose File ⇒   New ⇒   Project.**

    The New Project dialog box appears. There's a list of items along the left side of the dialog box. (See [Figure 16-2](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0002).)

3.  **In the dialog box's list, select JavaFX. (Refer to** [**Figure** **16-2**](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0002)**.)**
4.  **In the main body of the New Project dialog box, type a name for your project.**

    Typically, you don't have to mess with any other elements in the New Project dialog box's body.

    ![Warning](images/warning.png) Sitting here on my temporal perch in 2024, I notice a glitch in running JavaFX programs on a Mac. When I have some version of Java 23 in the dialog box's JDK drop-down menu, my JavaFX programs crash with a cryptic `NSInternalInconsistencyException` message. Fortunately, when I select some version of Java 17, the programs run just fine.

5.  **At the bottom of the dialog box, click Next.**

    As a result, another New Project dialog box appears. This box offers to add additional libraries to your project. For this chapter's example, you don't need any of these libraries. Leave their check boxes unselected.

6.  **At the bottom of the dialog box, click Create.**

    IntelliJ may ask you if you want your project in the current window or in a new window. Pick whichever you want.

    One way or another, IntelliJ shows you a window with your brand-new JavaFX project. (See [Figure 16-3](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0003).)

![“Screenshot of the 'New Project' window in an Integrated Development Environment (IDE). The window allows the creation of a new JavaFX project named 'MyJavaFXProject'. It includes fields for project location (set to '~/IdeaProjects'), language selection (Java, Kotlin, Groovy), build system (Maven, Gradle), group ID ('com.example'), artifact ID ('MyJavaFXProject'), and JDK version (jbr-17 JetBrains Runtime 17.0.12). The left sidebar lists various project types and generators, such as Java, Kotlin, Groovy, Jakarta EE, Spring Boot, JavaFX, Quarkus, Micronaut, Ktor, Compose for Desktop, HTML, React, Express, Angular CLI, Vue.js, and Vite.”](images/9781394289240-fg1602.png)

[FIGURE 16-2:](#c16.xhtml_rc16-fig-0002) The New Project dialog box.

![“An Integrated Development Environment (IDE) window displaying a Java project named 'MyJavaFXProject'. The project structure on the left includes directories and files such as HelloApplication.java, HelloController.java, and hello-view.fxml. The main editor pane shows the contents of HelloApplication.java, which includes a JavaFX application class extending Application. The code defines a start method that sets up the primary stage with a scene loaded from an FXML file named 'hello-view.fxml', and a main method that calls launch() to start the application.”](images/9781394289240-fg1603.png)

[FIGURE 16-3:](#c16.xhtml_rc16-fig-0003) A new JavaFX project.

### Running your bare-bones JavaFX project

In the previous section, you use IntelliJ IDEA to create a JavaFX project. Here's a brief overview of three files in the JavaFX project:

*   `HelloApplication.java`: This file contains the two methods that get the ball rolling. One of them is the omnipresent `main` method. The other is a method named `start`.

    The `start` method sets up your JavaFX app's initial scaffolding. In particular:

    *   This method calls the `Scene` constructor. The *scene* is a list of all the items that appear at any moment on the user's screen. For example, the scene may include a column with a text field and a button.
    *   JavaFX puts a new window, called a *stage*, on the user's screen. The `start` method connects the scene with this new window. Then the statement `stage.show()` makes this window appear.

*   `HelloController.java`: This file defines actions that can take place while the JavaFX application runs. For example, the code

    `private Label welcomeText;`

    `void onHelloButtonClick() {`
    `welcomeText.setText("Welcome to JavaFX Application!");`
    `}`

    says “When the user clicks the *Hello* button, display *Welcome to JavaFX Application!* on the `welcomeText` label.

*   `hello-view.fxml`: This file describes the layout of the buttons, labels, and other items in your JavaFX application window. The file also connects the graphical components with actions in the aforementioned `HelloController.java` file. For more information, see this chapter's nearby sidebar “[Code soup: mixing XML with Java](#c16.xhtml_c16-sec-0009).”

To run all this code, right-click anywhere inside the `HelloApplication.java` file's Editor panel. Then select Run 'HelloApplication.main()'. After a brief pause, you'll see the stuff in [Figure 16-4](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0004). It's a window with a button inside it. When you click the button, a Welcome message appears.

![“Two side-by-side screenshots of a JavaFX application. The left screenshot shows a window with the title 'Hello!' and a button labeled 'Hello!' in the center. The right screenshot shows the same window after the button has been clicked, displaying the text 'Welcome to JavaFX Application!' above the button. The image demonstrates the change in the user interface after the button is clicked.”](images/9781394289240-fg1604.png)

[FIGURE 16-4:](#c16.xhtml_rc16-fig-0004) Running the sample JavaFX application.

## Adding Stuff to Your JavaFX Project

GUI programs involve visual elements, and the best way to describe visual elements is to draw them. You can draw them with a pencil or draw them with a mouse.

Using Scene Builder and a mouse, you can draw your app's layout and describe the app's components. Scene Builder automatically turns your drawing into XML code.

![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) You probably aren't surprised to hear that instructions for installing Scene Builder are in this chapter's “[Installing Scene Builder](#c16.xhtml_c16-sec-0005)” section. I just figure I'll point that out.

### Where's my Scene Builder application?

IntelliJ IDEA isn't very good at finding Scene Builder on your computer. So, before you try to run Scene Builder, follow these steps:

1.  **Go to IntelliJ's main menu. On Windows, choose File and, on a Mac, choose IntelliJ Idea. In the resulting submenu, choose Settings ⇒ Languages & Frameworks ⇒   JavaFX.**

    Doing so gets you what you see in [Figure 16-5](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0005).

    In the main body of IntelliJ's Settings dialog box, you see a text field labeled Path to SceneBuilder.

2.  **Click the Folder icon on the rightmost edge of the text field and navigate to the SceneBuilder app on your computer's hard drive.**

    On my Mac, the text field says `/Applications/SceneBuilder.app`. (Refer to [Figure 16-5](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0005).) On my Windows computer, the text field says `C:\Users\`*`my_user_name`*`\AppData\Local\SceneBuilder\SceneBuilder.exe`. (Refer to this chapter's “[Installing Scene Builder](#c16.xhtml_c16-sec-0005)” section.)

3.  **Dismiss the Settings dialog box by clicking OK.**

![“Settings window showing the configuration for JavaFX in an Integrated Development Environment (IDE). The path to SceneBuilder is set to '/Applications/SceneBuilder.app'. The left sidebar lists various settings categories, with 'JavaFX' selected under 'Languages & Frameworks'.”](images/9781394289240-fg1605.png)

[FIGURE 16-5:](#c16.xhtml_rc16-fig-0005) Hope and Crosby's “Road to Scene Builder.”

### Building a scene

Having told IntelliJ where to find Scene Builder, you're ready to add features to the JavaFX app. Here's how you do it:

1.  **Find IntelliJ's Project tool window.**

    The Project tool window contains a tree of folders and files. (If you don't see IntelliJ's Project tool window, press the Project tool button. Appendix A, safely stored at [`www.dummies.com/go/javafd9e`](http://www.dummies.com/go/javafd9e), has the details.)

2.  **Expand the branches of the Project tool window's tree until you find the** `hello-view.fxml`**file.**

    The `hello-view.fxml` file is on the `src/main/resources/`*`your_project_package_name`* branch of the tree.

3.  **Right-click the** `hello-view.fxml`**file. From the resulting context menu, choose Open in SceneBuilder.**

    If all goes well, a Scene Builder window appears on your screen. (If all doesn't go well, return to the “[Where's my Scene Builder application?](#c16.xhtml_c16-sec-0011)” section.

    The Scene Builder window contains several areas. (See [Figure 16-6](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0006).)

4.  **Along the left side of the Scene Builder window, look for the TextField component. You'll find it in the Controls part of the Library panel. (See** [**Figure** **16-7**](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0007)**.)**![“Graphical user interface (GUI) of a JavaFX Scene Builder tool. The interface has three main sections: Library on the left, Design area in the center, and Inspector on the right. The Library lists UI components like TableView, TextArea, TextField, and ToggleButton. The Design area shows a VBox containing a Label with the text 'Hello!' and a Button labeled 'Hello!'. The Inspector section is currently empty, indicating no selection. The file being edited is 'hello-view.fxml'.”](images/9781394289240-fg1606.png)

    [FIGURE 16-6:](#c16.xhtml_rc16-fig-0006) Scene Builder starts running.

    ![A screenshot of the JavaFX Scene Builder interface. The window is divided into three sections: On the left, the Library pane lists UI components like TextField, TextArea, and Button under various categories such as Controls, Containers, and Menu. The TextField component is highlighted. The center section displays the Design area with a preview of a UI layout, featuring a TextField and a Button labeled 'Hello!'. On the right, the Inspector pane shows properties of the selected TextField, with the 'fx:id' property set to 'myTextField'.](images/9781394289240-fg1607.png)

    [FIGURE 16-7:](#c16.xhtml_rc16-fig-0007) Your GUI app has a text field.

5.  **Using the mouse, click to select the TextField component and then drag it to the Content panel. In particular, drag it to the empty space above the *Hello!* button.**

    When you release the mouse, a text field appears in the Content panel above the *Hello!* button. Refer to [Figure 16-7](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0007).)

6.  **Click the mouse on the text field in the Content panel.**

    When you do, the Scene Builder's Inspector panel lights up with a set of features belonging to the TextField.

7.  **In the Code part of the Inspector panel, look for the box labeled***fx:id***. (Refer to** [**Figure** **16-7**](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0007)**.)**
8.  **In that***fx:id***box, type the name** myTextField**.**

    Full disclosure: When you type something in the *fx:id* box, you're simply making up a variable name. Rather than type `textField`, you could type `montrose` or `hungadungaAndMcCormick`. But the name `myTextField` is more informative than those other two choices.

9.  **Back in the Content panel, double-click the *Hello!* button.**

    When you do, the word *Hello!* on the face of the button becomes highlighted. Anything you type will replace that word *Hello!*

10.  **On the face of the button, type** Capitalize**. (See** [**Figure** **16-8**](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0008)**.)**
11.  **With the Content panel's button still selected, look at the Code part of the Inspector panel.**

    Notice the Code part's OnAction value. (Refer to [Figure 16-8](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0008).) The default is `onHelloButtonClick`, and there's no reason for you to change it. This tells JavaFX to call a method named `onHelloButtonClick` when the user clicks that button.

12.  **From the Scene Builder's main menu, choose File ⇒   Save.**

    I often forget to perform this step. Don't be like me. Don't forget this step!

![The image shows a graphical user interface (GUI) design tool. The interface is divided into three main sections. The left section contains a list of UI components such as TextField, ToggleButton, TreeTableColumn, and TreeTableView, organized under categories like Gluon, Menu, Miscellaneous, Shapes, Charts, and 3D. The middle section displays a preview area where a TextField and a Button labeled 'Capitalize' are placed. The button is highlighted, indicating it is selected. The right section shows the properties and code configuration for the selected button. It includes fields for setting the button's ID, action, and drag-and-drop events. The 'On Action' field is set to 'onHelloButtonClick'.](images/9781394289240-fg1608.png)

[FIGURE 16-8:](#c16.xhtml_rc16-fig-0008) Tweaking the button.

### Tweaking some code

In the previous section, you finish working with Scene Builder. Now return to the IntelliJ window and glance at the `hello-view.fxml` code in IntelliJ's Editor. I've copied the code right here and used boldface font for all the text that Scene Builder generated.

`<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>`

`<?import javafx.geometry.Insets?>`
`<?import javafx.scene.control.Button?>`
`<?import javafx.scene.control.Label?>`
**`<?import javafx.scene.control.TextField?>`**
`<?import javafx.scene.layout.VBox?>`

`<VBox alignment="CENTER" spacing="20.0" xmlns:fx="http://javafx.com/fxml/1" xmlns="http://javafx.com/javafx/22" fx:controller="com.example.myjavafxproject.HelloController">`
`<padding>`
`<Insets bottom="20.0" left="20.0" right="20.0" top="20.0"/>`
`</padding>`
**`<TextField fx:id="myTextField" />`**

`<Label fx:id="welcomeText"/>`
`<Button onAction="#onHelloButtonClick"` **`text="Capitalize`**`"/>`
`</VBox>`

![Tip](images/tip.png) If the code in the Editor doesn't already have a `TextField` and a `Button` with the word `Capitalize`, you may need to update the contents of the Editor. To do so, navigate away from the `hello-view.fxml` file and then back to that file.

You still have to add some code to the project's `HelloController.java` file.

1.  **Double-click on the** `HelloController`**branch in IntelliJ's Project tool window.**

    More specifically, look for the tree's `src/main/java/`*`your_project_package_name`*`/HelloController` branch. When you double-click this branch, the `HelloController.java` code appears in IntelliJ's Editor.

2.  **Modify the** `HelloController.java`**file as shown in** [**Listing** **16-1**](#c16.xhtml_c16-fea-0001)**.**

    IntelliJ has already created most of the code in [Listing 16-1](#c16.xhtml_c16-fea-0001) for you. The only text you have to think about is the text I've highlighted with boldface font. Here's a quick tour of those parts of the code:

    *   You need `import javafx.scene.control.TextField` because you've added a `TextField` to your app. You may not have to type this declaration. IntelliJ may have added it automatically.
    *   The `myTextField` declaration (and the `@FXML` annotation immediately above it) tell Java to look in the stuff you created with Scene Builder for a `TextField` component whose *fx:id* is `myTextField`.

        ![Crossreference](images/crossreference.png) For the scuttlebutt on Java annotations, refer to [Chapter 10](#c10.xhtml).

    *   The `myTextField.getText().toUpperCase()` code tells Java to get whatever the user has typed in `myTextField` and display an all-uppercase version of that text in the `welcomeText` label.

[LISTING 16-1](#c16.xhtml_rc16-fea-0001) The Finished HelloController.java File

`package com.example.fxdemo;`

`import javafx.fxml.FXML;`
`import javafx.scene.control.Label;`
**`import javafx.scene.control.TextField;`**

`public class HelloController {`
`@FXML`
`private Label welcomeText;`
**`@FXML`**
**`private TextField myTextField;`**

`@FXML`
`protected void onHelloButtonClick() {`
`welcomeText.setText(`**`myTextField.getText().toUpperCase()`**`);`
`}`
`}`

### Running your new project

Now you can put this chapter's hard work to the test:

1.  **In the Project tool window, right-click the** `HelloApplication`**branch and choose Run 'HelloApplication.main()'.**

    Lo and behold! A new application window appears on your screen!

2.  **Type something in the text field.**

    (Try to make it something nice.)

3.  **Click the app's Capitalize button.**

    Wow! An all-uppercase version of the text field's code appears in your app's window. (See [Figure 16-9](#c16.xhtml_c16-fig-0009).)

![Tryitout](images/tryitout.png) Tweak the program in this section for some exciting results. (Okay. The results may be uninteresting to some people, but they're exciting to me!)

![“Two screenshots of a simple graphical user interface application. The left screenshot shows an empty text input field and a button labeled 'Capitalize.' The right screenshot shows the same interface after the button has been clicked, with the text input field containing the sentence 'The capital of “France” is “FRANCE.”' and the text below the input field displaying 'THE CAPITAL OF “FRANCE” IS “FRANCE.”' in all uppercase letters.”](images/9781394289240-fg1609.png)

[FIGURE 16-9:](#c16.xhtml_rc16-fig-0009) … But the *capitol* of France is Paris!

#### COPY TEXT TO A TEXT FIELD

Create a window with a button and two text fields. When the user clicks the button, Java copies text from the first text field to the second text field.

#### COPY BETWEEN TEXT FIELDS

Create a window with two buttons and two text fields. When the user clicks the first button, Java copies text from the first text field to the second text field. When the user clicks the second button, Java copies text from the second text field to the first text field.

#### CREATE A SMALL CALCULATOR

Create a window with two text fields, a button, and a label. The user types a number in one of the text fields and another number in the other text field. When the user clicks the button, Java displays the sum of the two numbers in the label.

Remember that Java distinguishes between `String` values like `"86"` and `int` values like `86`. Java reads `String` values from the text fields. But, to get the sum, you add two `int` values. Then you put a `String` value on the app's label.

People who want to run Java programs but don't *have* to write Java programs should visit [`www.java.com`](https://www.java.com).

## The Horse’s Mouth

Check the official Java API documentation at [`https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api`](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/index.html). And, to settle any argument about the way the language behaves, read the rigorous Java language specification at [`https://docs.oracle.com/javase/specs`](https://docs.oracle.com/javase/specs).

## Join Java User Groups

I happen to share leadership roles in the New York JavaSIG ([`www.javasig.com`](https://www.javasig.com/)) and the Garden State Java User Group ([`www.gsjug.org`](https://www.gsjug.org/)). Both groups have regular online meetings and in-person meetings. Come join us!

## Find the Latest News about Java

Bookmark these three sites:

*   [`https://foojay.io`](https://foojay.io)
*   [`https://dev.java`](https://dev.java/)
*   [`https://forums.oracle.com/ords/apexds/domain/dev-community`](https://forums.oracle.com/ords/apexds/domain/dev-community)

## Find News, Reviews, and Sample Code

For articles by the experts, visit InfoQ at [`www.infoq.com`](https://www.infoq.com) and TheServerSide at [`www.theserverside.com`](https://www.theserverside.com/). You can always find good reading at these two sites.

## Got a Technical Question about Anything?

If you're stuck and need help, search for answers and post questions at Stack Overflow ([`https://stackoverflow.com`](https://stackoverflow.com/)).

You can also post questions at Coderanch: “A friendly place for programming greenhorns” ([`https://coderanch.com`](https://coderanch.com/)). For questions specific to Java, visit [`https://coderanch.com/c/java`](https://coderanch.com/c/java).

## Become Involved in the Future of Java

The *Java Community Process* (JCP) “… is the mechanism for developing standard technical specifications for Java technology. Anyone can register … and participate in reviewing and providing feedback for the Java Specification Requests (JSRs).” For more info about JCP membership, visit [`www.jcp.org/en/participation/membership_ind`](https://www.jcp.org/en/participation/membership_ind).

Chapter 18

# Ten Bits of Advice for New Software Developers

IN THIS CHAPTER

![Bullet](images/check.png) **What to learn**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **How to learn more**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **What to do when you're looking for a job**

![Bullet](images/check.png) **Other free advice**

Ienjoy hearing from the people who read my books. “Nice job!” one reader says. Another reader asks, “Can I run your book's examples on older versions of Java?” Yet another posts this comment: “You're Barry Burd. Does that mean you're related to Larry Bird?”

In all the questions I receive from readers, one popular theme is “What to do next?” More specifically, people ask me what else to learn, what else to read, how to get practice writing software, how to find work, and other questions of that kind. I'm flattered to be asked, but I'm reluctant to think of myself as an authority on such matters. No two people give you the same answers to these questions, and if you ask enough people, you're sure to find disagreement.

This chapter contains ten pieces of advice based on questions I've received from readers. But remember that, in addition to these ten hints for living and learning, I have one additional, overriding piece of advice:

> Think critically about the advice you receive. When in doubt, trust your intuition.

Collect opinions. Talk to people about the issues. Try things and, if they work (or even if they don't work but they show some promise), keep doing them. If they show no promise, try other things. Sharing is important, too. Don't forget to share.

## How Long Does It Take to Learn Java?

The answer depends on you — on your goals, on your existing knowledge, on your capacity to think logically, on the amount of spare time you have, and on your interest in the subject.

The more excited you are about computer programming, the quicker you learn. The more ambitious your goals, the longer it takes to achieve them.

![Remember](images/remember.png) There's no such thing as “knowing all about Java.” No matter how much you know, you always have more to learn. I've written several Java books and, as far as I'm concerned, I've barely scratched the surface.

## Which of Your Books Should I Read?

Funny you should ask! I've written several books, including this book *(Java For Dummies)* and another, similarly titled book, *Beginning Programming with Java For Dummies*.

Both books start from scratch, so you don't need to know anything about app development to read either of these books. But *Java For Dummies* goes twice as fast and covers twice as much material as *Beginning Programming with Java For Dummies*. Which book you read depends on your level of comfort with technical subjects. If you're in doubt about where to start, find some sample pages from any of these books to help you determine which book is best for you.

## Are Books Other than Yours Good for Learning Java?

Jeanne Boyarsky and Victor Grazi have a book titled *Real-World Java*. The book has many good things going for it:

*   It's published by Wiley.
*   It covers the kinds of things all developers (especially Java developers) should know.
*   I'm the book's technical proofreader.
*   It includes a reference to my book.
*   Jeanne and Victor are friends of mine.

So what are you waiting for? Buy *Real-World Java*!

## Which Computer Programming Language(s) Should I Learn?

The answer depends on your goals. If you plan to write code for a living, the answer depends on the job opportunities where you live. The TIOBE Programming Community Index ([`www.tiobe.com/tiobe-index`](https://www.tiobe.com/tiobe-index/)) provides monthly ratings for popular programming languages. But the TIOBE Index might not apply specifically to your situation. In June 2021, the Haskell language ranked only 47th among the languages used around the world. But maybe there's a hotbed of Haskell programming in the town where you live.

Do you want to write applications for large enterprises? Then Java is a must-have language. Do you want to do data science? If so, you should learn Python. Do you want to create web pages? Learn HTML, CSS, and JavaScript.

## Which Skills Other than Computer Coding Should I Learn?

Sorry to disappoint you, but you're asking someone who has an axe to grind. I'm a college professor. I believe that no learning, no matter how impractical it might seem to be, is ever wasted.

If you insist on a more definitive answer, go learn a little about databases. Database work isn't necessarily coding, but it's important stuff. Also, read as much as you can about *software engineering* — the study of techniques for the effective design and maintenance of computer code. Don't be afraid of math, either (because learning math stretches your logical-thinking muscles). And, whenever you can, hone your communication skills. The better you communicate, the more valuable your work is to other people.

## How Should I Continue My Learning as a Software Developer?

Practice, practice, practice. Take the examples you find in my book (or anywhere else) and think of ways to change the code. Add an option here or a button there.

Find out what happens when you try to improve the code. If it works, think of another way to make a change. If it doesn't work, search the documentation for a solution to your problem. If the documentation doesn't help (and often, documentation doesn't help), search the web for answers to your problem. Post questions at an online forum. If you don't find an answer, put aside the problem for a while and let it incubate in your mind.

![Remember](images/remember.png) You don't learn programming by only reading about it — you have to scrape some knuckles while writing code and seeking solutions. Only after trying, failing, and trying again can you appreciate the work involved in developing computer software.

## How Else Should I Continue My Learning as a Developer?

How did you know that I have a second suggestion? I recommend finding like-minded people where you live and getting together with them regularly. These days, you can find tech user groups in almost every corner of the globe. Find a Java user group that meets in your area and attend the group's meetings frequently. If you're a novice, you might not understand much of the discussion, but you'll be exposed to the issues that concern today's Java developers.

Look for more tech groups and attend their meetings. Find meetings about other programming languages, other technologies, and other topics that aren't solely about technology. Meet people face-to-face and find out which topics will be in next year's books.

To complement those face-to-face meetings, search the web for screen-to-screen meetings. You can find free online technical sessions almost any day of the year.

## How Can I Land a Job Developing Software?

Do all the things you'd normally do when you look for a job, but don't forget my answer to the previous question. User groups are fantastic places for networking.

Go to meetings and be a good listener. Don’t think about selling yourself. Be patient and enjoy the ride. I landed a great consulting opportunity only after several years of attending one group’s meetings. In the meantime, I learned a lot about software (and quite a bit about dealing with other people).

## I Still Don’t Know What to Do with My Life

That’s not a question. But it’s okay anyway.

Everyone has to make ends meet. If you manage to put food on your table, the next step is to find out what you love to do. I've spent a lifetime teaching college students, writing books, and developing computer code — and I love doing all of it. (Well, I love most of it. I detest grading papers, and I dislike proofreading my own work.)

Fortunately, I can make money teaching, writing, and developing. I could make more money working 9-to-5 for a big company, managing a software team, or creating the next big start-up, but I don't like doing those things. My life has been enriched because I do what I like doing, whether I'm working or not.

My advice is, find the best match of the things you like to do and the things that help you earn a living. Compromise if you must, but be honest with yourself about the things that make you happy. (Of course, these things shouldn't make other people unhappy.)

Finally, be specific about your likes and dislikes. For example, saying, “I'd like to be rich” isn't specific at all. Saying, “I'd like to create a great game” is more specific, but you can do better. Saying, “I like to design game software, but I need a partner who can do the marketing for me” is quite specific and makes quite a tidy set of goals.

## If I Have Other Questions, How Can I Contact You?

That's easy. Send email to [`javafordummies@allmycode.com`](mailto:javafordummies@allmycode.com). Visit my ugly-but-informative website at [`https://users.drew.edu/bburd/`](https://users.drew.edu/bburd/) and look for the page's Chat with Barry panel. Attach two tin cans to a very long string. Put a note in an old pneumatic tube. Train a carrier pigeon to fly to my office. Hire a chimpanzee to …

## About the Author

**Barry Burd** received a master’s degree in computer science at Rutgers University and a PhD in mathematics at the University of Illinois. As a teaching assistant in Champaign–Urbana, Illinois, he was elected five times to the university-wide List of Teachers Ranked as Excellent by Their Students.

Since 1980, Dr. Burd has been a professor in the Department of Mathematics and Computer Science at Drew University in Madison, New Jersey. He has spoken at conferences in the United States, Europe, Australia, and Asia, and in 2020 he was honored to be named a Java Champion. He is the author of many articles and books, including *Beginning Programming with Java For Dummies* and *Flutter For Dummies*, both from Wiley.

Dr. Burd lives in Madison, New Jersey, with his wife of n years, where n > 44\. In his spare time, he enjoys being a workaholic.
